SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA ARBECHESKY et al. v. UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION BOARD OF REVIEW 

Unemployment compensation-Idleness caused by work stoppage- Refusal of employer to comply with contractural obligation-Lockout. 

An order of the Unemployment Compensation Board of Review denying compensation to claimants on the ground that their unemployment was the result of a strike, which in turn occurred as the result of a labor dispute, will be affirmed, where officials of the union to which claimants belonged ordered a work stoppage following the refusal of their employer to make contributions to a health and welfare fund under a contract between the union and an association to which the employer belonged; in such case evidence that the employer imposed terms of employment by refusing payments into the fund, did not constitute a lockout as defined in section 402 (d) of the Unemployment Compensation Law. 

Appeal from decision of Unemployment Compensation

Board of Review, appeals nos. B-3-H-109 to 143 inclusive. 

Affirmed, 174 Pa. Superior Ct. 217. 

Sidney G. Handler, for appellant. 

William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, for appellee. 

HIRT, J.:

During the period in question in this unemployment compensation case, Carmeda Arbechesky, the present appellant, and 34 others were employed by Larksville Fashions, Inc., a manufacturer of ladies' dresses. All of them were members of Local 249 of the International Ladies' Garment

Workers' Union. This employer in October 1950 became a member of the Pennsylvania Dress Manufacturers Association, Inc., herein referred to as the association. At that time there was a subsisting collective bargaining agreement between that association and the Northeast Department of the International Ladies' Garment Workers' Union.

As a member of the association, the above employer was bound by the terms of the agreement, made on behalf of all existing and future members of the association. 

The association on February 1, 1951, entered into a new collective bargaining agreement with the above Northeast Department which thereafter constituted the union-management contract between Larksville Fashions and Local 249 

of the International Ladies' Garment Workers' Union. The seventh section of the agreement provided: "(a) Each employer agrees to pay to the union at the end of each payroll period a sum equivalent to seven (7%) per cent of its total gross payroll . . . for such period of all its employes covered by this agreement . . . towards the union's health and welfare fund, a trust fund established . . . and maintained by the union in trust for the purpose of providing its members with health, welfare, and recreation benefits and services."

The thirty-third section of the agreement provided: "Any and all disputes, complaints, controversies, claims or grievances whatsoever between the union or any of its members and the association or any of its members, arising under, out of, or in connection with or in any manner relating to this agreement, shall be taken up for adjustment between the respective representatives of the association and the union. Should said representatives fail to agree, such disputes, complaints, controversies, claims or grievances shall be submitted to arbitration before the impartial chairman hereinafter named, or designated as hereinafter provided, as arbitrator, whose decision shall be final and binding."

After becoming a member of the association, Larksville Fashions continued its practice of paying wages to its employes during vacation periods, although vacation pay was

1954] 

 not required by the terms of the above agreement between the association and Northeast Department of the Union.

The employer insisted that it was entitled to some credit for these vacation benefits against the payments into the union's health and welfare fund, stipulated in section seventh (a) of the agreement. When the association indicated that the proposal was not acceptable to it, Larksville Fashions refused to make any payments into the fund and also refused to submit the dispute to arbitration pursuant to the thirty-third section of the union-management contract, above quoted. Thereupon the district officers of the union determined to exert pressure on the employer to compel compliance with its obligation under the contract, by calling a temporary stoppage of work in the plant of the employer. Accordingly, a representative of the local union on May 15, 1951, advised the employes of Larksville 

Fashions not to report for work on the following day. A stoppage of work resulted and continued for about three and a half months and until August 30, 1951, when the union employes returned to work pending negotiations for a settlement of the dispute. 

On May 24, 1951, appellant and her 34 coworkers registered for work. Their claims for benefits were declared invalid by the bureau under section 402 (d) of the Unem ployment Compensation Law, 43 PS §802. On successive appeals, both the referee and the Board of Review affirmed the Bureau. 

Upon agreed facts, the board found: "10. Consistent with the decision to temporarily create a work stoppage officials of the district union requested the union chairlady on May 15, 1951, to advise the employes not to report for work the day following. Those employes who were not contacted reported for work on May 16, 1951, but were advised by the union business agent as to the reason that other employes were not working and they were instructed to leave work at the noon hour. These employes, in compliance with the instructions given to them, left the employer's establishment and, as a consequence, operations of the plant were suspended.

"11. Immediately prior to the stoppage of work by the employes, a shipment of approximately 800 dresses had arrived at the plant of the employer and work was available to the employes when the stoppage was created. "12. In addition to Larksville Fashions, Inc., the employer operated another plant, the Advance Juniors, in New York City, and shortly after the work stoppage at the former plant pickets were established around the premises of the latter plant and a stoppage of work was effected there also. 

There was, however, no relationship between the dispute at Larksville Fashions, Inc., and the dispute at Advance Juniors. "13. No formal picket line was established at Larksville 

Fashions, Inc., but watchers were on hand in the immediate vicinity of the plant to discourage anyone from entering. 

Throughout the period here involved, no work was performed in the employer's plant and the claimant, [and the other 34 employes] who had been advised not to work because the employer had violated the contract by not paying into the health and welfare fund, did not report for work at any time.”

The board in the opinion in this case stated: "That the unemployment of claimant had its inception in a strike of the employes at Larksville Fashions, Inc., is evident. That the strike occurred as a result of a labor dispute is likewise apparent." 

And the board concluded that the work stoppage did not amount to a "lockout", although the strike was precipitated by the failure of Larksville Fashions to make contributions to the health and welfare fund under a contract which imposed that obligation upon it as a member of the Association. Accordingly, because the facts in this case did not bring it within the exception, as to a lockout of section 

402 (d) of the Unemployment Compensation Law, 43 PS $802, the board refused benefits because of a labor dispute, within the intent of that section of the act. Cf. Byerly 

Unemployment Compensation Case, 171 Pa. Superior Ct. 

303, 90 A. 2d 322.

1954] 

In Hogan Unemployment Compensation Case, 169 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 554, 560, 83 A. 2d 386, we said through Judge

Reno: 

"A lockout may be present in varying factual situations, and no definition can comprehend all its manifestations. The core of a lockout is the act of an employer in withholding work, which includes the physical closing of the place of employment, as in Burger Unemployment Compensation 

Case, 168 Pa. Superior Ct. 89, 77 A. 2d 737, but is not limited to the existence of that condition. An employer may impose a lockout without physically closing his plant or without forbidding access to it by his employes: Barnes v. 

Hall, 285 Ky. 160, 146 S. W. 2d 929."

But proof of a lockout in the present case, is not made out by evidence that the employer, as charged by the appellant and her coemployes, imposed terms of employment by refusing payments into the welfare fund, which it is alleged compelled them to work under less favorable conditions than those which the employer was bound to supply under its contractual obligation. "The Unemployment Compensation Law was not intended to promote stoppages of work by employes because of disputes with employers which could be legally determined without any cessation of work. . . . To call a suspension of work an act in concert to prevent a continuance of operations, without resorting to legal measures open to determine the rights of the parties, amounts to a voluntary suspension of work, with the necessary consequences provided by the Act": Miller v. Unemployment Compensation 

Board, 152 Pa. Superior Ct. 315, 31 A. 2d 740. On this principle, the board properly refused benefits in this case. 

There were adequate available legal measures open to appellant. The union had a remedy in an equity action to compel performance of its agreement with the employer, arising from the employer's membership in the association. By mandatory decree in an action in equity, arbitration of the dispute could have been imposed on the employer without stoppage of the work. Cf. Joint Board of Waist and Dressmakers' Union v. Rosinsky, 173 Pa. Superior Ct. 303, 306,

98 A. 2d 447. Moreover, these employes, even under their theory of a lockout, had an available remedy in assumpsit for the amount of lost wages, under section twenty-sixth of the contract, in the event the employer refused to resume operations on demand. Cf. Morris Unemployment Compensation Case, 169 Pa. Superior Ct. 564, 83 A. 2d 394;

Glen Alden Coal Co. v. Unemployment Compensation Board, 

168 Pa. Superior Ct. 534, 79 A. 2d 796.

The employer in this case had work for all of its employes and was ready and willing to continue operations of its plant. By acceding to the union's request, appellant and her coemployes and not their employer must be charged with responsibility for the work stoppage. 

Order affirmed. 

Ross, J., dissents.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD GINSBERG v. STATE WORKMEN'S INSURANCE FUND et al. 

Workmen's compensation-Permanent total disability—Receipt of 

Social Security benefits following 500 weeks of disability-Bar to compensation payments-Section 306(a) of the Act of 1937. 

The receipt of social security benefits by a claimant who has suffered permanent total disability in an accident for which he has received compensation for 500 weeks is a bar to further compensation payments, under Section 306 (a) of The Workmen's Compensation 

Act of June 4, 1937, P. L. 1552, where the Social Security payments are in excess of $30 per month. 

Appeal by defendant from order of Referee Fair. District 

No. 8, compensation agreement no. 9064352, A-38424. 

Reversed. December 16, 1953.

State Workmen's Insurance Fund, insurance carrier. 

Joseph Solomon, for claimant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for defendant.

1954] 

 MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

Defendant, Klinger Mills, Inc., has taken this appeal from 

Referee Fair's order dismissing its petition for termination of compensation payments due claimant, Abraham Ginsberg. The record discloses that claimant suffered permanent total disability in an accident on May 22, 1939, in the course of his employment by defendant. Claimant has received compensation for 500 weeks at the rate of $15.21 per week and thereafter at the rate of $30 per month, from December 24, 1948, to August 31, 1952, in accordance with the provisions of The Workmen's Compensation Act of 

June 4, 1937, P. L. 1552, in force at the time of the accident.

Defendant disclaims further compensation liability because claimant has been receiving Social Security payments since September 1, 1952, at the rate of $32 per month. 

The question may be stated briefly to be whether the receipt of Social Security benefits is a bar to compensation payments under section 306 (a) of the Act of June 4, 1937, P. L. 1552. No brief was submitted in behalf of claimant. 

Section 306 (a) provides: 

". . . Provided, That, if total disability shall continue for a period of four weeks or more, the employee also shall be entitled to receive compensation for the first seven days of total disability. Should total disability become permanent, then, in addition to the compensation provided for five hundred weeks, and beginning at the expiration of the five hundred weeks, the sum of thirty dollars per month shall be payable during such permanent total disability prior to death: Provided, however, that such monthly payments shall cease if the person receiving compensation accepts other public funds contributed to by the employer which equal or exceed the amount of compensation payments." 

The precise question, then, to be determined is whether the Social Security benefits constituted "public funds" contributed to by the employer within the contemplation and intendment of the act. In establishing the system of Social 

Security under the Federal Act, both the employer and the employe made contributions to the fund, which was created

to alleviate the financial burden of workmen who have ceased to labor: Social Security Board v. Nierotko, 66 S. Ct. 

637, 327 U. S. 358, 90 L. Ed. 718, 162 A. L. R. 1445.

We had occasion to consider a similar question in the case of Duff v. Pennsylvania Railroad Co., A-34784 (Board 

1949) in which an injured employe received compensation for the full 500 weeks period, was then receiving compensation at the rate of $30 monthly when the defendant filed a petition for termination on the grounds that claimant was receiving a pension under the Federal Railroad Retirement

Act of 1937 in excess of $30 per month. In that case, too, the question was whether the pension annuities received by that claimant were "public funds". What we said in that case, in which we reversed the referee's order dismissing defendant's petition, is pertinent and appropriate to the instant case and for that reason, is now repeated here: "It, therefore, becomes necessary to determine whether or not the annuities received by the claimant, under the Railroad Retirement Act, are public funds within the meaning of the Compensation Act of 1937. "The term 'public funds' has received various interpretations. A study of the textbooks and cases dealing with this subject discloses that the accepted definition of 'public funds' is funds belonging to any municipality or government raised by the operation of law arising from taxation, duties and the like. In 50 Corpus Juris 854, the term is defined as follows: 

""The term "public funds" means funds belonging to the state or any county or any subdivision of the state; more specifically, taxes, customs, money, etc., raised by the operation of some general law, and appropriated by the government to the discharge of its obligations, or for some public or governmental purpose. . . .' "A more comprehensive definition is set forth in Vol. 42, American Jurisprudence 718, sec. 2: 

"Public funds are moneys belonging to the United States or a corporate agency of the Federal Government, a State or subdivision thereof or a municipal corporation. They represent moneys raised by operation of law for the support

1954] 

 of the Government or for the discharge of its obligations. 

In other words, they constitute "revenue" which in turn is defined as "the income of the government arising from taxation, duties and the like"." "The aforesaid definition of public funds has been cited with approval in Lower Colorado River Authority v. Chemical Bank & Trust Co., 185 S. W. 2d 461; Wood Brothers 

Construction Co. v. Bagley, State Treasurer, 6 N. W. (2d) 397. "In Bedford v. White, 106 P. (2d) 469, where an action was brought by two justices of the Supreme Court for pensions alleged to be due them as retired justices, the court held: "All of the beneficiaries of these laws are paid benefits from revenues arising in whole or in part from the exercise of the state's power to tax. Even in those cases where partial payment is made by withholding a part of the salary derived from tax produced revenue, it has been held that such amounts merely to a reduction in salary and that the funds so withheld to be applied to pensions are public funds.' "In Gall v. City of Wheeling, 192 S. E. 116, 112 A. L. R. 

477, it was held that pension funds for the benefit of the members of the fire and police departments of a municipal corporation created from tax levies and assessments are public funds of the municipal corporation. See also Lawrence v. American Surety Co., 249 N. W. 3, 88 A. L. R. 535. 

In Speer v. School Directors of Blairsville, 50 Pa. 156, the question raised was the right of a borough to borrow money to procure volunteers to the Army by means of the payment of a bounty. The court in holding that money could be borrowed stated that the payment of a bounty is not a private transaction in which the individual involved benefits but was a benefit to the public. That it was not voluntary to those who pay it nor was it a confiscation of one man's property to another's use but was a contribution of the public treasurer for the general good and was a direct appropriation for a public purpose. The court held in effect, that the

moneys so borrowed were public funds and subject to appropriation by the proper authority. 

"The Railroad Retirement Act of 1937, 45 U. S. C. A., 

Sec. 228a, provides for the payment of annuities to employees of railroads designated in the Act. The eligibility requirements being set forth in Sec. 228b and the amount of the payments being computed in accordance with the provisions of Sec. 228c. 

"The Carriers Taxing Act of 1937, 45 U. S. C. A., Sec. 

261, which is a companion act to the Railroad Retirement Act, provides in Sec. 262 that employees who are to be the beneficiaries under the Railroad Retirement Act shall pay an income tax on a percentage basis as therein provided. 

Sec. 263 of the Act provides that the employer shall pay an excise tax with respect to the individuals in his employment as therein provided. Sec. 267 of the Act provides that the taxes imposed by the Act shall be collected by the Bureau of Internal Revenue and shall be paid into the treasury of the United States as revenue collections. It is to be noted that payment is made to the Collector of Internal Revenue, in the same manner as other taxes collected by the Bureau of Internal Revenue and that there is no provision made for the segregation of the funds collected. It is also to be noted that Sec. 228c of the Railroad Retirement Act provides that there should be created an account in the Treasury of the 

United States to be known as Railroad Retirement account and provides for an appropriation to said account of an amount sufficient for the payment of all annuity benefits and death benefits. In other words, the moneys are collected and paid into the Treasury of the United States in the same manner as other funds and taxes are collected, and thereafter are paid out in specified amounts by means of an annual appropriation. "We have, therefore, concluded that since the moneys are paid by the employee as income tax and by the employer as an excise tax and is collected by the Bureau of Internal Revenue in the same manner as for the collection of other taxes and is paid into the Treasury of the United States without being earmarked for any special purpose but is co-

1954] 

 mingled with other moneys paid into the Treasury of the United States and considering also that any payments made from the fund created under the Railroad Retirement Act is by virtue of appropriation of Congress that the funds are public funds within the meaning of the Compensation Act of 1937. "Since Sec. 263 of the Carriers Taxing Act of 1937 requires the payment of an excise tax by the employer with respect to the individuals in its employment, it is obvious that this condition of the Compensation Act of 1937 is met. "It is admitted in this case that the payments to the claimant in the form of an annuity under the provisions of the Railroad Retirement Act of 1937 exceeds the sum of $30.00 a month and this provision of the Compensation 

Act of 1937 is likewise met. "It is apparent, therefore, that the proviso of The Workmen's Compensation Act of 1937, which provides that payments shall cease if the person receiving compensation accepts other public funds contributed to by the employer which equal or exceed the amount of compensation payments have been clearly established in this case and that, by reason thereof, claimant is not entitled to further payments of compensation. "We are likewise of the opinion that there is no merit to the contention of the claimant that the 'public funds' referred to in the Compensation Act of 1937 refers to public funds solely of the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania. It is our opinion that if the Legislature so intended it would have made a specific provision to that effect and in the absence of such provision, it must be construed as public funds not only of the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania but of the government of the United States. The Statutory Construction Act of May 28, 1937, P. L. 1019, provides: ""The object of all interpretation and construction of laws is to ascertain and effectuate the intention of the Legislature. Every law should be construed, if possible, to give effect to all its provisions. When the words of the law are clear and free from all ambiguity the letter of it is not to be disregarded under the pretext of pursuing its spirit.'

"We are of the opinion that the Act is clear and free from ambiguity and to insert a provision in the Act that the term 'public funds' applies only to funds of the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania would be to effectuate an interpretation clearly not intended by the Legislature and would be, in effect, usurpation of legislative authority."

An analogous situation is presented in this case, and we are of the opinion that the decision in the Duff v. Pennsylvania Railroad Co., supra, is applicable and controlling.

We hereby amend the referee's findings of fact, by adding thereto the following

Findings of Fact 

11. We find as a fact, that claimant has been receiving $32 per month since September 1, 1952, under the Federal 

Social Security Act. 

12. We further find as a fact, that by virtue of the Social 

Security Act, defendant has made contributions thereunder to the Bureau of Internal Revenue of the United States. 

As amended, the referee's findings of fact are affirmed. 

We hereby vacate and set aside the referee's conclusions of law, and in lieu thereof, we substitute the following 

Conclusion of Law 

That since the pension received by claimant pursuant to the Social Security Laws is from public funds within the meaning of The Workmen's Compensation Act of 1937, con tributed to by the employer, which payments exceed the amount of monthly compensation payments of $30, defendant's petition should be granted and further payments of compensation terminated.

We hereby vacate the referee's order of dismissal and substitute therefor, the following Order 

Petition filed on behalf of defendant, Linger Mills, Inc., is herewith granted, and further payments of compensation to claimant, Abraham Ginsberg, are terminated, as of September 1, 1952. 

The appeal of defendant is sustained.

1954] 

 COMMON PLEAS OF LUZERNE COUNTY WELSH v. POWER ENGINEERING CORPORATION 

Workmen's compensation. Accidental injury - Rupture of inter vertebral disc from lifting-Aggravation of prior existing condition. 

Evidence that claimant, while assisting in the installation of a large fan which was to be installed on the fourth floor of a building and lifted by means of a derrick, was injured while attempting to remove the fan proper from its housing by lifting the axle with his arms and pushing it with his chest when the axle suddenly loosened with a jerk that caused a severe pain in the claimant's back, subsequently diagnosed as a ruptured intervertebral disc, was not sufficient to support an award, where the uncontradicted medical testimony was to the effect that claimant had a degenerative disease of the disc prior to the date of the occurence, and that if he had had a sound and normal disc he would not have been afflicted with the extrusion of the disc from the lifting.

Appeal from Workmen's Compensation Board. C. P. of 

Luzerne County, December term, 1952, no. 1390. Reversed. 

July 31, 1953. 

Hon. Arthur H. James, for claimant. 

Louis Wagner and White, Rowlands & Hourigan, for defendant. 

FLANNERY, J.: 

Defendant has appealed from a decision of the Workmen's Compensation Board affirming an award by the referee. The principal question raised by this appeal is: Do the facts of the case bring it within the well established rule of Gavula v. Sims Company, 155 Pa. Superior Ct. 206, which held that a compensable injury may occur in the course of normal duties of an employe when, without overexertion, a strain, sprain or twist causes a break or sudden change in the physical structure or tissues of the body, that is, a fracture or rupture? 

The court said in Rovere v. Interstate Cemetery Co., 164

Pa. Superior Ct. 233, at page 237, that the rule applies in that class of cases involving the "disability of a normal,

healthy workman, with no physical weakness, other than is common to all men". Is this case in that class?

The undisputed facts are as follows: On September 19, 1950, claimant was assisting in the installation of a large fan at the Sequoit Silk Company in Scranton. The fan weighed between 600 and 700 pounds and was to be installed on the fourth floor of the building. It was to be lifted there by means of a derrick. To facilitate the lifting of the equipment, claimant and his co-employes decided to remove the fan proper from its housing. To do this, one end of a chain which was hanging from the boom of the derrick was attached to one end of the fan axle. The position of the derrick was such that it would lift and pull the fan at the same time. Claimant placed himself on the opposite side of the fan housing, lifting his end of the axle while the derrick was lifting and pulling the other end. Claimant was lifting the axle with his arms and pushing it with his chest. This combined force, i.e., by the claimant and the derrick, suddenly loosened the axle and pulled it partly out of the fan housing. As it did so, claimant, while still holding the axle, was pulled against the fan housing and thereupon felt a severe pain in his back. 

On the following day, September 20, 1950, claimant notified his employer that he had been injured by an accident on the prior day. On September 21, 1950, claimant was unable to get out of bed and called Dr. Allen. On October 30, 1950, claimant entered the Robert Packer Hospital, where he remained until November 14, 1950. On 

December 28, 1950, he again entered the Robert Packer

Hospital, remaining there until February 13, 1951. During this stay at the hospital, two operations were performed. 

On December 30, 1950, a lumbar exploration on the right side was done, and the intervertebral disc at the fourth lumbar interspace was removed. On January 19, 1951, the remainder of the intervertebral disc, together with loose pieces of cartilage, were removed. Subsequent to the second operation claimant still complained of severe pain in the back and left hip. He complained of a great deal of pain in his legs and his arms. The pain extended to the joints of the fingers, the wrists, the shoulders and the musculature

1954] 

 of the arms. The exact relation of the complication involving the arms to that of the back was not clear from a medical point of view, but the condition followed immediately the second operation, and it was assumed the conditions were connected. The final diagnosis on the admission was that claimant had suffered a ruptured intervertebral disc. 

The referee found as a fact that claimant, "while still holding the axle, was jerked and thrown against the fan housing", and that "on September 19, 1950, claimant was injured by an accident in the regular course of his employment with defendant". The Workmen's Compensation Board found that the referee's findings of fact were warranted by competent and credible testimony and said: "The slipping of the axle shaft, throwing the claimant to the fan, constituted an accident within the meaning of the Workmen's Compensation Act. That accident, according to the medical testimony, resulted in a rupture to the claimant's intervertebral disc which caused claimant's total disability until November 30, 1951. "The defendant would have us frame the question as follows: 

"Where claimant's existing and progressive disease of the intervertebral disc is aggravated by his heavy work, which did not require any materially greater amount of exertion, risk or exposure than that to which he was ordinarily subjected, is the resulting disability compensable?" 

They cite Rathmell v. Wesleyville Borough, 351 Pa. 14, for the proposition that the aggravation of an employe's disease is not compensable unless caused by a risk materially greater than he was exposed to in the ordinary course of his employment. They contend that in the instant case claimant's disability is due to some disease antedating the accident, and the claim is that the disease has been aggravated by normal working conditions. They concede that claimant's condition could have been aggravated by the "occurrence" complained of, but they deny that the "occurrence" was so out of the ordinary as to be compensable in an occupation where the movement of heavy machinery is known to be sudden. They concede the possibility of a causal

relation between the heavy lifting and the aggravation of claimant's diseased back on the testimony of Dr. King, but they contest its legal sufficiency because the claimant did not stress his heavy lifting, considered by Dr. King, but stressed the "movement" of the fan, as to which Dr. King expressed no opinion.

As we see it, two questions are involved: 

1. Was claimant a sound, healthy man or did he have a disease which was aggravated by his work; and 

2. Did the movement of the fan constitute an accident within the purview of the Act resulting in the injuries complained of.

With respect to the question of claimant's physical condition, the referee found: "4. In August, 1927, claimant was involved in an automobile accident in which he injured the cervical portion of the spine. At that time, there was no involvement of the thoracic or lumbar spine. "5. In June, 1950, while installing a refrigeration machine for defendant in an establishment in Milton, Pa., he was required to do some very heavy lifting. While doing so, he felt a severe pain in his back. At that time, he lost three days work on account of the pain in his back. "6. Claimant has worn a supporting corset for several years prior to September 19, 1950. "7. In August, 1950, while working for defendant in 

Honesdale, Pa., claimant felt a pain in his back." 

The findings of fact are inadequate on the issue posed. 

As we view the record, a finding that claimant was a sound, healthy man on the date of the accident would be against the weight of the evidence, without support in the record. 

Dr. Arthur B. King, claimant's witness, testified: 

1. That claimant suffered a rupture of herniation of his intervertebral disc on September 19, 1950; 

2. That on that date there was an actual extrusion, "a piece of cartilage actually fell out of the capsule of the disc and was lying free in the canal"; 

3. That claimant had a degenerative disease of his intervertebral disc prior to September 19, 1950; 

4. That such a condition is generally without symptoms;

1954] 

5. That an unusual physical effort is required to extrude the disc, and normally "they don't recover once it is extruded"; 

6. That movements of the back including normal lifting, bending, etc., as well as abnormal movements may cause their extrustion and impingement on the nerve supply which produces in turn the pain which the patient experiences. 

7. That the only thing indicated by the history is that claimant "was lifting a heavy object on September 19th and felt something snap in his back"; 

8. That from the history, he did not know that the lifting done by claimant was above and beyond his normal activities, but that the lifting done aggravated the back condition and brought on the attack of pain; 

9. That "the final episode was a combination of a series of trauma to his back that he had received over a period of years"; 

10. That the spinal catastrophe suffered by claimant derived directly from the degenerated condition of the disc; and, 11. That if claimant had a sound normal disc, he would not have been afflicted with the extrusion of the intervertebral disc from normal effort. 

Owatt v. Rodman's Beverage, 169 Pa. Superior Ct. 339, involved an injury to the intervertebral disc. Claimant was performing his usual work in the usual manner, and his claim petition charged an injury compensable under the rule of Gavula v. Sims Company, supra. In directing that claimant be granted his request for rehearing, the court noted that he was prepared to correct a previous omission by offering proof of his prior good health. The court also noted the affidavit of claimant's surgeon, "that if called as a witness, he would testify that in his opinion the rupture of the disc resulted from the injury in September 1946," and that the "injury is of a type not likely to occur unless some unusual exertion, strain or twist is made." 

But that is not the situation here.

In view of the unchallenged testimony of Dr. King, claimant's witness, we are clearly of the opinion the instant

record cannot support a finding that claimant was a sound, healthy man on the date of the accident. According to this witness, the nature of intervertebral disc rupture is such that normal movements, as well as abnormal movements, are sufficient to accelerate the progress of the disease. It is true here that claimant did not know of his affliction. Yet the result, though unexpected to the claimant, is not unusual or abnormal from a medical point of view. 

There remains then the question whether the movement of the fan constitutes a compensable accident. And as the court said in Rathmell v. Wesleyville Borough, 351 Pa. 14, at page 16: "To constitute a compensable 'accident' it must be shown that the work in which he was engaged at the time of the occurrence was of a different nature and required a materially greater amount of exertion, risk or exposure, than that to which he was ordinarily subjected, so as to justify a conclusion that the increased severity of the work was the cause of, and not merely coincidental with, the aggravation of the previously existing infirmity." 

On September 19, 1950, claimant and another employe were removing a large fan from its housing. The fan weighed between six and seven hundred pounds and had to be dismantled before it would pass into the building. Claimant was on the right side lifting and shoving on the shaft which was stuck in the casting. He testified that when it "let loose and swung out it jerked (him) against the fan”, that when he raised up "and gave it a heave, it slipped out and throwed (him) against the fan".

Claimant further testified that he had been working for defendant since 1934, moving and installing heavy machinery; that the fan weighed about the same as other machinery he normally handled; that the method used on this job was the same method used on other jobs; that the heavy lifting was part of his regular duties; that the dismantling was being handled as usual; that the stuck fan was not unusual, there being normally "very little clearance for the fan to come out"; that the operation normally required considerable maneuvering; that when he lifted and heaved the

1954] 

 shaft slipped about a foot, with the crane on the other end pulling; that practically immediately he felt pain from the "jerk".

Sheridan June, the other employe, was standing on the other side of the fan. He testified that he was standing on that side to guide or steer the fan out through the opening; that "when it finally did come free, it came shooting out a little too fast" and he "jerked out of there"; that they usually ease out but this one came out too fast; that he always stood to one side, out of line with the direction the axle would travel because he knew big heavy equipment moved suddenly at times.

We agree with defendant's contention that claimant was making the customary effort in the customary way to free the axle, that it moved suddenly, and that this cannot be regarded as something so unexpected and unforeseen as to be outside the normal hazards of employment. The fan was removed from its casing as planned; the procedure followed was the normal one and both employes knew that heavy machinery moved suddenly.

It is also to be noted that while claimant testified the cause of his injury was the jerk as he was pulled against the fan housing, his medical testimony attributed the rupture to heavy lifting. Both experiences were common to claimant's employment.

Neither the fact that the axle stuck nor the fact that it suddenly came free and pulled claimant against the housing unit, with, as he described it, a "jerk", were unusual circumstances under the evidence. This situation had been encountered by claimant before in the course of his regular employment. In this case it cannot be said that there was an "accident" in the ordinary sense or within the meaning of the Act. As the court said in Cripin v. Leedom & Worrall 

Co., 341 Pa. 325, at page 328: "We have held that disability or death merely hastened by the work in which the employe has been regularly engaged (the situation before us) cannot be treated as accidental."

The facts of this case place it within the scope of the rule laid down in Rathmell v. Wesleyville Borough, supra, and outside the scope of the rule of Gavula v. Sims Company, supra.

The conclusion then is inescapable. 

At the time of the occurrence of which complaint is made, claimant was not a "normal healthy workman”, as required in Rovere v. Interstate Cemetery Co., supra, so that the rule laid down in Gavula v. Sims Company, supra, does not apply.

What has been described as an accident stands revealed as an occurrence not unusual in the course of claimant's regular employment. Were he a well man at the time, such an experience might be regarded under these authorities as an accident and, therefore, compensable. But, unfortunately, by the testimony of his own witness, claimant was not a "normal healthy workman", and, under such circumstances, the damage to the physical structure must be regarded merely as an aggravation or progressive deterioration with which the security of his work was a coincidence rather than a cause, as held in Rathmell v. Wesleyville Borough, supra. Such is the claimant's own testimony and we are bound by it. There is in the record no competent evidence to sustain the award. 66 Since we are of the opinion that the record is devoid of proof as to facts material to the award, we are not obliged to return the record to the board: Vorbnoff v. Mesta Machine Co., 286 Pa. 201, 210. As was pointed out in Apker v. Crown Can Co., 150 Pa. Superior Ct. 302, at page 304: "... where the Board makes an award and there is no evidence to sustain it and nothing to indicate that anything was omitted from the record which could have been proven, the Court has the power and duty to enter judgment for the employer without sending back the record. **** It has been frequently pointed out that in such cases the legislature did not intend that the Court should perform the senseless function of returning the record for the Board to enter the only judgment possible under its ruling. ****” 

And see Haas v. Brotherhood of Transportation Workers,

1954] 

21 158 Pa. Superior Ct. 291, where the court of common pleas reversed the board on a question of law and directed the entry of judgment for the plaintiff-claimant. This case does not involve the situation present in Owatt v. Rodman's 

Beverage, supra, where the record showed that the plaintiff on rehearing was prepared to offer proof of his previous good health. 

Accordingly, now, July 31, 1953, at 10 a.m., the decision of the Workmen's Compensation Board is reversed, the appeal of defendant is sustained and judgment is entered in favor of defendant Power Engineering Corporation and against claimant Fred Welsh.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA MARABLE v. UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION BOARD OF REVIEW 

Unemployment compensation-Voluntarily quitting work-Refusal of employer to grant paid vacations.

An order of the Unemployment Compensation Board refusing benefits on the ground that claimant voluntarily left her employment without good cause will be affirmed, where the board found that claimant quit work because her employer refused to grant paid vacations, there being sufficient competent evidence to sustain the board's finding. 

Appeal from decision of Unemployment Compensation 

Board of Review, decision no. B-33308. Affirmed, 175 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 34. 

Rufus S. Watson, for appellant. 

William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, and Robert E. Woodside, Attorney General, for appellee. 

GUNTHER, J.: 

Claimant left her job in August, 1952, and immediately applied for unemployment compensation. The bureau refused her benefits on the ground that she had voluntarily

left her employment without good cause. The referee affirmed the bureau, the Board of Review affirmed the referee, and claimant has appealed. 

Claimant was a nurse in a reducing salon. On Wednesday, August 6, 1952, she and her fellow employes attended a meeting at which they requested paid vacations. The employer refused their requests. Claimant was paid on Friday, August 8, 1952, and failed to appear for work the next morning. The following Monday she applied for benefits and alleged on her application the following reasons: "Employer's rash treatment of profanity and etc. Dissatisfaction with working 54 hours for $27.00 per week."

Claimant and her witnesses testified that the employer, at the aforesaid meeting, referred to the employes as "nigger nurses" and used the phrase "God damn". Claimant maintained that she had been insulted and decided to leave her employment, but remained through the next two days in order to get her pay check. The employer denied claimant's allegations of profanity and slurring remarks, and her witnesses attested to her friendliness toward her colored employes. The board found as facts that the employer did not make the alleged remarks and that claimant left her job because of dissatisfaction due to the refusal of paid vacations. Claimant objects to the board's findings. 

It is the board's duty to determine credibility, and if the findings of fact are consistent with each other and the conclusions of law, and can be sustained without a capricious disregard of the evidence, the order will be affirmed: Bako 

Unemployment Compensation Case, 171 Pa. Superior Ct. 

222, 90 A. 2d 309. In light thereof, we find no error in the board's ruling. The employer flatly denied the alleged remarks. Her witnesses attested to her feelings toward colored employes and general absence of profanity. Moreover, claimant's statement on her application of dissatisfaction with her wages tends to support the findings, as well as her remaining two days after the alleged insults. There was sufficient competent evidence to sustain the board's findings, and there was no capricious disregard of competent evidence as contended by claimant. .

1954] 

The findings of fact of the board, which we affirm, hold that claimant voluntarily quit work because her employer refused to grant paid vacations. This is clearly not a necessitous or compelling circumstance which would constitute good cause, and claimant is therefore ineligible for benefits under §402 (b) of the Unemployment Compensation Law. 

See McGuire v. Unemployment Compensation Board, 169 

Pa. Superior Ct. 467, 82 A. 2d 324. 

Decision affirmed.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA KAMINSKI v. UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION BOARD OF REVIEW 

Unemployment compensation-Voluntarily leaving work-Absence of good cause.

Unemployment compensation was properly disallowed to a claimant where it appears that he left work without stating any reason, later expressing his intention to return to school, it further appearing that he made no effort to reestablish his employment when he did not resume his studies.

Appeal from Unemployment Compensation Board of 

Review. Affirmed, 174 Pa. Superior Ct. 242.

Norbert J. Kaminski, for appellant, in propria persona. 

William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, for appellee.

PER CURIAM: 

The only question presented in this unemployment compensation case is whether claimant had reasons for leaving his employment, which were sufficiently compelling and necessitous as to constitute good cause under section 402 (b) of the Unemployment Compensation Law of December 5, 1936, P. L. (1937) 2897, as amended, 43 PS §802 (b).

The appeal by claimant is from the decision of the Unemployment Compensation Board of Review, affirming the referee's disallowance of benefits.

Claimant admittedly terminated his employment with the Pioneer Construction Company, of Mount Carmel, Pa., of his own accord on September 12, 1952. Therefore the burden of establishing good cause for the separation was upon claimant: Kaylock Unemployment Compensation Case, 165 

Pa. Superior Ct. 376, 67 A. 2d 801.

Claimant had worked intermittently, when he was not attending school, as a truck driver for the Pioneer Construction Company up to September 12, 1952. Work was available for him after that date if he had desired to remain employed. His testimony discloses that he made no effort to maintain or reëstablish the employment relationship with his employer. He testified: "Q. What reason did you give the company for leaving? "A. That I was just leaving the job. I gave them a week's notice. "Q. Didn't tell them why? "A. No, I didn't."

The reason for leaving his job on September 12, 1952, as subsequently stated by claimant, was to return to school. 

But his school attendance was a matter of personal choice rather than a necessity and does not constitute good cause for leaving work: Pelecorich Unemployment Compensation 

Case, 172 Pa. Superior Ct. 646, 94 A. 2d 154.

When he did not resume his studies, claimant did not again contact his employer, and he made no effort to reëstablish his employment. Alleged dissatisfaction with his employment appears to be an afterthought, as he never registered any complaint when he voluntarily quit work. 

Claimant failed entirely to show any meritorious or compelling reason for the voluntary termination of his employment. See Wescoe Unemployment Compensation Case, 166 

Pa. Superior Ct. 355, 357, 71 A. 2d 837. 

Decision is affirmed.

1954] 

25 

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA 

CUSTER, etc., v. REITZ COAL CO. et al. 

Workmen's compensation-Dependency-Grandmother standing in loco parentis.

A grandmother standing in loco parentis to a deceased employe is entitled to death benefits under The Workmen's Compensation Act, where deceased was killed during the course of his employment and where he had lived with claimant since he was an infant and was her sole means of support. 

Appeal from order of C. P. of Somerset County, C. D. 

term, 1952, no. 782. Affirmed, 174 Pa. Superior Ct. 595. 

Robert G. Rose, with him Francis A. Dunn, Paul E. C.

Fike, Joseph N. Cascio, associate counsel, and Fine & Cascio, for appellants. 

Harold Kaminsky, with him William P. Kelly, for appellee. 

GUNTHER, J.: 

The sole question in this appeal is whether a grandmother standing in loco parentis to a deceased employe is entitled to death benefits under The Workmen's Compensation Act. 

The deceased was killed during the course of his employment in a coal mine. He had lived with claimant, his grandmother, since he was one month old and was her sole means of support. He was unmarried and had no other dependents. 

The referee denied compensation, but the board set aside the referee's conclusion of law and awarded compensation in an appropriate amount. The court below affirmed the board, and the defendants have appealed. 

The applicable portion of The Workmen's Compensa tion Act of June 2, 1915, P. L. 736, art. III, sec. 307, as amended by the Act of May 14, 1949, P. L. 1369, sec. 1, 77

PS §561, provides: 

"In case of death, compensation shall be computed on the following basis, and distributed to the following persons: . . .

"5. If there be neither widow, widower, nor children entitled to compensation, then to the father or mother, if dependent to any extent upon the employe at the time of the accident, twenty-five per centum of wages, but not in excess of seven dollars and fifty cents per week: Provided, however, that in the case of a minor child who has been contributing to his parents, the dependency of said parents shall be presumed: And provided further, that if the father or mother was totally dependent upon the deceased employe at the time of the accident, the compensation payable to such father or mother shall be forty-five per centum of wages, but not in excess of thirteen dollars and seventy-five cents per week."

The Workmen's Compensation Act must be liberally construed to achieve its broad humanitarian purpose: Harvey v. Philadelphia Warehouse and Cold Storage Co., 167 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 435, 74 A. 2d 815. The word "mother" is not defined in the act and, therefore, becomes a question of statutory construction for the court. In line with the policy of liberal construction, this court in Ottavi v. Timothy 

Burke Stripping Co., 140 Pa. Superior Ct. 389, 14 A. 2d 

188, construed the term "mother" to include an adoptive mother. That case differed in its facts from this one only in that there the mother went through formal adoption proceedings. Otherwise, the rationale of the Ottavi case is applicable here, and that rationale depended on the common sense meaning of "mother" in light of the purposes of the act. "The Workmen's Compensation Act was designed to aid those relatives of the deceased who were dependent upon and relied upon the support and aid of the decedent": 

Glenn v. State Workmen's Insurance Fund, 133 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 121, 2 A. 2d 32.

The enumeration in the act of those entitled as claimants is to a great extent for the purpose of establishing priority of right. It is evident that the limitation of those entitled to claim to spouses, children, parents, and brothers and sisters was intended to limit rights to those standing in a

1954] 

 normal family relationship. This purpose is further revealed by the provision of the act which states that "child" shall include stepchildren, adopted children and children toward whom the decedent stood in loco parentis, if dependent on decedent for support. The intent is clearly to compensate those in a filial relationship.

The term "mother" has infinitely more meaning than merely indicating the woman who bore the child. It embraces the conception of tender, loving care and mutual affection commonly found between mother and child, and the rights, duties and liabilities normally incident to such a relationship. All the usual incidents were proven to have been present between this decedent and the claimant, except that claimant did not give birth to him. To deny compensation here would defeat the spirit and basic purpose of the law by adhering to a technical definition. In the circumstances of this case, we hold that this claimant, though the grandmother of the decedent, stood in that relationship of parent and child as to bring her within the categories set forth and intended by The Workmen's Compensation 

Act. 

Judgment affirmed.

COMMON PLEAS OF BEDFORD COUNTY 

BARNETT et al. v. BOWSER 

Workmen's compensation - Employe - Casual employment not in regular course of business of employer-Carpenter hired to assist in erection of store building.

A referee's award based on findings that claimant's deceased husband was employed in the regular course of defendant's business and that his employment was not casual in character when he suffered fatal injuries will be affirmed, where it appears that defendant had hired decedent as a carpenter at an hourly wage rate to help erect a building in which defendant intended to operate a grocery business, that defendant, while so engaged, was struck by a falling beam and

killed, it appearing that decedent was a carpenter with many years of experience, that he had no other employment and, that the job he had undertaken for defendant would have lasted for a considerable time.

Appeal from Workmen's Compensation Board. C. P. of 

Bedford County, September term, 1953, no. 62. Affirmed, 176 Pa. Superior Ct. 17.

Don C. Reiley, for claimants. 

Paul E. Beaver and J. William McIntyre, for defendant.

SNYDER, P. J.: 

This matter comes before the court on an appeal by R. C. Bowser from the decision of the Workmen's Compensation Board. 

Decedent, Meade J. Barnett, husband of Ora M. Barnett, claimant, and father of Meade J. Barnett, Jr., a minor son, died on August 11, 1952, when he was struck by a falling beam while assisting in constructing a building for defendant.

On November 13, 1952, claimant filed a claim petition which was referred to a compensation referee. A hearing was held on December 12, 1952, and on December 31, 1952, the referee handed down his decision in which he disallowed compensation. On January 29, 1953, claimant appealed to the Workmen's Compensation Board. After a hearing the board affirmed the referee's findings of fact but vacated and set aside his conclusions of law and allowed an award. 

On June 5, 1953, defendant filed his appeal with exceptions to findings of fact and conclusions of law. 

Defendant based his appeal upon the contention that the employment of decedent was casual and not in the regular course of defendant's business.

The findings of fact by the referee, affirmed by the board, as well as inferences therefrom, are conclusive upon the court of common pleas where sufficiently supported by competent and substantial evidence: Simon v. Fine, 167 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 386 (1950); Rozauski v. Glen Alden Coal Co.,

1954] 

165 Pa. Superior Ct. 460 (1949). Unless it can be said that there is no competent evidence to support the findings and conclusions of the referee and board, they cannot be disturbed by the courts: Yerko v. Clearfield Bituminous Coal 

Corp., 145 Pa. Superior Ct. 269 (1941).

The facts are relatively simple and are not in dispute. 

Defendant, at the time of the accident on August 11, 1952, and for several years prior thereto, was employed by the 

East Broad Top Railroad Company as a blacksmith. He had previously operated a grocery store. In the summer of 

1951, he purchased an old railroad station, dismantled it and moved the materials to his own land in Stonerstown. 

It was his intention to erect a building in which he would operate a grocery business. With the help of one employe, he had completed the outside frame work when he found the work too complicated for them and hired the decedent as a carpenter. Decedent was a retired coal miner who for many years had engaged in carpentry work when he was not working as a miner. It was agreed that the decedent should receive wages at the rate of $1.50 per hour. The duration of his employment was not specified, but it was not to extend beyond the time his services were needed in the erection of this building. Decedent reported for work on August 11, 1952, and after working about one-half day was struck by a falling beam and killed. He was survived by claimant, his widow, and by Meade J. Barnett, Jr., a minor son.

Unquestionably, decedent was an employe of defendant and actively engaged in his employment at the time of the accident resulting in decedent's death. The only question involved in this case is whether or not the services of decedent to defendant were casual in character and not in the regular course of the business of the defendantemployer.

Under section 104 of The Workmen's Compensation Act of June 2, 1915, P. L. 736, as amended, 77 PS §22, employes are expressly excluded, whose employment is (1) casual and (2) not in the regular course of the business of the employer. If both these concurring elements are not present,

claimant is entitled to compensation: Fedak v. Dzialdowski, 113 Pa. Superior Ct. 104; Snavely v. Redemptorist Fathers, 151 Pa. Superior Ct. 625; Parisi v. Freedom Oil Co., 150 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 260.

The above question really involves two questions. Was the employment of decedent casual in character? Was it in the regular course of the business of defendant? If it was not casual in character, claimant is entitled to an award whether or not such employment was in the regular course of defendant's business. Further, even though the employment be found to be only casual, but in the regular course of defendant's business, claimant is entitled to an award. 

The board concluded from the established facts that the employment of decedent was not casual. We believe there is competent evidence to support that conclusion. 

Defendant planned to construct a building on his premises and operate a store business therein. He was in the process of construction when he found himself in need of an experienced carpenter. Decedent was a carpenter with many years of experience. He had no other employment. He was employed by defendant to do carpentry work until the building was completed. It was contemplated that the job would last for considerable time.

The controlling principle has been set forth in Cochrane v. William Penn Hotel, 339 Pa. 549, 552 (1940), where the court said: "If the work is not of an emergency or incidental nature but represents a planned project and the tenure of service necessary to complete it and for which the employment is to continue is of fairly long duration, the employment is not casual, and it is immaterial that the accident to the employee for which compensation is sought may occur within a very short period after his entry upon the work." See also 

Deviney v. J. H. France Fire Brick Company et al., 339 

Pa. 553 (1940).

As applied to the facts in the instant case, we find that the work in question was not of an emergency or incidental nature; it represented a planned project; and the contemplated employment was of a fairly long duration.

1954] 

In the case of Parisi v. Freedom Oil Co., supra, defendant was engaged in the business of refining oil and had purchased a tract of land upon which had been erected several buildings. Claimant was employed for the purpose of demolishing the buildings and constructing a garage for the use of defendant's trucks. The job was to last about two months. Claimant was injured during his course of employment. In holding that such employment was not casual the court said, at page 264, "The project which defendant undertook, and in which claimant was engaged as an employee, was not merely an incidental job or an emergency repair, but it was a premeditated, planned, and extensive undertaking related to the conduct of its business. It did not come about by chance or fortuitously." The above case was cited at length and with approval in 

Snavely v. Redemptorist Fathers, supra. 

Counsel for defendant contends that the instant case is controlled by the case of Blake v. Wilson, 268 Pa. 469, (1920). The facts in that case show that Blake, claimant, was by occupation a school teacher. During a temporary suspension of the schools, he was employed by Wilson, defendant, to roof and paint a silo on defendant's farm. He was fatally injured in the course of his employment. In finding that such employment was casual in character, the court said, page 476, "The character of his employment may well be regarded as casual considering not only that it was out of the line of his regular employment, and occurred only because of the temporary suspension of that employment, but as well because of the accident, or whatever it was, that had occasioned the suspension of the work on the silo that left it incomplete, and the very limited time that would be required to complete the work for which he engaged. . . . An employee's engagement is casual in character when it comes about by chance, fortuitously, and for no fixed duration of time. In this case it was the concurrence of two fortuitous events that gave rise to the employment, the expiration of the school term and the convenience of the employer for a

resumption of work on the silo. There is nothing in the facts to warrant a belief that such contingency was foreseen."

That case is clearly distinguishable upon its facts and cannot control the instant case.

Having found that decedent's employment was not casual in character, it would not be necessary to determine whether or not it was in the regular course of defendant's business. 

However, we are of the opinion that the employment of decedent was in the regular course of the business of defendant as those terms have been interpreted by the appellate and lower courts of this State. The board was of the opinion that the construction of a building in which a business is to be conducted is an enterprise in itself and constitutes a business. The case of Deviney v. J. H. France Fire Brick 

Co., supra, supports that opinion. In that case, claimant was employed by the defendant company to repair old buildings and construct new sheds and new kilns, preparatory to the carrying on of its business of manufacturing and selling fire brick. The court found that the construction and the preparation of the plant constituted the immediate business of the company, and held that claimant who was injured while engaged in reconstruction work for the defendant company was employed in the regular course of the business of the company. The court cited with approval the case of Bordo et al. v. Grayek et al., 136 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 124, which held that the business in which defendant was engaged at the time of the accident was that of erecting the office building and the theater which he intended to, and subsequently did, rent and operate; and that the employment in which claimants were engaged at the time of the accident was in the regular course of that business. 

In Snavely v. Redemptorist Fathers, supra, defendant was chartered for the purpose of public worship, religious and educational instruction. Under the supervision of a superintendent and architect, it built a church and a caretaker's house on its premises. Claimant, while employed in this construction work, was injured. In holding that the employment of claimant was in the regular course of the business of defendant, the court said, at page 628,

1954] 

"The project which defendant undertook, and in which claimant was engaged as an employe, was not merely an incidental job or an emergency repair, but it was a premeditated, planned, and extensive undertaking related to the conduct of its business. Assuming, for the sake of argument, that the employment was not in the course of its regular business, which is the support of religious worship and religious and educational instruction and missionary labors, still, at the time claimant was hired, the defendant was indubitably engaged in the business of building a church and caretaker's house, as the board properly found." 

It is interesting to note that counsel for defendant in his brief agrees with the decision of the court in this case. We believe that the facts are essentially similar to the facts in the instant case. Both claimants were injured while employed in the construction of buildings, one to be used as a church and the other as a store; both planned and premeditated, and both related to the conduct of the business of the employers.

The fact that defendant was regularly employed by the East Broad Top Railroad Company at the time of the accident has no bearing on the case. It was held in Dobrich, Admx., v. Pittsburgh Term. Coal Corp., 145 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 87, and in Miller v. Farmers National Bank et al., 152

Pa. Superior Ct. 405, that an employer may be engaged in more than one business at one time.

We conclude that no error is found in the conclusions of the Workmen's Compensation Board that claimant was not engaged in casual work, and that he was engaged in the regular course of the business of defendant. Accordingly, the exceptions of defendant are overruled and refused, the appeal is dismissed and the award of the board is affirmed. 

And now, December 8, 1953, judgment is directed to be entered in favor of claimant, Ora M. Barnett, widow of 

Meade J. Barnett, deceased, on behalf of herself and as natural guardian of Meade J. Barnett, Jr., a minor, and against W. C. Bowser, defendant, in the sum of $5,778, the maximum amount allowed by statute, being compensation at a rate of $22.50 per week for a period of 84 weeks, beginning August 18, 1952, and continuing until March 30,

1954, when said minor child shall attain the age of 16 years, and being compensation at a rate of $18 per week, beginning March 30, 1954 and continuing for a period of 216 weeks, making a total of 300 weeks, the maximum period. 

In addition thereto, the sum of $250 is awarded to claimant for funeral expenses advanced by her. This judgment shall bear interest on default payments of compensation at the rate of six percent per annum as provided by section 410 

of The Workmen's Compensation Act, commencing August 

18, 1952, and shall be subject to termination, modification or commutation thereof as provided by law. Costs of appeal are to be paid by defendant.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA BULETZA v. UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION BOARD OF REVIEW 

Unemployment compensation-Leaving work without good cause- 

Reduction of wages.

Unemployment compensation was properly denied to a claimant where it appeared that he quit his employment because of dissatisfaction with a slight reduction in wages and of the distance he had to travel to his home when he had time off from his regular duties; such circumstances did not constitute good cause. 

Appeal from Unemployment Compensation Board of 

Review. Affirmed, 174 Pa. Superior Ct. 248.

Mike Buletza, appellant, in propria persona. 

William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, with him Frank F. Truscott, Attorney General, for appellee, submitted a brief.

RHODES, P. J.: 

Claimant in this unemployment compensation case was last employed by the Iron City Sand and Gravel Company,

1954] 

 of Pittsburgh, for a period of two days. He had previously been employed by the McCrady-Rodgers Company, of Pittsburgh. Claimant had a valid separation from his last employment but the compensation authorities found that he had voluntarily terminated his previous employment without good cause, and that under the factual situation he was not entitled to benefits. Claimant has appealed from the decision of the Unemployment Compensation Board of Review.

The total earnings of claimant with his last employer amounted to $29.95, which is less than eight times his weekly benefit rate. It was, therefore, necessary to determine whether he had a valid separation from his previous employment in order to ascertain whether he was qualified for compensation under section 401 (f) of the Unemployment Compensation Law of December 5, 1936, P. L. (1937) 

2897, as amended, 43 PS §801 (f), which reads as follows: "Compensation shall be payable to any employe who is or becomes unemployed, and who- . . . "(f) Has, subsequent to his voluntarily leaving work without good cause or to his discharge or suspension from work for willful misconduct connected with his work, been paid remuneration for services in an amount equal to or in excess of eight (8) times his weekly benefit rate.' 

Claimant had been employed by the McCrady-Rodgers Company for about six months. He worked on a river boat as a loader or fireman and was engaged in dredging operations on the Ohio river. The work was confined to about two miles on the river. He lived on the boat and was paid an hourly wage of $1.30, with subsistence and quarters. A day and a half before his separation on November 4, 1952, he was classified as a deck hand at a wage of $1.17 an hour. 

He terminated his employment of his own accord on November 4, 1952, because of dissatisfaction with the reduction in wages, the living quarters on the boat and the distance he had to travel to his home when he had time off from his regular duties. He was unmarried and resided with his parents, who lived about 70 miles from where the boat

usually docked. The compensation authorities found that, as claimant lived on the boat and went home only when he had time off from his regular duties, the distance traveled did not constitute good cause for the termination of his employment. The living conditions on the boat had remained the same during the entire period of his employment and no complaint was ever made to his employer. Claimant worked only a day and a half after the nature of his employment on the boat was changed. There was a decrease in the work as well as in the wages. If the small reduction in wages was the reason for his dissatisfaction, he could have continued to work temporarily while seeking other employment which would pay a higher wage. But the slight wage reduction furnished no reasonable, substantial or necessitous reason for leaving his employment. Under the circumstances, as the compensation authorities found, the reduction and the change of status did not constitute good cause for termination of his employment. See McGuire v. 

Unemployment Compensation Board of Review, 169 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 467, 82 A. 2d 324; Kaylock Unemployment

Compensation Case, 165 Pa. Superior Ct. 376, 67 A. 2d 

801; Dawkins Unemployment Compensation Case, 358 Pa. 

224, 237, 56 A. 2d 254.

It is obvious from the testimony that claimant became dissatisfied with the terms and conditions of his employment and, therefore, voluntarily separated therefrom. 

Claimant had an opportunity to continue his employment, he was not compelled to quit, and the conditions of which he now complains were never communicated to his employer. 

As a matter of fact, he gave notice to his employer that he was leaving, and the record does not disclose that he made any complaint or assigned any reason therefor. He manifested no desire to remain employed. The finding that claimant voluntarily left his employment without good cause and, therefore, is not entitled to compensation benefits under section 401 (f) is sustained by the evidence. 

Decision is affirmed.

1954] 

 SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA WHITE v. KELLY, INC., et al. 

1 

Workmen's compensation. Accidental injury - Ruptured disc 

Bricklayer performing usual duties-Prior accidental injury.

An order of the court below affirming a disallowance by the Workmen's Compensation Board of a claim for compensation based on the herniation of an intervertebral disc will be affirmed, where it appeared that claimant, a bricklayer working on a scaffold, turned in the course of his work to pick up a tile from the scaffold and in so doing suffered pain in his back so severe that he was unable to continue working, that in a subsequent operation an intervertebral disc which had ruptured was removed, that the board found claimant was performing his usual duties in the usual manner at the time he was stricken, and further found that the herniation was a natural development from injuries sustained by claimant as a result of a prior accidental fall, the findings of the board being supported by the evidence. 

Appeal from order of Court of Common Pleas of Mont gomery County, June term, 1952, no. 488. Affirmed, 174 

Pa. Superior Ct. 262. 

William L. Huganir, for appellant. 

Peter P. Liebert, 3rd, with him John J. McDevitt, 3rd, for appellees.

HIRT, J.: 

In this case, claimant sought to recover compensation for the herniation of an intervertebral disc, contending that the rupture resulted from accident in the course of his employment on March 23, 1948. The board ultimately disallowed the claim and the lower court, in affirming the action of the board, dismissed claimant's appeal. 

Claimant was a bricklayer in the employ of the defendant. 

On the day in question, he was facing the walls of a room in the Adam Scheidt Brewing Company with tile. The individual tiles were 8 x 6 x 2 inches in size and weighed about four pounds each. The ceiling in the room was eight feet high. Claimant was working on a scaffold about three feet from the floor and because of an overhead beam in the

ceiling had headroom of only four feet. On turning in the course of his work to pick up a tile from the scaffold, he suddenly suffered pain in his back so severe that he was unable to continue working and he went home. He had not fallen nor was he struck by anything, and he could not recall that he had made a misstep or slipped on the scaffold where he was working when he suffered the disabling seizure. In bed on the following May 7th, claimant discovered that he suddenly had lost all sensation in his hips and legs and had become paralyzed in both legs. He later submitted to an operation in Graduate Hospital in Philadelphia and a large intervertebral disc, which had ruptured, was removed and the pressure on the spinal cord thus relieved. Claimant, since the operation, has gradually recovered some movement of his legs but he is still totally disabled. 

The board, after hearing, substituted the following controlling findings for those of the referee: "Fifth: We find as a fact from a preponderance of the testimony, that at the time claimant sustained pain in the area of his back on March 23, 1948, claimant was performing his usual duties in the usual manner and that there is no evidence of exertion, risk [sic], exposure or unusual occurrence. "Fifth-A: We further find as a fact, from a preponderance of the testimony, that the herniation of the nucleus pulposa was a natural development of the injuries sustained by the claimant on October 29, 1946, as a result of his falling from a scaffold while employed by one Richard A. Warner." (Italics supplied).

Accordingly, the board, in sustaining the appeal by the employer from an award made by the referee, concluded: "Since claimant has failed to establish the happening of an accident, while acting within the course of his employment with the defendant herein, he is not entitled to the payment of compensation and his claim petition must be dismissed."

On October 29, 1946, while working as a bricklayer in the employ of Warner, claimant had fallen from a scaffold, and he then sustained an injury to his pelvis. He had diffi-

1954] 

 culty with his hip for some time thereafter and received compensation for that injury until November 11, 1946, when he signed a final receipt. Dr. Robert A. Groff who performed the operation for the removal of the ruptured disc, testified in the present proceeding that a herniation of the nucleus pulposa¹ develops from one of two causes; either trauma or "ordinary wear and tear of an individual who has apparently a previous defect which would lead to such a thing." Dr. Groff received a history of the 1946 injury from the claimant himself. And from a consideration of all of the facts, including the circumstances attending the disabling injury of March 23, 1948, he gave it as his professional opinion that the rupture of the disc was not related to the work claimant was performing for the present defendant, but that the former injury sustained on October 29, 1946 "led to the subsequent complete herniation of the disc". It is of some significance that claimant's first petition for compensation, following the incident of March 23, 1948, attributed his disability to the 1946 accident when he sought to set aside the final receipt in the prior proceeding on the ground that he signed the receipt on the mistaken belief that his disability from that injury had ended. Moreover, his claim petition in the present case subsequently filed on February 18, 1947 averred that ". . . the normal duties of his job complicated an existing injury to a disc. 

As a result of the complications and intensification thereof, paralysis has set in."

He later amended the averment to read: "The normal duties of his job caused an injury to a disc as a result of which paralysis has set in." 

The amendment is of no avail, for the proofs do not show that claimant was a healthy workman, with no previous 

1 He described that type of hernia thus: "Well, between the bones of the spine are pads called intervertebral discs. These discs are composed of outside membrane called the annulus fibrosus. The material within the center of this pad is called the nucleus pulposa and what happens is in a herniation of the nucleus pulposa the outside membrane splits and allows the material within the disc pad to herniate or extrude."

disability when he suffered the disabling hernia on March 

23, 1948.

The same controlling principles apply to claims for compensation based on the herniation of an intervertebral disc as for an inguinal hernia, the most common type. In reality, there is little dispute as to the facts in the present proceeding. The findings of the board are amply supported by the evidence and we are bound by them. The herniation of the disc clearly resulted from the natural development of injuries sustained by the claimant on a prior occasion in 1946 as found by the board. No accident, therefore, may be inferred from the disability suffered by claimant in the usual course of his employment on March 23, 1948. We have reviewed the applicable principles of law in an opinion filed this day in the case of Rosso v. Aetna Steel Products 

Corp., 174 Pa. Superior Ct. 258. We need not repeat the discussion here. 

Order affirmed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD BOWSER v. SHACKLEFORD, etc. 

Workmen's compensation—Injury in course of employment—Opera tion of truck without driver's license-Section 801(a) of the Work men's Compensation Act. 

Claimant employed by defendant as a delivery clerk, who was injured while riding in his employer's truck will not be denied compensation where it appears that at the time of the accident the truck was being operated with claimant's permission by a minor aged 16, who had only a learner's permit, and who thereby violated the Motor Vehicle Code, since the violations amounted neither to felonies nor misdemeanors, and were therefore not within section 301 (a) of the Workmen's Compensation Act, defendant having failed to prove any violation of positive orders. 

Appeal by defendant from order of Referee Haines. District No. 8, compensation agreement no. 5737227, A-38554. 

Affirmed as amended. January 13, 1954.

1954] 

 Car & General Insurance Corp. and Curley Adjustment 

Bureau, insurance carrier. 

Frank Reich and Lewis E. Mai, for claimant. 

Jordan & Jordan, for defendant.

MCGRATH, Commissioner:
