Kenneth Bowser, claimant, and W. R. Shackelford, trading as Shackelford Flowers, defendant, entered into an agreement for payment of compensation which sets forth that claimant, a delivery clerk, sustained a compound dislocation of the left ankle, avulsion of the astragalus with loss of bone and laceration of the lateral ligaments, while riding in his employer's truck which upset on April 15, 1951. Defendant filed a petition to set aside the agreement on November 5, 1951, on the grounds that at the time of the accident the truck was being operated by one Robert 

Huber, aged 16, who had no driver's license, thereby violating the Motor Vehicle Act; that claimant was injured as a result of violation of positive orders given by defendant, and that the agreement of compensation was entered into by the parties under a mistake as to the factual situation and the law applicable thereto. 

It appears that defendant also entered into a compensation agreement with Huber, and similar action to set aside that agreement was taken. Both cases were heard together by the referee, the same testimony being applicable. In each instance the referee dismissed the petition. Huber filed an appeal to the Workmen's Compensation Board at 

A-38553. The appeal here is by Bowser.

It is uncontradicted that at the time of the accident Huber was aged 16, having secured a learner's permit to operate a motor vehicle on April 10, 1951, four days before the accident. 

Section 621 of the Act of May 1, 1929, P. L. 905, art. 

VI, as amended, provides: "It shall be unlawful for any person under the age of eighteen years to operate a motor vehicle upon any highway as a paid employee, or for any person to cause or permit any such operation.

"Penalty-Any person violating any of the provisions of this Section shall, upon summary conviction before a magistrate be sentenced to pay a fine of ten dollars and costs of prosecution and, in default . . ."

Section 622 of the act makes it illegal for any person to authorize or permit a motor vehicle owned by him or under his control, to be operated by any person who has no legal right to do so, and violation of this section calls for a fine, upon summary conviction.

It may be conceded that Huber and Bowser were each guilty of violating one of the laws above quoted. The first question before us is whether such violation, of itself, was sufficient to remove them from their course of employment in view of section 301 (a) of The Workmen's Compensa tion Act. We have concluded this question should be answered in the negative.

Section 301 (a) of The Workmen's Compensation Act provides that no compensation shall be paid when the injury is caused by the employe's violation of law, but the burden of proof of such fact shall be upon the employer. 

However, violation of law barring recovery has been construed to mean a felony or a misdemeanor: Curran v. Lang Construction Co., 286 Pa. 245; Rich Hill Coal Co. 

v. Bashore, 334 Pa. 449; Hopwood v. City of Pittsburgh, 152 Pa. Superior Ct. 398, 403; Yowkoski v. Hudson Coal 

Co., 159 Pa. Superior Ct. 256, 262. Since the violations in the instant case were neither felonies nor misdemeanors, but summary convictions, they were not such violations as would be within the contemplation of section 301 (a) of the act. Neither Huber nor Bowser is barred from receiving compensation by their violation of sections 621 or 622 of 

The Vehicle Act. 

Defendant complains of the referee's inadequacy of findings and we shall make appropriate additions. 

The second ground, relied upon by defendant is that Huber and Bowser were guilty of violation of positive orders of defendant that Huber was not to drive the defendant's truck.

The testimony on this point is contradictory. Anthony 

B. Vergato, defendant's manager, testified that he had, on

1954] 

 more than one occasion, given instructions that Huber was not to drive. Bowser testified that at various times he saw Huber back cars and trucks out of defendant's lot; he did not recall Vergato giving him specific instructions not to permit Huber to drive on the day of the accident. Huber stated that Vergato at times directed him to "put the defendant's trucks in the garage", that Vergato did not tell him that he should not drive the truck on the day of the accident.

The referee found that defendant failed to prove a violation of positive orders. The burden in this connection rested upon defendant. In our independent judgment, we would have found similarly. It is uncontradicted that Huber operated the truck around the parking lot and backed cars in and out. Since defendant permitted this, it seems inconsistent that defendant would give positive orders that Huber was not to drive, especially after he had secured a learner's permit.

There is sufficient credible testimony to indicate Huber was permitted by defendant to accompany Bowser on the last trip, some testimony indicating even that his services were needed on the truck. We conclude that Huber's employment had not terminated for that day.

We find that both Bowser and Huber are entitled to compensation; that the defendant's petition to set aside the agreement should be dismissed.

We affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order and add the following findings of fact: 

Findings of Fact 7. The Workmen's Compensation Board finds as a fact that claimant, when injured, was in defendant's truck operated by one Robert Huber. 

8. The Workmen's Compensation Board finds as a fact that claimant knew Huber had a learner's permit and was under 18 years of age. 

9. The Workmen's Compensation Board finds as a fact that claimant permitted Huber to operate said truck of defendant at the time of the accident. 

Defendant's appeal is dismissed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD WEGMAN v. CITY OF PHILADELPHIA Workmen's compensation-Injury in course of employment-City fireman-Injury on way to work-Necessity to change into uniform at home-Transportation provided by employer. 

A fireman employed by defendant who was injured from a fall on a street while en route to boarding a street car to take him to his fire station was held to be in the course of his employment at the time of the accident and entitled to compensation, where it appeared that he was required to change into uniform at home, that he was using the street car for transportation pursuant to an arrangement between the City and the transit company, and that he was subject to call for duty at any time during the twenty-four hour period.

Appeal by claimant from order of Referee Clayton. District No. 1, claim petition no. 126,434, accident no. 5,899,123, A-38685. Reversed. December 16, 1953. 

Alexander F. Barbieri, for claimant. 

Raymond Kitty, for defendant.

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

The facts in this case, upon appeal by claimant, Joseph M. Wegman, from the order of Referee Clayton, are not in dispute. Claimant, a fireman employed by defendant, City of Philadelphia, sustained a fractured right fibula on December 14, 1951, from a fall on the icy street near 60th Street and Theodore Avenue, while en route to boarding a street car to take him to his fire station at 50th Street and Baltimore Avenue. He was due at the station at midnight and the accident happened about 11:20 p.m. The question involved is whether claimant was in the course of his employment at the time of the accident. 

The general rule of law is well established that injury occurring to an employe while going to or returning from work is not compensable, unless he was actually engaged in furthering his employer's business at the time: Krchmar v. Oakland Beach Company, 155 Pa. Superior Ct. 430; Palko

1954] 

 v. Taylor-McCoy Coal & Coke Co., 289 Pa. 401. The opinion in the latter case refers to the case of Stahl v. Watson 

Coal Co., 268 Pa. 452, the court said: "An employee, having no regular working hours, but subject to call at any time, was engaged from six to three. 

He then started for home. His body was found on the property but not the premises of his employer. Held, that when he left for home, though subject to immediate recall, the accident did not happen when he was actually engaged in the master's affairs or in the course of employment." 

Is the principle of law different in the case of a public employe, such as a policeman or fireman so that the employe is in a duty status, when he leaves his home, in uniform, to report for work? Claimant testified that, as a fireman, he was subject to call at all times during the 24-hour period. 

By arrangement with Philadelphia Transportation Com pany, firemen in uniform enjoy free transportation in going to and from the firehouse. In the case of Cardacia v. City of Pittston, 28 Luz. 403, a policeman was held in the course of employment, when injured as he stepped off a trolley car on his way to his home from special duty. The rules and regulations of the Police Department of the City of Pittston provided: "Each member of the police force, excepting substitute policemen, shall devote his whole time and attention to the service of the department and is expressly prohibited from following any other calling, or being engaged in any other business. . . . Although certain hours are allotted for the performance of 'regular tours of duty', officers are considered to be at all times on duty, and must act promptly at any time their services are required." 

An award was upheld.

According to orders issued by authority of the Philadelphia Bureau of Fire, "All members of the bureau, when driving a private automobile, (you) will do so out of uniform. This order to be strictly complied with, and the officers will promptly report any member violating this order." 

An order promulgated under date of November 23, 1940, or reissued under that date, directed to officers and members of the bureau states:

"It is again called to your attention that an order exists in the bureau strictly prohibiting the wearing of uniforms or any parts of the uniform while off duty. Exception is made to the two hours before or after a rollcall. This is to permit members sufficient time to report for duty, or to get out of uniform after a rollcall hour. This does not apply to members when on official business while off regular duty. 

The order against members riding in automobiles while in uniform is also again called to your attention. Officers will coöperate in enforcing this order by calling the attention of any member whom they notice violating the same. By order of Ross P. Davis, Chief Engineer."

These rules, promulgated by claimant's superior, were binding upon him. From the comments upon the record, there appears to be a dispute whether the order was enforced, or to what extent. Be that as it may, the wearing of a uniform, per se, would not determine whether the fireman was in the course of employment; the pivotal question here is whether claimant was on duty while wearing his uniform. 

Wegman testified: 

"Q. Now, in your capacity as a fireman, what are your duty conditions? "A. Well, we're subject to call 24 hours a day. To leave the city we have to obtain written permission, and actually we only work an eight-hour tour of duty, but we're subject to call at all times. "Q. Now, on your tour of duty, when you talk about your actual tour in the firehouse, it would have begun at what time on that day? "A. It would have begun at 12:00 midnight."

Now since a fireman is not permitted to drive his own car to work while in uniform, and arrangements are made with the PTC for free transportation to and from the fire station, the direction by the city fire bureau and limitation upon the fireman's medium of transportation, places the employe in the course of his employment, as embraced by our appellate court cases: Malinoski v. Kessler et al., 126 

Pa. Superior Ct. 522; Beck v. Ashton et al., 124 Pa. Superior Ct. 307. In the latter case, the court said:

1954] 

"Our decisions uniformly hold that if transportation to and from place of employment is supplied, the employer is responsible to the employee or his dependents for compensation where accidental death is caused by injury sustained while being transported by the employer."

If claimant here had been injured while on a street car or in alighting therefrom, he would clearly be entitled to compensation. Since the employer circumscribed his freedom of personal liberty to the extent that he was required to change into uniform at home so that he could use the method of transportation arranged for by the bureau, it is our considered opinion that he was also in the course of his employment when he left his home, in uniform, for work. 

It does not follow, however, that any fireman, in uniform, who is injured would be similarly entitled to compensation. 

There may well be a deviation from employment, as where a fireman stops in a store to do personal shopping, or visits a friend, or enters a place of amusement for his personal pleasure or entertainment. Here the time of the accident is so close to the time for the commencement of his work shift, as to preclude any inference that he was not en route to his work. Under the circumstances, we believe the claimant is entitled to compensation.

We hereby vacate and set aside the referee's fifth finding of fact, and in lieu thereof, we substitute the following 

Findings of Fact 

5. We find as a fact that the accident in question occurred during the course of claimant's employment as he was required to change into uniform at home in order to ride a street car to work, without charge, under an arrangement between the employer and the traction company. 

7. Claimant suffered a fractured right leg fibula in said accident on December 14, 1951, and was totally disabled therefrom until March 6, 1952.

As amended, the referee's findings of fact are affirmed. 

We affirm the referee's first conclusion of law. We vacate and set aside the referee's second conclusion of law, and in lieu thereof, we substitute the following

Conclusion of Law 

2. Having found that claimant suffered an accidental injury during the course of his employment, therefore, he is entitled to compensation.

We hereby vacate and set aside the referee's order of disallowance, and in lieu thereof, we enter the following Award

The defendant, City of Philadelphia, is ordered and directed to pay to claimant, Joseph M. Wegman, compensation at the maximum rate of $25 per week from December 21, 1951, and continuing until March 6, 1952, when all disability ceased; defendant to receive credit for wages paid to claimant during this period of time. 

Interest is to be paid on deferred installments on accrued compensation in accordance with section 410 of the act.

The appeal of claimant is sustained. 

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA GREEN v. UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION BOARD OF REVIEW 

Unemployment compensation—Quitting work without good cause— 

Altercation with fellow employe.

Claimant for unemployment compensation did not have good cause for voluntarily leaving his employment, where it appears that, as a result of some ill feeling between him and another employe, the claimant was struck, that he suffered no injury except hurt feelings, that when the superintendent attempted to settle the controversy claimant refused and quit; hence, an order of the Unemployment 

Compensation Board of Review holding him ineligible for benefits will be affirmed. 

Appeal from decision of Unemployment Compensation 

Board of Review. Affirmed, 174 Pa. Superior Ct. 286.

49 

Louis Di Giovanni, for appellant. 

William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, for appellee.

WRIGHT, J.: 

This is an appeal from a decision of the Unemployment Compensation Board of Review, holding appellant ineligible for benefits under section 402(b) of the Unemployment 

Compensation Law of December 5, 1936, P. L. (1937) 2897, as amended, 43 PS §802. Affirming the findings of fact made by the referee, the board concluded that claimant quit his employment because of a personal difference with a co-worker which did not constitute good cause for the separation.

For 16 years claimant had been employed as a carding machine operator for Hardwick and Magee Company in 

Philadelphia. Both the shop foreman and the superintendent testified that claimant was a competent workman. He was employed on the day shift, working until 3 p.m. Another employe reported at 2:45 p.m. and operated the same machine during the second shift. A difference arose between the two men over the manner in which the work was being performed, culminating in an incident on Friday, March 23, 1951, in which the other man struck claimant. On the following Tuesday (the plant having been closed down for the Easter holiday), claimant and the other man were called to the superintendent's office and requested to "shake hands and make up". The other man was willing to do this, but claimant refused and quit work. 

Section 402 (b) of the Pennsylvania Unemployment Compensation Law provides, inter alia, that an employe shall be ineligible for compensation for any week "in which his unemployment is due to voluntarily leaving work without good cause". Appellant contends that his leaving was with good cause and not a mere whim or impulse, citing Department of Labor and Industry of Commonwealth of Pennsyl-

vania v. Unemployment Compensation Board of Review, 

164 Pa. Superior Ct. 421, 65 A. 2d 436; Barclay White Co. v. Unemployment Compensation Board of Review, 356 Pa. 

43, 50 A. 2d 336, and Sun Shipbuilding and Dry Dock Co. v. Unemployment Compensation Board of Review, 358 Pa. 

224, 56 A. 2d 254.

Good cause means cause sufficient to justify an employe voluntarily leaving the ranks of the employed and joining the ranks of the unemployed: Sun Shipbuilding and Dry 

Dock Co. v. Unemployment Compensation Board of Review, supra. It is impossible to give a general definition of good cause, and the meaning of the words must be determined in each case from the particular facts: Barclay White Co. v. Unemployment Compensation Board of Review, supra.

To constitute good cause, claimant's conduct must meet the standards of ordinary common sense and prudence: Kaylock v. Unemployment Compensation Board of Review, 165 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 376, 67 A. 2d 801. He must be compelled to quit by necessitous circumstances or because of legal or family obligations: Bliley Electric Co. v. Unemployment

Compensation Board of Review, 158 Pa. Superior Ct. 548, 

45 A. 2d 898.

In the Barclay White case, supra, it was held that good cause might include reasons which were personal to the employe and extraneous to the employment, providing they were substantial and reasonable. In the Sun Shipbuilding case, supra, the term was said to include situations where the working conditions were dangerous or detrimental to health. In Department of Labor and Industry v. Unemployment Compensation Board of Review, supra, we held that the vitalizing element of good cause is good faith, which term embraces not only freedom from fraud, but also positive conduct consistent with a genuine desire to work and be self supporting.

Applying these criteria to the present factual situation, it is apparent that claimant has not sustained the burden of showing good cause. The board found that ill feeling had been developing between claimant and the other employe over a period of some time; that claimant did not

1954] 

 suffer any injury as a result of being struck, only that "his feelings were hurt"; that, when the superintendent attempted to settle the controversy, claimant refused and voluntarily quit his job, and that he could have continued in his employment without any undue hardship. These findings of fact are consistent with each other and with the board's conclusions of law, and can be sustained without a capricious disregard of the competent evidence: Lavely v. Unemployment Compensation Board of Review, 163 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 66, 60 A. 2d 352; Tronieri v. Unemployment 

Compensation Board of Review, 164 Pa. Superior Ct. 435, 65 A. 2d 426.

In the words of Chief Justice Maxey in Sun Shipbuilding and Dry Dock Co. v. Unemployment Compensation Board of Review, supra, "even in matters connected with his employment there must be some limit to the legally approved list of 'good causes' for quitting employment. The quitting must be for such a cause as would reasonably motivate in a similar situation the average able bodied and qualified worker to give up his or her employment with its certain wage rewards in order to enter the ranks of the 'compensated unemployed'. . . . An employe may contend that the character and habits of his fellow employes are distasteful to him, yet these and similar reasons cannot be legally accepted as 'good cause' for leaving one's employment". 

Decision affirmed. 

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA MARTIN v. UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION BOARD OF REVIEW 

Unemployment compensation - Availability for suitable work- 

Operation of bar owned by wife.

An order of the Unemployment Compensation Board of Review denying claimant unemployment compensation on the ground that claimant who had been employed intermittently by a mining com-

pany until he was laid off by reason of lack of work, operated a bar and liquor establishment with his wife, to which business he devoted five hours a day will be reversed and the record remanded, where it appears that the business was purchased with the wife's money, was owned by her alone, that the profits were hers, and where there was no finding that claimant was not available for other work, and no evidence that he refused a referral to any work. 

Appeal from Unemployment Compensation Board of 

Review. Vacated and remanded, 174 Pa. Superior Ct. 412. 

W. J. Krencewicz, with him Adolph A. Rugienius, for appellant. 

William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, for appellee.

RENO, J.: 

The Unemployment Compensation Board of Review de nied appellant's claims for benefits, basing its decision upon the referee's findings of facts which it affirmed: "1. The claimant had been employed intermittenly as a jig runner by the Morea Mining Company, Morea Colliery,

Pa. His last day of work was December 23, 1952, when he was laid off due to lack of work. "2. Claimant and his wife operate a bar and liquor establishment since September 1949. The liquor license issued by the Pennsylvania Liquor Control Board is in the name of Andrew [appellant] and Catherine Martin. During the period involved herein the claimant devoted five hours each day in the operation of this business." 1

The conclusion of the referee, also affirmed by the board, follows: 

"While the claimant contends that the income derived from the business is solely his wife's, he admitted that the 1 The bureau reported that "the claimant and his wife are the owners and operators of a taproom." (Italics supplied.) The referee and the board found that "claimant and his wife operate a bar”, etc. 

This court, of course, reviews only the final decision of the board.

1954] 

 license is in both names and during the period in question he devoted five hours each day in the operation of the business. He, therefore, cannot be regarded as unemployed and must be disqualified under Section 401 of the [Unemployment Compensation] Law."

The referee also concluded that appellant was not unemployed within the meaning of the law, supra, section 4 (u), which until 1951 read as follows: "An individual shall be deemed unemployed with respect to any week during which he performs no services and with respect to which no remuneration is paid or payable to him, 

This provision was, however, clarified by the amending 

Act of September 29, 1951, P. L. 1580, sec. 4 (u), 43 PS §753, and now reads: "An individual shall be deemed unemployed (I) with respect to any week (i) during which he performs no services for which remuneration is paid or payable to him and (ii) with respect to which no remuneration is paid or payable to him, ." (Italics in the amending act.) ..

Here the able deputy attorney general argued that since appellant was engaged "in his own business" he was not unemployed and not available for suitable work. However, neither the referee nor the board found, as a fact, that appellant was engaged "in his own business." The finding is merely that appellant "and his wife operate a bar and liquor establishment", and the uncontradicted testimony of appellant and his wife is that the business was purchased with the wife's money, is owned by her alone, and that the profits of the enterprise are wholly hers.

The license was issued to her and her husband because, as both testified, the agents of the Liquor Control Board insisted that unless appellant's name was joined with hers the license would not be issued.2 True, they filed a joint income tax return but unless the return, when produced, 2 This may be true; but would the Liquor Control Board have issued a license to appellant and his wife unless both were "pecuniarily intererested in the business?" See Act of June 16, 1937; P. L. 1762, sec. 

403; Liquor Code of April 12, 1951, P. L. 90, sec. 404, 47 PS §4-404.

indicates that the income from the business was reported as a joint earning, it must be presumed that the return reflected and reported only the separate and individual income of the parties to the return. The testimony concerning the license and the income tax return, standing alone and without evidentiary implementation, does not furnish a solid base for the conclusion that appellant shared in the ownership of the business, and the higher echelon of the compensation authorities carefully refrained from finding ownership as a fact.

Even if, during the period in question, appellant was paid for his services in his wife's business, he would not thereby be disqualified for benefits. The law, section 4(1), 43 PS 

§753, provides: 

"The word 'employment' shall not include (4) Service performed by an individual in the employ of his son, daughter, or spouse, and service performed by a child under the age of twenty-one (21) in the employ of his father or mother."

The argument concerning his availability for suitable work rests entirely upon the fact that he devoted five hours a day to the taproom business. Again there is no finding that he was not available for other work, and there is no evidence that he refused a referral to any work. Whether or not he would have discontinued the work at the taproom and taken other offered work is pure speculation. Presumptively at least, having claimed benefits and registered for work, he was ready and willing to engage in other work and the presumption stands until rebutted by substantial evi dence. Sturdevant Unemployment Compensation Case, 158 

Pa. Superior Ct. 548, 45 A. 2d 898. It cannot be said, as the board's representative argues, that appellant had, during his lay-off from his work in the mines, detached himself from the labor force.

Conceivably, where a person divides his time and labor between work for another and potentially profitable work for himself, as where, e.g., a factory worker also operates, say, a store, a farm or a work-shop, a suspension of work at the factory may not and probably does not expose him

1954] 

 to the rigors of unemployment which the law is designed to alleviate. See Unemployment Compensation Law, sec. 3, 43 PS §752.3 But, as the findings and the evidence now stand, this is not such a case. 

The decision is vacated and the record is remanded to the board for further consideration, with leave to take additional testimony. 

3 However, income from pensions, savings accounts, bonds, mortgages and other sources which do not involve or require work or service by the recipient will not disqualify an employe for benefits: 

Pendleton Unemployment Compensation Case, 167 Pa. Superior Ct. 

256, 75 A. 2d 3.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA ZIELINSKI v. UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION BOARD OF REVIEW 

Unemployment compensation-Leaving work without good cause- Remaining away from work-Alleged illness-Failure to furnish doctor's certificate.

Claimant was properly denied unemployment compensation where it appears that she remained at home for a period of 10 days without good cause, and when she returned to her employment failed to submit a doctor's certificate as required by the terms of a union-management agreement, there being no evidence that the work had an adverse effect on her health.

Appeal from Unemployment Compensation Board of 

Review. Affirmed, 174 Pa. Superior Ct. 244. 

George A. Dawson, for appellant. 

William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, for appellee.

PER CURIAM: 

Claimant, Minnie Zielinski, was denied unemployment compensation benefits by the Unemployment Compensation

Board of Review. The reason given for the disallowance was that claimant had left her last employment without good cause and was, therefore, ineligible under section 

402 (b) of the Unemployment Compensation Law of Decem ber 5, 1936, P. L. (1937) 2897, as amended, 43 PS §802 (b).

Our review on appeal by claimant is confined to the decision of the board, which adopted the findings and conclusion of the referee in disallowing claimant's claims for benefits. 

Claimant had been employed by the P. McGraw Wool 

Company in Pittsburgh for about 16 years prior to September 29, 1952, which was the last day she worked for this employer. She had been employed as an examiner at a wage of $1.32 an hour until July 25, 1952, when the examining department was closed and claimant was transferred to the twisting department. She worked in the latter department for about 40 days, or until September 30, 1952, when she failed to report for work. She had requested a coworker to give notice that she was ill. Claimant did not return to the plant where she had been employed until October 10, 1952, when she met the company superintendent and requested a transfer to some other type of work. At that time she was informed that, having quit, she was no longer employed. Under an agreement between the union of which claimant was a member and the company, it was necessary that all employes after an absence of five days submit a doctor's certificate.

Claimant testified that the work in the twisting department made her nervous, but she did not consult a doctor. 

She was fully aware of the contract between the union and management but failed to comply with its provision which does not appear to be unreasonable.

The board found (fifth and sixth findings of the referee): "5. On October 10, 1952, claimant . . . was advised that she had been absent for a period of five days and had not submitted a doctor's statement, as required by the unionmanagement agreement, [and that] she had been removed from the payroll. This action on the company's part was upheld by the union of which the claimant was a member.

1954] 

"6. Claimant did not present a doctor's statement as she was not under the care of a doctor."

All the findings of fact were clearly based on the evidence which discloses that claimant brought the employment relationship to an end by remaining at home for 10 days without doing anything during that time to comply with the terms of the union-management agreement. Her unsupported statement that the work made her nervous furnished no justification for non-compliance. Beyond that testimony, there was no proof that the work had an adverse effect upon her health. She apparently was dissatisfied with her job and decided to remain at home. In O'Donnell Unemployment Compensation Case, 173 Pa. Superior Ct. 263, 267, 98 A. 2d 406, 408, we made this applicable statement: "An employe is bound to exercise reasonable diligence in the conduct of his own affairs. 'It seems entirely reasonable to hold that a claimant who neglects to take those precautions to guard his job, which a reasonably prudent person would take, . . . in effect leaves his employment voluntarily': Vernon Unemployment Compensation Case, 

164 Pa. Superior Ct. 131, 135, 63 A. 2d 383, 384." 

Decision is affirmed. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD GUNN v. CONTINENTAL FOUNDRY MACHINE CO. 

Workmen's compensation-Death-Fall from crane-Violation of safety regulation.

A claim by a dependent widow will be disallowed where it appears that her husband was killed when he fell from an overhead crane at the place where he was employed, where defendant denied liability on the ground that his presence on the crane was not required by his employment and death resulted from his violation of the law, and where the evidence, though insufficient to establish violation of positive orders by the employer, did tend to establish that decedent was attempting to enter the crane cage when he fell, which act was in

direct violation of rules and regulations issued by the Secretary of 

Labor and Industry. 

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Haines. 

District No. 8, claim petition no. 125,936, accident no. 

5863155, A-38282. Amended and affirmed. November 15, 1953. 

Thomas E. Barton, for claimant. 

William A. Challener, Jr., and Francis A. McQuilkin, for defendant.

BRENNAN, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by claimant from an order of disallowance by the referee.

Claimant, in her petition filed as dependent widow on 

January 29, 1952, alleged that Tommie Lee Gunn died on

December 15, 1951, as a result of a fractured skull suffered when he fell 40 feet from an overhead crane on December 

15, 1951. Defendant's answer, filed on February 11, 1952, denied liability on the ground that decedent's presence on the crane was not required by his employment and that his death resulted from a violation of the positive orders of his employer and in direct violation of the law. 

After hearings, the referee found that decedent's death resulted from his violation of the express orders of his employer and in violation of certain regulations of the Secretary of Labor and Industry, covered by the Act of 

May 18, 1937, P. L. 654, and in accordance with such findings, dismissed claimant's petition. This appeal followed. 

The facts in this case, as gathered from the testimony presented, shows that decedent was employed by defendant as a shake out man and his duties consisted of unhooking and removing castings brought to a place where he was working, on the floor, near an overhead crane. On the night of his accidental death, he, his buddy, John Lee, and the crane operator, Oliver Banner, ate lunch together, between 8 and 8:30 p.m. At the conclusion of the lunch period,

1954] 

Lee returned to the place where he and decedent customarily worked, Banner started back to his crane and decedent also walked away from the place where the parties had been having their lunch.

Lee testified that about 15 minutes later, he saw decedent, who was already dead, lying in the flask (a mold container) and that decedent "had fell off the crane down in the flask". The witness further testified that signs had been posted in the old mill (the parties worked and the accident happened in the new mill which was next door to the old mill) forbidding employes to go up the crane without orders to do so. The witness further testified that he had not actually seen decedent fall but that decedent's body landed directly beneath the crane. The witness further stated that the signs forbidding employes to ascend the crane were not always posted.

Oliver Banner testified that at lunch time decedent had asked him to teach him how to operate the crane and that he had promised to do so "sometime"; that after the lunch whistle blew, he did not see decedent again until he fell.

The witness admitted that he had not seen the place from which decedent fell, but testified that he had seen decedent in midair as he was falling. The witness further testified that, though he did not see from where decedent had fallen, he knew decedent had not fallen from the cab, since he, the witness, who was the crane operator was in the cab of the crane as decedent's body hurtled past.

Defendant introduced in evidence a copy of "Regulations of Cranes and Hoists" issued by the Secretary of Labor and

Industry, providing that "no one but an authorized person shall enter a crane cage" and providing for summary proceedings before an alderman for violation, and upon conviction fixing penalties as set out by section 15 of the Act of May 18, 1937, P. L. 654.

Decedent in this case died on his employer's premises, as a result of an accident, and the employer, in order to escape liability, has to show that the accident resulted from a violation by decedent of positive orders or from a violation of the law.

We do not think that defendant met the burden of proof imposed upon it, as to the violation of positive orders. The evidence does show that signs were posted in the "old mill" forbidding unauthorized employes from ascending cranes, but it does not appear that said signs were posted during the time decedent was employed in the "old mill" (decedent only worked in the old mill for three or four months). 

There was no testimony that such signs were posted in the "new mill" where decedent was working at the time of his fatal accident. There is no testimony that decedent had received oral orders. Therefore, we must conclude that defendant did not meet the burden of proof on this point: 

Young v. Workmen's Insurance Fund, 137 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 405.

However, we are convinced that it has been shown that decedent violated the regulations issued by the Secretary of Labor and Industry. While there is no direct evidence that decedent attempted to enter the cage of the crane, the attending circumstances strongly point to that fact. Decedent had requested the crane operator a few minutes before his death to teach him to operate the crane, and he was seen falling from the crane. The only logical destination of decedent after ascending the crane would be the cage. We, therefore, hold that decedent violated the law and that claimant is not entitled to recover: Demark v. Pittston Coal Co., 131 

Pa. Superior Ct. 49; Skiba v. Calvitti Coal Co., 153 Pa. Superior Ct. 628; Rich Hill Coal Co. v. Bashore, 334 Pa. 449.

In view of the above, we amend the referee's fifth finding of fact to read as follows: 

Finding of Fact 5. That decedent's death was the result of his own actions, which in the opinion of the board was in violation of certain regulations of the Secretary of Labor and Industry, covered by the Act of May 18, 1937, P. L. 654.

We affirm all other findings of fact, conclusions of law and the order of the referee. Claimant's appeal is dismissed.

1954] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD PAUL v. STATE WORKMEN'S INSURANCE FUND et al. 

Workmen's compensation-Occupational disease-Section 108(m) 

of act-Tuberculosis in occupation of nursing-Orderly in hospital.

Section 108 (m) of The Occupational Disease Act, defining as an occupational disease "tuberculosis in the occupation of nursing in hospitals or sanitaria involving exposure to such disease", applies to the duties performed by an orderly in a State sanatorium, where such duties brought him in proximity to the tuberculosis patients therein, and, therefore, an award of compensation for disability as a result of tuberculosis contracted by him will be affirmed. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Schwing, District No. 7, claim petition no. 129,443, accident no. 

6,050,048, A-38686. Affirmed, January 27, 1954.

State Workmen's Insurance Fund, insurance carrier. 

Sidney E. Rosenblum, for claimant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for defendants.

KNOLL, Chairman: 

This is an appeal by defendant from the order of award of Referee Emil C. Schwing, District No. 7.

On February 6, 1953, claimant filed a petition pursuant to the provisions of The Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act, averring that he became totally disabled on January 30, 1953, as a result of tuberculosis. To this petition responsive answers were filed by the State Workmen's Insurance Board, custodian of the Occupational Disease Fund, and by the State Workmen's Insurance Fund, denying the alle gations of the said petition.

The matter was duly referred to Referee Schwing before whom testimony was taken. The referee, by decision of 

August 4, 1953, found as a fact that claimant became totally disabled on January 30, 1953, as a result of tuberculosis contracted in the course of his employment and, based upon

this and other findings of fact, entered an order of award against the State Workmen's Insurance Fund. From this ruling the State Workmen's Insurance Fund has appealed, contending that the act applies only to registered nurses who have been authorized to practice the occupation of nursing.

We have made a careful study of the record in this case and are of the opinion that the ruling of the referee should be affirmed.

The testimony in this case discloses that claimant was employed as an orderly at the Cresson State Sanatorium from January 16, 1950, to January 29, 1953, and that his duties consisted of taking care of urinals and bedpans of some 25 patients who were bedridden; that, in addition thereto, claimant provided the patients with water for bathing and for cleaning their teeth; that claimant disposed of sputum cups of the patients, removed other waste materials, assisted in cleaning the wards and performed other duties required of him from time to time, all of which brought him in proximity to the tubercular patients in the said sanatorium. The testimony further discloses that claimant became totally disabled on January 30, 1953, as a result of active tuberculosis, which disability will continue for an indefinite period.

Section 108 (m) of the act defines as an occupational disease "tuberculosis in the occupation of nursing in hospitals or sanitaria involving exposure to such disease." 

Nursing has been defined as "to take care of or attend a sick person or invalid; the tending of the sick or infirm". 

Under the Statutory Construction Act, 46 PS §533, words and phrases shall be construed according to the rules of grammar and according to their common and approved usage. Statutes are presumed to employ words in their popular sense: Commonwealth v. Bay State Milling Co., 312 Pa. 28. Words and phrases are given their common interpretation: Butler County v. Leibold, 107 Pa. 407. 

It is obvious that the duties performed by claimant in this case were those of nursing in the acceptive use of that term. We do not believe that there is any merit to defend-

1954] 

 ant's contention that the act applies only to registered nurses who have been authorized to practice the occupation of nursing. Had the legislature intended the act to apply only to registered nurses, it could have easily so stated in defining to whom the provisions of Section 108 (m) should apply. In the absence of such restriction, it is our opinion that the legislature intended that the act apply to one who performs the duties of nursing in its accepted term, which is the tending of the sick or infirm. It is our opinion that claimant meets the requirements of the act and, by reason thereof, is entitled to the payment of compensation. 

We, therefore, affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of award. 

The appeal of the State Workmen's Insurance Fund is dismissed.

COMMON PLEAS COURT OF LACKAWANNA COUNTY HUGHES v. THE CONTINENTAL-ARCHBALD COAL COMPANY et al. 

Workmen's compensation-Anthraco-silicosis-Sufficiency of notice 

-Notice of total disability prior to actual occurrence-Right to change date on basis of medical testimony-Liability for compensation-Occu pational Disease Act of June 21, 1939, sec. 311-Amendments of Au gust 24, 1953 to section 301 (g). 

1. Where an employe has notified the superintendent and general manager of his employer that he was suffering from anthraco-silicosis, and could not work, and has filed a claim petition asserting total disability as of the date he left work, he has given sufficient notice within the meaning of section 311 of the Occupational Disease Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 566, even though it has subsequently developed as a medical fact that the disability did not become total until several months after the notice was given and the claim filed, and even though the claim was erroneously filed against a predecessor of the employer which, however, had an interlocking relationship with its successor. 

2. A finding by the Workmen's Compensation Board that claimant suffered total disability as of a certain date may not be disturbed by the court, where supported by competent medical testimony.

3. A claimant is not barred from establishing that his total disability occurred as of a date later than that originally claimed by him, where the earlier date was the result of his own self-diagnosis, but the later date is supported by competent medical evidence. 

4. The amendment to section 301 (g) of the Occupational Disease 

Act of August 24, 1953 (Act No. 395), providing that all compensation for anthraco-silicosis incurred under certain conditions shall be paid by the Commonwealth, instead of partially by the Commonwealth and partially by the employer as required by preëxisting law, changes substantive rights and is not therefore applicable to a case in which not only the disability occurred but the award was made prior to its passage.

Appeal from Workmen's Compensation Board. C. P. of 

Lackawanna County, September term, 1953, no. 1485. 

Affirmed. November 18, 1953. 

Milton J. Kolansky, for claimant.

J. H. Oliver, Franklin B. Gelder and Anthony F. X. 

D'Iorio, for defendants.

HOBAN, P. J. 

Defendants and Commonwealth, joined in appealing from a decision of the Workmen's Compensation Board affirming an award for total disability due to anthraco-silicosis, under the provisions of the Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act. 

These pertinent facts appear:

On July 11, 1951, claimant, who knew he had anthracosilicosis to some degree, became ill at his work at the Continental Colliery of defendant coal company, had to cease work and never returned. On August 18, 19, 1951, claimant informed Sanders, superintendent of the Continental Mine, and Lamb, general manager of defendant company, that he was ill from miners' asthma (anthraco-silicosis) and could not work. On August 27, 1951, he filed a claim petition against Moffat Coal Company, asserting total disability as of July 11, 1951.

Claimant who had been engaged in mining for 47 years, had worked continuously for 14 years prior to July 11, 1951,

1954] 

 in the Continental Mine. It appears that up to 1944 the mine had been operated by the Moffat Coal Company, but from 1944 up to July 11, 1951, it was operated as a property of Continental-Archbald Coal Company, under the same general management as Moffat Coal Company, Lamb being the general manager of both corporations. During the pendency of the claim against Moffat Coal Company, claimant received medical treatment and advice, being finally advised by his family physician that at Thanksgiving time 1951 he considered claimant then totally disabled. From this circumstance claimant sets the date of total disability in the case at bar as November 25, 1951.

The case against Moffat Coal Company proceeded to the point of hearing before Referee Donlan on August 21, 1952. 

Testimony of claimant and of Doctors McAndrew and Foy for claimant was heard. At the hearing the question of the proper corporate defendant was raised, and counsel for Moffat Coal Company moved to dismiss the claim. Counsel for claimant thereupon announced that he would file a new claim immediately. Accordingly a new claim petition, naming Continental-Archbald Coal Company, was prepared and filed with the board on August 22, 1952. This is now before us.

At the hearing of this petition it was agreed that the medical testimony offered in the hearing on the first petition could be considered as if given in this proceeding. 

The referee found total disability due solely to anthracosilicosis as of November 25, 1951, and that the disease progressed to a point of disablement over a period in excess of five years. An award for total disability was made, and assessed 60 percent against the Continental-Archbald Coal Company and 40 percent against the Pennsylvania State 

Workmen's Insurance Fund. The board on appeal affirmed the award and the appeal to this court followed. 

In this appeal appellants raise these questions: 

1. Claimant is barred from receiving compensation because he failed to notify Continental-Archbald Coal Company within 90 days after the beginning of his alleged disability, as required by section 311 of the act.

2. Claimant is barred from receiving any compensation because he is not in fact totally disabled from anthracosilicosis, as required by section 301 (e) of the act. 

3. Claimant is barred from receiving any compensation for failure to file his claim in this case within one year after the alleged disability began, as required by section 315 of the act.

As to proposition 1, appellants' theory is that the only notice given as to claimant's disability was that of August 18, 1951, which was in fact some three months prior to the actual date of established total disability which he alleges in the current claim petition. Appellants argue that a notice of impending or anticipated disability is not sufficient to comply with the notice requirements of the act, but under the general principles of construction of the Workmen's Compensation Laws as remedial legislation to be construed liberally to effect the purposes of the acts, it is quite clear claimant gave notice to responsible officials of both Continental-Archbald Coal Company and Moffat Coal Company that he was disabled by reason of anthraco-silicosis, even though incorrectly believing at the time that disability was then total and permanent. Furthermore, it is inconceivable that the officials of this company did not have continuing awareness and notice that during the pendency of the action against Moffat Coal Company he claimed total disability from whichever corporate entity was in fact his employer at the time he was obliged to quit work. All the purposes comprehended by the notice provisions of the act were, therefore, adequately served, and in our opinion it would be a harsh, unjust and conscienceless application of the notice rule to deny this claimant an opportunity to be heard on this petition because of the technical provisions of section 311 of the act.

As to proposition 2, there is competent and substantial medical evidence to support the finding that claimant was in fact totally and permanently disabled from anthracosilicosis as of November 25, 1951. Evidence as to the degree of disability is controverted, it is true, but we are required to view the evidence in the light most favorable to claimant.

1954] 

Granting there is competent evidence to sustain the finding, it is not our duty to balance the evidence nor substitute findings of fact for those made by the referee and affirmed by the board.

Proposition no. 3 rests upon the theory that if claimant is totally disabled from anthraco-silicosis, the actual date of disability must have been July 11, 1951. The argument is that claimant, having asserted that as the date of disability in his petition against Moffat Coal Company, cannot now disavow it and claim a later date so as to bring the current petition within the time limitation for filing his claim. We do not believe that claimant's effort at selfdiagnosis or personal speculation as to the incidence or degree of his disability ought to destroy the value of competent medical evidence, which definitely fixes another date for the beginning of total disability. As to this matter, the opinion of Chairman Knoll of the Workmen's Compensa tion Board is significant: “Although, in his first petition, claimant alleged total disability on July 11, 1951, claimant testified that he did not know that he was then disabled from anthraco-silicosis and that his doctor first advised him in November 1951 of his total disability from anthraco-silicosis. We believe that it would be unfair to claimant to hold that he knew that he was totally disabled from the disease on July 11, 1951, to impute to him a knowledge of total disability, the existence of which was denied by defendant's medical expert as of January 1953. In view of the conflict of medical opinion, we could not expect claimant to make a true diagnosis of the cause of his disability and we cannot regard his allegation of total disability as of July 11, 1951, as destructive of the credibility of the testimony on his behalf."

Obviously, if we accept, as did the referee and the board, the actual date of total disability as November 25, 1951, this petition clearly was filed within the prescribed period of limitation.

On the whole record and particularly considering the obvious interlocking relationship between Moffat Coal Company and Continental-Archbald Coal Company, we believe

that defendants had adequate and proper notice of the claim for total and permanent disability from anthraco-silicosis; that the finding of total disability is supported by competent and substantial evidence, and that the claim petition was filed within the required time. Hence, the award of the 

Workmen's Compensation Board must be affirmed. 

By brief counsel for defendant Continental-Archbald Coal Company advanced another proposition, to wit, that by an amendment to section 301 (g) of the Act of August 24, 1953 (Act no. 395), it is provided that in the five-year development cases where disability or death is not conclusively proven to be the result of last exposure, all compensation shall be paid by the Commonwealth. No such conclusion was made here, nor was it required under the law prior to August 24, 1953. It is argued that since this portion of the act is procedural rather than substantive, it ought to apply to pending litigation. Here it is to be noted that the 

Commonwealth has not had any opportunity to argue this contention and if we thought there was merit in it, we would have to return the record to the Workmen's Compensation Board for review on this point. However, it is quite clear that this amendment imposing new and hitherto unknown liability on the Commonwealth, was passed not only after this action was commenced, but after the Workmen's Compensation Board rendered its opinion affirming the award of the referee. Any act of the legislature which imposes a financial liability on the Commonwealth, or any other person for that matter, which had not hitherto been a legal responsibility of the Commonwealth or the party concerned, is certainly not procedural but substantive in nature and cannot affect any liabilities which accrued under the law as it stood before this amendment. We are not, therefore, warranted either in changing the rule as to the proportions payable by defendant coal company and the Commonwealth, or in returning the record to the board for further consideration. 

Now, November 18, 1953, the appeal of defendant Continental-Archbald Coal Company and the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania from the decision of the Workmen's Com-

1954] 

 pensation Board in the above-captioned case is dismissed, and judgment is directed to be entered in favor of claimant, in conformity with the award of the referee and the Workmen's Compensation Board. The form of definitive judgment is to be prepared by counsel for claimant, submitted for approval to counsel for defendant and the Commonwealth, and then to the court for signature. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD ZEPKO v. McFEELY BRICK COMPANY Workmens' compensation-Occupational disease-Death-Claim petition-Limitations. 

A claim petition by a widow for compensation for death allegedly due to silicosis not filed within one year after the date of death, is barred by section 315 of The Occupational Disease Act of 1939, in the absence of any misconduct on the part of the employer in lulling the claimant into a false sense of security as to his rights, and it is, therefore, immaterial that the claimant had no knowledge of the fact that death resulted from silicosis until a time within one year prior to the filing of the claim petition. 

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Foust, District No. 9, claim petition no. 130,179, accident no. 

6069271, A-38661. Affirmed, January 13, 1954. 

Penna. Mfrs. Assn. Cas. Ins. Co., insurance carrier. 

Gene E. McDonald, for claimant.

H. R. Belden and C. M. McCarthy, for defendant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for Commonwealth of Pennsylvania.

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

Claimant, Julia Kabala Zepko, has taken this appeal from the order of Referee Foust disallowing compensation for

the death of her husband, Thomas Kabala, on August 9, 1950, allegedly due to silicosis. The claim petition was filed on April 24, 1953. The referee found that the death was not caused solely by silicosis and also that the claim was barred by the statute of limitations, since the petition was not filed within one year after the date of death. 

In the brief filed in behalf of claimant, it is contended that claimant had no knowledge of the fact that the death certificate showed silicosis as the cause of death until March 3, 1953. It is argued that the intention of the legislature was to give a claimant "one year after the date of reasonable knowledge" of the cause of death in which to file a claim under the act: Section 315 of the Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 566, provides: "In cases of death all claims for compensation shall be forever barred, unless, within one year after the death, the parties shall have agreed upon the compensation payable under this Article, or unless, within one year after the death, one of the parties shall have filed a petition as provided in Article Four hereof." 

The language is clear and unequivocal and strongly indicates that the compensation authorities have no discretion in allowing a period of grace or indulgence, unless the employer has been guilty of wrongdoing or misconduct in lulling a claimant into a false sense of security as to his rights. There is considerable court authority to this effect. 

In the case of Reichert v. Pennsylvania Railroad Co., 156 

Pa. Superior Ct. 213, the court said: "We have ruled that such a provision in workmen's compensation statutes is not a mere technical statute of limitation, but is a statute of repose intended to bar the claim, or right to compensation, unless its provisions are complied with, and that it need not be specially pleaded to be enforced. 'We are of the opinion that it was its (the Legislature's) intent to make the filing of a claim petition within the specified time an express condition of the right to obtain an award of compensation, and that failure so to do should operate as an absolute bar of the right. The court below was entirely correct in holding that such a statute need not

1954] 

 be specially pleaded': Ratto v. Pennsylvania Coal Co., 102 

Pa. Superior Ct. 242, 247; 156 A. 749. See also, Cosenza v. General Baking Co., 147 Pa. Superior Ct. 591, 595-6, 24 A. 2d 735.”

In the case of Ratto v. Pennsylvania Coal Co., supra, the court pointed out that the limitation contained in section 315 extinguishes the right and not merely the remedy and may be invoked without having been specially pleaded. Section 315 is a fatal bar to claimant's present petition. We see no merit in the contention that claimant's late discovery of the cause of death should toll the statute. It is, therefore, not necessary to consider whether the death was due solely to silicosis within the contemplation of the act. However, we have reviewed the testimony, and we are in accord with the referee that the record does not establish satisfactorily that death was due solely to silicosis. 

Dr. Joseph F. Lechman, claimant's witness, gave a coronary occulsion as the cause of death, and silicosis and duodenal ulcer as antecedent causes. He testified that exmination in 1944 indicated a moderately advanced silicosis and that in April 1945, he prescribed digitalis for a heart condition. The duodenal ulcer was disclosed by X-ray on 

November 4, 1949. In direct examination, the doctor testified: "Q. After his death, do you recall whether you ever advised Mrs. Kabala as to the cause of death? 

A. Mrs. Kabala came to my office and asked me whether 

I thought silicosis was the cause of his death, and I said I think it was probably a contributing factor and she said, 'Is he entitled to compensation?" I said, 'I don't know about that but you might try to establish a claim.' I don't know the date she came to see me about it.”

He thought claimant's visit was in 1952. The doctor's testimony does not convince us that silicosis was the sole cause of death, as required by the statute.

We hereby affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of disallowance.

The appeal of claimant is dismissed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD BAUMGARDNER v. STATE WORKMEN'S INSURANCE FUND et al. 

Workmen's compensation-Occupational disease-Section 108(m) 

of act-Tuberculosis in occupation of nursing-attendant in hospital. 

Section 108 (m) of The Occupational Disease Act, defining as an occupational disease "tuberculosis in the occupation of nursing in hospitals or sanitaria involving exposure to such disease”, applies to an attendant at a State hospital, where it appears that his duties were performed in a tubercular ward of the hospital and consisted of the taking of general care of the patients therein, and therefore an award for disability resulting from tuberculosis which he contracted will be affirmed. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Dillman,

District No. 8, claim petition no. 130,217, accident no. 

6077639, A-38688. Affirmed, January 27, 1954.

State Workmen's Insurance Fund, insurance carrier. 

Sidney E. Rosenblum, for claimant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for defendants.

KNOLL, Chairman: 

This is an appeal by the State Workmen's Insurance Fund from the order of award of Referee Alvin E. Dillman, District No. 8.

On April 27, 1953, claimant filed a petition pursuant to the provisions of The Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act averring that he became totally disabled on March 30, 1953, as a result of tuberculosis. To this petition responsive answers were filed by the State Workmen's Insurance Board, custodian of the Occupational Disease Fund, and by the State Workmen's Insurance Fund, denying the alle gations of the said petition.

The matter was duly referred to Referee Dillman before whom testimony was taken. The referee, by decision of

1954] 

August 7, 1953, found as a fact that claimant became temporarily totally disabled as a result of tuberculosis on April 8, 1953, contracted by claimant as a result of his employment with the Mayview State Hospital and, based upon this and other findings of fact, entered an order of award against the State Workmen's Insurance Fund. From this ruling the State Workmen's Insurance Fund has appealed contending that the act applies only as to registered nurses who have been authorized to practice the occupation of nursing.

We have made a careful study of the record in this case and are of the opinion that the ruling of the referee should be affirmed.

The testimony in this case discloses that claimant was employed as an attendant at the Mayview State Hospital from December 16, 1949, to April 8, 1953; that the duties of the claimant were performed in the tubercular ward of the hospital and consisted of clothing, bathing, feeding and shaving patients together with the handling of soiled laundry of the patients, the removal of sputum cups and other waste material and the general care of tubercular patients. As a result of his duties, claimant was exposed to the disease of tuberculosis which he contracted, causing him to become disabled as of April 8, 1953. 

The issue in this case is whether or not, under the aforesaid set of facts, claimant is entitled to compensation under the provisions of The Occupational Disease Act. 

Section 108 (m) of the act defines as an occupational disease "tuberculosis in the occupation of nursing in hospitals or sanitaria involving exposure to such disease". 

Nursing has been defined as "to take care of or attend a sick person or invalid; the tending of the sick or infirm". 

Under the Statutory Construction Act, 46 PS §533, words and phrases shall be construed according to the rules of grammar and according to their common and approved usage. Statutes are presumed to employ words in their popular sense: Commonwealth v. Bay State Milling Co.,

312 Pa. 28. Words and phrases are given their common interpretation: Butler County v. Leibold, 107 Pa. 407. 

It is obvious that the duties performed by claimant in this case were those of nursing in the accepted use of that term. We do not believe that there is any merit to defendant's contention that the act applies only to one who is a registered nurse and has been authorized to practice the occupation of nursing. Had the legislature intended the act to apply only as to registered nurses, it could have easily so stated in defining to whom the provisions of section 108 (m) should apply. In the absence of such restriction, it is our opinion that the legislature intended that the act apply to one who performs the duties of nursing in its accepted term which is the tending of the sick or infirm. 

It is our opinion that claimant meets the requirements of the act and, by reason thereof, is entitled to the payment of compensation.

We, therefore, affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of award. 

The appeal of the State Workmen's Insurance Fund is dismissed.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA KUGRIS v. HAMMOND COAL COMPANY et al. 

Workmen's compensation-Occupational disease-Death-Claim for burial expenses-Receipt by deceased of full compensation in lifetime 

-Limitations—Section 301(c) of Act of June 21, 1939, as amended. 

Where claimant for occupational disease compensation collected during his lifetime the entire award of $4,000, a claim by his widow for burial expenses, following his death as a result of the disease, in the amount provided by the Occupational Disease Act was not barred by section 301(a) of the act, providing that the maximum compensation payable for disability and death shall not exceed the sum of $4,000, nor by the further provision of section 301 (c) that where an

1954] 

 employe dies more than three years after the date of his last employment the claim is barred.

Appeal from C. P. of Schuylkill County, May term, 1952, no. 501. Reversed, 174 Pa. Superior Ct. 376. 

Thomas B. Noonan, with him Thomas L. Kennedy, Jr., for appellant.

E. Mac Troutman, with him Penrose Hertzler, for appellee. 

C. A. Whitehouse, associate counsel, with him Ralph H.

Behney, counsel, for State Workmen's Insurance Fund. 

RENO, J.:

Appellant's husband, Frank Kugris, became totally disabled by anthraco-silicosis on July 19, 1947, and his last day of employment was July 18, 1947. He was allowed occupational disease compensation and during his lifetime collected the entire award of $4,000. He died on May 27, 1951, as a result of the disease, and on June 27, 1951, appellant filed a claim for $200 for burial expenses. The claim was disallowed by the referee and the Workmen's Compensation Board affirmed.

The referee based his disallowance upon the conclusion: "2. Having found claimant's decedent was paid compensation in full during his lifetime, no compensation is payable."

The board affirmed that conclusion and added another: "3. Since the decedent's death occurred more than three years after his last employment in an occupation having a silica hazard, claimant is not entitled to the expenses of the burial of her deceased."

The court below affirmed the board, resting its judgment upon the referee's conclusion. The widow appealed. The employer, Hammond Coal Company, and its insurance carrier, Coal Operators Casualty Company, are the appellees. 

The appeal calls for a review of both conclusions quoted above.

Kugris' last day of employment was July 18, 1947 and his disability began on the day following. Therefore appeallant's case must be adjudged according to the legislation then in force which was The Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 566, as amended by the 

Act of May 18, 1945, P. L. 661, which became effective on 

July 1, 1945. The pertinent sections of the acts follow: 

Act of 1945, supra, section 301(a)2: "The maximum compensation payable under this article for disability and death resulting from silicosis, anthraco-silicosis, or asbestosis shall not exceed the sum of four thousand dollars ($4000), which shall be full and complete payment for all disability, present or future, or for death from such occupational diseases arising out of employment by any and all employers in this Commonwealth." 1 (Italics supplied.) 

Act of 1939, supra, section 301 (c): “... Wherever death is mentioned as a cause for compensation under this act, it shall mean only death resulting from occupational disease and occurring within three years after the date of his last employment in such occupation or industry." (Italics supplied.) 

Act of 1945, supra, section 307: "In case of death resulting from occupational disease, compensation shall be computed on the following basis and distributed to the following persons, subject to the limitations of section 301." (Italics supplied.) 

Act of 1945, supra, section 307(8): "Whether or not there be dependents as aforesaid, the reasonable expense of burial, not exceeding two hundred dollars, which shall be paid by the employer or insurer directly to the undertaker (without deduction of any amounts theretofore paid for compensation or for medical expenses)."2 (Italics supplied.) 

1 Increased to $6,500 by the Act of January 2, 1952, P. L. 1811, sec. 1, 77 PS §1401, and by the Act of August 24, 1953 (No. 395), sec. 1, to $7,500. 

2 Increased to $250 by the Act of May 14, 1949, P. L. 1379, sec. 2, 77 PS §1407, and by the Act of August 24, 1953 (No. 395), sec. 3, to $425.

1954] 

Section 307, supra, provides a schedule for the distribution of the compensation of a deceased compensated or compensable employe. It designates the persons who are to take the compensation of the deceased employe and the amounts and the proportions they are to take. The persons so designated are the deceased's dependent blood relatives. 

Section 307 (8) provides for burial expenses and they are payable, not to the deceased's relatives, but to his undertaker, "whether or not there be dependents" of the deceased. 

So, although the provision for burial expenses appears, oddly enough, in the schedule of distribution it constitutes a separate and distinct obligation of the employer to the deceased's undertaker.

Moreover, since the undertaker is to be paid "without deduction of any amounts theretofore paid for compensation or medical expenses",³ the provision for burial expenses is plainly and unequivocally an extra and additional item of the employer's liability. For subrogation purposes, burial expenses are properly regarded as compensation, because they are truly a part of the total liability of the employer: 

Myers v. Philadelphia Daily News, 168 Pa. Superior Ct. 

561, 79 A. 2d 787. Where the employer pays the burial expenses he is entitled to subrogation for them: Leach v. 

Meadow Gold Dairies, Inc., 171 Pa. Superior Ct. 594, 91

A. 2d 293. There is a palpable distinction between the compensation paid to an employe or his dependents to reimburse him or them for the loss of wages or earning power, which is perhaps the prime object of the statute, and the compensation provided for medical services and burial expenses which relieve him or his dependents from the payment of necessary expenditures, directly caused by the compensable occupational disease. Both are compensation but patently they differ as to their objectives, and payment in 

3 A similar provision occurs in the act, supra, sec. 306 (g), 77 PS §1406, where, after providing that unpaid compensation shall be distributed to the deceased's dependents under section 307, it is enacted that "no reduction shall be made for the amount which may have been paid, or contracted to be paid, for medical and hospital services and medicines, nor for the expenses of the last sickness and burial." (Italics supplied.)

full of one category of compensation does not exonerate an employer from payment of another. That is the plain and obvious meaning of the phrase, "without deduction of any amounts theretofore paid for compensation or medical expenses". "Compensation" in that phrase pointedly refers to reimbursement for loss of wages or earning power. By joining "compensation" and "medical expenses” in the same sentence and thereby revealing and emphasizing the categorical difference between them, the act itself recognized the fundamental distinction, and "burial expenses" are but another separate and distinct item of compensation for which the employer is liable. The case is closely akin to Kopchak v. Lincoln Gas Coal 

Co., 79 Pa. Superior Ct. 536, 539, decided in the early days of the workmen's compensation legislation. The original workmen's compensation statute contained a provision strikingly similar to the one in question. Act of June 2, 1915, P. L. 736, sec. 307 (9). The Act of 1915 was amended by the Act of June 26, 1919, P. L. 642, sec. 306 (e), whereby the expense of hospital treatment was imposed upon the employer. Against an employer's contention that, having paid the hospital expenses of a deceased employe, he was relieved from the payment of burial expenses, this court held: "The clear meaning of paragraph seven [of the Act of 1919, supra, which reenacted paragraph nine of the Act of 1915, supra,] is that in case of death the dependents, or if there be none, then the personal representatives of the deceased, shall receive from the employer an amount not exceeding one hundred dollars, in the nature of an exemption fund, to meet burial and other expenses of the last illness." (Italics supplied.) See also Scott v. Hower & 

Stender, 88 Pa. Superior Ct. 17.

The doctrine of the cases is that compensation, regarded 4 "Whether or not there be dependents, as aforesaid, the reasonable expenses of the last sickness and burial, not exceeding one hundred dollars (without deduction of any amounts theretofore paid for compensation or for medical expenses), payable to the dependents, or, if there be no dependents, then to the personal representatives of the deceased." (Italics supplied.)

1954] 

 as a whole, comprehends various classes of indemnity payments, i.e., loss of wages or earning power, medical expenses, hospital treatment, and burial expenses. This view accords with, and is largely drawn from, that expressed in 

Haley v. Matthews, 104 Pa. Superior Ct. 313, 316, 158 Atl. 

645, followed in the Myers case, supra, where Judge 

Baldridge, later President Judge, said: "The word 'compensation' appears very frequently in article III [of the Workmen's Compensation Act of June 

2, 1915, P. L. 736, as amended]. It is used, in our judgment, both in its limited sense-contemplating the loss of wages only, and in the more comprehensive sense-including medical and hospital expenses.' 

In addition to the Myers case, supra, the doctrine of the Haley case has been followed in variant factual situations in Staller v. Staller, 144 Pa. Superior Ct. 83, 18 A. 2d 537, affirmed 343 Pa. 86, 21 A. 2d 16; Shank v. Consolidation 

Coal Co., 161 Pa. Superior Ct. 304, 54 A. 2d 289; Leach v. 

Meadow Gold Dairies, Inc., supra. See also Patterson v. 

General Refractories Co., 42 D. & C. 628.

It is in what Judge Baldridge called "its limited sense" that the word "compensation" is employed in section 301 (a) 2, supra. The limitation of $4,000 therein provided for disability and death resulting from anthraco-silicosis plainly refers only to the loss of wages and does not include the amounts required to be paid by other sections of the statute, such as surgical and medical expenses, hospital treatment or burial expenses. Nor does the proviso in section 

307, "subject to the limitations in section 301" bar recovery for the burial expenses provided by section 307 (8). Since section 301 (a) 2 refers only to compensation for loss of wages, section 307 applies only to so much of section 301 as provides for the distribution of compensation for loss of wages of a deceased employe to his relatives and dependents. The limitation does not apply to burial or other expenses. So that, although Kugris received during his life the maximum compensation for the loss of wages, his widow is still entitled to claim on behalf of the undertaker the sum of $200 for burial expenses.

Turning to the contention that appellant's claim is barred by section 301 (c), supra, we agree with the court below that the principles enunciated in Toffalori v. Donatelli Granite Co., 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 311, 315, 43 A. 2d 584, apply to the instant case and definitely settle the issue. There, the employer and its insurance carrier sought to defeat a widow's claim for the unpaid balance of compensation awarded to her husband in his lifetime. There, as here, the husband died more than three years after the date of his last employment. In a characteristically logical opinion our late colleague, Judge Dithrich, said, inter alia: "In our opinion the words of Section 301 (c), 'Wherever death is mentioned as a cause for compensation . . . are applicable in a case where no claim is made during the employee's lifetime for compensation for disability. In such a case, where the first claim is filed after the employee's death, death is the cause for compensation. If the employee dies more than three years after the date of his last employment, the claim is barred. Section 301 (c) was intended to prevent stale claims: Meyers v. Moxham Coal Co., supra, [293 Pa. 7, 141 A. 643]. It was not intended to bar dependents from receiving the balance of compensation that has already been awarded on a prompt claim. This is the only just and reasonable construction that can be put on this provision of the act." (Italics supplied.)

As stated, section 301 (c) applies only to defeat stale claims, to original claims, and not to claims for the continuation of compensation awarded during the lifetime of the deceased and for burial expenses. The argument that all rights to compensation benefits cease after the expiration of three years from the date of the last employment, although founded upon the literal terms of the act, embraces a dangerous fallacy. It is simply incredible that the General 

Assembly should have intended by section 301 (c) to abruptly deny compensation, either for loss of wages or burial expenses, after three years, where a valid claim was made during the lifetime of the employe. In many cases, as in Toffalori and the instant case, the period during which compensation is to be paid extends beyond the three-year

1954] 

5 period. In the instant case, Kugris was awarded compensation for 200 weeks, that is, for three years and 11 months. (He died four days after the date of the check which paid his last instalment of compensation.) If appellees' argument were valid and had Kugris died three years and one day after the date of his last employment, his burial expenses would be a charge against his family, although his death was due to a disease contracted in the service of his employer, and notwithstanding that on the day of his death he was still receiving compensation payments. It cannot be believed that section 301 (c) was legislatively designed to produce such a result.
