The operation of section 301 (c) must be confined to stale claims, that is, to original claims for compensation or burial expenses in instances where no valid claim had been filed within the three-year period after the last day of employment. 

Appellees rely upon Wonderlick v. Phila. & Reading Coal & Iron Co., 170 Pa. Superior Ct. 65, 84 A. 2d 233, but that case strongly fortifies our position. There the employe filed a claim for disability compensation which was dismissed because he had failed to give notice to his employer. After his death, which occurred more than three years after the date of his last employment, his widow filed a claim for compensation, which was refused because it was the first or original claim filed more than three years after her husband's last day of employment, his own claim having been invalid. However, the case definitely stands for the proposition that where an employe files a valid claim in his lifetime, his widow is, after his death, entitled to the balance of his compensation for loss of wages and burial expenses, even though her claim is filed more than three years after his last employment.

The record contains no allegation, evidence, or finding of the identity of the undertaker. Since the burial expenses "shall be paid by the employer or insurer directly to the undertaker", a finding should indicate to whom the payment shall be made. If it should appear that appellant has 

5 Curiously, the final receipt offered in evidence, signed by Kugris' mark, is dated June 4, 1951, a week after his death.

fully paid the undertaker for his services, the award for burial expenses in the sum of $200 may be made directly to her.

The judgment is reversed and the record is remitted to the court below with direction to return it to the Board for an award conforming to this opinion.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA PUSKARICH v. PUSKARICH, Jr. 

Workmen's compensation-Appeal-Interlocutory or final order— 

Order remanding record to board to include insurer as defendant. 

An order of the court below sustaining exceptions to a Workmen's Compensation Board's decision that an employer was not insured because his policy had been cancelled prior to the accident, which order remanded the record to the board with directions to modify the award to include the insurer as a defendant, was a final appealable order, since it required no further testimony nor findings of fact by the board, and where the insurer appealed only from the modified order of the board, the issue was res adjudicata. 

Appeal from Workmen's Compensation Board. C. P. of 

Fayette County. Affirmed, 174 Pa. Superior Ct. 581.

C. A. Whitehouse, associate counsel, with him Ralph H. 

Behney, counsel, and Frank F. Truscott, Attorney General, for appellant. 

Thomas A. Wagoner, Jr., with him Joseph P. Matuschak, for appellee.

GUNTHER, J.: 

Claimant applied for compensation for injuries sustained while working for his father, the defendant employer. The other defendant, State Workmen's Insurance Fund, contended that the employer was not insured because his policy had been canceled prior to the accident. After hearings the referee awarded compensation against the employer only,

1954] 

 finding as a fact that there was no insurance coverage at the time of the accident. The board affirmed the referee, but the court below sustained exceptions to the board's decision, overruled that decision, and remanded the record to the board with directions to modify the award to include the Insurance Fund as a defendant. The Insurance Fund took no appeal from that order of the court below. When the board entered an order, modified as directed by the court, the Insurance Fund then appealed, and the court below dismissed the appeal on the grounds that the issue was res adjudicata. The Insurance Fund has appealed to this court contending that the order of remission of the court below was not a final, appealable order, but that it properly waited to appeal until the board had issued its modified order in accordance with the decree of court. 

There is no question that if the first decree of the court below was a final order the matter is res adjudicata, since the record discloses that the parties and the issue of insurance coverage were the same in both cases. The court below by its order held that there was insufficient competent evidence to sustain the board's conclusion of law that the employer was not insured. Such decision was a question of law, since the court cannot make findings of fact, the Board being the ultimate fact finding body: Gill v. Fives, 170 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 564, 88 A. 2d 109. If the court decides that additional evidence is required or new findings of fact must be made, it must remit the record to the board for further action, and an order thereon will be interlocutory and not appealable. Section 427 of The Workmen's Compensation Act, 77 PS §§877 and 879; Shemanchick v. M. & S. Coal 

Company, Inc., 167 Pa. Superior Ct. 350, 74 A. 2d 764.

However, in this case, the order of remission required no further testimony or findings of facts by the board, but only the formal entry of a new order in accordance with the court's conclusion of law. Where the question is one wholly of law it is not necessary that the record be remitted for the entry of the only possible order under the court's ruling. 

Diaz v. Jones & Laughlin Steel Corp., 170 Pa. Superior Ct. 

608, 88 A. 2d 801. Where the court nevertheless remits the

record for the entry of a modified award, the court's order is final and appealable: Strickland v. Baugh & Sons Co., 139 Pa. Superior Ct. 273, 11 A. 2d 547; Kline v. Kiehl, 157 

Pa. Superior Ct. 392, 43 A. 2d 616.

The court below, therefore, entered a final order from which no appeal was taken within the statutory period. 

Order affirmed.

COMMON PLEAS COURT OF PHILADELPHIA COUNTY BUSH v. LEHIGH BRICK COMPANY et al. 

Workmen's compensation-Partial disability Satisfaction of award 

-Payment or receipt of equivalent wages—Petition for reinstatement after expiration of 800-week period.

Where it appeared that an agreement for total disability was superseded by an award for 50 percent partial disability, which disability the board found continued until the end of the 300-week period from the time of the accident, it was error for the board to reverse an award of the referee pursuant to claimant's petition filed after the end of the 300-week period upon the ground that the first award of the referee had failed to properly compensate him, where the referee upon the petition for reinstatement found that claimant had not received equivalent earnings; an award for partial disability can be satisfied only by payment of the specified compensation or the receipt by claimant of the equivalent wages during the period. 

Appeal from Workmen's Compensation Board. C. P. 

No. 6 of Philadelphia County, June term, 1953, no. 10163. 

Reversed. March 12, 1954. 

A. F. Barbieri, for plaintiff. 

J. B. Martin and H. T. Reath, for defendants. 

FLOOD, J.:

Claimant was injured in March 1946 and entered into a compensation agreement with his employer's insurance carrier the following month providing for compensation at the rate of $20 per week. On March 15, 1948, the referee terminated the agreement and awarded $16.15 per week from February 4, 1948, based upon a finding of 50 percent partial disability.

1954] 

On March 14, 1950, the referee found that the 50 percent partial disability continued but that, by reason of outside earnings, claimant had a loss in earning power of only $7.11 per week so that compensation was awarded at the rate of $4.74 weekly "to continue until such time as the claimant's disability should cease or change in extent". Compensation was paid at this rate until the end of a 300-week period from the time of the accident. Three months after the end of the 300-week period, claimant filed a petition for reinstatement of compensation upon the ground that the award failed to properly compensate him for his disability during the 300-week period. At the hearing upon this petition, the referee found that the 50 percent disability continued until the end of the 300-week period but that the claimant's earnings during 1950 averaged $15.89 and during 1951, $30.06. 

He concluded that he should have been paid at the rate of $16.15 per week during 1950 and $12.27 per week from 

January 1, 1951 until January 6, 1952, the end of the 300week period, and awarded him the difference between these amounts and the $4.74 per week actually paid him during the periods in question with interest at the rate of six percent per annum on the deferred payments. This award was based upon the fact that the claimant's earning capacity was different from that found by the referee in the order of March 14, 1950. Upon appeal to the Workmen's Compensation Board, the board affirmed the referee's finding that the 50 percent disability continued until the end of the 300-week period and did not reverse his findings as to the claimant's earnings during the period for which additional compensation had been awarded, but reversed his award upon the ground that he was without authority to award additional compensation since there had been no change in the claimant's disability since 1950 and that the change in earnings since that time were not due to a change in his physical condition but rather to other conditions in no way related to his disability. We have before us an appeal from the decision by the board.

We think the board must be reversed upon the authority of Giannetti v. Seib Co., 163 Pa. Superior Ct. 530 (1949).

There the court held that since there was an award of 25 percent disability, claimant was entitled to receive $3.90 per week for 400 weeks from the date of the injury since the award was never thereafter modified or terminated. 

The court, through Arnold, J., said on page 531: "The claimant could receive $3.90 per week by either of two methods-the payment of compensation by the defendant or the receipt by the claimant of equivalent wages during the period."

In that case claimant was not paid the full amount because credit was given against the award for earnings which he had not actually received during the period. He did not ask to be paid the additional amount until more than a year after the 400-weeks period had expired. The court said: "The referee and the board correctly determined that the question was one of satisfaction and not termination and that the award was not satisfied."

We have the same situation here. Claimant's 50 percent disability, under the reasoning of Justice Arnold who spoke for the Superior Court in the Giannetti case, entitled him to a weekly compensation of $16.15 from March 14, 1950, to the end of the 300-week period. In the court's language in that case, he could receive this $16.15 either by payment of compensation or the receipt of equivalent wages. He could also receive it by a combination of both. Until he had received it in one of these ways, the award could not be satisfied. Therefore, he is entitled to the amount awarded him by Referee Alessandroni in the order of March 2, 1953. 

Defendant argues that claimant's earning power was determined by the order of March 14, 1950, and that this is res judicata. He cites certain cases under the 1939 amendment to section 306 (b) of the act, 77 PS §512, indicating that earning power under the amended act means not what it usually means at common law but the actual earnings of claimant, so that since Referee Alessandroni decided what they were in 1950 and, since his decision was not appealed from in the statutory time, this matter is res judicata. It is quite clear from the Giannetti case that the act has not been so interpreted by the Superior Court, which evidently considers the amendment providing that "earning power"

1954]  as used in section 306 (b) "shall in no case be less than the weekly amount which the employee receives after the accident" as limiting not the award, but only the amount payable by the employer or insurance carrier. Consequently, it gives no prospective effect to a finding as to the amount of the claimant's earnings. These earnings are of concern merely in determining whether or not the award has been satisfied and, therefore, cannot be determined finally until the period is ended. This leads to the eminently sensible conclusion that a finding as to the amount of earnings before they are earned is not res judicata. 

It is also clear from the opinion in the Giannetti case that since the change in earnings has nothing to do with the right to compensation but only with the amount thereof that must be paid by the employer or the insurance carrier, the fact that this change is not related to any change in the claimant's disability or physical condition is of no importance.

Defendants seem to assume that this award must be considered res judicata because the referee did not make the award of $16.15 and suspend it as to the difference between that and $4.11 because of the earnings of claimant during this period. Any error in the form of the award cannot change the result. It is at most an error of law and must not be considered as res judicata. While Mr. Justice 

Arnold had before him in the Giannetti case an order which was in the form of a full order with a suspension, it does not appear that the form of this order had any effect upon his decision. His language in that case is a complete answer to the defendant's argument. The court says: "The distinction between earning power (or disability) and earnings must be kept in mind. In the instant case, 'the earning power' of this claimant was 25 percent reduced. 

But 'earning power' under the 1939 amendment is not to be taken as less than the wages the employee receives after the accident. The present claimant received no equivalent wages after March 9, 1943, and continued to suffer 25 percent reduction of his 'earning power'.'

The opinion then quotes Holtz v. McGraw and Bindley, 161 Pa. Superior Ct. 371 (1947) at page 378:

"Equivalent wages simply remove the claimant from the compensation rolls of the defendant during the period that the claimant receives such equivalent wages and neither fixes the amount of the claimant's disability or loss of earning power."

We can see here, as Justice Arnold says in the Giannetti case, "on this state of the record, and because the claimant had not taken action before the expiration of 400 weeks the appellants insist that they must be given credit on the award for that which admittedly the claimant did not receive and that which admittedly they never paid. The mere statement of the proposition demonstrates its injustice." 

The injustice is not less here. The award was merely improperly stated in that, instead of $16.15 per week with a suspension of all amounts above $4.11, the mathematical problem was worked out on the basis of the earnings at the date of the award and the award was made $4.11 per week.

Defendant argues that no appeal lies after the end of the 300-week period, or in the language of the act, "during the time such . . . award has to run": Section 413 of the act, as amended, 77 PS §772. But this applies only to petitions grounded upon a change of disability or status of a dependent. We are dealing with neither of these matters here. 

The point is expressly ruled against the defendant in Giannetti v. Seib, supra, at p. 532.

The order of the board must be reversed. The award of the referee should be reinstated and judgment entered thereon.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA POLINCHAK et al. v. UNEMPLOYMENT COMPEN- SATION BOARD OF REVIEW 

Unemployment compensation-Period required to repair plant following work stoppage.

Claimants for unemployment compensation were properly denied compensation on account of their unemployment following a work stoppage during the time required to restore the employer's plant to normal operation, where the delay in resuming production was due to the necessity of repairing damage to the employer's steam plant

1954] 

 occasioned when the maintenance men were withdrawn by the union during the period of the strike and work stoppage. 

Appeal from decision of Unemployment Compensation 

Board of Review. Affirmed, 175 Pa. Superior Ct. 181. 

Charles F. McKenna, with him Ernest G. Nassar, for appellant. 

William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, for appellee. 

John G. Wayman, with him Franklin L. Morgal, Francis 

St. Clair O'Leary and Reed, Smith, Shaw & McClay, for intervenor, appellee.

HIRT, J.: 

This is a group appeal by a number of employes of the Pittsburgh Steel Company at its Monessen plant from the decision of the Unemployment Compensation Board of 

Review denying compensation.

All of the claimants are members of the United Steelworkers of America, CIO. On April 29, 1952, they left their work when an industry-wide strike was called by the president of the union. As a result, all operations at the Monessen plant ceased on that date. The strike ended on May 2, 1952, and the actual work stoppage, the following day. The 

Monessen plant is an integrated steel mill and in the sequence of operations from the blast furnace to the finished product each department is dependent upon others. Because of a failure of the steam plant and the interdependence of the various departments, it was not possible to resume full operations immediately upon termination of the work stoppage. The plant did proceed according to an orderly plan for the gradual resumption of operations in all departments and each claimant was recalled when work was available in his particular unit. These claimants presented themselves for work on May 3rd but work was not available until May 

11th for some of them, and May 12th for the others, when they were recalled to their jobs. Compensation was claimed for weeks ending on one or the other of those dates. 

In anticipation of the impending labor dispute the union had agreed that, in the event of a strike, standby maintenance employes would be afforded access to the plant to

prevent damage to it and to expedite resumption of production at the end of the work stoppage. The local union had pledged coöperation in effecting an orderly shutdown of the plant and had agreed specifically to keep vital steam plants in operation. The agreement was repudiated on April 30, 1952, and of necessity the steam plant was completely closed down on that day. From a too rapid cooling of the steam plant, when the maintenance men were withdrawn by the union, ruptures in the steam pipes and separations in the joints of conducting units resulted. Steam was essential to resumption of operations, and the time necessarily consumed in repairing the damage to the steam plant was the principal factor contributing to the delay in resuming production. 

Repair of the damage was not completed until May 6th. 

Steam was available to the blast furnace on that date and the first hot metal was delivered to the open hearth furnaces on May 8th. It was not until May 12th, however, that steel could be processed and delivered to the "finishing side of the mill" where claimants were employed. 

Concededly, claimants were not eligible for compensation for the period of the stoppage of work ending May 3, 1952, because of the labor dispute culminating in the strike: Section 402 (d) of the Unemployment Compensation Law, as last amended by the Act of May 23, 1949, P. L. 1738, 43 

PS §802. But the period of a disqualifying work stoppage does not necessarily end on the date of the termination of a strike. Cf. Larely Unemployment Comp. Case, 166 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 481, 485, 72 A. 2d 300. The time reasonably required to restore the plant to normal operation is a part of the disqualifying period under section 402 (d). We have so decided in Bako Unemployment Compensation Case, 171 

Pa. Superior Ct. 222, 90 A. 2d 309. In that case Judge Reno, speaking for this court, said: "The rationale of the second Larely case is applicable also during the time reasonably required to put the plant in normal operation after the strike ends. What is a reasonable period will always 'depend upon the kind of work and the circumstances in which it is conducted.' In a department store, for instance, resumption of employment might follow the strike's termination in the course of a few hours.

1954] 

Perhaps a textile mill would require a longer time. In an industry, such as Bethlehem Steel, operating several departments which are dependent for power upon a central plant, with equipment to be repaired, machinery cleaned, and other preparatory steps to be taken, a longer time must necessarily be allowed. Possibly, the duration of the strike becomes a relevant factor. At all events, the Board will consider all the circumstances and override the management only when it finds that it failed to exercise honest judgment. 

It follows that, however willing employes may be to return. to work immediately after the termination of the strike, the continuing stoppage of work must be held to be due to the original labor dispute."

The findings of the board reflecting the essential facts to which we have referred are amply supported by the testimony. And we are in complete accord with this discussion by the board based on its findings: "Under this decision [Bako Unemployment Compensation Case, supra] the board may 'override the management only when it finds that it (management) failed to exercise honest judgment.' Under the facts found, we are agreed that the employer's action was in complete consonance with the situation with which it was confronted and was, in all respects, predicated upon 'honest judgment.' Obviously, the employer was not obligated to alter a normal and reasonable resumption schedule to accommodate a particular worker or group of workers. • • 

Under the peculiar problem confronting management in the present case, it is unimportant that after a strike lasting only four days, a longer time was required to resume production than in the Bako case where the strike closed the Bethlehem Steel plant for a month. In the Bako case the opportunity was afforded the Bethlehem Company by the union for "tapering off" the operations of its highly integrated steel plants in an orderly manner to prevent substantial damage to equipment. Here, that coöperation was denied. 

Decision affirmed.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA COZZONE v. UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION BOARD OF REVIEW 

Unemployment compensation-Discharge for willful misconduct— 

Leaving before quitting time without permission.

Claimant was properly denied unemployment compensation by reason of willful misconduct connected with his work, where it appears that he had left his job early on several occasions without permission after he had been warned against the practice prior to the termination of his employment. 

Appeal from decision of Unemployment Compensation 

Board of Review. Affirmed, 175 Pa. Superior Ct. 170.

Joseph Cozzone, appellant, in propria persona. 

William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, for appellee.

PER CURIAM: 

Claimant, the present appellant, had been in the employ of Lukens Steel Company for two years. He was discharged on November 24, 1952, for violation of company rules. On his claim for unemployment compensation, the bureau ruled that he was ineligible for benefits under section 402 (c) of the Act of December 5, 1936, P. L. (1937) 2897, as amended, 43 PS §802(e). In affirming the bureau, the referee, after hearing, found: "The claimant was in the habit of leaving his job before the regular quitting time, although he was paid for the full day. He had been warned numerous times that this conduct would not be tolerated. He was also suspended for one week for a violation of the Company rule regarding absenteeism. During the week ending November 24th, the claimant had left his job early on three different occasions. without permission, and since he had been warned against this practice, and also suspended for one week for the same thing, he was terminated on November 24, 1952, by the Company."

1954] 

Based on that finding, the referee also concluded that claimant was ineligible under section 402 (e) because of his willful misconduct connected with his work. The Board of 

Review affirmed the decision on the referee's findings. 

Employees in the Lukens plant were scheduled to work eight hours and a half a day and claimant's shift ended at 4:15 p.m. He admitted that he "rang out before quitting time" not only on three occasions in one week as found by the referee but on 6 of 10 consecutive working days ending on November 20, 1952. Claimant had been warned a number of times on other occasions for leaving his job before quitting time. His excuse was that his work was completed at 4 o'clock on each of these days and for that reason he felt justified in leaving shortly thereafter. On one occasion in the previous April claimant had left his work for two hours and 19 minutes before quitting time although he received pay for a full day. His excuse was that he left with the permission of his supervisor, but the warnings which he admittedly received cast doubt on his contention. The credibility of the witnesses was for the referee and his findings, adopted by the board, are amply supported by the evidence. 

Under these findings, claimant clearly by his willful misconduct violated duties owed to his employer which justified his discharge and disqualified him from benefits: Detterer

Unemployment Compensation Case, 168 Pa. Superior Ct. 

291, 77 A. 2d 886.

On April 7, 1953, an assistant supervisor of employment of the Lukens Company wrote the board as follows: "This is to advise you that following the referee's decision, mailing date March 10, 1953, on the above subject, we have obtained additional information which indicates that our original objections to Cozzone's claim for unemployment compensation were erroneous. Therefore, we are withdrawing such objections."

The final order of the board was entered, after receipt of the above communication, on May 18, 1953. We may assume, therefore, that the board in denying benefits con- Icluded that the withdrawal of objections by the employer did not go to the merits of the claim. 

Decision affirmed.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA MUCHANT v. UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION BOARD OF REVIEW 

Unemployment compensation-Miner-Employment in other work during layoff-Operating farm.

Claimant, who was laid off from his work as a coal miner as the result of a work stoppage in the steel industry, was held not entitled to unemployment compensation where it appeared that during the layoff, he operated a farm owned by members of his family, where he kept live stock and cultivated the land, although he received no remuneration from the farm during the time he was laid off from the mines. 

Appeal from decision of Unemployment Compensation 

Board of Review. Affirmed, 175 Pa. Superior Ct. 85.

Robert W. Muchant, appellant, in propria persona. 

William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, for appellee.

RHODES, P. J.: 

Claimant was regularly employed as a coal miner at the 

Bethlehem Mines Corporation, Ellsworth, Pa. By reason of lack of work due to a work stoppage in the steel industry, he was laid off from June 2, 1952, to July 28, 1952. He filed applications for unemployment compensation benefits for various weeks during that period. The bureau found him ineligible for the reason that he was not unemployed during those weeks as prescribed by the Unemployment Compensation Law.

The referee affirmed the decision of the bureau and found that during such period, claimant worked part time as a bartender and janitor at a taproom owned and operated by his sister, and that he was not totally unemployed. Upon appeal the Unemployment Compensation Board of Review vacated the referee's finding to that effect, and found that claimant did not perform any services for remuneration in the taproom during the period of his layoff. However, the board made the following findings of fact, upon the basis of which it affirmed the decision of the referee: "3. Claimant resides, rent free, on a farm containing 

80 acres, which farm is owned by members of his family.

1954] 

In connection with a plan to establish a beef cattle herd of approximately 30 cattle, claimant has acquired and maintains upon the farm 8 beef cattle. He also has acquired and maintains upon the farm two sheep, intending to build up a sheep herd of approximately 10. In order to maintain the cattle and sheep, claimant cultivates approximately 50 acres, producing from such cultivation hay, corn, wheat and oats, all of which is used as feed for the cattle and sheep. "4. During the period in question, claimant devoted full time to the operation of his farm. Even when otherwise employed, claimant performs the necessary work in connection with the farm operation." 

The board thereupon concluded that claimant was engaged in an activity sufficiently substantial in nature to constitute "employment," and that by reason of such farming operations he was disqualified under section 4 (u) of the 

Unemployment Compensation Law, 43 PS §753 (u). Claim ant appealed to this court from the board's decision. 

Section 4 (u) of the law, as amended by the Act of September 29, 1951, P. L. 1580, 43 PS §753, provides that "An individual shall be deemed unemployed (I) with respect to any week (i) during which he performs no services for which remuneration is paid or payable to him and (ii) with respect to which no remuneration is paid or payable to him. ." The claimant admittedly had a relatively large farm which he personally worked during the period in question.¹ 

The fact that he received no remuneration therefor during that time does not remove him from the purview of the above definition of "unemployed". The efforts expended in those weeks may well contribute to his receipt of remunera- 

1 Q. How many acres do you cultivate? A. 50 acres. Q. What do you raise? A. Hay, corn, wheat, oats. . . . Q. You started out with how many [cattle]? A. Six heifers, one registered bull, and one steer, an offspring of one of the heifers. Q. How much time is spent per day in connection with this farm? A. It varies, depending upon the urgency of the work. Haying time requires longer hours, but there have been days when I spent as much as 10 hours, and then days where I do nothing but a little feeding.. Q. During the period of the strike, did you spend a good deal of time on the farm? A. Practically all of the time. Q. And you intend to add to the number of cattle and sheep? A. Yes." ...

tion in the form of future profits from his farming enterprise. It cannot be said that such services are of a type with respect to which no remuneration is payable. See Phillips v. Michigan Unemployment Compensation Commission, 323 

Mich. 188, 35 N. W. 2d 237.

We have no doubt that claimant was available for recall to the mines at all times during the layoff period. But the fact remains that he operated his farm during this interval, and, therefore, he was not unemployed while awaiting the call for the basic reason that one who is self-employed is not unemployed. Claim of Emery, 281 App. Div. 426, 120 N.

Y. S. 2d 142. We agree with the observation of the board that such a ruling may tend to penalize the ambitious and energetic, but it must be borne in mind that the purpose of the Unemployment Compensation Law is to provide temporary benefits for the worker who is unemployed through no fault of his own. See Dawkins Unemployment Compensation Case, 358 Pa. 224, 234, 56 A. 2d 254. It was not designed to insure a weekly income to those engaged in business ventures who may not realize a profit therefrom during various weekly periods. There may be a hardship in some cases where a regularly employed worker is also engaged in the conduct of a part-time business enterprise. 

However, if, as here, it is established that claimant is actually engaged in the conduct of such an enterprise, the fact that he realizes no present weekly profit from the business for the services rendered cannot operate to make him eligible for unemployment compensation benefits.

We indicated our position in this respect in Martin Unemployment Compensation Case, 174 Pa. Superior Ct. 412, 101 A. 2d 421, wherein we stated: "Conceivably, where a person divides his time and labor between work for another and potentially profitable work for himself, as where, e.g., a factory worker also operates, say, a store, a farm or a work-shop, a suspension of work at the factory may not and probably does not expose him to the rigors of unemployment which the Law is designed to alleviate. See Law, §3, 43 PS §752." 

The decision is affirmed.

1954] 

 COMMON PLEAS COURT OF LUZERNE COUNTY 

WRIGHT v. GLEN ALDEN COAL COMPANY et al. 

Workmen's compensation Anthraco-silicosis Claim by widow

Computation of eight-year period preceding date of disability-Section 

301(d) of the Occupational Disease Act. 

Where a widow claims compensation for the death of her husband resulting from anthraco-silicosis, the eight-year period next preceding the date of disability during which he was required by section 301 (d), to have had an aggregate employment of at least four years, must be computed from the date of his death, rather than from the date of his last exposure to the silica hazard, and where it appeared that in this eight-year period he had worked in an occupation having a silica hazard only a total of 874 days, an award to her by the board 

I will be reversed.

Appeal from Workmen's Compensation Board. C. P. of 

Luzerne County, March term, 1953, no. 1075, A-36738. 

Reversed. March 3, 1954. 

Thomas L. Kennedy, Jr., for claimant. 

J. H. Oliver, Franklin B. Gelder and William J. Davis, for 

Glen Alden Coal Company. 

A. J. Zawoiski, for Commonwealth. 

PINOLA, J.: 

Both defendants have appealed from an award made by the referee and affirmed by the board under the Occupational Disease Act for the death of claimant's husband due solely to anthraco-silicosis.

The sole question before us is whether decedent had an aggregate employment of at least four years during a period of eight years next preceding "the date of disability" in an occupation having a silica hazard as required by section 

301 (d).

Thomas Wright filed a claim alleging he had become totally disabled from anthraco-silicosis on December 14, 1950. 

On March 21, 1951, before an adjudication could be had, he died. Thereafter his claim was withdrawn and the widow instituted this claim on account of his death. 

The facts are not in dispute. The parties agree that: 

1. In the eight years' period preceding March 21, 1951,

the date of death, Thomas Wright worked in an occupation having a silica hazard a total of 874 days. 

2. During the eight years preceding December 14, 1950, the date of alleged total disability, Thomas Wright worked a total of 935 days. 

3. During the eight years preceding July 9, 1948, the date of last employment with the Glen Alden Coal Company and date of last exposure to the silica hazard, Thomas

Wright worked a total of 1,443 days. 

It is also established that deceased worked with another employer in an occupation. not involving the silica hazard from July, 1948 to August 8, 1949.

From the foregoing, it is obvious that if either the date of death or date of alleged total disability is selected as the date from which the eight years' period is to be computed, the statutory requirement has not been met and the claim must be disallowed. However, claimant urges that the eight years' period should be computed from the date of last exposure to the silica hazard, July 9, 1948, in which case the statutory requirement will have been fulfilled. 

As far as we have been able to determine, the exact question has never been decided by any court, although certain cases seem to point the way. 

Section 301 (d) (77 PS §1401)), provides: 

"Compensation for silicosis or anthraco-silicosis, and asbestosis, shall be paid only when it is shown that the employee has had an aggregate employment of at least four years in the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, during a period of eight years next preceding the date of disability, in an occupation having a silica or asbestos hazard." 

If this were the claim of Thomas Wright for total disability, the statutory requirement of four years of exposure to the hazard during a period of eight years "next preceding the date of disability" would defeat it, because as to a claim based on disability, there is no ambiguity or doubt as to the date from which the eight years' period is to be computed "[W]here the requirements of the Statute are clear, the intention and meaning of the Legislature, as expressed therein, may not be ignored": Bingaman v. Bald win Locomotive Works, 159 Pa. Superior Ct. 29, 33.

1954] 

Unfortunately, the statute is not so explicit where, as here, the cause for compensation is death. It does not state in so many words that the eight years shall be "next preceding the date of death".

It is settled that the right of a widow for compensation under this act is independent of that of the deceased employe and is not derivative. Hers is an inchoate right which does not accrue until her husband's death: Holahan v. 

Bergen Coal Co., 164 Pa. Superior Ct. 177.

In Lambing v. Consolidation Coal Co., 161 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 346, the court said: "The widow's right, altho it must be based upon the injury, does not come into existence until the death of her husband employee and is dependent upon his death." 

Since it is the death which gives rise to the widow's claim and it is upon the death that her claim must depend, it would seem that the date of death must govern the computation of the eight years' period referred to in section 

301 (d). 

In Holahan v. Bergen Coal Co., supra, the court stated the following to be one of four questions raised by the appeal; "(3) Was there an exposure to a silica hazard during an aggregate employment of four years for a period of eight years next preceding the date of death?"

It, however, computed the eight years from the date of disability which occurred two days before the death. Singularly, that date was also the date of last exposure. 

However, in Jalpneck v. Jarecki Mfg. Co., 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 609, President Judge Rhodes, in affirming a disallowance, declared: • • "[T]he burden was on claimant to prove that deceased had an aggregate employment of at least four years in this Commonwealth, during a period of eight years next preceding his death, in an occupation having a silica hazard; and that defendant was the last employer in whose employment deceased was exposed to such hazard." (Emphasis ours).

Such a construction seems reasonable because "statutes are to be construed as best to effectuate the intention of the

Legislature, though such construction may seem contrary to the letter": In re Umholtz, 191 Pa. 177, 43 Atl. 75; 

Commonwealth v. Provident Trust Co., 287 Pa. 251, 134 

Atl. 377.

Accordingly, we enter the following Order Now, March 3, 1954, at 3:45 p.m., the appeals are sustained, the action of the board is reversed, and judgment is directed to be entered in favor of defendants. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD BEGGS v. RETREAT STATE HOSPITAL et al. (No. 1) 

Workmen's compensation · Occupational disease· Tuberculosis Nurse employed in hospital for mental patients-Section 108(m) of the Occupational Disease Act. 

Claimant employed as a practical nurse at a hosiptal for mental patients who contracted tuberculosis as the result of coming in contact with patients suffering from that ailment was entitled to compensation for disability therefrom under the Occupational Disease Act; section 108 (m) of the act providing that disability from tuberculosis in the occupation of nursing in hospitals or sanitaria involving exposure to such disease shall be compensable is not limited to disability resulting from tuberculosis contracted in hospitals or sanitaria for tubercular patients.

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Donlan, District No. 3, claim petition no. 129,896, accident no. 

6070806, A-38637. Affirmed. March 17, 1954.

State Workmen's Insurance Fund, insurance carrier. 

Milton J. Kolansky, for claimant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for defendant.

BRENNAN, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by defendant from an award in an occupational disease case.

The referee found that claimant, an employe of the Retreat State Hospital, was employed as a practical nurse;

1954] 

 that he was exposed to a tuberculosis hazard from March 10, 1952 until January 24, 1953; that as a result of said exposure, he became totally disabled on February 2, 1953 by minimal pulmonary active tuberculosis.

The defendant's appeal is directed to the claimant's qualifications to recover under section 108 (m) of the act, which reads: "Tuberculosis in the occupation of nursing in hospitals or sanitaria involving exposure to such disease."

Dr. Howard T. Fiedler, called by claimant, testified that he was the superintendent of the Retreat State Hospital and described claimant's duties as follows: 

"His duties were to supervise and care for patients under the direction of our graduate personnel, that is, trained nurses, and treat them, in addition. That consisted of attending patients, to their bathing, taking temperatures, untidy patients to help keep them clean, take patients out to the various activities, take them to the various laboratory examinations and functions, all under the direction of our trained personnel, take them for recreation, to clinics for treatment and for examination, because, you see, our various facilities are at different places, and they would be taken there by the attendant, and out to recreation, for ball, the movies, etc., dances, he aided in assisting them, as well as the ward patients."

According to the doctor's testimony, claimant came in contact with about six tuberculosis patients from April 9, 1951, until February 3, 1953. On cross-examination, the witness stated that the Retreat State Hospital is primarily a mental hospital.

We have previously held that a person engaged in work similar to that of claimant is, in fact, a nurse, regardless of title or nursing degree and that the benefits of the act are not limited to registered nurses.

The only question remaining is to pass on the merits of defendant's contention that this section of the act applies only to tuberculosis hospitals or sanitaria.

We have given serious consideration to this contention and cannot agree with it. To adopt such an interpretation would, in our judgment, place an unjustifiable limitation

on the act. If the legislature had meant to place such interpretation, it could and would have stated so and would not have used the general language it did. Furthermore, it is the opinion of this board that a nurse in a mental hospital or a general hospital is as much exposed to the hazard as a nurse in a specialized hospital. It is the contact with the disease that counts, not the name or specialization of the institution where the contact is made. We are of the opinion that the legislature intended to protect all nurses exposed to the hazard.

We, therefore, affirmed the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and award. 

Defendant's appeal is dismissed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD SMITH, Jr., v. STATE WORKMEN'S INSURANCE FUND et al. 

Workmen's compensation· 

Occupational disease. Nursing-Brace fitter in hospital for crippled children-Section 108(m) of the Occu pational Disease Act. 

Claimant who was disabled from tuberculosis following his employment as a brace fitter at a hospital for crippled children where he came into contact with patients who were suffering from tuberculosis, was not entitled to compensation for his disability, since his duties as a brace fitter did not constitute nursing within the meaning of section 108 (m) of the Occupational Disease Act. 

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Carmalt, specially presiding in the 4th District, claim petition no. 130,736, accident no. 6,098,400, A-38892. Affirmed. 

March 17, 1954.

State Workmen's Insurance Fund, insurance carrier. 

Eugene R. Hartman, for claimant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for defendants.

KNOLL, Chairman: 

This is an appeal by claimant from the referee's order of dismissal in an occupational disease case.

1954] 

Claimant's petition, filed June 23, 1953, alleged disability from tuberculosis as the result of exposure to tubercular patients during the course of his employment, the last date of exposure alleged as of June 10, 1953. The defendant filed a responsive answer, averring that claimant was not employed in the occupation of nursing as provided by section 108 (m) of the Occupational Disease Act. 

Claimant testified that he had worked as a brace maker at the Crippled Children's Hospital for about 3 years and a half prior to June 10, 1953; that he contacted and fitted braces to patients, some of whom were suffering from tuberculosis; that he became ill in June, 1953, and X-ray examinations reported spots in his lungs and that he had not worked after June 10, 1953.

Dr. Warren A. Gette, called by claimant, interpreted 

X-rays, taken December 2, 1952, as negative for tuberculosis and other X-rays, dated March 23, 1953 and June 24, 1953, as indicating tuberculosis. Dr. Gette diagnosed an active pulmonary tuberculosis which could have been contracted from contact with tubercular patients.

Dr. Thomas Forker, claimant's witness, testified that several tubercular patients were fitted for braces according to records of the defendant hospital but could not say that he personally knew that the fittings were by claimant. 

Claimant, recalled, testified that he remembered fitting braces to some of the patients mentioned by Dr. Forker. 

Eugene Teufel, called by claimant, testified that he was employed in the brace department of the hospital and that some of the patients fitted for braces by claimant were indicated as tubercular patients.

The referee's order of disallowance is based on findings of fact that claimant failed to prove that his condition was the result of exposure in the course of nursing as provided by the Occupational Disease Act. 

Section 108 of the Occupational Disease Act, defining 

"occupational disease" provides by paragraph (m) as follows: "Tuberculosis in the occupation of nursing in hospitals or sanitaria involving exposure to such disease."

It is our opinion that claimant's duties as a brace maker

and fitter did not constitute nursing as contemplated by the provisions of the act. While his employment may have brought him into contact with tubercular patients, he did not attend or administer treatment to such patients in the "occupation of nursing", and we believe that we are required, therefore, to accept the referee's decision in this case.

Accordingly, the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of dismissal are affirmed. 

Claimant's appeal is dismissed.

COMMON PLEAS COURT NO. 3 OF PHILADELPHIA COUNTY KLIGERMAN v. TIPTON et al. (No. 2) Workmen's compensation—Limitations—Agreement to pay death claim-Delay in receipt of executed agreement beyond period of year 

-Denial of liability by insurer-Estoppel. 

A petition by an insurance carrier to review a compensation agree ment was properly dismissed by the board where the carrier claime that no agreement had been entered into between the parties during the period of limitation but where prior to the expiration of the period, the insurance carrier had prepared an agreement and forwarded the same to claimant with instructions to sign and return three copies, that the agreement contained all the details of the accident and the terms of payment, the typewritten names of the employer and insurer, that the claimant signed the forms and gave them to her attorney, and that the only uncompleted act beyond the period of a year was the return of the executed agreement to the insurance carrier, especially where there was evidence that the insurance company had stated in other proceedings that it admitted its liability to claimant and when it changed its attitude with intention to deny liability, it failed to inform claimant of the fact before the period of limitation had run.

Appeal from Workmen's Compensation Board. C. P. No. 

7 of Philadelphia County, September term, 1952, no. 5948. 

Affirmed. February 24, 1954. 

S. R. Ginsburg, for claimant. 

L. Wagner and R. Smith, for Jay E. Tipton, defendant. 

G. H. Detweiler, for American Casualty Co., insurance carrier.

1954] 

PER CURIAM: 

This is an appeal under the Act of June 2, 1915, P. L. 

736, art. IV, sec. 427, 77 PS §872, as amended, from the action of the Workmen's Compensation Board in affirming a referee's award, and holding the insurance carrier liable to the claimant.

In December of 1942, claimant's husband met with an accident in the course of his employment which resulted in his death that same month. Notice of the accident was given to the insurance company, and an investigation was held. Thereafter, in February of 1943, a compensation agreement was prepared in quadruplicate by the carrier, and forwarded to claimant with instructions to sign and return three copies, after which payment would be made. 

The agreement contained all the details of the accident, and the terms of payment. The typewritten names of the employer and the insurer were contained in the space provided for their signatures. There was also a blank line with an "X" alongside of the place where claimant was to sign. 

During March of 1943, claimant signed the forms and gave them to her attorney. The forms were, unfortunately, not returned to the insurance carrier until more than a year after the accident. (This incident is the crux of the entire litigation, since the basic question is whether or not there was an "agreement" between the parties within the meaning of sections 315 and 407 of the act, which require a written agreement or the filing of a claim within one year of the accident.) There is some testimony that claimant had sought a lump sum settlement during this time, but, in any event, the insurance company did nothing to indicate it had changed its attitude; it did nothing to deny liability, although it admitted it reviewed its file on this matter every eight weeks.

After the year had expired, claimant also procured the signature of the employer on the forms. The carrier, however, refused to honor the agreements, claiming the statute of limitations barred recovery and that there was, in fact, no agreement. The Workmen's Compensation Bureau at first refused to accept or approve the agreement, and claim-

ant then brought mandamus proceedings in Dauphin County to compel approval. The insurance company intervened in these proceedings, contending, inter alia, that the script signature of the carrier was required. Before these proceedings were decided, however, the bureau approved the agreement, and the action was discontinued. 

At this point the carrier filed a petition for a declaratory judgment in this court to decide whether or not there was a valid agreement under The Workmen's Compensation Act. 

This court held there that the agreement was valid and binding. The insurance company then appealed to the Supreme 

Court, which decided this court had no jurisdiction to entertain the petition, and the declaratory judgment was vacated on a purely jurisdictional basis; the Supreme Court did not consider the merits of the case. 

The next step was the entry of judgment by claimant on a certified copy of the approved agreement then on file with the bureau. A petition to strike this judgment was denied. 

Then the carrier filed a petition to review this agreement under section 413 of The Workmen's Compensation Act, alleging fraud or other improper conduct of the claimant and mistake of law and fact. Claimant denied these allegations, as did the employer, who alleged that he had complied with all his obligations under The Workmen's Compensation Act. He also alleged the carrier had assumed responsibility for payment, and was now estopped from denying liability.

A referee held a hearing and the insurance company's petition was dismissed. An appeal to the Workmen's Compensation Board followed; the board found there was a valid agreement and that there was no fraud or improper conduct on the part of claimant. The board dismissed the appeal and affirmed the referee. The present appeal to this court followed.

In all the previous proceedings, including the one now on appeal from the Workmen's Compensation Board, the decision has been in favor of claimant. The rule is that where compensation authorities have found in favor of a claimant, this court, on appeal, must review the evidence in a light most favorable to the claimant and give him the

1954] 

 benefit of every inference reasonably deducible therefrom:

Rice v. Public Meat Market, 166 Pa. Superior Ct. 328 

(1950); Halloway v. Carnegie-Illinois Steel Corp., 173 Pa.

Superior Ct. 137 (1953). Where the findings of fact made by the compensation board are based on competent evidence, they are conclusive, and the courts have no power to weigh the evidence and revise those findings or reverse the final action of the board: Diaz v. Jones & Laughlin Steel Corp., 170 Pa. Superior Ct. 608 (1952).

The petition alleges two grounds for relief. The first is fraud, coercion or other improper conduct on the part of claimant in procuring the agreement; the second is that there was a mistake of law in that there was not actually any agreement in existence before the one-year period of limitation expired.

The referee and the Workmen's Compensation Board con sidered all the evidence and decided that there was no fraud, coercion or improper conduct and that there was a valid agreement. The entire record fails to disclose any evidence whatever of fraud or improper conduct on the part of claimant in procuring the agreement. Therefore, in light of the above decisions, it is clear the board's finding on this point must not be disturbed.

The next problem is a mixed question of law and fact; that is, whether or not there was an agreement entered into between the parties within the period of limitation. If there was, the carrier is liable to claimant. 

We think that an agreement was entered into before the expiration of the period of limitation. The only thing necessary to complete the agreement as prepared by the insurance company was claimant's signature, and this was affixed well within the one-year period. The moment she signed her name in the space provided, the parties had agreed. Except for the narrow question of jurisdiction under the Declaratory Judgment Act, we adopt and incorporate in toto the opinion of Judge Crumlish on this question, which appears on pages 220a to 233a of the printed record of the Supreme Court in the declaratory judgment proceedings and which is part of the record in the present appeal. This question was completely and exhaustively treated by the lower court

in that case, and further discussion would be pointless. See 

American Casualty Co. v. Kligerman et al., C. P. No. 7, December Term 1947, No. 1299.

The compensation authorities, in reaching the decision that a valid agreement existed, had before them the entire record of the previous proceedings, including the following facts: The insurance carrier itself typed, prepared and forwarded the document to claimant; the agreement contained the typewritten signature of the employer and the typewritten signature of the insurance carrier; the agreements were completed in every detail except for the one blank, designated with an "X" for the script signature of claimant; there was testimony of a claims representative of the insurance company to the effect that the employer's signature was not required.

In addition, there was in evidence the fact that the insurance company stated in the mandamus proceedings that it admitted its liability to claimant; that the Workmen's Compensation Bureau approved the agreement; that the insurance company reviewed its records every eight weeks, and knew of the existence of the outstanding claim, yet took no steps whatever to notify claimant; and the fact that the insurance company, during this time, changed its attitude, intending to deny liability, yet failed to inform claimant of this fact before the period of limitation had run within which claimant could have filed a claim. 

There was more than enough evidence before the board to sustain a finding that an agreement had been entered into prior to the time the statute had run, especially when the evidence is reviewed here in a light most favorable to claimant, giving her the benefit of every inference reasonably deducible therefrom.

We have carefully considered all the points raised by appellants on this appeal, but find them without merit. 

Accordingly, the appeal is dismissed, and the decision of the Workmen's Compensation Board is affirmed.

1954] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD BALL v. SCHLOSSMAN, etc. 

Workmen's compensation - Employe - Casual employment not in regular course of business-Claimant engaged in painting defendant's restaurant.

Claimant's employment was casual and not in the regular course of defendant's business and, hence, a referee's order dismissing his claim for compensation will be affirmed, where it appears that at the time of his injury resulting from a fall from a ladder he was employed by defendant to paint the exterior of the latter's restaurant. 

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Smith, District No. 11, claim petition no. 130,260, accident no. 

6069314, A-38681. Affirmed. March 17, 1954. 

Will J. Schaaf, for claimant. 

John G. Gent, for defendant. 

BRENNAN, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by claimant from an order of disallowance by the referee.

Claimant, in his petition filed on April 20, 1953, alleged that while descending a ladder, in the course of his employment with defendant on October 5, 1952, he caught his foot in a rung and was thrown to the ground, suffering a fracture of the right hip. Defendant's answer, filed on May 26, 1953, denied that claimant was an employe of defendant. 

After hearings, the referee found that claimant's employment was casual in nature; that defendant's business was that of a restaurateur and claimant had been merely engaged to paint the premises. In accordance with such findings, the referee dismissed claimant's petition. This appeal followed.

Section 104 of The Workmen's Compensation Act defines 

"employe" as follows: "All natural persons who perform services for another for a valuable consideration, exclusive of persons whose

employment is casual in character and not in the regular course of the business of the employer." 

The question for determination in this appeal is whether claimant's employment was casual in character and not in the regular course of defendant's business. In order that defendant be relieved of liability, it is necessary that both factors exist.

The testimony presented and accepted by the referee is to the effect that on October 4, 1952, claimant was hired by defendant to do some exterior painting of defendant's restaurant. Defendant supplied claimant with a ladder, the necessary paint and brushes and agreed to pay claimant the sum of $1 per hour for the time worked, claimant supplied the other equipment. There is no testimony relative to any control which defendant might have had over the manner or means of claimant doing his work, and we, therefore, must assume that there was no such control retained by the defendant.

Reviewing the entire record, we are of the opinion that claimant's employment was casual in nature and not in the regular course of defendant's business. "Casual" has been defined by the courts as something that comes without regularity. An employment is casual where it is occasional, irregular or incidental as distinguished from regular and continuous. Involved in casual employment are the ideas of fortuitous happening and irregularity of occurrence; it denotes what is occasional, incidental, temporary, haphazard and unplanned.

As was stated by the court in Bradley v. Chester Materials 

Co., 151 Pa. Superior Ct. 485: "[Claimant] occupation or business was distinct from that of the defendant (who conducted a restaurant business at the place where the accident occurred). He was paid by the job on an hourly basis . . . . and was not in the regular employ of [the defendant]. The need for his services was occasional. . . . In his relation with the defendant, he was employed to accomplish a definite result, i.e., paint the exterior of defendant's restaurant and not to paint regularly.

1954] 

The fact remains that defendant did not supervise the work nor reserve the right of supervision. Though he worked on an hourly basis he fixed his own working hours and was responsible to defendant only for the result of his work." 

By the use of the words "regular course" the legislature evidently intended to give them some definite significance and the most natural meaning is that they refer to the normal operations which regularly constitute the business in question, excluding incidental or occasional operations arising out of that business, such as, now and again, repairing the premises. Callihan v. Montgomery, 272 Pa. 56.

Thus, where defendant is admittedly in the restaurant business and, upon occasion, employs someone to paint the exterior of the building where the restaurant business is being conducted and such person is employed for that specific purpose only, such work is casual in character and not in the course of regular business of defendant. 

We therefore affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of dismissal. 

Claimant's appeal is dismissed. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD WORRALL v. PRECISIONWARE, INCORPORATED 

Workmen's compensation-Truck driven at time of accident by unauthorized agent of employe while employe slept.

Claimant, a truck driver, who was requested by employer to make a trip before being properly rested from a prior trip and, who, fearing he would be laid off if he refused, requested an unauthorized agent, who was driving at the time of the accident, to drive the truck while claimant slept, was properly denied workmen's compensation where he had been instructed not to permit riders and the circumstances were not of such emergency nature that required claimant to delegate his duties to an unauthorized person in the furtherance of his employer's business.

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Patterson. District No. 6, claim petition no. 130,332, accident no. 

6083752, A-38812. Affirmed. March 10, 1953. 

U. S. Fidelity & Guaranty Co., insurance carrier.

Horace J. Culbertson, for claimant. 

John C. Dowling, for defendant.

KNOLL, Chairman: 

This is an appeal by claimant from the order of the referee dismissing his claim petition. 

The petition, filed May 8, 1953, alleged injuries by accident on April 9, 1953. Defendant filed a responsive answer alleging that claimant was not in the course of his employment at the time of the accidental injury. 

The record shows that on April 9, 1953, and for some time prior thereto, claimant was employed by defendant as a truck-trailer driver; that he was injured on April 9, 1953, when a truck in which he was riding left the highway and hit a tree; that, at the time of the accident, the truck was driven by one Charles Ritchey who was not an employe of defendant and that claimant was totally disabled from the accident until May 29, 1953, when he returned to work. 

The circumstances leading to the operation of the truck by Ritchey was described by claimant. He, claimant, had returned from a trip to Long Island, N. Y., at about 8:30 p.m., on April 8, 1953, and was called back to work at about 

10:30 p.m., reporting for work at about 12:30 a.m., on 

April 9, 1953. He testified that he asked Ritchey to ride with him because he was tired and sleepy from the previous trip; that he asked Ritchey to drive while he went to sleep and that he was sleeping when the accident occurred. Claimant testified further that he knew of no order by defendant and that he was never told that he could not take riders on the truck; that defendant laid off other drivers who refused to take trips when they were sleepy, and that he did not protest nor complain of sleepiness because he would have

1954] 

 been laid off. Upon cross-examination, he identified Clarence 

Arnold as a driver who was laid off for refusal to drive, and he fixed the date as about two weeks before the hearing date of June 11, 1953. At a second hearing, claimant said that another driver was laid off for the same reason before the accident on April 9, 1953.

Clarence Arnold, claimant's witness, testified that he had worked for defendant as a driver for about three months; that he had seen no bulletin orders and had not been warned against taking riders with him and that he and other drivers took riders with them on their trips.

Lester H. Cohen, called by defendant, testified that he was defendant's plant manager; that he employed seven or eight drivers on April 9, 1953, who were paid by the length of the trips taken and that a notice, dated March 12, 1952, forbidding riders was posted on a bulletin board in the office where wage rates were also posted. 

Walter C. Willow, defendant's shipping clerk, called in behalf of defendant, testified that he gave shipping orders to claimant on April 9, 1953; that he did not see anyone with claimant; that he had instructed claimant to read the bulletin about riders on trips and that he did not know that Ritchey had made trips with claimant.

The referee's order of dismissal is based on findings of fact that claimant had no authority to secure the services of Charles Ritchey as a driver and upon conclusions of law that claimant was not in the course of his employment at the time of the accident.

We have carefully reviewed the testimony, and it is our opinion that the referee's decision is fully warranted under the facts in this case. Claimant did not require assistance as in emergency whereby he could delegate his duties to an authorized person in the furtherance of his employer's business. See Ginther v. J. P. Graham Transfer Co., 348

Pa. 60. Here, claimant knew that he was too sleepy to drive and his arrangement with Ritchey was made in advance and without the knowledge of the employer and in deroga-

tion of the defendant's right to hire employes of its own choice.

Accordingly, the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of dismissal are affirmed. 

Claimant's appeal is dismissed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD YAKOVAC v. NATIONAL ALLOY STEEL-BLAW- KNOX COMPANY Workmen's compensation—Occupational disease-Multiple claims for compensation—Prior payments not fatal bar to subsequent claims.

Payments of workmen's compensation under an earlier petition are not a fatal bar to a subsequent petition brought more than one year after the last payment of compensation where the claimant has fully satisfied the statutory requirements of exposure to the hazards of the occupation for an aggregate period of four years during the eight-year period immediately preceding the last date of disability. 

Appeal from disallowance of Referee McGovern, District 

No. 8, claim petition no. 130,582, accident no. 6098317, A-38755. Reversed. March 24, 1954. 

Penna. Mfrs. Assn. Cas. Ins. Co. insurance carrier. 

Herman L. Foreman, for claimant.

Murray J. Jordan and C. M. McCarthy, for defendant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for Commonwealth of Pennsylvania.

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

This appeal is by claimant, Anton Yakovac, from the order of Referee McGovern, disallowing compensation in an occupational disease case. The claim petition avers that 

Yakovac became totally disabled on November 25, 1952, due to silicosis. The record discloses that claimant filed an earlier

1954] 

 petition (no. 103,445) and upon hearing held before Referee 

Dillman, an award was entered. Claimant was paid compensation for total disability by defendant, National Alloy 

Steel-Blaw-Knox Company, from June 7, 1945, to January 17, 1946. It also appears that on March 19, 1951, claimant filed a second petition (no. 123,081) alleging that he became totally disabled on January 10, 1951. While that petition was pending and undetermined, claimant filed the present petition on June 8, 1953. By letter from claimant's counsel, request was made for leave to file the third petition in lieu of the second petition because claimant had returned to work subsequent to filing the second petition. The present appeal relates to the third claim petition filed. 

At a hearing upon the second petition, Dr. Harry R. 

Goldstein, called by claimant, testified that he has been treating claimant about seven years for silicosis. As a result of X-rays and clinical examination, the doctor was of the opinion that claimant suffered from a chronic pulmonary silicosis with a possible superimposed tuberculosis. He felt that claimant was totally disabled for all practical purposes. 

Under cross-examination, the doctor admitted that he had pronounced claimant totally disabled on May 31, 1945, as the result of silicosis. Claimant testified that when he returned to work in 1946, he resumed his former work as a molder. He was off work from January 10, 1951, to April 

10, 1951, because of his condition. He was off work again from August 18, 1952, to September 8, 1952. Upon the conclusion of claimant's case, defendant's counsel moved for dismissal of the petition on the ground that it was barred because it was filed more than one year after the last payment of compensation, (section 413) and the claim is, therefore, res adjudicata. It should be pointed out that Referee 

Dillman terminated compensation payments under the first petition as of January 16, 1946, because claimant's total disability had then resolved itself into one of partial disability. It is agreed by all of the interested parties that the testimony taken on the second petition would apply to the instant petition.

We are satisfied that claimant is totally disabled due to silicosis. The case then presents a legal question whether the payments under the earlier petition are a fatal bar to the instant case, in that, more than one year has elapsed since the last payment of compensation. We think not. Since claimant "went off" compensation on January 16, 1946, he has performed his usual and regular work as a molder in a silica sand hazard for almost seven years, with the exception of irregular periods off from work due to his physical condition. In all that period of time, he has earned the wages which he received for the work he performed: Tracey v. M. & S. Coal Co., 165 Pa. Superior Ct. 569. It is signifi cant that claimant has fully satisfied the statutory requirement of exposure for an aggregate period of four years during the eight-year period immediately preceding last date of disability. Since claimant worked from January 18, 1946, through November 24, 1952, the present claim is new, independent and wholly unrelated to the earlier proceedings. 

An analogous situation was before the board in the case of Quatro v. Universal-Rundle Corp., A-37700 (Board, 1953). There the claimant filed a claim petition for total disability due to silicosis and was paid compensation from 

February 19, 1943, to November 1, 1944. He returned to work, after special treatment, on November 1, 1944, and continued working in a silica hazard until June 29, 1951, when he again became disabled and died on November 10, 1951, as a result of silicosis. In an opinion by Commissioner 

Brennan, we said: "The defendant's position would be well taken if the silicosis last suffered by the decedent resulted from his former condition. We do not agree with the defendant's contentions. We believe that claimant's total disability from silicosis, which occurred on June 29, 1951, after six and one-half years employment in a silica hazard, gave rise to a new cause of action. The decedent's prior disability terminated on November 1, 1944, when he recovered from the disease. He had no claim until he again became disabled on June 29, 1951 and his widow had no claim until he died

1954] 

 on November 10, 1951. Neither the decedent nor his widow would have been able to renew the earlier claim for compensation, which expired on November 1, 1944."

Upon appeal to the Court of Common Pleas of Lawrence County, June term 1953, no. 8, the court, in an opinion by 

Judge Lamoree, said: "The fact that the deceased returned to work on November 1, 1944 for the defendant company at his former employment, subject to the silicosis hazard, and worked continually at his occupation for six and one-half years, bolsters not only the referee's findings of fact, but, in our opinion, justifies the awards made by the Workmen's Compensation Board."

Claimant in the present appeal is entitled to compensation in accordance with the schedule in force at the date of his last exposure, which coincided with the date of his total disability. The Act of January 2, 1952, P. L. 1811, applies. 

We hereby vacate and set aside the referee's fifth and sixth findings of fact, and in lieu thereof, we substitute the following:

Findings of Fact 

5. We find as a fact that claimant was exposed to a silica hazard for the entire period from January 18, 1946, to November 24, 1952, except for the periods noted in the fourth finding of fact, and that he became totally disabled on November 24, 1952, as a result of silicosis due to such exposure. 

6. We find further that claimant's disability occurred after an exposure of more than five years in a silica hazard in his employment by the defendant. 

As amended, the referee's findings of fact are affirmed. 

We hereby affirm the referee's first conclusion of law. 

We vacate and set aside the referee's second conclusion of law and substitute therefore, the following:

Conclusions of Law 

2. Having found that claimant is totally disabled due to silicosis incurred in the course of his employment by de-

fendant for the required statutory period, claimant is entitled to compensation therefor, within the limitations of the act. 

3. Since the disability occurred after an exposure of more than five years, defendant and the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania are liable for payments in accordance with the proportions specified in the act.

We vacate and set aside the referee's order of disallowance and substitute therefor the following: Award

Compensation is awarded to claimant, Anton Yakovac, at the rate of $30 per week from December 2, 1952, to continue until such time as claimant's disability shall cease or change; the total amount of compensation is not to exceed the sum of $6,500 to be paid as follows: Sixty percent, or the sum of $18 per week to be paid by the defendant employer, National Alloy Steel-Blaw-Knox Company; 40 percent per week, or the sum of $12 per week to be paid by the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania.

The above order is to bear interest at the rate of six percent on deferred payments.

The appeal of claimant is sustained. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD BARNETT v. EICHELBERGER Workmen's compensation—Accident resulting in hernia-Extension of time limitation for reporting accident-Claimant on distant trip at time of accident. 

Although, under the hernia amendment of May 18, 1945, P. L. 671, sec. 1, notice of accident must be given to employer within 48 hours, a minimal extension of time will be granted an employe injured while on a trip a great distance from employer if he reports the injury to employer immediately upon his return from the trip. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Carmalt, specially presiding in the 4th District, claim petition no.

1954] 

121,139, accident no. 6095454, A-38858. Affirmed. March 

24, 1954. 

Penna. Mutual Indemnity Co., insurance carrier. 

Robert O. Beers, for claimant. 

Frank B. Boyle, for defendant.

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by defendant, Robert J. Eichelberger, from a hernia award entered by Referee Carmalt, November 6, 1953. It involves only five weeks of compensation and charges for hospitalization and surgical services.

Claimant, Charles M. Barnett, was the only witness called at the hearing. As he described his case, he worked out of York, driving a truck for defendant. On April 14, 1953, he arrived in Elkhart, Ind., where he discharged a load.

Then while pushing upward to close a bent door on his truck, he twisted his body and was seized with a cramp in his right groin. After resting for 10 or 15 minutes, he drove on to Chicago in accordance with his orders to see about a return load. While on the way he felt a lump which had developed in his right groin. On arrival at the Daniel's 

Motor Express terminal in Chicago, Frank Miller "city man" for the last named company, took over for the balance of his deliveries, and claimant told him about his accidental injury. There is also in his testimony this statement, "I think I did tell Ray Kyle, the dispatcher at that particular time". As the account goes on, he got back to York with a return load on the evening of April 17, 1953, and reported his accidental injury to his employer at that time. A signed statement secured by the carrier's adjuster, indicates that the formal notice was given the following morning, but the referee has not accepted this as ruling out the verbal notice given on arrival, and we can see no reason to decide otherwise. At any rate, he consulted his family physician the same evening that he arrived back in York and was operated

upon by Dr. F. H. Weaver on May 11, 1953, at the York

Hospital. 

On appeal, defendant takes exception to timely notice only, insisting that the 48-hour limitation under the hernia amendment (77 PS §515) was not complied with. We take it from this that employment is not denied (Ridenour v. 

Baltimore & Pittsburgh Motor Express Co., A-38583. Compare Puhlman v. Excelsior Express & Standard Cab Co., 259 Pa. 393) that accidental injury is not disputed and that the nature of claimant's hernia is conceded to be acute: 

Harvey v. Philadelphia Warehouse & Cold Storage Co., 

167 Pa. Superior Ct. 435.

On the single issue posed, we are of the opinion that we must agree with the referee. Defendant's truck with claimant as driver was leased to Daniel's Motor Express, and the business involved required his presence so far away from defendant's base of operations that it was to all interests and purposes impossible for the required notice to be given any sooner than it was when communicated to the employer immediately upon completion of the return trip. 

It has been consistently held that if the 48 hours after accidental injury ends on Sunday, a Monday morning report is good enough and in our view the exigency of making such a long trip back to establish contact with the employer is, if anything, a better excuse for a minimal extension of time. It might be said that claimant could have telephoned or requested the lessee express company to transmit his report, but as we see the situation, such a demand upon claimant for compliance with a technicality would be entirely unreasonable.

The findings of fact, conclusions of law, award and bill of costs of Referee Carmalt, dated November 6, 1953, are affirmed. 

Defendant's appeal is dismissed. 

[ED. NOTE: By amendment of August 24, 1953, P. L. 1382, Act
