No. 394, sec. 1, where claim is made for hernia, the time for giving notice is extended to within five calendar days after the occurrence of the accident.]

1954] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD LOGUE v. CITY OF PHILADELPHIA 

Workmen's compensation - Two independent causes of death- Separate petitions-Amendment of claim to include cause of death barred by statute of limitations.

Claimant who seeks to prove that either of two independent sets of circumstances contributed to and caused decedent's death must file two separate claim petitions within one year after the death and may not amend a valid petition to include an independent cause which would be otherwise barred by the statute of limitations. 

Appeal from disallowance of Referee Reynolds. District 

No. 1, claim petition no. 128,433, accident no. 5997527, A-38905. Affirmed as amended. March 24, 1954. 

John C. Noonan, for claimant.

James Francis Ryan and James C. McCrudden, for defendant. 

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

This case involves a death claim. It is before the board upon an appeal by claimant, Margaret C. Logue, from the order of Referee Reynolds disallowing compensation for the death of her husband, James J. Logue, which occurred on 

December 21, 1951. The claim petition avers that decedent suffered an accident on October 20, 1951, later amended to September 19, 1951, as the result of an explosion of a stove in a guard house, causing burns to the arms and chest. 

Logue was employed by defendant, City of Philadelphia, as a park guard. The claim petition was filed on October 

17, 1952.

At the hearing before the referee, claimant testified that her husband was hospitalized after the accident in September 1951, at the Philadelphia General Hospital from September 19, 1951, to October 1, 1951, when he returned home and suffered a relapse so that he was again hospitalized from October 7 to October 19, 1951. He resumed his employ-

ment as a park guard in November, 1951. According to claimant, her decedent was on traffic duty 16 hours on 

December 14, 1951, "the night of the big storm", on the

Girard Avenue Bridge. Decedent died suddenly at his apartment, preparatory to leaving for his day's work. Dr. John 

A. Kahler, claimant's witness, testified that he had treated decedent during the first week of December 1951 for a serious heart condition, and he had warned claimant about the danger of hurrying or lifting and had cautioned him to "get out of the elements, the stormy weather particularly". The doctor stated that exposure and chilling would aggravate the heart condition. He also felt that the burning accident had contributed to the "weakened condition" when he saw the patient and that the burns and working in the storm were contributing factors in the death. Under cross-examination, the doctor testified that he had treated decedent a number of times and that he had found "roughening in the heart valves" in March 1951. Dr. Frank Swallow testified in behalf of claimant that he first saw decedent on July 27, 1951. The doctor suggested an electrocardiogram. He next saw the patient on November 5, 1951, and found marked congestion in the chest, severe cough and temperature. 

Examination on December 17, 1951, disclosed marked congestion in the throat and chest, cough, chills and temperature. The doctor stated that all kinds of complications can ensue from burns, depending upon the extent of the burns, and it was possible that the burns and associated shock toxima could contribute to the death. The doctor testified: "Q. In your opinion, would the exposure on December 

14th be an attributing or participating factor for his death seven days later, on the 21st? "A. When I examined him I suspected he had some pain, and this definitely could have hastened his death because he had a coronary and acute bronchitis, and at 6:45 on the 

21st of December he died."

Harry E. Reichardt, a sergeant, called by claimant, testified that decedent's record of employment showed that he was absent from duty from September 20, 1951, to October

1954] 

9, 1951, and was carried on the payroll as injured. Decedent was absent from duty on account of illness from October 10, 1951, to October 30, 1951. The records from which the witness testified, did not go beyond October 31, 1951. The sergeant also testified that a report of the accident on September 19, 1951, showed that decedent had received burns about the hands and under both arms.

At the conclusion of claimant's case, counsel for claimant asked leave of the referee to amend the claim petition "to include as an accident any unusual occurrence which the testimony reveals, speaking specifically of the unusual exposure on December 14th". The referee made no ruling at that time.

Dr. Albert A. Bockman, called by defendant, testified that he treated decedent as far back as October 9, 1945, when he made a diagnosis of coronary thrombosis with myocardial infarction. The doctor took electrocardiograms on April 

12, 1950, May 8, 1950, October 5, 1951 and October 29, 1951.

The doctor's medical service covered periods from February 

14, 1951, to March 6, 1951 for pneumonia; September 19, 1951, to October 1, 1951 for treatment of burns; from October 6, 1951, to October 19, 1951 for pleurisy pneumonia.

Dr. Bockman was of the opinion that the burns in no way aggravated the heart condition. He felt that decedent "died a natural death from the diagnosis of 1945, and he died six years later of coronary thrombosis, the usual coronary artery disease".

It is clear that decedent suffered from a heart ailment for several years. It is the contention of claimant (1) that the accident on September 19, 1951, aggravated the heart condition and was a contributing factor in the demise and (2) that decedent's exposure to the elements on December 14, 1951, likewise aggravated the heart condition resulting in the sudden death on December 21, 1951. The preëxisting cardiac condition would not necessarily be a fatal bar to an award: Mosser v. Borough of Mt. Union, 158 Pa. Super ior Ct. 277. We are in accord with the referee that the burns suffered in the accident, upon which the present claim peti-

tion is predicated was not a responsble or causative factor in the death. We may then proceed to a consideration of claimant's contention that decedent's duties during the storm on December 14, 1951, is causally related to the death. 

The referee found that claimant's proposed amendment to include this occurrence was barred by the statute of limitations, since the amendment was made at the time of the first hearing on February 4, 1953, which was more than one year after the death. Counsel for defendant had opposed the amendment at the hearing on the grounds that it introduced a new and different cause of action and came too late. 

The pertinent rules of the board governing amendment to pleadings are as follows: "Article III. Rule 9. Any petition, answer, appeal, or other pleading pending before the Board or referee may be amended before final action by the referee or the Board, by a written agreement, or by a petition filed with the Secretary of the Board at the principal office at Harrisburg. "Rule 10. A copy of such petition to the Board or referee must be served upon each adverse party or his counsel. A petition must set forth, under affidavit, the original pleading, the amended pleading desired, the reasons why the amendment is desired, and such facts as are alleged as grounds for amendment, and the date of service of the copy to each adverse party. "Any adverse party may, within ten (10) days after the copy of the petition is served upon him, file an answer under affidavit denying any fact alleged in the petition for amendment and giving any other reason why the amendment prayed for therein should not be granted. "Rule 11. The petition may be disposed of by the Board or the referee without argument."

No formal amendment to the claim petition was filed. 

Counsel for claimant cites the case of Hannigan v. Globe 

Furniture Co., 15 Dale 309, as being closely analogous to the instant case. There, claimant filed a claim petition alleging an accident on October 3, 1930, when struck by a case of merchandise. Decedent died on October 12, 1930. At the

1954] 

 hearing on October 5, 1933, after a physician testified that death was due to lobar pneumonia, claimant moved to amend the petition as follows: 

"A window in the board partition back of where he sat driving a truck in the course of employment was broken in an accident on or about October 3, 1930, subjecting him to unusual exposure to air currents, and he caught cold, pneumonia developed and he died."

The Philadelphia Court of Common Pleas held that the referee and board erred in denying leave to amend the original claim petition and remanded the record for further hearing upon its merits. The court said: "There is nothing in the law which specifically prohibits an amendment to a claim petition after a year has elapsed, even though the amendment might, considered strictly, introduce a new cause of action, in so far as the cause of death, for which compensation was claimed in the original petitions, is changed. The basis for compensation in the present case is the death of claimant's husband, and so long as the amendment does not introduce another claim in addition to, instead of in lieu of, the original claim, the amendment should be allowed, even though a year from the death has elapsed."

In that case both the claimed injury caused by the case of merchandise and the unusual exposure to the elements stem from an accident on the same day. Here, we are concerned with what purports to be two separate and entirely unrelated accidents which occurred on two different dates, burns caused by explosion on September 19, 1951, and pneumonia caused by exposure on December 14, 1951. Claimant seeks to prove that either circumstance contributed to the death. In the contemplation of the law, the two incidents were not part of a single accident but two entirely independent occurrences for which it was necessary to file separate claim petitions in the same manner as if an employe suffered injuries to two different body members in different happenings: Jones v. Philadelphia & Reading Coal & Iron 

Co., 154 Pa. Superior Ct. 465.

In order to constitute a valid

amendment, the pneumonia which struck decedent down would have to result from the accident on September 19, 1951, and not from some foreign occurrence. Since the alleged accident, relating to exposure on December 14, 1951, was raised for the first time as a new accident on February 4, 1953, which was more than one year after the death on December 21, 1951, the claim therefore is barred: Section 315.

We hereby amend the referee's third finding of fact by substituting December 21, 1951, for December 21, 1952, as the date of death.

As amended, the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of dismissal are affirmed. The appeal of claimant is dismissed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD EVANS v. TURNER CONSTRUCTION COMPANY 

Workmen's compensation-Eye injury caused by unusual jolt of bulldozer-Medical testimony-Prior operation on eye.

Workmen's compensation was properly allowed claimant, who suffered a detached retina to the left eye, where it was proven by adequate medical testimony that the injury was the result of an unusual jolt and jar of a bulldozer operated by claimant over rough terrain, although the eye might have been weakened by a prior operation on it. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Carmalt, specially presiding in the 4th District, claim petition no. 

124,400, accident no. 5763906, A-38833. Affirmed as modified. March 31, 1953. 

Liberty Mutual Insurance Co., insurance carrier.

Walter A. Herley, for claimant. 

J. P. Erwin, for defendant.

1954] 

 KNOLL, Chairman: 

This is an appeal by defendant from the order of the referee awarding compensation to claimant. 

Claimant's petition, filed August 20, 1951, alleged an accident on May 16, 1951, and described the injury as a detached retina to the left eye. Defendant filed an answer in denial of the occurrence of an accident. 

Claimant, on his own behalf, testified that he operated a bulldozer for defendant for about two months before May 

16, 1951; that on May 16, 1951, while he was driving the machine over a ditch, which was 18 to 20 inches wide and 312 inches deep, the front wheels came up off the ground and then struck the ground with some force and jolted him; that his left eye went black and pained a little bit; that he thought he had a flash weld to the eye and reported the incident to the office; that nothing was done for him at the office on that or the following day, and he saw a doctor on the next day. He testified further that he was hospitalized from May 20, 1951, to July 3, 1951, for operations to the eye and that he was disabled until November 15, 1951. Upon cross-examination, claimant said that he had operated bulldozers for about 10 or 12 years and that he had undergone an operation to the left eye in 1942 when he was disabled about three months. Claimant identified his signature to a statement given by him to the insurance carrier's representative in which there was no mention of the accident testified to by claimant. 

Dr. C. Fremont Hall, eye specialist, claimant's witness, testified that he examined claimant on May 18, 1951, diagnosed a detached retina and referred him to Dr. Joseph S. 

Lynch. In answer to the hyopthetical question, based on claimant's testimony, the doctor expressed the opinion that it was altogether possible that the bouncing around could cause a detachment of the retina. Upon cross-examination

Dr. Hall testified that he recalled no history from claimant relating to crossing a ditch and that he did not know if claimant's eye operation of 1942 had any bearing on the detachment of the retina of the eye.

Dr. Joseph S. Lynch, an ophthalmologist, called on claimant's behalf, testified that he examined claimant on May 20, 1951, and that he later made two operations on the left eye to reattach the retina. He described his operative findings as indicating a not long standing detachment and stated that he felt that the detachment occurred from the incident described by claimant. Upon cross-examination, Dr. Lynch testified that the operation to the eye in 1942 might have weakened the retina and made it more likely to detach upon a sudden blow or jar. 

Dr. Carroll R. Mullen, an ophthalmologist, called by defendant, testified that he examined claimant on June 8, 1951, and that claimant gave him a history of blurring of the left eye upon the flash of a weld and a jar of the bulldozer. 

He described the examination of the left eye and diagnosed a detachment of the retina in the left eye with old chorioretinitis and proliferating retinitis. In his opinion, the detachment had resulted possibly from the old injury to the eye, and the detachment could also have occurred spontaneously and without trauma. Dr. Mullen testified further that claimant's statement of the injury was insufficient to cause detachment.

Defendant argues on appeal that, since claimant had a preëxisting weakness to the left eye, the detachment of the retina alleged on May 16, 1951, must be proved to have occurred after an unusual, untoward incident. If we attach credibility to claimant's testimony, as the referee apparently did, we do have an "unusual, untoward incident" not usual to the regular course of claimant's work as a bulldozer operator. He described an incident not normal or usual to his work when he testified that front wheels of the machine left the ground while he was crossing the ditch and making a sharp turn and that the bulldozer hit the ground with force. We find no reason to question the referee's judgment of the credibility of the testimony, and it is our opinion that the described occurrence constituted an accident. That the detachment occurred at the time of the accident is verified by the testimony of the medical expert, Dr. Lynch, who

1954] 

 said that he felt that the detachment occurred from the incident testified to by claimant.

We believe, however, that the referee's finding of fact relating to the accident should be amended, to describe the alleged occurrence, by adding thereto as follows:

Findings of Fact 

4. That during the operation of the bulldozer, over the unusually rough terrain, claimant suffered a jolt and jar when the front wheels of the machine struck the ground with some force after having left the ground while crossing a ditch and making a sharp turn. That the jolt and jar, as described, was not normal or usual to claimant's work. 

The remaining findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of award are affirmed. Defendant's appeal is dismissed. 

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA KELLEHER v. UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION BOARD OF REVIEW 

Unemployment compensation-Discharge for willful misconduct—

Excessive absence from work-Sufficiency of evidence. 

An order denying unemployment compensation on the ground that claimant's unemployment resulted from her discharge by reason of excessive absenteeism from work without good cause, will be reversed and the record remanded, where the only evidence to support the board's findings that claimant had been absent without giving notice was the testimony of the employer's foreman that claimant had been absent for one period for five days but the foreman was unable to say whether she gave notice of the cause of her absence. 

Appeal from decision of Unemployment Compensation 

Board of Review, decision no. B-34276. Reversed and remanded, 175 Pa. Superior Ct. 261.

William H. Mendlow and Ralph D. Tive, for appellant. 

William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, with him Frank F. Truscott, Attorney General, for appellee. 

Ross, J.:

In this unemployment compensation case, the compensation authorities denied benefits to claimant, Virginia D. 

Kelleher, under section 402 (e) of the Unemployment Compensation Law, 43 PS §802, which provides: "An employe shall be ineligible for compensation for any week . . . (e)

In which his unemployment is due to his discharge or temporary suspension from work for willful misconduct connected with his work." Claimant has appealed to this court. 

At the hearing before the referee, the employer was represented by one J. M. Murray, who described himself as "foreman of the Hubbard and Company", claimant's last employer. Claimant did not appear because she thought the hearing was to be held the following day. Murray had a very limited knowledge of claimant's relations with the employer and apparently represented the employer only because "the personnel man couldn't make it". He testified that claimant had been employed by the company as a stenographer and clerk and that her salary was "about 80 some dollars every two weeks" and that she was discharged on February 20, 1953 "for being absent too much. . . she was warned quite a number of times and we gave her quite a bit of consideration and all that". Claimant, it appeared, in a period of five months preceding her dismissal had been absent from work a total of 29 days. The several absences varied in duration from one day to ten days. Her last absence from work which resulted in her discharge was from February 16 to February 20, 1953.

The board held a hearing at which claimant and her brother appeared and gave testimony. No representative of the employer appeared at this hearing. Claimant produced a certificate from a doctor to the effect that he had been treating her for a year for "neuroasthma" brought on

1954] 

 by "a nervous strain following the sudden death of her sister, and she is taking care of her brother, who has been ill for the past four years since his discharge from the army". 

Claimant testified that certain of her absences were due to illness and others due to the necessity of caring for her brother when he was ill.

Excessive absenteeism without good cause in the face of a warning by the employer that further absence from work would result in dismissal, constitutes willful misconduct: 

Devlin Unemployment Compensation Case, 165 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 153, 67 A. 2d 639; Sauer Unemployment Compensation 

Case, 172 Pa. Superior Ct. 202, 92 A. 2d 896. Absence from work with good cause may justify an employer in discharging an employe, but such absence does not amount to willful misconduct: Crib Diaper Service v. Unemployment Compensation Board of Review, 174 Pa. Superior Ct. 71, 98 A. 

2d 490. The referee made, and the board affirmed, the following findings of fact: "2. During the course of her employment the claimant had been absent quite frequently without notice and had been warned that a continuance would result in her dismissal "4. On February 16, 1953 the claimant did not again report for work and did not give notification of any reason for this absence to the employer. The claimant failed to report for work from February 16 thru February 20, 1953, and at no time during this absence did she contact the employer."

An order of the board denying unemployment compensation benefits is conclusive only when based upon findings supported by the evidence (Sauers Unemployment Compensation Case, 168 Pa. Superior Ct. 373, 77 A. 2d 891) and, as stated by Judge Reno in Stillman Unemployment Compensation Case, 161 Pa. Superior Ct. 569, 56 A. 2d 380, at pages 571-72: "Where an act ascribes conclusiveness to evidentially supported findings, substantial evidence is contemplated,

that is, evidence affording a substantial basis of fact from which the fact in issue can be reasonably inferred." 

Here there is no substantial evidence to support the findings of the compensation authorities to the effect that claimant was "absent quite frequently without notice" and that she did not "contact" the employer during her last period of absence, from February 16, 1953, to February 20, 1953.

The only evidence upon which these findings could be based is the following testimony of Murray: "Q. She was off from February 16 to February 20th, that was five days and did she report her absence? "A. I couldn't say absolutely for sure. I would say no.

There was times she reported and a lot of times that she did not report off."

Ordinarily, a claimant, an unemployed worker in a covered employment, is presumed entitled to benefits when he registers for work and files a claim; but such presumption is, of course, rebuttable: Sturdevant Unemployment Compensation Case, 158 Pa. Superior Ct. 548, 560, 45 A. 2d 

898; Hassey Unemployment Compensation Case, 162 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 14, 17, 56 A. 2d 400; Mattey Unemployment 

Compensation Case, 164 Pa. Superior Ct. 36, 41, 63 A. 2d 

429. The burden was upon the employer to show circumstances which would bring claimant under the condemnation of a disqualifying provision of the Act, and on the record before us it is not clear that it met that burden. 

It does not follow, however, that we can direct the compensation authorities to award benefits to this claimant. 

Her testimony in regard to the several reasons which caused her to miss work, even though uncontradicted, need not be accepted by the compensation authorities. If it is found upon substantial evidence that claimant was absent from work without good cause and that she had been warned that further absences would result in her dismissal, under the principle of the Devlin case, 165 Pa. Superior Ct. 153, 67 A. 2d 639, supra, she would not be entitled to benefits.

The decision of the board is vacated and the record is remanded for further proceedings in accordance with this opinion.

1954] 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD KRAMER v. CITY OF PHILADELPHIA Workmen's compensation-Patrolman-Death resulting from in juries sustained while cleaning motorcycle off employer's premises- Relationship between injuries and death-Medical testimony. 

Where it is the responsibility of a patrolman to keep his motorcycle clean, such cleaning is deemed to be done in the course of his employment even though it is done in a garage off the employer's premises in which the cycle is kept for employe's convenience, and workmen's compensation was properly allowed claimant where death resulted from injuries sustained while cleaning the cycle under these circumstances. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Reynolds. 

District No. 1, claim petition no. 128,398, accident no. 

5867637, A-38893. Affirmed. March 31, 1954. 

Alexander F. Barbieri, for claimant. 

James D. McCrudden, for defendant.

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

This appeal by defendant, City of Philadelphia, from the order of Referee Reynolds, awarding compensation to claimant, Helen Kramer, for the death of her husband, Valentine 

Kramer, raises two questions. First, was decedent engaged in the course of his employment at the time he suffered an accident on November 15, 1951? Second, was his death on 

April 28, 1952, due to said accident? The first question is a legal question, and the second is a medical one. Decedent, aged 39 years, was employed by defendant as a motorcycle patrolman. Reciting what happened on the date of the accident, claimant testified that her decedent had worked the 3 to 7 p.m. shift and returned home about 7:30 p.m. After partaking of dinner, he left to wash his motorcycle which he garaged in the neighborhood. She stated that when her husband returned in about 20 minutes, he was in "a mess".

She noticed that the left side of decedent's face was cut and that he complained of pains in the left shoulder, arm and

chest. Helping him undress, she noticed bruises from the left shoulder to the waist. A neighbor took the patrolman to Temple Hospital for treatment. Decedent returned to full time duty on December 3, 1951, as a member of a police car squad. Claimant testified that decedent enjoyed good health and was on the "gay" side prior to the accident, except for an occasional cold or attack of grippe, but that there was a decided change after January 1, 1952, in that he became morose and appeared exhausted, his hair turned quite gray, he lost weight and complained of headaches and chest pains. She stated that decedent customarily kept the motorcycle near the home, left for an assignment directly from the house and he took care of it. Decedent died suddenly on April 28, 1952, while dressing, preparatory to entraining very early for Washington, D. C., in answer to a Federal Bureau of Investigation subpœna. 

Mary Fenerty, claimant's sister, testified that decedent had appeared to be in good health and spirits prior to the accident of November 15th but that he seemed depressed immediately after that occurrence, and she observed that he lost considerable weight. Paul Lingkchort, a neighbor and friend of claimant and decedent for about seven years, offered similar testimony. The witness took decedent to the hospital on the night of the accident. He testified: "Q. What did you notice about his complaints the night he fell off the motorcycle? "A. I noticed his face was pretty well bruised and scratched and that he was complaining about his shoulder and pain, terrific pain, inside of here (indicating) and they strapped- "Q. His left chest? "A. Yes."

Emma Lingkchort, wife of Paul, gave corroborative testimony as to the previous good health of decedent and that following the accident, he lost considerable weight, became quite gray and complained of headaches and chest pains. 

Dr. Thomas Dowd, claimant's witness, testified that he had known decedent for some years before his death, but he never attended him. The doctor saw decedent profes-

1954] 

 sionally on the morning following the accident and found "severe contusion of the entire left face; traumatic injury to the left elbow". The doctor was called on April 28, 1952, but the patient was already dead upon the doctor's arrival. 

He said that he did not make up a death certificate because it was a coroner's case.

Dr. Frank Glauser, assistant coroner's physician, testified in behalf of claimant that he performed an autopsy on the body of decedent on April 28, 1952. His pertinent observations were "an acute congestive heart and it caused a cardiac dilatation and cardiac insufficiency and contusion of the heart, was the cause of death". In reply to a hypothetical question propounded to him, the doctor expressed an opinion that the injuries sustained on November 15, 1951, were a major contributing factor in the death. Under cross-examination, Dr. Glauser testified that the first report submitted by him did not contain any reference to an old contusion to the heart, but he had made a corrected report after he obtained a clinical history of the accident. The doctor testified: "Q. You did see the heart, didn't you? "A. Yes. "Q. I mean at autopsy? "A. Certainly. "Q. Did you note any contusion? "A. This occurred six months afterwards, six months after the accident, and the gross evidence of contusion wouldn't be there at that time. "Q. And on examination there was no evidence of any contusion? "A. I wouldn't say that. I'd say I couldn't tell. "Q. In your first report to the coroner, no mention of the heart showing contusion was made on your first report? "A. Nothing was said about it at all. "Q. And you did give the cause of death following the first autopsy? "A. Yes, and that was omitted, contusion of the heart. "Q. Just what did you say at the time? "A. I stated cardiac dilatation and cardiac insufficiency.

"Q. And that was all that you did say? "A. That was all I said at the time. "Q. Well, what made you change your mind? "A. I was given a clinical history."

The doctor was questioned in regard to his testimony at the hearing before the coroner, whether the accident may have been causally related to the death, in which his answer was: "I cannot say definitely it had a great deal to do with his death."

The doctor explained: 

“That is a misprint definitely in view of what went afterwards. In other words, I didn't say 'cannot say' and then go on and explain that it had a great deal to do with his death. If you will read it on you will find that it is a misprint."

He stated there were no gross evidences of contusions of the heart at the post-mortem. The doctor stated that the coroner's office is a busy service, and it is not always possible to have a history before making a report to the coroner as to the cause of death. He gave it as his definite opinion that "that injury had unquestionably contributed to his death".

Captain John J. Mellon, defendant's witness, testified that decedent was under his command and that the duties assigned to decedent were to patrol and enforce the traffic laws. Decedent was required to report for duty at the Highway Patrol Headquarters, where he received his work assignment for the day at roll call. He stated that decedent asked and received permission to take the motorcycle to his home. Others did the same. Under cross-examination, the officer testified: "A. Well, we assign certain cycles to patrolmen, and if they requested us to let them use them and we find that we don't need that equipment for other tours of duty, we do allow the men to take them home as a convenience to the man. Patrolman Kramer was a good officer and he attended a lot of court cases and he requested 'the wheel' to be used as transportation to and from work to his home and to

1954] 

 attend court cases and hearings, and we gave him permission to use it because we didn't need the 'wheel' after his particular tour of duty. On his particular tour of duty, we didn't have another man that was available to ride that equipment he was riding. "Q. It was his duty, was it not, Captain Mellon, to care for that piece of equipment, it was in his custody? "A. That is a rule that he must care for it. "Q. When he washed and cleaned it and put it away for the night that was his obligation to do that? "A. Yes, sir."

He stated that very infrequently would a patrolman go directly from his home to a work assignment, in which case the men were allowed to report in by telephone. The captain testified that in going to the station from their homes on the city-owned motorcycles, the men were required to be in uniform and respond to any ordinary police emergency. 

On redirect examination, the witness testified that on a "solo" motorcycle, such as decedent was riding at the time of the accident, the person to whom the vehicle is assigned is responsible for its cleanliness.

The evidence is clear, uncontradicted and convincing that claimant's decedent suffered a fall from the motorcycle assigned to him on November 15, 1951. Did this accident occur while decedent was engaged in furthering his employer's business? In the recent case of Miller v. Greene

County, 171 Pa. Superior Ct. 494, the court again points out that where the injury complained of by accident, occurs off the employer's premises, "it must appear that the injury was sustained while doing an act necessary to further the employer's business or be done at the employer's direction or order". The principle of law is applicable here, but the facts there are in no wise analogous to the instant case, so that the case is not controlling in the facts presented in this appeal. The motorcycle was well-marked by identification as belonging to the City of Philadelphia and decedent could ride it only while in uniform. This combination of rider and vehicle represented the city's authority for the protection of the health, safety and well being of its citizenry. There

was an invitation to the public generally, at any time, to enlist the services of man and wheel in any emergency. See

Wegman v. City of Philadelphia, A-38685, Board 1953. This is particularly true here, where decedent was a member of the mobile unit of the city's police squadron, so symbolic of constituted authority. Maintaining the motorcycle, cleaning and washing it, were all necessary and furtherance of the employer's interests. Since a well ordered machine was so essential to carrying out decedent's duties as a patrolman and a well kept piece of equipment is a credit to the employe and to the employer whom he serves, we are of the opinion that decedent was engaged in the furtherance of his employer's business when he suffered the fall while riding defendant's motorcycle and resulting injuries. In the 

Wegman case, we granted an award where a city fireman was injured on a fall near his home while in uniform en route to the fire station. Here, Kramer, the decedent, was embarked in a service for the actual benefit of the defendantemployer. In McIntyre v. Strausser, 365 Pa. 507, the Supreme Court said: "But where the contract of employment provides that the employer shall furnish the means of going and returning from work, the employe is regarded as engaged in the furtherance of his employer's business during such transportation: Butrin et al. v. Manion Steel Barrel Co. et al., 316 

Pa. 166, 63 A 2d 345."

We are of the considered opinion that decedent's cloak of employment protected him at all times when he was engaged in attending upon the motorcycle, in going to and from the place where it was stored and when mounted and on city streets. Clearly so, where there is not the faintest hint or suspicion that decedent was engaged in a purely personal venture or one foreign to his employment. 

Was the death causally related to the accident?

We are also of the opinion that this question must be resolved in the affirmative. The question depends upon medical opinion. The testimony of the pathologist, Dr. 

Glauser, satisfied the referee and convinces us of the necessary relationship. His testimony was not contradicted.

1954]  We hereby affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and award. 

The appeal of defendant is dismissed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD LAYFIELD v. SUSQUEHANNA CHEMICAL CORPORATION 

Workmen's compensation-Claim by wife supported by others than her husband during his life-Decedent living away from home at time of death because of working conditions-Act of June 21, 1939 sec. 

307 (7).

Claim for workmen's compensation was properly denied under section 307 (7) of the Act of June 21, 1939, where decedent had for 18 or 19 years prior to his death lived intermittently with claimant and only occasionally contributed to her support, although he was not living with her at the time of his death allegedly because of employment conditions. 

Appeal from disallowance of Referee Smith. District 

No. 10, claim petition no. 130,116, accident no. 6006646, A-38593. Affirmed. November 25, 1953. 

Penna. Mfrs. Assn. Cas. Ins. Co., insurance carrier. 

Henry A. Onofrio, for claimant.

J. A. Fitzgibbon, C. E. Mader and C. M. McCarthy, for defendant.

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

Claimant, Mrs. Ruth Layfield, has taken this appeal from the order of Referee Smith, disallowing her claim for compensation for the death of her husband, James Layfield, on 

October 15, 1952. Defendant, Susquehanna Chemical Corporation, admits that the death was accidental in the course of decedent's employment as a wood cutter. The referee

found that claimant was not dependent upon decedent at the time of his death. Did the referee err?

Claimant testified that her decedent was employed by defendant from the latter part of August 1952 until the date of his death. Her testimony discloses that decedent was hospitalized in Warren where she visited him and upon his discharge from the hospital on June 21, 1951, she was visiting a sister in Corning but did not return to the home until July 7, 1951, because her brother-in-law's car needed repairs. Shortly afterwards, decedent and a son went to 

Florida and then returned to McKean County about June 

11, 1952. She testified: "Q. And where did he go to live when he came back from Florida? "A. He was all over. "Q. What do you mean by 'All over'? "A. First he was out to Helen's and Clara's and then he was home. "Q. You say he was home. You mean your home at West Branch? "A. Yes. "Q. When did he come back there to stay? "A. That was off and on. "Q. When did he come back there to stay? "A. He didn't come home to stay for good. He was just in and out, because he wanted to go to work and when he got the woods job he didn't come home only on week ends or whenever he could get somebody to bring him home." 

Claimant testified that after Layfield obtained employment with defendant, he "batched it up in the shanty" and would come to the home on some Sundays. She stated that she was on relief "straight through" until July 23, 1952, when a daughter, Janet, started working. Claimant testified that decedent did not have very much money but gave her sums "now and then", and that he did not support her. The sums he gave her were fifty cents to one dollar, which, of course, were wholly inadequate for support. On the Sunday before his death, she claims he gave her $10. The daughter, Janet, testified that decedent sent no money home while he

1954] 

 was in Florida and that upon his return, he stayed part of the time at home and partly with a sister, Clara. After he started working, the daughter testified that he would come home on some week-ends and stay a day or several hours and that she saw decedent give claimant some money on one occasion. A brother of decedent, Lyman Layfield, who lives about 600 feet from claimant, testified that upon visits to the home, claimant and decedent seemed to get along all right and that he observed decedent working around the house at different times. The brother's testimony discloses that at the time of the fatal accident, decedent had been working about 12 or 13 miles distant from claimant's residence and that he lived in a shanty in the woods area where he was working. He stated that this manner of living is customary and that, in the instant case, there was no adequate transportation from claimant's home to the place where decedent worked. Harry Anderson, a neighbor of claimant, testified that he saw decedent at claimant's home on several occasions while he was employed by defendant. 

Lawrence Wright, another neighbor, offered similar testimony as to decedent's presence around the home. 

A daughter, Clara Breese, called by defendant, testified that decedent had not lived with claimant for about 18 years and that decedent gave her his pay check regularly, from which she purchased his groceries and that she saved the balance for him. At the time of the death, this balance was $80. William R. Leipold, defendant's manager, testified that there is no set pattern as to how wood cutters live in the woods. He testified: "Q. Is there any custom as to how wood cutters live in the woods? "A. I wouldn't say there is any set pattern. We have wood cutters living in the woods in camps alone, and with other wood cutters, and others living in boarding camps where a man and his family maintain the camp. Others live with their wives and families in the camp and still others drive back and forth to work. "Q. Then there is no established custom. It depends on the individual?

"A. Not now. Years ago possibly it could have been more or less of a pattern established, but as of now I wouldn't say there is any set pattern for it."

The particular question posed in this case is governed by section 307 (7) of the Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 520, as amended which provides: "No compensation shall be payable under this section to a widow, unless she was living with her deceased husband at the time of his death, or was then actually dependent upon him and receiving from him a substantial portion of her support."

Counsel for claimant contends that there was no evidence of repudiation of marital obligations, of legal separation or of estrangement at the time of the death, citing Schaeffer v. Penn Dairies, Inc., 161 Pa. Superior Ct. 583. The facts in that case are readily distinguishable from the instant case. There, claimant and decedent were married on November 15, 1941, and the husband died on January 12, 1942.

After marriage, they continued to reside in separate rooms in the houses in which they had dwelt before the marriage, because of claimant's parents, with whom claimant resided and supported. They saw each other daily. The opinion states: "He contributed to her maintenance, purchased some of her clothing, paying several dollars weekly for groceries, and supplying fuel oil for the heating of her house." 

In the present case, the parties were married in 1911 and from at least the summer of 1951, he did not constitute a member of the household nor discharge his marital obligations. During the entire year that decedent was in Florida, he did not send claimant any money at all, and for many years prior thereto she received assistance from the county or State for her support. Even upon his return from Florida, in the words of claimant as to where her husband went to live, she replied that "he was all over", explaining that he stayed at the homes of several of the daughters. During the period of employment by defendant, decedent on only a few occasions contributed to claimant one dollar or less. 

Clearly, claimant for many years did not receive "a sub-

1954] 

 stantial portion of her support" from her husband as is contemplated in the law. While he was employed by defendant, claimant's support was provided by her daughter, Janet, and not by decedent. Clara Breese, a daughter of claimant and decedent, testified that the parents did not live together for 18 or 19 years and that decedent gave her his full pay. 

Her testimony supports the referee's finding that the parties were not living together, within the contemplation of the law, and since the issue involved is primarily one of fact, the credibility of the respective witnesses was particularly within the province of the referee. Under the act, a subsisting marriage between a claimant and her deceased husband is not the test for payment of compensation. The statutory prerequisites are "living together" "and receiving from him a substantial portion of her support". The record does not convince us that claimant has met the necessary burden of proof upon either requirement. 

We hereby affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of disallowance.

The appeal of claimant is dismissed. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD MCNULTY v. GRAND UNION TEA COMPANY 

Workmen's compensation-Door-to-door canvasser-Injuries sustained during lunch hour-Course of employment.

Compensation was properly awarded to a door-to-door canvasser, who sustained injuries when she slipped and fell on a waxed floor in a restaurant at which she, her supervisor and the remainder of the crew were eating lunch, although she had the privilege of eating where she chose and paid for her own lunch; not having duties connected with the plant or premises of the employer, claimant was in the course of her employment from the time she left her home until she returned thereto, as long as she did not engage in activities completely foreign to her employment.

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Phillips.

District No. 3, claim petition no. 130,622, accident no. 

6093137, A-38917. Affirmed, April 14, 1954.

Liberty Mutual Insurance Co., insurance carrier. 

James J. Powell and Richard P. Conaboy, for claimant. 

J. Paul Erwin, for defendant.

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

Mary L. McNulty, claimant, was employed by Grand 

Union Tea Company, defendant, as a canvasser or solicitor. 

On April 21, 1953, claimant was in a restaurant together with defendant's supervisor and the remainder of her crew during lunch hour. On her way to the ladies' room, claimant slipped on the waxed floor and fell causing injuries which totally disabled her for a period of about eight weeks, and partially thereafter. Claimant suffered multiple bruises of the left and right scapula, left hip and left and right leg. 

She suffered a hemorrhage of her left buttock, resulting in a tumor mass, which required operative measures for removal. The referee entered an award and defendant took this appeal.

The real and serious question here is whether claimant was in the course of defendant's employment when injured. 

It appears that defendant's supervisor called for the various members of the crew of canvassers each morning and took them to the area to be canvassed. Around noon he would find them and drive in his carryall automobile to a nearby restaurant. The crew was not obligated to eat at any specific restaurant; they could, if they desired, carry their own lunch. In practice, they all ate together, discussing their experiences in canvassing that day. Each canvasser paid for her own lunch. 

Claimant stated: "Q. My question is, when you went to a lunch room did anybody ever tell you that you had to eat at that lunch room? "A. No.

1954] 

​"Q. And you paid for your own lunch? "A. Yes. "Q. And each of the other girls paid for their lunches? "A. Yes, sir." 

And on page 51: "Q. Did he ever tell you that you could go any place you wanted to on your lunch hour? "A. Yes, he said if you want to go anywhere, but we always went together. "Q. Does he usually give you instructions where you are to work in the afternoon? "A. After we are back out, after lunch. "Q. Now as to the statement that I read to you from the exhibit, 'However, we can eat lunch anywhere we please since we pay for it ourselves', did you say that to the gentleman who took the statement? "A. He asked me that and I probably said yes."

The referee rendered an award and defendant took this appeal. There is no dispute as to the facts. 

Whether an employe is injured in the course of his employment is, upon the facts found, a question of law, open to review by the courts: Combs v. Cole Brothers Circus, 

Inc., 165 Pa. Superior Ct. 346. "No general formula has been developed which will enable us to determine readily whether an accident off the premises occurred in the course of employment even though the elementary facts are not in dispute. Experience has shown that many cases involving this question must be disposed of on their own peculiar facts and the question is frequently, as it is here, a reasonably close one": Healy v. Hudson Coal 

Co., 130 Pa. Superior Ct. 462, 464; Combs v. Cole Brothers 

Circus, Inc., supra. "Course of employment does not cover all the time during the day; it does not extend to intervals of time between regular working hours. It does include intervals of time. for leisure interspersed in regular working hours for rest or refreshment on the premises (emphasis supplied) 

Pennsylvania Workmen's Compensation Law, Skinner 4th ed., vol. 1, p. 227. 

---

Thus, generally, an employe who leaves his employer's premises to go home or to a restaurant for lunch and is injured off the premises, has not been injured in the course of employment. There are exceptions, as where the employer directs or orders the employe where to go, or where to eat. In the instant case, although claimant was not ordered where to eat lunch, the particular type of employment, that of a canvasser or house-to-house saleslady, is such as to cause inquiry as to whether claimant might not be an exception to the general rule also. 

In the case of Everard v. Woman's Home Companion 

Reading Club, 234 Mo. App. 760, 122 S. W. 2nd 51, a claim was filed for injury by a door-to-door magazine salesman who injured his foot while leaving the restaurant where he had eaten lunch. There he met, by accident, the crew foreman and other crew members. Claimant paid for his own meal. In awarding compensation, the court stated: "Employee cannot be said to have departed from his employment merely because he stopped canvassing long enough to obey the call of nature for food. On the contrary, we think he was not only within the course of his employment, but the injury arose out of employment because of the very nature of the particular employment."

The court felt it was reasonably incidental to the type of work in which the employe was engaged. 

In Laubeck v. Toc's Products Co., 285 N. Y. 577, claimant sold cleaning products on commission, house to house. Her working hours were nine to four. While the noon hour was recommended for lunch, it was optional, and claimant was free to eat where she chose. She paid for her own meals. 

About noon on the day of the occurrence, claimant set out for a drug store to eat and rest. Before she arrived there, she fell on a public sidewalk. The court pointed out claimant had no duties connected with any plant or premises of her employer, that she entered her employment when she left home and remained therein until she returned home. 

In the instant case, we have a sales unit bound together by their employment. They traveled in the same car and did the same type of work. While they could have gone

1954] 

 their individual ways at lunch time, such a course of action would have caused confusion and delay. It was expedient that the crew keep together. Lunch hour began whenever the crew manager picked them all up. After lunch he again set them canvassing where they had stopped. At lunch time, business or canvassing problems and experiences were discussed. To say the least, although not the criterion for an award, it was to the employer's benefit that the crew lunch at the same place. Defendant's operation was so conducted that under the particular facts, we think claimant was in the course of her employment from the time she left home in the morning until she was returned home at night, unless she engaged in something wholly foreign to her employment. 

From the nature of the employment, we conclude claimant was still in the course of defendant's employment during her lunch hour and that she is entitled to compensation.

We hereby affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order. 

Defendant's appeal is dismissed. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD LATTER v. STATE WORKMEN'S INSURANCE FUND 

Workmen's compensation-Interpretation of word "nursing"-Occu pational Disease Act, sec. 108(m). 

The word "nursing" as used in section 108 (m) of the Occupational Disease Act is to be interpreted in its generic and unrestricted sense and is not limited to registered nurses; therefore, anyone who performs the same duties as a registered nurse is exposed to the same hazards and risks resulting from close and personal contact with tubercular patients, and, as a result thereof, contracts tuberculosis, is entitled to compensation under the act.

Appeal by State Workmen's Insurance Fund, et al. from award of Referee Carmalt, specially presiding in the 4th 

District, claim petition no. 130,327, accident no. 6051788, A-38914. Affirmed. May 12, 1954.

State Workmen's Insurance Fund, insurance carrier. 

George J. Joseph, for claimant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for defendants.

BRENNAN, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by the State Workmen's Insurance Fund and the Commonwealth from an award entered by the referee under the Occupational Disease Act. 

Claimant, in her petition filed on May 8, 1953, alleged that she became disabled on July 31, 1952, as a result of tuberculosis contracted in the course of her employment as a nurse at the Allentown State Hospital. Responsive answers, denying liability were filed by the Commonwealth and the 

State Fund. 

After hearings, the referee found that claimant had become totally disabled on July 31, 1952, as a result of her employment as an attendant in the tuberculosis ward of the Allentown State Hospital and entered an award in accordance with such findings. This appeal followed. 

Section 108 of The Workmen's Compensation Act, as amended in 1951, provides for the payment of compensation for one who is totally disabled as a result of contracting "tuberculosis in the occupation of nursing in hospital or sanitarium involving exposure to such disease".

The uncontradicted testimony in the case before us shows that claimant was employed at the Allentown State Hospital during the period from March 16, 1950, until July 31, 1952; that she was assigned to a ward for female patients who were in a highly disturbed state and required close personal attention and special care; that some of these patients were suffering from tuberculosis. Claimant was also required to perform duties in an isolation ward maintained by the employer defendant for tubercular patients.

Claimant's work shift started at 2:30 p.m. and continued to 11 p.m. When her employment commenced, there was one nurse and one other attendant upon claimant's work shift, but, some time later, the nurse was transferred to an-

1954] 

 other ward and during approximately the last year of her employment, claimant was in charge of the ward during her working time and no nurse was present, claimant performing all of the duties previously performed by the nurse such as administering medication, both internal and external, taking the temperature, pulse and respiration of the patients, feeding and bathing certain patients, placing disturbed patients in packs, caring for epileptic patients, administering first aid and handling emergencies arising from violence on the part of the patients, supervising the ward, filling out all necessary reports and attending to all administrative details.

It is further agreed that despite the fact that claimant performed a large measure of duties customarily performed by a nurse, claimant is not a registered nurse. 

The question for our determination is whether the referee properly found that claimant contracted tuberculosis "in the occupation of nursing" as required by section 108 of the act. 

In its argument upon this appeal, defendant argues that if the above cited section of the act was intended to apply to others than "registered" nurses, the act would so state, adding descriptive words limiting, qualifying or describing the word "nursing".

We find ourselves unable to agree with this attempted restricted and limited interpretation of the words "occupation of nursing" attempted to be placed upon them by defendant, especially in view of the fact that The Workmen's Compensation Act and the Occupational Disease Act are to be liberally constructed to carry out their remedial and humanitarian purposes. We are of the opinion that as used in the act, the word "nursing" is to be interpreted in its generic and unrestricted sense, unless the legislature, in its wisdom, deliberately added descriptive words making such interpretation impossible.

Generically as has been pointed out by claimant in her brief, "nursing" has been defined "to take care of or tend, as a sick person or an invalid; to attend upon; to care or provide for tenderly or sedulously". From a review of the duties of claimant, as set out above, it is obvious that claim-

ant did perform the duties of a nurse, despite the fact that she was not a registered nurse. 

In determining the intention of the legislature, we must presume that the legislature did not intend its result to be unreasonable. Nursing duties are performed by others than registered nurses, graduate nurses and licensed nurses, and it would be unreasonable to assume that, where the duties are the same, it was the intention of the legislature to provide for only those who had a nursing degree and ignore and leave unprotected those who do not have such degree. 

If the duties are similar, each is exposed to the same hazard and any distinction made between the two groups insofar as entitlement to compensation payments are concerned, would be arbitrary, unjust and unfair. We are of the opinion that such distinction was not the intention of the legislature and where, as here, a claimant performs the same duties as a registered nurse, is exposed to the same hazards and risks resulting from close and personal contact with tubercular patients and, as a result thereof, contracts tuberculosis, such claimant is entitled to compensation under section 108 (m) 

of the Occupational Disease Act. 

We therefore affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order. 

The appeals of the State Fund and the Commonwealth are denied.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD DORIAN v. PENNSYLVANIA COAL & COKE CORP. 

Workmen's Compensation-Occupational disease—Passive agent— 

Death caused by nonoccupational disease—Occupational Disease Compensation Act of January 2, 1952, sec. 301(e).

Death benefits will not be paid under section 301 (e) of the amenda tory Occupational Disease Compensation Act of January 2, 1952, P. L.

1811, where silicosis was only a passive agency responsible for

1954] 

 lowering decedent's vitality or resistance so that another disease gained entry and caused death.

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Schwing. 

District No. 7, claim petition no. 130,328, accident no. 

6069351, A-38701. Affirmed. January 20, 1954. 

Eureka Casualty Co., insurance carrier. 

J. W. Stephenson, for claimant. 

Francis A. Dunn, for defendant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for Commonwealth.

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

This case is before the board upon appeal by claimant, Anna Dorian, from the order of Referee Schwing disallowing her claim for compensation for the death of her husband, August Dorian, on April 22, 1953, allegedly due to anthracosilicosis. Claimant testified that her decedent worked until 

April 3, 1953, when he quit because of ill health and shutdown of the mine. She testified that after decedent was laid off, he received one unemployment compensation check. However, records of the Bureau of Employment Security show that Dorian received unemployment compensation for two weeks. Russell Clifford, claimant's witness, testified that decedent worked as a motorman in the mine of defendant, Pennsylvania Coal and Coke Corporation, and described the dust conditions in operating the motor due to use of sand for traction. He stated that the colliery shut down on April 

2, 1953, and has not been in operation since that date. 

Dr. Ralph C. Greene, a pathologist called by claimant, testified that he performed an autopsy upon the body of decedent on April 22, 1953, which disclosed anthraco-silicosis upon microscopic examination but not upon gross examination. The pathologist also found bronchopneumonia as the terminal condition. The doctor testified: "Q. And as a terminal condition what relation would there be, in your opinion, between the bronchopneumonia and the anthraco-silicosis?

"A. One cannot say with great certainty. However, people with anthraco-silicosis are predisposed to all types of infectious processes in the lungs, and generally expire with a secondary infection. "Q. Well, is that? "A. And the bronchopneumonia is a secondary infection. "Q. Well, is that your opinion in this particular case? "A. My opinion is that this man developed bronchopneumonia superimposed upon and aggravated by anthracosilicosis. However, I don't mean to imply that one couldn't develop bronchopneumonia per se without any underlying process of any sort." 

Dr. Greene stated that he also found a cor pulmonale, which was not far advanced, and which he did not believe played a significant factor in the lung picture. In effect, the doctor was of the opinion that the cause of death was bronchopneumonia and the underlying cause was anthracosilicosis, which predisposed to the pneumonia. He stated: "There was presumably an infection that caused the bronchopneumonia. But the infection finds in the lungs a point of least resistance when there is already an anthracosilicosis."

Under cross-examination, Dr. Greene testified that with the silicosis present, there was less resistance to the inroads of the bronchopneumonia. 

Hugh Ellis, defendant's superintendent, testified in regard to decedent's employment as a motorman and that his last working day was April 2, 1953, at which time decedent was laid off because the mine was shut down. Dr. W. R. 

Hawkins, defendant's witness, testified that he first saw decedent professionally on April 13, 1953, for complaints of cough and chest pains. The doctor's diagnosis was influenza and bronchitis, for which he administered pencillin and directed decedent to stay in bed for a few days. On 

April 18, 1953, decedent suffered a fall during a dizzy spell on the stairway outside the doctor's upper floor office. The patient was sent by ambulance to the Memorial Hospital under the care of Dr. Edward Pardoe. The latter doctor

1954] 

 testified that he examined the patient on April 18, 1953, and found no ill effects from the fall. Dr. Pardoe stated that the patient's temperature was 98 degrees upon admission and rose to 103 degrees within a few hours, which is characteristic of infection. The doctor gave bronchopneumonia as the direct cause of death and pneumoconiosis as the incidental cause. Under cross-examination, the doctor testified: "Q. What in your opinion, Doctor, was the connection between the anthraco-silicosis in this case and the pneumonia? "A. Well, I don't know that there was any direct connection. I couldn't directly connect the two, but I would say of the silicotic it is likely that he would bear a severe infection with less resistance or less fortitude than a strong young fellow that has no lung disease. I think that would be reasonable." 

Marriage and dependency are established and decedent's exposure to a silica hazard is not challenged. The referee found: "That the death of decedent was the direct result of bronchial pneumonia, an infectious disease; that decedent had an advanced stage of anthraco-silicosis in his lungs, a condition that predisposed him to any infectious disease, and in this case it lowered decedent's resistance that made him susceptible to the bronchial pneumonia that resulted in his death." 

Is claimant entitled to compensation? Section 301 (e) of the amendatory Occupational Disease Compensation Act of 

January 2, 1952, P. L. 1811 provides: "Compensation shall be payable, as otherwise provided in this Act, for total disability or, death caused by silicosis, anthraco-silicosis, or asbestosis, or by silicosis, anthracosilicosis, or abestosis, when accompanied by active pulmonary tuberculosis. "

A radical change was affected by the legislature in that it is no longer necessary to prove that death was due solely to the occupational disease. Does the amended statute embrace the facts in this case so that the claim is compensable? 

We think not. Death was due to the bronchopneumonia which was established by the post mortem and by medical

testimony. We believe that compensation is reserved for those cases where a death is due to silicosis and other ailments, but not in cases, where, as here, the silicosis is a passive agency in the demise and only a responsible factor in lowering decedent's vitality or resistance, so that another disease gained entry and caused the death. The picture is clear here that the bronchopneumonia was the terminal factor in the death. Decedent, despite the existing silicosis worked until April 2, 1953. On April 13, 1953, he was ill from influenza and bronchitis. Five days later, April 18th, he was hospitalized for bronchopneumonia and death occurred on April 22, 1953. This is a typical pneumonia case. 

We do not believe that the Act of 1952 changes the legal requirements in proving silicosis as the cause of death, where the silicosis is not the cause directly, but only a weakening agent in lowering resistance or vitality, or in predisposing the individual to infectious processes. We are of the opinion that this case is ruled by Safin v. Jones & 

Laughlin Steel Corporation, 166 Pa. Superior Ct. 142, in which the court said: "Although they (doctors) testified that silicosis was the primary cause of death, it is altogether clear that their opinions rested wholly upon the circumstances that the employe's silicotic condition reduced his power of resistance so that he could not combat the additional pneumonic burden. 

They did not deny that pneumonia is a germ disease, and that it bears no relation to silicosis. They did not testify that silicosis causes pneumonia, or that deceased's silicosis produced or brought about the pneumonia. His silicosis was, according to a fair reading of their testimony, only a weakening process, an accelerating, a contributory, a complicating, a secondary, and notwithstanding their positive assertions not the primary cause of the death. 'A silicotic condition that merely contributes to death along with cooperating infirmities, or which simply serves to aggravate a pre-existing disease or state of physical weakness into fatal consequences would not be sufficient to sustain an award under the Act'. 

Treaster v. North American Refractories Co., 156 Pa. Superior Ct. 567, 572, 41 A. 2d 53."

1954] 

Although the opinion cited deals with a claim under the 

Act of July 2, 1937, P. L. 2714, we believe that the pronouncement of the court is applicable to a claim under the Act of 1952, even though it is no longer necessary for a claimant to establish that the occupational disease was the primary or sole cause of the death. Where the occupational disease merely predisposes the person to an infectious process, or lowers his resistance, so that he becames vulnerable to another disease, a claim is not compensable. If the occupational disease, independent of any other ailment, is an active agency in causing death, then the presence of other unrelated conditions will not bar a recovery. The testimony established that decedent's work record shortly before his death was such that he was not disabled due to the anthracosilicosis existing and, it logically follows that the death was not caused by this disease.

We hereby affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of disallowance.

The appeal of claimant is dismissed. 

COMMON PLEAS NO. 6 OF PHILADELPHIA COUNTY 

STARON v. PRIMUS et al. 

Workmen's compensation-Modification of disability payments- 

Burden of proof-Evidence must be specific. 

Where defendant seeks a modification of disability compensation on the ground that claimant is only 25 percent and not totally disabled, he has the burden of proof, but testimony of a doctor, introduced by defendant to the effect that claimant is not totally disabled "but is at least 25 percent disabled" is not sufficient evidence to sustain defendant's burden of showing the specific reduction in disability. 

Appeal from Workmen's Compensation Board. C. P. No. 6 

of Philadelphia County, March term, 1953, no. 5903. Reversed, April 30, 1954.

F. J. O'Boyle, for plaintiff. 

John B. Hannum, 3rd, for defendants.

FLOOD, J.: 

This appeal must be sustained.

Following an accident on July 14, 1950, a compensation agreement, approved by the Workmen's Compensation 

Board, was entered into on August 3, 1950, providing that claimant should receive total disability payments. On July 

30, 1951, defendant filed a petition for modification upon the ground that disability had changed from total to 25 percent loss in earning power. A hearing was held and on October 

23, 1952, the referee granted the petition. This order was affirmed by the board on April 8, 1953. 

On a petition for modification the burden is upon de fendants: Barckhoff v. Westmoreland Coal Co., 161 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 146 (1947); Boyle v. Boyle, 174 Pa. Superior

Ct. 188 (1953). For this reason the finding cannot be sustained because defendant's have produced no evidence to support it. The matter is as simple as this: The testimony of the doctor produced by defendant is that claimant is not totally disabled but is at least 25 percent. This is not equivalent to saying that he is 25 percent disabled but that he is somewhere between 25 percent and 100 percent disabled. 

This is not a sufficient basis for a finding that he is 25 percent as it would be, for instance, if the doctor had said that claimant was, at most, 25 percent, or if claimant had the burden of proof. Therefore, the finding of the Workmen's 

Compensation Board must be reversed, since it is not based upon any evidence. The only specific evidence we have is that he is not totally disabled. This is not enough to sustain defendants' burden of showing specifically what the reduction in disability is.

The finding of the Workmen's Compensation Board is reversed, and the matter is remitted to the board for further proceedings in accordance with this opinion.

1954] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD COLANAVAGE v. STATE WORKMEN'S INSURANCE 

FUND et al.

Workmen's compensation-Occupational disease-Claimant disabled "for all practical purposes"-Claimant also suffering from non occupa tional disease-Occupational Disease Compensation Act of January 

2, 1952, sec. 301 (e).

Compensation is properly awarded under section 301 (e) of the 

Occupational Disease Compensation Act of January 2, 1952, P. L. 

1811, where claimant is totally disabled "for all practical purposes" from existing silicosis, although he may also be suffering from other ailments which contribute to his disability.

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Stutzman. District No. 5, claim petition no. 130,390, accident no. 6069382, A-38942. Reversed. May 5, 1954.

State Workmen's Insurance Fund, insurance carrier. 

Thomas L. Kennedy, Jr., for claimant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for defendants. 

C. E. Bottiger, for employer. 

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

This case is before the board upon an appeal by claimant, Joseph Colanavage, from the order of Referee Stutzman, disallowing compensation in an anthraco-silicosis claim. The only question involved is a medical one. Dr. W. A. Schmidt, claimant's witness, testified that claimant came under his care on April 22, 1953, and as a result of his examination and X-ray report of Dr. P. B. Mulligan, his diagnosis was advanced anthraco-silicosis and cor pulmonale due to the silicosis present. He stated that he found no marked hypertension. The doctor was of the opinion that claimant is totally disabled due to the anthraco-silicosis. Dr. Mulligan's 

X-ray report stated an impression of advanced anthracosilicosis. The X-ray report also stated that the heart was enlarged in all diameters.

Dr. Mulligan, called by defendant, testified that he Xrayed claimant on April 27, 1953, and as a result of X-ray and clinical examination, he was of the opinion that claimant was not totally disabled due to the advanced anthracosilicosis, but that unrelated hypertensive cardiovascular diseases, plays a part in the disability picture. He testified that he found no evidence of cor pulmonale and that he felt the silicosis, per se, was not totally disabling. Dr. W. R. Glenney testified in behalf of defendant, that he examined claimant on June 12, 1953, and his pertinent findings were: "No. 1, Arterio-sclerotic cardiovascular disease with arterial hypertension. "No. 2, Left ventricular enlargement. "No. 3, Arterio-sclerosis of the aorta with dilatation. "No. 4, Anthraco-silicosis in the early phase of stage 3. "No. 5, Arterio-sclerotic retinopathy with early lenticular cataracts. "No. 6, Bunions, bilateral."

Dr. Glenney was of the opinion that claimant is not totally disabled due to the anthraco-silicosis, but is totally disabled because of the other unrelated conditions which he found. 

In view of the conflicting medical opinion, the referee requested the board to appoint an impartial physician. Dr. 

Martin J. Sokoloff was appointed in that capacity. The doctor examined claimant on November 17, 1953. His conclusions were: "This man has third-stage anthraco-silicosis. In addition he has hypertensive cardiovascular disease with enlargement of the heart. In my opinion he is totally disabled but his disability is due to a combination of pulmonary disease and disease of the cardiovascular system.'

Asked by the referee, whether the anthraco-silicosis, standing alone would be totally disabling, the doctor answered, "I wouldn't think so". Interrogated further, the impartial physician testified: "Q. Would it have to be selective light work? "A. Yes, selective light work of a general nature is complicated but I mean by that the man is not totally prohibited from earning a living.

1954] 

"By Mr. Kennedy "Q. In other words, Doctor, there would be jobs available to him if he had an education, such as a filing clerk or clerical work? "A. Yes, sedentary work, probably. "By Mr. Krencewicz "Q. And in your opinion, Doctor, that cardiovascular disease contributes to the disability, Doctor? "A. Yes. "Q. So the combination of the cardiovascular disease and the anthraco-silicosis is what causes the amount of impairment of function which he suffers? "A. Yes. "By Referee Stutzman "Q. Doctor, you mean it takes a combination of the anthraco-silicosis and the cardiovascular disease that disables him, so he can only do work of a light selective nature, or do you mean the anthraco-silicosis alone would disable him to that work? "A. I think that would disable him to that extent and the cardiovascular condition would cut down to make his work even more selective. "Q. Whether in your opinion, the combination of the two diseases totally disable him? "A. He is pretty well disabled. I still don't think it does.

I would hire this man if I were an employer and give him something to do sitting down, with the two diseases he has, but he is pretty much disabled. "By Mr. Krencewicz "Q. Could he operate an elevator? "A. I don't think it would be safe for the passengers. "Q. It is your opinion those two causes, the cardiovascular disease and the anthraco-silicosis, in combination are what causes the impairment of the amount of function which he suffers? "A. Yes."

The referee found (fourth) that claimant is only partially disabled by virtue of anthraco-silicosis and can do light work generally. We do not think so. Dr. Sokoloff's testimony as

to the scope of claimant's ability to work, in his opinion, is extremely limited, in which he even excludes claimant's ability to run an elevator, so that we believe he is totally disabled for all practical purposes, from the existing silicosis. It is significant that the legislature, by amendment to the Occupational Disease Compensation Act, approved January 2, 1952, P. L. 1811, changed the language of section 301 (e) to eliminate "solely" as qualifying silicosis, anthraco-silicosis or asbestosis as a prerequisite for compensation. Thus, the presence of other elements in the disability picture will not disqualify a meritorious claim. Although the impartial physician stated, generally, that considering the silicosis alone, he thought claimant could perform light work of a general nature, he did not so state unequivocally. 

The doctor's conclusion is further considerably emasculated by the cross-examination, which followed his opinion of partial disability as noted above. It appears to us that the doctor's testimony indicates an inescapable inference that claimant, with the amount of silicosis that he has, can perform work only of a selective light nature rather than general light work. Certainly, there is no yardstick or measuring device, to determine with any reasonable accuracy, the amount of disability which stems from the anthraco-silicosis and the amount for which the cardiocvascular disease is responsible. In making the necessary determination, it is necessary to apply common sense and sound judgment and to keep in mind the humane and beneficient purposes of compensation legislation: Roschak v. Vulcan

Iron Works, 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 227; Masovski v. Ham mond Coal Co., 172 Pa. Superior Ct. 409. The amendatory

Act of January 2, 1952 is a guide in this connection. Our view in this case is further buttressed by the recent opinion of the Supreme Court in the case of Unora v. Glen Alden 

Coal Co., 377 Pa. 7, in which Justice Musmanno said: "Thus, the determination of total disability is one which requires a consideration and weighing (in addition to the anatomical facts) of such factors as the claimant's mental outlook, his industrial background, his education, the occu-

1954] 

 pation, if any, he could perform where his particular physical impairment would not be a total bar, and whether such work exists. Where the injured person can handle only a specially-created job, one light of effort and responsibility but laden with rest and comfort (employment plums that do not often dangle from the tree of everyday economics) the burden is on the defendant-employer to show that such a job is in fact within reach. If proof of that fact is not presented, the claimant then is entitled to a finding of total disability."

Thus, in the instant case, we are of the considered opinion that it is fair, reasonable and only just, to conclude that claimant is totally disabled and is entitled to compensation. 

The referee's order of disallowance is in error and will be reversed.

We hereby vacate and set aside the referee's fourth finding of fact and in lieu thereof, we substitute the following 

Finding of Fact 

4. We find as a fact that claimant suffers from a thirdstage anthraco-silicosis and hypertensive cardiovascular disease; the anthraco-silicosis is advanced and exists to such an extent as to disable claimant for all practical purposes, so that he is totally disabled on account thereof. 

As amended, the referee's findings of fact are affirmed. 

We vacate, the referee's conclusions of law and substitute therefor, the following 

Conclusions of Law 

1. On March 4, 1953, both parties were bound by the provisions of the Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act of 

1939, P. L. 566, its amendments and supplements. 

2. Having found that claimant is totally disabled due to anthraco-silicosis, he is entitled to compensation therefor, within the limitations of the act.
