3. Since claimant's disablement developed only after an exposure of five or more years, compensation shall be paid jointly by defendant and the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania in accordance with the proportions provided for in the act.

We hereby vacate the referee's order of disallowance and in lieu thereof, we substitute the following Award

Defendant, Philadelphia & Reading Coal & Iron Company and the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania are hereby directed to pay to claimant, Joseph Colanavage, compensation at the rate of $30 per week, beginning March 11, 1953, and continuing at the same rate in accordance with the limitations of the act, but not to exceed the aggregate sum of $6,500. 

Said compensation to be apportioned 60 percentum, or $18 per week, against defendant, Philadelphia & Reading Coal & Iron Company and 40 percentum, or $12 per week, against the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania.

The above award is to bear interest in accordance with the provisions of section 410 of the Occupational Disease Act. 

Defendant, State Workmen's Insurance Fund, is ordered and directed to pay to Dr. Martin J. Sokoloff, Medical Tower, 255 South Seventeenth Street, Philadelphia, impartial physician, the sum of $150.

The appeal of claimant is sustained. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD SWABOSKI v. STATE WORKMEN'S INSURANCE FUND et al. 

Workmen's compensation-Death caused by emotional stress. 

Death caused by emotional stress and excitement, unaccompanied by actual physical injury or impact, does not constitute such death by accident as is compensable. 

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Stutzman. District No. 5, claim petition no. 125,959, accident no. 

5851853, A-38940. Affirmed. May 12, 1953.

State Workmen's Insurance Fund, insurance carrier.

1954] 

Thomas L. Kennedy, Jr., and Louis Cohen, for claimant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for defendants.

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

This case is before the board upon appeal by claimant, Elizabeth Swaboski, from the order of Referee Stutzman disallowing compensation for the death of her husband, John A. Swaboski, on November 17, 1951, allegedly due to an accident on November 16, 1951. Decedent was employed by defendant, Boro School District of Mt. Carmel, as an instructor. Claimant testified that when her decedent left for work on November 16, 1951, "he was well and happy".

Under cross-examination, she testified that prior to the accident, her husband dragged one of his limbs "slightly" and he was hospitalized shortly after their marriage on April 10, 1948, for a "check up" as far as she knew. A number of students testified as to an occurrence on November 16, 1951, at the school where decedent was employed. 

Edward Klimowicz, claimant's witness, testified that about 6 p.m., a student, Francis Manley and the instructor seemed to have some sort of an altercation and that the instructor kept walking around the classroom, with the student following him. A little later a constable, Robert Hancock, appeared to serve a warrant upon Manley, and when it appeared that the two were about to engage in fisticuffs, the instructor went between them and then he (Klimowicz) and fellow-students intervened. The fracas subsided when the witness signed out from the classroom. He said that decedent "seemed awful confused". He did not know whether the instructor "was touched or not" in the melee. The constable, Hancock, testified that when he attempted to serve the warrant on Manley, there were words between them and the two got into a tussle. Hancock described the incident as follows: 

"Four or five of those fellows came up to break it up. 

Swabby (the decedent) was right by us there. We got tussling. You know how it is with four, five men grabbing

at you, what you're going to do. I just kept myself busy. 

We give Swabby a bump and he did strike the blackboard- 

Swaboski, I mean to say." 

He stated that decedent was not struck by any direct blow.

He stated that "I'm pulling this lad around and Swaboski gets bumped, so I have my hands full enough with Manley without watching what's going on".

Dr. William A. Lustusky, claimant's witness, testified that he saw decedent professionally on September 28, 1951, and at other times for about six months, for hypertension. He stated that on the evening of November 16, 1951, he was called from his office to see decedent, who was sitting in a car outside and who complained of pains in his head. He ordered the two students who were with decedent to take him immediately to the Shamokin State Hospital. On September 28, 1951, the doctor stated, decedent told him that he was employed but that he was having trouble with two students who had threatened him with bodily harm. The doctor was of the opinion that the subarachnoid hemorrhage, given on the death certificate as the cause of death, was precipitated by emotion, exertion and trauma. Under crossexamination, the doctor admitted that subarachnoid hemorrhage could occur by reason of hypertension alone. He also stated that the hypertensive condition would cause decedent to be more susceptible to subarachnoid hemorrhage due to trauma, overexertion or anxiety. 

John Nalis, a student called by defendant, testified as to the occurrence in the schoolroom on the evening in question. 

His testimony is similar to that of Klimowicz relative to words between decedent and Manley and about the latter following decedent about the room. He stated that the constable came in, talked to Manley "and they started to grab each other", so that he and other students intervened, at which time the constable declared his identity so that the boys withdrew and no further hostilities took place. The constable then left the premises. As there was a recess period, the student and others left the room for some refreshments and according to Nalis, when he returned about

1954] 

1 2 

20 minutes later, he found decedent sitting at one of the front student desks, with his hat and jacket on, in an apparently dazed condition, although he was coherent. 

Swaboski was complaining about his head hurting. Nalis and another student, Durdock, took decedent in an automobile to Dr. Lustusky's office, where the doctor attended him in the car and directed the boys to take him to the hospital. 

The student stated that he did not see decedent struck or fall against the blackboard, but that he (decedent) was in the middle of the commotion. Following the disturbance, the instructor said he was getting sick and sat down at his desk. 

Anthony Durdock testified in behalf of defendant that he did not see decedent struck or get pushed. Walter P. Jankowski, another student called by defendant, testified: "By Referee Stutzman: "Q. Did you see Swaboski get struck or get pushed? "A. I seen him pushed because I was right coming up at my seat at the time. "A. You saw Swaboski get pushed? "A. He did get pushed. "Q. Where did he end up? "A. At that time I was already on top of Hancock. "Q. You don't know whether he hit his head or struck the blackboard? "A. I couldn't say that but I do know he was actually pushed because I was right around Hancock at the time." 

He stated that he did not see decedent pushed against the blackboard but that he "was right in amongst the scuffling". 

Peter A. Seckan testified that he did not see decedent get pushed, bumped or hit his head against the blackboard. 

Francis Manley, the center of the altercation, testified in behalf of defendant that decedent was outside of the classroom door when the argument started about the service of the summons, and that he (Manley) has his back to the open door "and we started to move around and Hancock was still on the outside of that door, and they broke it up and that was the end of that". He stated that decedent was inside the room where he could not see him and that he "didn't see anybody get flying around the blackboards or anything".

Dr. W. R. Glenney, called by defendant, testified: "Subarachnoid hemorrhage is limited to those cases of cerebral hemorrhage which occur spontaneously, from a rupture of one of the cerebral meningeal blood vessels, which occurs from trauma or from rupture of blood vessels which go into the brain tissue. The common causes are either generalized or a focal arteriosclerosis or aneurism, either congential or of an infectious nature. They are very frequently associated with hypertensive disease, high blood pressure disease. Precipitating factors in this type of hemorrhage are unimportant. The rupture is due to a disease of a certain part of the blood vessels, and it is the natural sequence of events that it is going to rupture at some time or other irrespective of what goes on." 

The doctor stated that he had heard the testimony, seen the death certificate and reviewed the hospital records, and was of the opinion that there was no connection between the classroom incident and the death, which he termed "purely coincidental". Under cross-examination, the doctor testified that he did not consider trauma, overexertion or overanxiety as the cause of subarachnoid hemorrhage. 

In view of the medical question involved, the referee requested the board to appoint an impartial physician, and Dr. 

Stanley Stanulonis was so appointed. The doctor testified, after a review of the record and hospital records, that the hospital records were unsatisfactory to determine whether decedent suffered from intra-cerebral or extra-cerebral hemorrhage, but that intra-cerebral hemorrhage is more common where hypertension is present and is usually precipitated by emotional stress or exertion, attested by sudden severe headache and confusion. These symptoms were markedly present in this case. The doctor explained that extra-cerebral hemorrhage (subarachnoid) is "the result of rupture of an aneurysm of one of the branches of the arterial Circle of Willis", and is also usually precipitated by emotional stress or exertion, the onset of which is sudden and accompanied by severe headache and confusion, and that coma usually comes on gradually. He stated that these

1954] 

aneurysms are usually congenital but may be produced by cancer, hypertension and trauma. Under cross-examination by claimant's counsel, the doctor stated that emotional distress could have increased the intra-arterial pressure so as to produce a cerebral hemorrhage, which he felt was present in this case.

It is clear that decedent suffered from hypertension which preëxisted November 16, 1951. We are satisfied that trauma did not play an important part as a major or contributing factor in the demise. The evidence on this point as to whether decedent was pushed so that his head struck the classroom blackboard is conflicting, and since the referee made no affirmative finding to that effect, we independently are of the opinion that the evidence is wholly inadequate to support such a finding. We, therefore, eliminate trauma as a causative factor. It is clear, however, that decedent was the victim of extraordinary emotional stress on the date in question, which had a serious adverse effect upon his hypertension and caused the demise on the following day. Decedent was subjected to considerable aggravation by the badgering of his student, and it was further accentuated by the episode which followed when the constable came to serve the warrant. The results to decedent were immediate and apparent. Does this emotional stress and strain constitute an accident within the meaning and contemplation of the law? In the brief filed by claimant's counsel, it is contended that the emotions materially aggravated an existing disease which culminated fatally. In support of this contention, the case of Frick v. Pittsburgh School District, 167 

Pa. Superior Ct. 431 is cited. A study of that case indicates that the facts are not analoguous and, therefore, not controlling. The reported facts were that "Frick stooped to get some sponges, from a steel cupboard and, in straightening up, struck his head against a projecting metal lock of the cupboard. He was dazed by the blow and fell into a chair behind him". He suffered a "wound or gash" and it was bleeding. There an injury, by trauma, was suffered, although not serious. In the instant case, we eliminate trauma

as a contributing or controlling element and consider only the emotional stress and excitement, which, in our opinion, was the causative factor in the death. We start with the premise that the burden of proof rests upon claimant: 

Seitzinger v. Fort Pitt Brewing Co., 294 Pa. 254. Our appellate courts have likewise held that where a claimant suffers from a preëxisting disease and death or disability ensues, an award cannot be granted if the employe was performing his regular duties in the usual way: Crispin v. 

Leedom & Worrall Co., 341 Pa. 325; Goettel v. Pittsburgh 

Coal Co., 140 Pa. Superior Ct. 516. In determining the precise situation presented here relative to emotional distress, the Superior Court has declared in the case of Liscio v. S. 

Makransky & Sons, 147 Pa. Superior Ct. 483, that emotional disturbance will not be the basis for an award unless accompanied by trauma or strain, saying: "Emotional disturbance which is unaccompanied by physical force, violence or strain, is not a basis for an award of compensation." 

In the case of Hoffman v. Rhoads Construction Co., 113

Pa. Superior Ct. 55, compensation was disallowed where the employe died following a heated argument with his foreman over the employes arriving late for work. No blow was struck and no assault was made by either party. Decedent suffered from chronic myocarditis. The general principle is stated in 71 C. J. §389, p. 634 as follows: ". . . the disability resulting from mere shock or fright, unaccompanied by actual physical injury, by impact, does not constitute such an injury as is compensable."

We are constrained to hold that the beneficent purposes of compensation legislation cannot be enlarged or expanded so as to include emotional distress as an accident. To adopt such a tenet would be to establish a precedent that would prove dangerous, and we believe is unwarranted under the law or the authority of our court decisions. 

We hereby affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of disallowance. 

The appeal of claimant is dismissed.

1954] 

COMMON PLEAS NO. 1 OF PHILADELPHIA COUNTY MUCHA v. M. L. BAYARD & CO., INC., et al. (No. 2) 

Workmen's compensation—Claim made nine years after accident—

Workmen's Compensation Act, sec. 315. 

Claim made almost nine years after the accident occurred, for cost of an eye operation and incidental compensation, is not barred by section 315 of the Workmen's Compensation Act where neither the employer nor the insurance carrier at any time prior to the operation denied liability, although they had ample opportuntiy to do so, and claimant in good faith continued to work for employer until his eyes were in proper condition for such operation. 

Appeal from decision of Workmen's Compensation Board. 

C. P. No. 1 of Philadelphia County, March term, 1952, no. 

6444. Reversed, December 2, 1953. 

Before Kun, P. J., Parry and Hagan, JJ.; Parry, J., dissents. 

Rappaport, Lagakos & Blank, for plaintiff. 

P. H. Ferguson, for defendants. 

KUN, P. J.

Claimant, Edward F. Mucha, was employed by defendant, M. L. Bayard & Co., Inc. On October 14, 1941, while working, he was struck in the right eye by a piece of metal. He reported the accident immediately to his superiors and was sent by them to a physician, Dr. Cassa, employed by defendant, for medical attention, who, in turn, sent him to Dr. 

Shipman, of the University of Pennsylvania and the Wills

Eye Hospital. Claimant was told by the physicians that a cataract would develop in the eye and that an operation would have to be performed when the cataract matured. 

Dr. Cassa sent a report of the case to the insurance carrier. 

The proofs show clearly that claimant was continuously assured by the defendant company that they would take

care of the operation whenever claimant was ready for it. 

Claimant continued to work. 

In the brief of the insurance carrier it is conceded that it ordered Dr. Shipman to proceed with the operation in the latter part of 1942. Claimant admitted receiving a letter in December of that year to make arrangements for the operation. However, because the other physician, Dr. Cassa, did not believe that the cataract was sufficiently matured for an operation, the matter was deferred. In this situation, claimant in 1943 was inducted into the United States Army and qualified for limited service, no doubt because of the limitation of his sight caused by the growing cataract. 

On his release from the army in 1946, he was rehired by the defendant company, with a continuation of assurances by defendant that his operation would be taken care of. 

What claimant was waiting for was that the cataract should "ripen" for the operation. At no time in the interim did defendant or the insurance carrier, directly or indirectly, suggest or indicate to claimant any denial of liability or the necessity of filing a formal claim petition. In 1948 or 1949, claimant was again sent to Dr. Cassa, defendant's physician, to make arrangements for the operation, which was a continuing recognition of defendant's responsibility to claimant. 

Claimant was gradually losing his sight in his right eye, but did not completely lose it, so he continued working. 

When early in 1950 claimant decided he was ready for the operation, and defendant's representative called the insurance carrier to arrange for it, it, for the first time, refused to pay for the operation. Claimant then engaged counsel and two month later, on March 14, 1950, filed his claim with the Workmen's Compensation Board, to which the insurance carrier pleaded that under section 315 of The Workmen's Compensation Act, 77 PS §602, the claim was barred because it had not been filed within a year after the accident, a strange plea indeed in view of the above recital of facts. 

In the course of its opinion, the board stated, on page 3: "It is plain that this claimant was, from defendant's conduct, led to believe that an operation could be had, without expense to him, at a time of his own selection."

1954] 

However, the board made the following finding of fact:

"The Workmen's Compensation Board finds as a fact that defendant's representations to claimant related only to medical and hospital treatment, that no promise of compensation was given."

The board dismissed claimant's petition. 

While it is true that we cannot on appeal disturb facts found by the board on sufficient evidence, the conclusion of the board that the admitted representations to claimant to take care of the operation on his eye did not cover the cost of an operation and incident compensation, is not a finding of fact but rather a legal conclusion which may be reviewed: Behanna v. Meyers, 158 Pa. Superior Ct. 208. 

And whether there has been estoppel is a question of law:

Meyers v. Lehigh Valley Transportation Co., 138 Pa. Su perior Ct. 569. If there could be such a thing as liability under the compensation act for the cost of an operation but not for compensation incident thereto, the ruling of the board in this case would be inconsistent because, after stating, as quoted above, that claimant had been led to believe that "an operation could be had without expense to (claimant), at a time of his own selection", the board nevertheless dismissed his whole petition and did not make any allowance for the cost of the operation.

The proofs in this case and the fact findings of the board present in law a clear case of estoppel against defendant's and the insurance carrier's plea that the claim is barred. 

There is no basis in fact or in law for the board's finding that defendant's and insurance carrier's representations to claimant related only to medical and hospital treatment. 

Certainly, medical and hospital treatment would include the cost of an operation where it related to a growing cataract caused by the accident and no other medical service was involved, and if claimant was entitled to have that paid, he was obviously entitled to have the incident compensation paid.

We think the board fell into error because of a misunderstanding of the case of Paolis v. Tower Hill Connellsville

Coke Co., 265 Pa. 291 and cases which followed it. They are not at all controlling on the question before us. In that case, claimant contended that the limitation in the statute on his claim for compensation did not bar it because he filed it within a year from the last payment made to him. However, what had been paid to claimant in that case was not compensation at all, but merely medical expenses, and as the statute (77 PS §602) provided, "where, however, payments of compensation have been made in any case, said limitations (for filing of claim) shall not take effect until the expiration of one year from the time of the making of the most recent payment ', it was held that that provision had no application to a claim filed more than a year after the accident where no payments of compensation had been made. In that case, there was never any payment of compensation or any representations of any kind to claimant that an operation or anything of the like would be paid for, nor was there any referral by defendant of claimant to its doctor for treatment. The question of estoppel was not involved in that case. It turned merely on the proper application of the quoted language of the statute. There, the question was the effect of payments for one thing (medical expenses) on tolling the statute for something else, compensation, which might or might not be due claimant. Obviously, nothing could be predicated on a mere payment of medical expenses which might be entirely gratuitous without implying any legal liability or recognition of liability and which the employer could discontinue at will, leaving claimant to whatever legal remedies he had. The quoted section of the statute is that if "compensation" is paid, claimant has one year after the last payment (of it) within which to file his claim. There is no provision in the statute that the mere payment of medical expenses extends the time for filing a claim for compensation, as in the case where "compensation" is paid.

In the matter before us, the right of claimant does not depend on any payments having been made to him for anything. In fact he could not assert a claim until the cataract

1954] 

 was removed. It was then that he would be entitled to the cost of its removal and the incident compensation. That defendant and the insurance carrier are estopped from pleading that that claim is barred by statute is amply proven by the record and by the board's own finding of fact. As stated in Behanna v. Meyers, supra, at p. 213: "Where a defendant's representatives so deport themselves in their dealings with a claimant as to mislead or deceive him, whether innocently or with fraudulent purpose, and induce him to postpone the filing of his petition until the period of limitation has expired, the defense of inordinate delay is not available, as a defendant cannot be permitted to benefit from a neglect for which he is himself primarily responsible." "

Not only is there ample evidence that representations were continually made to claimant that the operation on his eye would be paid for, as found by the board, but even if there were no such representations, what is decisive of their liability is the fact that defendant and the carrier both sent claimant to physicians for treatment. This is the key to the case, for it was held in Meyers v. Lehigh Valley 

Transportation Company, supra, page 573, "after the insurance carrier moved to have claimant examined (in the matter before us claimant was first sent by defendant to its doctor, and later the insurance carrier actually authorized its doctor to have the operation performed), it devolved upon it to make a prompt report to claimant as to its attitude in order that (claimant) might protect his rights by consulting counsel and filing his claim petition if it denied liability". In the case before us, neither defendant nor the insurance carrier ever denied liability, or ever expressed any attitude other than that the operation would be taken care of.

The insurance carrier seems to concede all this, but contends that claimant waited too long to have the operation performed on his eye. We do not understand how the carrier can avoid its liability on any such ground. Like many people would do in similar circumstances, claimant put off the operation on his eye as long as possible. To his credit,

claimant kept on working while the cataract was maturing, until it finally blinded his eye, when he asked to have the operation performed and incident compensation awarded to him. Then, for the first time the insurance carrier asserted that claimant's right was barred. Every case in which the question of estoppel is raised is sui generis, depending on many factors and circumstances. In a case such as this, where claimant was injured in the course of his employment, about which there was no question, and immediately reported the injury, and was immediately sent to a physician by defendant, a report of which was sent to the insurance carrier and the insurance carrier actually authorized an operation, where there is no denial of liability at any time either on the part of defendant or the insurance carrier, but on the contrary claimant was acting in the very best of faith throughout and kept on working until he was ready for the operation, it would in our opinion be a reproach to the law if a claimant in such a position should have his claim denied on the ground asserted by the insurance carrier. 

The board's findings of fact are affirmed. Its conclusion of law and order dismissing claimant's petition are reversed and judgment is entered for claimant for his medical and hospital expenses incident to the removal of the cataract on his right eye, and for compensation in accordance with the provisions of the compensation act, payable on account of the removal of the cataract and for any injury caused thereby to the other eye.

HAGAN, J.: 

Concurring Opinion 

This case is before us on an appeal by claimant from a decision of the Workmen's Compensation Board, in which it reversed a referee's decision awarding medical expenses and compensation to claimant.

On appeal from a decision of the board, the sole function of the court of common pleas is to decide whether the board's findings of fact are based upon competent evidence and are consistent with each other and with its conclusions of law and its order, and can be sustained without a capricious

1954] 

 disregard of the competent evidence: Gill v. Fives, 170 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 564.

After carefully reading the record in this matter, we conclude that the board's findings of fact are supported by competent evidence; and the only question for consideration is whether, on the basis of these facts, the board's conclusion of law that claimant was not entitled to be awarded compensation and medical expenses was consistent with its findings of fact.

The board affirmed all seven of the referee's findings of fact, and as all of these findings are relevant to the question at issue, they are recited in full as follows: "1. That on October 14, 1941, claimant was an employe of defendant and on that same day suffered an accident. "2. That the nature and cause of the accident are as follows: 'While walking to tool crib he was struck in the right eye by a piece of steel, resulting in the complete loss of sight of the right eye and sight of the left eye now being affected'. "3. That claimant was treated by the defendant company's doctor and later referred to an eye specialist at the Wills Eye Hospital, where he was advised that a cataract had developed and that an operation would be necessary when the cataract 'ripened'. "4. The report of the eye specialist was also given the employer and claimant was advised by his immediate superiors that 'everything would be taken care of', leaving claimant under the impression that he might expect defendant to assume responsibility for such an operation and treatment at any time in the future that the cataract could be removed. Claimant, therefore, resumed his employment with this in mind until February 1943, when he was inducted into the Army of the United States. "5. Claimant was released from the armed service in

April 1946, when he was rehired by the defendant company. 

On several occasions the condition of his eye was discussed and from the testimony offered at the hearing in this case, his employer, through its works manager and personnel officer, assured him that the operation would be taken care of when the operation became necessary.

"6. When claimant notified defendant that the time had come for the performance of the operation on his eye, he was informed that the insurance carrier would be notified. 

The insurance carrier refused to assume any responsibility for the same, however, on the ground that no claim had been filed in accordance with The Workmen's Compensation Act and further stated that the entire file on claimant's matter had been destroyed. "7. Claimant, therefore, filed a claim for compensation on March 14, 1950 in which he stated the facts regarding the accident and the assurance of protection by defendant, which led him to believe that no further action on his part would be necessary, pending the ripening of the cataract then developing and the possibility of an operation to correct the same at some time in the future."

The board added an eighth finding of fact, as follows: "The Workmen's Compensation Board finds as a fact that defendant's representations to claimant related only to medical and hospital treatment, that no promise of compensation was given."

The board, however, vacated the referee's conclusion and award that ". . . because of the assurances of protection given claimant . . ." claimant was entitled to medical expenses for removal of the cataract and to compensation for any time lost as a result of the operation. The board thereupon substituted its own conclusion, as follows: 

"The Workmen's Compensation Board concludes as a matter of law that since defendant's conduct and representations related only to hospital and medical services, the statute of limitations was not tolled and claimant's petition was filed too late."

The question on appeal, therefore, is whether on the basis of the foregoing findings of fact the board was correct in concluding that defendant was not estopped from asserting the statute of limitations.

In passing it may be noted that there would seem to be an inconsistency between findings nos. 4 and 8, since finding no. 4, is that ". . . claimant was advised by his immediate superiors that 'everything would be taken care of" ";

1954] 

 whereas finding no. 8 is that defendant's representations related only to medical and hospital treatment. This inconsistency need not concern us in view of our ultimate conclusion that, accepting finding no. 8 in the light most favorable to defendant, claimant is, nevertherless, entitled to compensation and medical expenses.

We are not here confronted with the usual questions of whether the accident was in the course of claimant's employment or of causation. The only question is whether claimant is prevented from asserting his claim because of section 315 

of The Workmen's Compensation Act, 77 PS §602, which 

"In cases of personal injury all claims for compensation shall be forever barred, unless, within one year after the accident, the parties shall have agreed upon the compensation payable under this Article; or unless, within one year after the accident, one of the parties shall have filed a petition as provided in Article Four hereof." 

Defendant contends that the above-quoted section of the act effectively bars the assertion of the claim under consideration. Claimant asserts that under the findings of fact of the board, defendant is estopped by its actions and representations from asserting this limitation as a defense.

The basic principle with respect to estoppel to assert the time limitation imposed by The Workmen's Compensation 

Act has been frequently enunciated and applied by the Superior Court. Thus, in the case of Meyers v. Lehigh Valley 

Transportation Co., 138 Pa. Superior Ct. 569, 571, the court said: "The conduct of defendant and its insurance carrier may be such as to estop them from presenting the statutory limitation as a defense in bar of the claim for compensation, if the effect of such conduct was to mislead or deceive claimant, whether intentionally or not, and induce him to withhold or postpone filing his claim petition until more than a year had elapsed from the occurrence of the accident." 

And, in the case of Behanna v. Meyers, 158 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 208, 213, the court said:

"Where a defendant's representatives so deport themselves in their dealings with a claimant as to mislead or deceive him, whether innocently or with fraudulent purpose, and induce him to postpone the filing of his petition until the period of limitation has expired, the defense of inordinate delay is not available, as a defendant cannot be permitted to benefit from a neglect for which he is himself primarily responsible.""

The question of law, then, which should have been considered and applied by the board in the instant case, was whether defendant, or its agents, acted in such manner as to lead claimant to believe that it was unnecessary for him to file a claim petition; or, as it has sometimes been put, whether claimant was lulled into a false sense of security by the representations and actions of defendant. 

The board held that the principle of estoppel can only apply where defendant tells claimant not to file a claim, or makes representations to that effect; and that this principle can never be invoked where representations are made as to medical expenses and do not deal directly with the filing of a claim petition. In reaching this conclusion the board relied on Paolis v. Tower Hill Connellsville Coke Co., 265 

Pa. 291, and the subsequent cases of Guy v. Stoecklein 

Baking Co., 133 Pa. Superior Ct. 38, and Rowles v. State

Workmen's Insurance Fund, 141 Pa. Superior Ct. 193, which cited the Paolis case. Thus, the board stated, on page 3 of the opinion: "It is well settled at law that payment of hospital and doctor bills does not stop the running of the statute. (Citing the Paolis, Guy and Rowles cases, supra). A fortiori, it follows that a mere promise to pay such bills does not toll the statute."

The issue of law in this case is squarely presented at page 3 of the opinion of the board, as follows: "It is plain that this claimant was, from defendant's conduct, led to believe that an operation could be had, without expense to him, at a time of his own selection. The real question is whether under these circumstances, the statute was tolled.”

1954] 

The board's conclusion of law that a defendant in a workmen's compensation case can never be estopped from asserting the limitation period when its representations to a claimant go only to medical expenses and do not specifically refer to the filing of a compensation claim, is not supported by the cases cited by the board. 

All that was decided in the Paolis case was that payment of medical expenses is not a "payment of compensation" within the meaning of that portion of section 315 of The 

Workmen's Compensation Act which reads: 

"Where, however, payments of compensation have been made in any case, said limitation shall not take effect until the expiration of one year from the time of making the last payment.' 99 Thus, it will be seen that the case was decided strictly on the basis of statutory interpretation and the question of estoppel was not in issue.

In the Guy case, which cited the Paolis case as authority for the proposition that the payment of hospital and doctor bills does not stop the running of the statute, this principle was not in issue; and what the case actually decided was that defendant was estopped from asserting the limitation period as a defense by reason of his conduct, on the theory that "where a person is intentionally deceived as to his rights by one who has authority to act in the premises, the courts will not, if it is possible to prevent it, permit such deception to work an injury to the innocent party".

In the Rowles case the court cited both the Paolis and Guy cases for the proposition that payment of medical expenses does not toll the statute of limitations, but again that question was not in issue. The court there found that defendant was not estopped from asserting the limitation period as a defense on two grounds: (1) Whatever representations were made to claimant were made by the agent of claimant and not of defendant, and (2) claimant, not having relied upon any alleged statements, could not assert an estoppel.

Defendant also relies upon the principle that no estoppel can be raised unless representations have been made to the

effect that the claimant need not file a claim petition because defendant would file one for him, and that representations as to payment of medical expenses can never be sufficient to raise an estoppel. Defendant attributes this rule of law to the same three cases which the board had relied on, and cites, in addition, the following cases: Weyant v. General 

Refractories Co., 150 Pa. Superior Ct. 502; Demmel v. Dilworth Co., 136 Pa. Superior Ct. 37; Meyers v. Lehigh Valley

Transportation Co., 138 Pa. Superior Ct. 569; Sweeney v. 

Reading Co., 146 Pa. Superior Ct. 539; Cosenza v. General 

Baking Co., 147 Pa. Superior Ct. 591; Behanna v. Meyers, 158 Pa. Superior Ct. 208, and Behanna v. Meyers, 163 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 200.

None of the cases cited, however, supports defendant's contentions. The Cosenza and Weyant cases do not deal with the question of estoppel and are not at all on point. In the Sweeney case claimant waited five years before filing a claim and defendant did nothing to mislead him. The 

Demmel case held that a defendant's conduct in preparing an improper petition for claimant constituted a basis for an estoppel, but the case does not hold that this is the only basis for an estoppel. The Meyers v. Lehigh Valley Transportation Co., and the two Behanna v. Myers cases, rather than supporting defendant's contention, tend to support the position of claimant. These cases will hereafter be discussed in more detail.

Our own research has disclosed no cases which would support the principle of law applied by the board to the facts of the instant case. The very most that the cases cited by the board and defendant hold is that payment of medical and hospital expenses does not toll the statute of limitations, and, as pointed out heretofore, it is very doubtful whether they can be cited as authority for such a principle, since the Paolis case, supra, which the later cases cited as authority for the contended principle, did not actually so hold.

1954] 

Even if it be assumed that these cases hold that the payment of medical expenses does not toll the statute of limitations by way of estoppel, it does not follow, a fortiori, as the board concluded, that a promise to pay medical and hospital expenses cannot, under any circumstances, raise an estoppel.

Estoppel is an equitable doctrine, and each case in which it is invoked must be considered upon its own peculiar facts: 

Ervin v. Pittsburgh, 339 Pa. 241, 255-56. What the board has here attempted to do is to create a rigid and inflexible rule of law which would apply in every workmen's compensation case where a claim petition is filed beyond the statutory period. The board would hold that no claimant can be misled, deceived or lulled into a false sense of security by representations of defendant that do not specifically refer to the filing of a claim for compensation. We do not believe that the equitable doctrine of estoppel can be so limited and restricted. As has been heretofore stated, there are many cases where the Superior Court has held that the basis of the estoppel principle with which we are here concerned is that claimant must be so misled by representations of defendant that he is lulled into a false sense of security to the extent that he justifiably believes that it is not necessary to file such a claim petition. It follows that any representation or conduct on the part of a defendant which has a reasonable tendency to lull a claimant into a false sense of security is sufficient to raise an estoppel. 

It is apparent that a claimant, injured in the course of his employment, who has had his medical expenses paid by his employer but has not received compensation from his employer, could not be heard to say that he had been misled as to his employer's intention concerning the payment of compensation. When an employe is injured in the course of his employment, he is entitled to certain medical and hospital expenses, and he is entitled to compensation for the time lost from work. Where, therefore, an employer pays the medical expenses of the injured employe but fails to pay compensation, the employe is on notice that it is the

employer's intention not to recognize the claim for compensation. On the other hand, where, as in the instant case, although the employe has been injured, there will be no loss of work until after an operation has been performed, and where, as the board said in its opinion, at page 3, “it is plain that this claimant was, from defendant's conduct, led to believe that an operation could be had, without expense to him, at a time of his own selection", it would be manifestly unjust to deny compensation to the employe on the ground that the employer had not informed the employe that the filing of a claim petition was unnecessary. In the case of Behanna v. Meyers, 158 Pa. Superior Ct. 

208, mentioned previously in this opinion as having been cited by defendant in its brief, the court said, at page 213: "In Meyers v. Lehigh Valley Transportation Co. et al., 138 Pa. Super. Ct. 569, we stated that where a claimant at the request of the insurer submits to a physical examination by the insurer's physician, the insurer is under a duty to reasonably notify him of its attitude toward his claim, and if it failed to do so until after the statutory period for filing a claim petition had to run, it would be estopped from asserting that it was too late." (Emphasis added).

If a failure to notify a claimant of its attitude toward his claim is sufficient to estop a defendant, then it would seem a far stronger case for the application of an estoppel where the employer has notified claimant that it intends to recognize his claim as valid, as the employer did in the instant case by assuring claimant he could have an operation at defendant's expense whenever he wanted. Claimant in the instant case was lulled into a belief that it was not necessary to file a claim petition by reason of the representations and conduct of the defendant's agents just as certainly as though he were told not to file a claim petition, and the board's conclusion of law that no estoppel can exist because representations were made as to medical expenses and not as to the filing of a compensation claim must be reversed. 

When this case goes back to the board, it should be noted that the referee awarded to claimant the medical and hos-

1954] 

 pital expenses for the removal of the cataract from his eye, and allowed compensation for whatever time he might lose from his employment as a result of the operation. The board quite properly pointed out in its opinion that it had been found as a fact that claimant had permanently lost the use of his right eye, within the meaning of The Workmen's Compensation Act; and, therefore, if entitled to compensation, he was entitled to compensation for the loss of his right eye; Criner v. McKee Glass Co., 162 Pa. Superior Ct. 

627; Wildman v. Pennsylvania Department of Highways, 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 301; Diaz v. Jones & Laughlin Steel Corp., 155 Pa. Superior Ct. 177. Under section 306 of The Workmen's Compensation Act, 77 PS §513, the exclusive compensation for the loss of an eye is fixed at 6623 percent of wages during 125 weeks. Therefore, the referee's award for compensation should have been for the fixed statutory amount rather than for whatever time claimant might lose as a result of the operation.

For the above reasons, I concur in action of the court. 

COURT OF COMMON PLEAS OF LUZERNE COUNTY 

STACHOWIAK v. GLEN ALDEN COAL COMPANY et al. 

Workmen's compensation-Occupational disease-Medical diagnosis five months after last day worked-Sufficiency of notice of disability given within 90 days after diagnosis-Statement by claimant that he was disabled from last day worked-Occupational Disease Compensa tion Act, secs. 301(g) and 311-Retroactivity. 

1. Notice of disability given to employer within 90 days after an occupational disease has been diagnosed by a medical doctor is sufficient compliance with section 311 of the Pennsylvania Occupational 

Disease Compensation Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 566, to substantiate a claim for compensation, even though the medical examination was not made until five months after claimant stopped working and claimant, subsequent to the medical examination, stated in a Veterans Administration questionnaire that he had been disabled from the disease from the date he last worked.

2. Section 301 (g) of the Occupational Disease Act, as amended, providing that in those cases where disability or death is not conclusively proven to be the result of last exposure, all compensation shall be paid by the Commonwealth, is not retroactive and has no effect on claims made prior to September 1, 1953. 

Appeal from decision of the Workmen's Compensation 

Board. C. P. of Luzerne County, December term, 1953, no. 

339. Affirmed. April 5, 1954. 

Before Aponick, Flannery, Lewis and Pinola, JJ. 

Thomas L. Kennedy, Jr., for plaintiff.

J. H. Oliver, G. S. McClintock, M. J. Davis and F. B. 

Gelder, for Glen Alden Coal Co. 

A. J. Zawoiski, for Commonwealth.

LEWIS, J.: 

This case comes before us on appeals filed by defendants, Glen Alden Coal Company and the Commonwealth of Penn sylvania, from a decision of the Workmen's Compensation Board affirming an award made by Referee Joseph P. Olexy for alleged total disability of claimant from anthracosilicosis. The claim arises upon a petition filed April 18, 1950 by Frank Stachowiak, claimant, alleging that he had last worked for Glen Alden Coal Company on November 2, 1949, and had become totally disabled from alleged anthracosilicosis on April 12, 1950.

The first hearing was held before Referee Olexy on June 19, 1950. At that hearing, claimant testified he had worked in coal mines for about 36 years, and that he stopped work on November 2, 1949, because he had no wind. Dr. Hubert

N. Jones, called in behalf of claimant, testified that he examined claimant on April 4, 1950, and as the result of that examination, which included X-ray disclosures, he was of the opinion that claimant suffered from third-stage anthraco-silicosis caused by claimant's employment in the anthracite coal mines for a period in excess of five years. 

Dr. Jones stated that claimant became totally disabled as

1954] 

 of April 4, 1950. This was the first time claimant knew that he was totally disabled from third-stage anthracosilicosis. 

Defendant called Dr. William J. Corcoran, who likewise had examined claimant. Dr. Corcoran confirmed the findings of Dr. Hubert N. Jones, diagnosing claimant's condition as third-degree silicosis.

Dr. W. T. Davis, called by defendant, testified that he examined claimant on May 8 and June 5, 1950, and expressed the opinion that claimant was not totally disabled by reason of anthraco-silicosis, and in the opinion of Dr. Davis, claimant was able to do light work.

In disposing of the case, Referee Olexy found, inter alia: "4. On April 18, 1950, claimant notified defendant that he had become totally disabled by anthraco-silicosis on April 

12, 1950. "5. Claimant was employed by defendant for a period of eight years immediately preceding November 2, 1949. He was employed in the mines for a total period of 36 years. 

During this entire period, he was continuously exposed to an anthraco-silica hazard. 

6. On April 4, 1950, claimant's physician advised him that he had become totally disabled by anthraco-silicosis. "7. On April 4, 1950, claimant became totally disabled by anthraco-silicosis in the third stage. He had been contracting this disease for more than five years prior to the date of his last employment and contracted it as a result of his employment in defendant's anthracite mines."

He concluded as a matter of law that claimant was entitled to compensation and accordingly made an award. 

Defendants, Glen Alden Coal Company and the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, filed appeals to the Workmen's 

Compensation Board. 

On March 17, 1951, the referee filed an amended finding of fact in which he stated that there was a controverted medical question as to the degree of claimant's disability, which the referee was unable to decide without the benefit of impartial medical advice, and for the purpose of creating a record for presentation to the then medical board, the

referee finally found as a fact that claimant was totally disabled by third-stage anthraco-silicosis as of April 4, 1950. Defendants again filed appeals.

The Workmen's Compensation Board, on May 29, 1951, vacated the foregoing amended finding of fact, sustained defendants' appeals, vacated all the referee's findings of fact and conclusions of law and award, and remanded the record for definite finding of medical facts, conclusions of law and order.

In compliance with the order of the Workmen's Compensation Board, the referee, on August 10, 1951, restated his original findings of fact, conclusions of law and award. 

Defendants again filed appeals.

On November 15, 1951, the record was referred to the medical board for determination of the medical facts involved in this case. However, before the medical board could dispose of the case, the board was abolished. 

The Workmen's Compensation Board was of the opinion that an impartial doctor should be appointed and, therefore, on April 9, 1952, it ordered that the case be remanded to the referee with instructions to request the board to appoint an impartial doctor who specialized in diseases of the lungs to examine claimant and the record and testify before the referee subject to cross-examination by the parties. The referee, then taking into consideration the entire record, was directed to make appropriate findings of fact, conclusions of law and enter an order and award, or an order of disallowance, as the case may be. The previous findings of fact, conclusions of law and award of the referee were vacated and set aside and the defendants' appeals were sustained.

In compliance with the order of the Workmen's Compensation Board, Dr. Edward W. Bixby, who was appointed as the impartial physician, examined claimant on July 22, 1952. 

He also examined the X-rays of claimant taken by Dr. Jones on April 4, 1950, and by Dr. Corcoran taken on May 8 and 

June 5, 1950.

The referee held a hearing on October 28, 1952, at which time Dr. Bixby was a witness. His report on claimant's

1954] 

 condition was received into evidence. Dr. Bixby was of the opinion that claimant was totally disabled solely as the result of anthraco-silicosis.

Claimant was called in his own behalf and admitted that he had been receiving disability payments as a veteran of the United States. The date of the beginning of these payments became an issue and defendants thereupon requested a continuance for the purpose of presenting the records of the Veterans Administration. The request was granted. 

The final hearing was held on February 24, 1953. Claimant testified that he had been receiving payments from the Veterans Administration in the sum of $63 per month, and that his first check was received in November of 1950; that he had filed his application for benefits in August of 1950, and was examined by the Veterans Administration during 

October of 1950. 

In applying for benefits at the Veterans Administration, claimant stated he had last worked on November 2, 1949, and had stopped working because he had become ill from third-degree anthraco-silicosis and that he had been totally disbaled from third-degree anthraco-silicosis since November 2, 1949.

At the final hearing, it developed that X-rays taken by the Veterans Administration disclosed the existence of other diseases, and defendants therefore contended that the disability was due to causes other than anthraco-silicosis. 

Dr. W. T. Davis was again called by defendants and testified that he now was of the opinion that claimant is totally disabled not because of anthraco-silicosis, but by arthritis and other diseases revealed by X-rays and examination at the Veterans Administration.

Defendants contend in view of the information contained in his application for benefits from the Veterans Administration, that claimant actually knew he was disabled as the result of anthraco-silicosis on November 2, 1949. However, they overlooked the fact that on the same statement wherein he gave information to the Veterans Administration, he also gave the name and address of his doctor, to wit, Hubert N. Jones, and the record already indicated that Dr.

Jones had examined claimant on April 4, 1950, and it was not until that date that claimant actually had knowledge that he was suffering from third-degree anthraco-silicosis In a decision dated February 26, 1953, the referee made, inter alia, the following findings of fact: "4. On April 18, 1950, claimant notified defendant that he had become totally disabled by anthraco-silicosis on April 

12, 1950. "5. Claimant was employed by defendant for a period of eight years immediately preceding November 2, 1949. He was employed in mines for a total period of 36 years. During this entire period, he was continuously exposed to an anthraco-silica hazard. "6. On April 4, 1950, claimant's physician advised him that he had become totally disabled by anthraco-silicosis. "7. On April 4, 1950, claimant became totally disabled by anthraco-silicosis in the third stage. He had been contracting this disease for more than five years prior to the date of his last employment and contracted it as a result of his employment in defendant's anthracite mines."

The referee concluded as a matter of law that claimant was entitled to compensation.

From this decision of the referee, defendants appealed to the Workmen's Compensation Board, alleging, inter alia, that the claim was barred under the provisions of section 

301 (e), art. III, 77 PS §1401, and section 311, art. III,, 77

PS §1411, of The Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act. 

The Workmen's Compensation Board affirmed the ref eree's findings of fact, conclusions of law and award. 

We have before us defendants' appeals from the opinion of the Workmen's Compensation Board. 

Section 301 (e), art.

III, of The Pennsylvania Occupational 

Disease Act, 77 PS §1401 (e) provides: 

"Compensation shall not be payable for partial disability due to silicosis, anthraco-silicosis, or asbestosis. Compensation shall be payable, as otherwise provided in this Act, for total disability or death caused by silicosis, anthraco-silicosis, or asbestosis, or by silicosis, anthraco-silicosis or

1954] 

 asbestosis, when accompanied by active pulmonary tuberculosis."

We are of the opinion that this section does not bar a recovery by claimant under the provision of The Pennsyl vania Occupational Disease Act. 

The medical testimony was conflicting as to whether or not claimant was totally disabled as a result of anthracosilicosis.

At the final hearing, Dr. W. T. Davis, called on behalf of defendants, and who originally testified that claimant could do light work, changed his mind and stated that in his opinion he would be unable to do any work because of the existence of chronic arthritis in addition to the anthracosilicosis which he contended was only partially disabling. 

In Rozauski v. Glen Alden Coal Co., Appellant, 165 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 460, 462, the court held: "The credibility of the witnesses and the weight of conflicting evidence on the factual issues were for the Board in this case, it could appraise the medical testimony and resolve any conflict therein."

The board could accept or reject, in whole or in part, the testimony of any witness. The findings of fact of the board are supported by competent and substantial evidence sufficient to justify the findings. The case presents a purely factual issue which has been resolved in favor of claimant by the board. We find no capricious disregard of evidence by the board. Such findings are binding on the court of common pleas. See Rozauski v. Glen Alden Coal Co., supra.

Section 311, art. III of The Pennsylvania Occupational 

Disease Act, 77 PS §1411, provides: 

"Unless the employee or someone on his behalf, or some of the dependents or someone in their behalf, shall give notice of disability to the employer liable for compensation under this Article, within fourteen days after the disability begins, no compensation shall be due until such notice be given, and unless such notice be given within ninety days after the beginning of disability no compensation shall be allowed."

Under the foregoing section, the statute begins to run on the date claimant knew or should have known he was totally disabled from anthraco-silicosis: Roschak v. Vulcan Iron

Works, 155 Pa. Superior Ct. 227; Coma v. Hudson Coal Co., 

166 Pa. Superior Ct. 503.

The contention of defendants is that claimant was aware that he was totally disabled as the result of anthraco-silicosis on November 2, 1949. This contention is based on the fact that claimant filed an application on August 22, 1950 with the Veterans Administration for benefits. In this application claimant stated that he was totally disabled as a result of anthraco-silicosis on November 2, 1949.

It is clearly discernible that if the claimant knew he was disabled by the aforesaid occupational disease, then he has not filed his claim within the period prescribed under section 311, supra, of The Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act. 

The 90-day period runs from the time the employe is disabled and definitely knows he is disabled by the occupational disease. See Valent v. Berwind-White Coal Mining Co., 172 Pa. Superior Ct. 305.

We are aware, as stated supra, that claimant in answering one of the questions in the application for benefits from the Veterans Administration, stated that he was totally disabled from anthraco-silicosis as of November 2, 1949.

However, we are of the opinion that this fact will not bar claimant's recovery under the provisions of The Pennsyl vania Occupational Disease Act. 

In the referee's hearing on June 19, 1950, Dr. Hubert N. 

Jones testified that claimant became totally disabled as a result of anthraco-silicosis on April 4, 1950.

The record before us clearly indicates that claimant last worked in defendant's occupation on Novmeber 2, 1949. 

Claimant filed his petition for compensation under The Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act on April 18, 1950, alleging that he had become totally disabled from anthraco-silicosis on April 12, 1950. Defendant applied at the Veterans 

Administration for benefits on August 22, 1950.

On November 2, 1949, the last day claimant worked for the defendant coal company as a layman, he was incom-

1954] 

 petent to diagnose that he had anthraco-silicosis. His statement to the Veterans Administration in his application for benefits, that he had anthraco-silicosis, was accurate because Dr. Jones had already diagnosed his condition prior to the time he applied for benefits.

Claimant's statement to the Veterans Administration that he was totally disabled from anthraco-silicosis since November 2, 1949, was an inadvertence on his part for the reason that it was not until April 4, 1950, that Dr. Jones diagnosed his condition as anthraco-silicosis.

In Hughes v. Continental-Archbald Coal Co. et al., 86 

D. & C. 430, 434, the court stated: "We do not believe that claimant's effort at self-diagnosis or personal speculation as to the incidence or degree of his disability ought to destroy the value of competent medical evidence, which definitely fixes another date for the beginning of total disability."

In Nockolay v. Hudson Coal Co., 164 Pa. Superior Ct. 

550, 552 the court held: "We are dealing with a statute which must be liberally construed to effectuate its remedial and humanitarian purposes, and a claimant can be debarred from its benefits only by a finding of fact which definitely and expressly excludes him.”

Viewing the evidence in the light most favorable to claimant, as we are required to do since the finding is in his favor, we find there is substantial, competent evidence to support the finding that claimant gave notice of his disability to his employer within the time prescribed by The Pennsylvania 

Occupational Disease Act. 

Defendant, Glen Alden Coal Company, in its appeal to this court, raises another issue which it did not raise in its appeal to the compensation board or in its argument before this court en banc. 

The defendant coal company contends: "13. The learned compensation board erred in affirming the referee's award because the Glen Alden Coal Company is not liable for any compensation in this case for the reason that under the provisions of section 301 (g), art. III of The

Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act, all compensation, if any, shall be paid by the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania." 

With this contention we cannot agree. Under the facts before us, section 301 (g), as amended, 77 PS §1401, does not apply. The particular part of section 301 (g), supra, relied upon by defendant coal company, was not enacted until August 24, 1953, and did not become effective until 

September 1, 1953.

It is clear that this section of the act is inapplicable to the facts before us for the reason that it was enacted subsequent to the date this cause of action accrued and following the award by the referee and the appeal to the Work men's Compensation Board. See Hughes v. The Continental- 

Archbald Coal Co. et al., supra.

Accordingly, now therefore, April 5, 1954, at 3 p.m., we enter the following

Order The exceptions are overruled, the appeals are dismissed, judgment is directed to be entered in favor of Frank Stachowiak against the Glen Alden Coal Company and the 

Commonwealth of Pennsylvania; the Glen Alden Coal Company and the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania are directed to pay compensation to claimant at the rate of $25 per week, beginning April 11, 1950, and continuing within the limitations of the Occupational Disease Act, but not to exceed the sum of $5,000.

The weekly payments of compensation shall be prorated by defendants as follows:

The Glen Alden Coal Company shall pay 90 percent of each installment, and

The Commonwealth of Pennsylvania shall pay 10 percent of each installment.

Interest shall be payable on deferred installments of compensation from the due date thereof.

Defendant Glen Alden Coal Company shall pay $150 to Dr. Edward W. Bixby, the impartial physician.

1954]  

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD PERIN v. STINEMAN COAL & COKE COMPANY 

Workmen's compensation-Reduction in average weekly wage not due to injury-Equal wage rate and opportunity to work-Workmen's 

Compensation Act, secs. 306(b) and 309(d).

By reason of section 306(b) of The Workmen's Compensation Act, claimant is not entitled to compensation where he returns to his old job and receives the same hourly rate as other employes similarly employed, works every day his employer is open for business, and has the same opportunity to work as have other employes engaged in similar employment, although his average weekly wage, calculated in accordance with section 309 (d) of the act is lower than that received for the same period prior to the injury; in such case, compensation will be suspended.

Appeal by claimant from order of Referee Schwing. District No. 7, suspending compensation under agreement no. 

5934538, A-38852. Affirmed as amended, May 19, 1954. 

J. W. Stephenson, for claimant. 

Albert L. Allen Co., Inc., and Francis A. Dunn, for defendant.

BRENNAN, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by claimant from an order of suspension of payments of compensation.

Claimant was injured on March 19, 1952. The parties executed an open agreement on April 12, 1952, in which the accident and injury were described as follows: "Caught right foot in tail end of chain conveyor. Simple and compound fracture, crush-laceration, traumatic amputation of right great toe", and providing compensation for total disability.

On June 24, 1952, the parties executed a supplemental agreement, in which they agreed that "claimant sustained traumatic amputation of right great toe". The supplement agreement further provided for compensation at the rate of $30 a week for 40 weeks beginning March 26, 1952. On 

August 3, 1953, claimant filed a petition to modify the sup-

plemental agreement, alleging that he suffered from disability other than the loss of the great toe, the specific loss referred to in the agreement. Defendant filed a denial. The referee granted claimant's petition and found as a fact that claimant sustained a partial disability of 222 percent, in addition to the specific loss as of December 31, 1952. He ordered compensation suspended as of said date as long as claimant continued to receive a weekly wage equal to the wage received when injured. Claimant has taken this appeal. 

Claimant's wages during the 52 weeks prior to the accident were: 

First period, 3-16-51 to 6-15-51 

Second period, 6-16-51 to 9-15-51 

Third period, 9-16-51 to 12-15-51 

Fourth period, 12-16-51 to 3-15-52 ... .$919.04 844.77 1040.20 .... 1167.11 which gave claimant an $89.78 average weekly wage, calculated in accordance with Sec. 309 (d) of the Act.

Claimant returned to work and his wages were for the first period, 10-1-52 to 12-31-52 .. or an average weekly wage of $49.15.

Second period, 1-1-53 to 3-31-53 

.$ 664.00 1106.16 or an average weekly wage of $85.09. 

Third period, 4-1-53 to 6-30-53 731.74 or an average weekly wage of $58.21.

Fourth period, 7-1-53 to 9-30-53 684.22 or an average weekly wage of $52.64.

It is, therefore, clear that claimant's weekly wages have been lower after the accident than they were before the accident. The record, however, shows that claimant went back to his old job and has been receiving, since December 31, 1952, the same hourly rate as other employes similarly employed, has worked every day the mines have worked and has had the same opportunities to work as other employes engaged in similar employment; thus his disability has caused no loss of wages.

We conclude that claimant is now receiving and has received, since December 31, 1952, the same wages as other employes similarly employed and that, therefore, he is not entitled to receive compensation payments.

1954] 

 Section 306(b) of the act states: 

"The term "earning power", as used in this Section, shall in no case be less than the weekly amount which the employee receives after the accident, and shall in no case be less than five times his actual daily wage as fixed by the day, hour, or by the output of the employee; and in no instance shall an employee receiving compensation under this Section receive more in compensation and wages combined than a fellow employee in similar employment.' 

We vacate the referee's 6th finding of fact and substitute the following

Findings of Fact 

6. We find that the claimant has returned to work at a lower average weekly wage rate than that received prior to the accident. Claimant's average weekly wages before the accident being set forth in the agreement as $91.97 and being $89.78 taking his most advantage 13-week period average weekly wages before the accident. Claimant's average weekly wages after the accident being from 10-1-52 to 12-31-52 1-1-53 to 3-31-53 4-1-53 to 6-30-53 ... $49.15 85.09 58.21 52.64 7-1-53 to 9-30-53 7. Claimant has returned to his former work and has received the same weekly wages as fellow employes engaged in similar employment since December 31, 1952.

We affirm the referee's other findings of fact. 

We vacate the referee's second conclusion of law and substitute the following 

Conclusions of Law 

2. That although claimant suffers from a 2212 percent partial disability, he has sustained no loss of wages due to the accidental injury of March 19, 1952. 

3. That since claimant has returned to his former work on December 31, 1952, and has received weekly wages equal to those paid to fellow employes engaged in similar employment, defendant is entitled to a suspension of future payments of compensation as of December 31, 1952.

We affirm the referee's 1st conclusion of law. 

We amend the order to read as follows:

Order The supplemental compensation agreement is hereby modified as of December 31, 1952, to show thereon that claimant suffers a partial disability of 22½ percent. Al! payments under the modified agreement are hereby suspended as of December 31, 1952, the suspension to continue as long as claimant continues to receive wages equivalent to those received by fellow employes in similar employment. 

Claimant's appeal is dismissed.

COURT OF COMMON PLEAS OF LUZERNE COUNTY SUDNICK v. GOFF LUMBER COMPANY et al. 

Workmen's compensation—Incisional hernia-Notice-Workmen's 

Compensation Act, sec. 306(h).

Where claimant could not ascertain that his injury resulted from an incisional hernia within 48 hours after the accident occurred and claimant's physician did not ascertain that fact until the twelfth day after the accident, his claim will not be dismissed because he did not comply with the notice provisions of section 306 (h) of The Workmen's Compensation Act, but will be allowed where notice was given in accordance with the general provisions of the act. 

Appeal from Workmen's Compensation Board. C. P. of 

Luzerne County, May term, 1954, no. 314. Reversed. May 4, 1954. 

Before Aponick, Flannery, Lewis and Pinola, JJ. 

Henry Greenwald, Murray Mackson and Solomon Lubin, for claimant. 

James P. Harris, for defendants.

1954] 

 PINOLA, J.: 

This is an appeal from the disallowance of compensation by the Workmen's Compensation Board which affirmed the referee.

Frank Sudnick, an employe of the Goff Lumber Company of Wilkes-Barre, was, on July 17, 1952, engaged in unloading a truck load of one-inch and two-inch planks at the fruit terminal of the Thomas C. Thomas Company in that city. 

Adjacent and parallel to a platform at the rear of the terminal the Lehigh Valley Railroad maintains a spur track. 

The truck operated by claimant was backed over the ties and rails against the platform. At approximately 3:30 p.m., while lifting a plank from the truck to the pile of the lumber on the platform, claimant slipped on a railroad tie and fell against the truck. He felt a sharp pain in the right side of the small of his back.

On returning to defendant's place of business, he immediately notified his foreman of the accident. He was instructed to make a report to Miss O'Brien, who was not in the office when claimant went there. He did report to Richard Goff after seeing the doctor.

He continued to work until July 22nd, when because of the excruciating pain in his back above the right hip he was required to stay in bed. On that date he called Dr. Phillip 

Morgan, who was unable to definitely diagnose his condition. 

A week later, the doctor concluded that an incisional hernia existed which required an operation. Claimant entered the hospital on August 13th and the operation was performed on August 14th. He was not able to return to work until 

November 17, 1952.

Dr. Morgan had operated upon claimant for a ruptured appendix on March 31, 1952, on which date drainage was instituted. He left the hospital about 12 days later, but he was not discharged as cured until May 29, 1952.

After two hearings, the referee concluded, inter alia: "1. That claimant had suffered an accidental injury on

July 17, 1952, in the course of his employment which resulted

in an incisional hernia in his right lower abdominal region; and "2. That notice of the accident was given to William C. 

Weiss, foreman, on the same day."

However, the referee disallowed compensation and dismissed claimant's petition because claimant had failed to comply with the requirement "that notice of an incisional hernia must be given within 48 hours after the occurrence of the accident under the provisions of the act”. 

The board on March 10, 1954, denied compensation not only because of the failure of the notice, but, as it said, because "no evidence was presented which would warrant findings of fact that claimant's incisional hernia had not been manifest to him or possible of diagnosis within the statutory period."

Discussion 

We will consider the board's second reason first, the alleged lack of evidence.

The board has stated its conclusion in a double negative. 

It said: "We find no evidence which would warrant findings of fact that claimant's hernia had not been manifest to him or possible of diagnosis within the statutory period.” 

Stating it positively, the board declares that claimant must have known or could have known within the 48 hours' period that he had suffered a hernia.

This is entirely unwarranted. Claimant knows nothing about medicine, and if Dr. Morgan, who is a qualified physician, could not diagnose the condition as a hernia on his first visit on July 22nd, just how could claimant possibly have known that he had a hernia within 48 hours after 

July 17th? The answer is too obvious.

We believe that the board has erred also in the matter of notice.

Reference to Gray's, Attorneys' Textbook of Medicine 

(3d Ed.) reveals that the phrase "incisional hernia" is not to be found therein. Nor is it to be found in Gould's Medical 

Dictionary (1941 Ed.). It is included, however, in Blakis-

1954] 

 ton's New Gould (1949 Ed.), where it is defined as "one occurring from an operative or accidental incision, the predisposing factors being wound infection, prolonged drainage, or interference with nerve supply".

While here the hernia did not result from any of the enumerated predisposing factors, it nevertheless did result from the separation of an operative incision' made March 31, 1952, and we, therefore, will assume that the description given by the doctor is correct. 

In our opinion, an incisional hernia is not within the purview of the compensation laws. When the legislature used the word hernia it had in contemplation only those hernia commonly known as ruptures.

Prior to 1927 hernias were compensible only under the general provisions of the compensation act; thereafter, by statutory amendments to the act. Both the 1927 and 1939 amendments, in almost identical language, set up a statutory presumption to the effect that "hernias shall be considered as a physical weakness or ailment, which ordinarily develops gradually." See Act of April 13, 1927, P. L. 186, sec. 306 (g), and Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 520, sec. 306 (g).

In rebuttal of the statutory presumption and in order to make hernia compensable, certain standards of proof were required, to wit: "(1) That the hernia be precipitated at once by severe strain; "(2) That the descent of the hernia follow the cause immediately; "(3) That there be actual pain in the hernia region at the time of the descent; "(4) That notice of same be communicated to the employer or his representative within forty-eight hours after the occurrence of the accident."

The legislature, intending to liberalize the compensation laws pertaining to hernias, eliminated this statutory presumption and removed the burdensome requirements of proof in refutation of it, through the Act of May 18, 1945, P. L. 671, sec. 306 (h), 77 PS §515. Only the requirement that notice be given within 48 hours after the occurrence

of the accident remained: Act of August 24, 1953, P. L. 1382, which extends the time to five days does not apply.) 

As Gray says, vol. 1, p. 781: "When the word hernia or rupture is used without qualification, it is intended to mean a protusion from the abdomen, usually of the intestine, through an abnormal opening in the abdominal wall. "A hernia may pass through any part of the abdominal wall, and characteristic names are given, indicative of that part of the wall through which they pass. We thus have: "Diaphragmatic hernia, passing through the diaphragm. "Umbilical hernia, passing through abdominal wall near umbilicus, or navel. "Femoral hernia, passing through femoral canal, which carries femoral artery to the front of the leg. "Inguinal hernia, passing through inguinal canal, which carries the structures from the testicle to the abdomen. "Ventral hernia, passing through the front of the abdomen at points not mentioned above. "Lumbar hernia, passing through back of abdomen in region of the small of the back." 

About a dozen types of hernias are internal and the protusion is not visible. How could one know that he suffered a hernia of the heart or of the spleen? In such cases, how could one give notice within 48 hours? "In ascertaining the intention of the Legislature in the enactment of a law, the courts may be guided by the following presumption (s) among others: (1) That the Legislature does not intend a result that is absurd, impossible of execution or unreasonable. . . ." Statutory Construction Act of 

May 28, 1937, P. L. 1019, art. IV, sec. 52, 46 PS §552.

The courts have, so far, given a reasonable construction to the hernia provision.

In Pollock v. Clairton School District, 100 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 333, Judge Cunningham, referring to Berner v. Phila delphia & Reading Coal & Iron Company, 100 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 324, said (p. 337): "We . . . there held that the term 'hernia' was used by the legislature in a popular, rather than a surgical, sense

1954] 

 and as implying a noticeable protusion from some part of the abdominal cavity and that the 'manifestations' required to be communicated to the employer within the time limited were the descent (evidenced to the claimant by the protusion) and the 'actual pain in the hernial region.' That was a case of ordinary inguinal hernia to which the amendment clearly applied. In a surgical sense, hernia includes not only the protusion of parts of the viscera from the abdominal cavity but also of any soft interior organ from its normal cavity, as a hernia of the brain, lung or eye, and of course includes an intestinal hernia of the type disclosed by the operation described in this case. But when the use by the legislature of the word 'hernia' is considered in the light of all the provisions of the amendment, we think it is clear that it did not intend to bring within the scope of the operation of the amendment any hernias other than the ordinary hernias, or 'ruptures,' which gives evidence of their occurrence by a protusion from some part of the abdominal cavity."

In that case, claimant's husband while engaged in shoveling coal into a furnace about 10:30 o'clock on the night of

April 24, 1929, suffered a strangulated inter-abdominal hernia. The family physician was called on the morning of
