Claimant returned December 6th and was, in effect, discharged by the employer.

The record supports a finding that claimant knew exactly what he was doing and deliberately violated the express orders of his employer, who was fulfilling a rush order and needed all his men at work on the day in question. 

Although a single instance of misconduct is usually not such as to bring an employe within the prohibitions of section 402 (e), we have previously affirmed an order denying compensation for an incident of much smaller import to an employer: Wilsey Unemployment Compensation Case, 169 Pa. Superior Ct. 368, 82 A. 2d 503.

In this case, the misconduct was clearly inimical to the employer's interests and was deliberate and flagrant according to the board's findings of fact, which were supported by substantial, competent evidence. 

Decision affirmed.

1954]  

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA ALBRIGHT UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION CASE 

Unemployment compensation-Leaving employment without good cause-Resigning job because of insufficient work. 

Claimant, a bookkeeper who resigned his job on the ground that he did not have enough work to do, and thereafter filed a claim for unemployment compensation was held not entitled to recover. 

Appeal from Board of Review. Affirmed, 176 Pa. Superior Ct. 290.

Edward W. Albright, appellant, in propria persona, submitted a brief. 

William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, with him Frank F. Truscott, Attorney General, for appellee.

GUNTHER, J.: 

Claimant was a bookkeeper who resigned his job on July 17, 1953. Thereafter, he filed claims for unemployment compensation benefits, which were successively denied by the bureau, the referee and the Board of Review. The board held claimant ineligible under section 402 (b) of the Unem ployment Compensation Law of December 5, 1936, P. L. 

(1937) 2897, as amended, 43 PS §802, in that he voluntarily left his job without good cause.

Claimant admits that he voluntarily quit his employment but justifies his leaving on the ground that he didn't have enough work to do. He was employed in answer to an advertisement for a bookkeeper. He received $75 a week and remained on the job for 15 weeks. He maintains that he anticipated being invested with some responsibility after learning the office routine but was never given any. Instead, he testified that he actually worked only about one hour a day, though on the job for eight hours. He admitted that

he never protested the lack of work. The employer testified that his services were satisfactory and that he was not being retained on sufferance, but that he was wanted and work could be found for him.

This is a most unusual case and claimant's desire for sufficient and responsible work is commendable. However, the facts do not disclose a situation requiring unemployment compensation. In order to qualify for benefits, an employe who voluntarily leaves his job must do so only for good cause. Good cause has been defined as a circumstance of a compelling or necessitous nature such as would impel a reasonable person to leave: Sturdevant Unemployment

Case, 158 Pa. Superior Ct. 548, 45 A. 2d 898. The facts in this case were certainly insufficient to establish compelling or necessitous circumstance. If the claimant was dissatisfied, he should have discussed the situation with his employer, who testified that the work load could have been rearranged. The evidence was more than sufficient to sustain the findings and conclusions of the board. Cf. Allen 

Unemployment Compensation Case, 174 Pa. Superior Ct. 

514, 102 A. 2d 195. 

Decision affirmed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD GUDALEFSKY v. LEHIGH VALLEY COAL COMPANY Workmen's compensation—Occupational disease—Limitation—Oc currence of disease within one year after last employment—Later occurrence of total disability.

A referee's order dismissing claimant's petition for compensation for disability as the result of anthraco-silicosis, on the ground that disability did not occur within one year from the date of his last employment in an occupation having a silica hazard will be reversed and an award entered, where it was admitted that claimant suffered from the disease on the date of cessation of his employment; under section 301 (c) of the Occupational Disease Act it is essential that the disease occur within one year after the date of last employment

243 

 but not essential that disability become total within one year from the date of such employment.

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Bufalino. District No. 11, claim petition no. 126,042, accident no. 5,872,556, A-38956. Reversed. June 23, 1954. 

Thomas L. Kennedy, Jr., for claimant. 

J. C. Valentine, for defendant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for Commonwealth.

BRENNAN, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by claimant from an order of disallowance by the referee.

In his petition filed on February 8, 1952, claimant alleged that he became totally disabled on February 7, 1952, the result of anthraco-silicosis contracted in the course of his employment with defendant. Responsive answers were filed by defendant and the Commonwealth.

After hearings, the referee found that although claimant was totally disabled solely as a result of anthraco-silicosis, such disability did not occur within one year from the date of his last employment in an occupation having a silica hazard, as required by section 301 (c) of the Occupational Disease Act. In accordance with such findings, he dismissed claimant's petition. This appeal followed. 

The referee has found that at the time claimant involuntarily terminated his employment with defendant on 

September 3, 1948, he was afflicted with anthraco-silicosis, but the referee denied the claim for the reason that claimant's disability had not become total within one year after his employment ceased. This, in our opinion, was error. 

Under the Occupational Disease Act, as amended by the Act of 1945, the criterion is the date when the disease occurred and not the date when total disability began. 

Therefore, under section 301 (c) of the Occupational Disease Act (which provides that compensation for an occupational disease shall be paid only if the disease occurs within

one year after the date of last employment) the criterion was whether claimant suffered from an occupational disease within one year of September 3, 1948, and not whether his disability therefrom had become total within one year of September 3, 1948. The referee has found that on the date of the cessation of his employment, claimant was already suffering from an occupational disease and no appeal from this finding has been taken by defendant or the Commonwealth.

It is the exposure that gives rise to the ultimate liability, but no compensation is payable until the disability becomes total: Agostin v. Pittsburgh Steel Foundry Corp., 157 Pa.

Superior Ct. 322. There is, therefore, sound reason for the extension of time that protection is afforded for an occupational disease resulting from exposure to unhealthful conditions over a long period, as disability often does not manifest itself until the employment has ceased: McIntyre v. Lavino & Co. et al., 344 Pa. 167.

In view of what we have stated above, we strike out the referee's eighth finding of fact. All other findings of fact are affirmed.

We strike out the referee's second conclusion of law and in lieu thereof, substitute the following

Conclusion of Law 

2. Claimant's total disability having been caused solely by anthraco-silicosis, during employment within the meaning of the act, and as a result of his extended exposure to a hazardous occupation for a period of five years or more, prior to his disability, he is entitled to the benefits of the act. 

We strike out the referee's order of disallowance and in lieu thereof, substitute the following Award

Defendant, Lehigh Valley Coal Company, employer, is hereby ordered and directed to pay to claimant, Adam Gudalefsky, compensation at the rate of 60 percent of $20 maximum under the act, or $12 a week, from February 14,

1954]  1952, and continuing within the limitations of the act and not to exceed the total sum of $2,400. 

Codefendant, Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, Department of Labor and Industry, Bureau of Workmen's Compensation, is ordered and directed to pay to claimant, Adam Gudalefsky, compensation at the rate of 40 percent of $20 maximum under the act, or $8 a week, from February 14, 1952, and continuing within the limitations of the act and not to exceed the total sum of $1,600. 

Interest at the rate of 6 percent per annum to be paid in accordance with section 410 of the act. 

The appeal of claimant is sustained. 

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA WEISS v. FRIEDMAN'S HOTEL (No. 2) 

Workmen's compensation-Injury in course of employment-Cook in summer hotel-Fall in bathtub while taking a shower. 

An order of the court below affirming an award by the Workmen's Compensation Board for disability as the result of accidental injuries sustained by claimant will be affirmed on the ground that she was in the course of her employment, where it appeared that at the time of the accident claimant was employed as a cook and pastrymaker in defendant's summer hotel, under a contract by which she was required to live on the premises and be available at all times for the efficient operation of the kitchen; that at the time of the accident after serving a meal, she had gone to a shower room upon the same premises upon which she was employed and was injured when she slipped and fell while taking a bath; under the circumstances, it could not be said that claimant had removed herself from her employment at the time of her accident. 

Appeal from C. P. of Erie County. Affirmed, 176 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 98. 

John M. Wolford, for appellant.

Howard N. Plate, with him Quinn, Leemhuis, Plate & 

Dwyer, for appellee.

WRIGHT, J.: 

This is a workmen's compensation case. The controlling issue is whether claimant was injured in the course of her employment (Act of June 2, 1915, P. L. 736, sec. 301 (c), as amended, 77 PS §411), which presents a question of law: 

Rybitski v. Lebowitz, 175 Pa. Superior Ct. 265, 104 A. 2d 

161. The lower court affirmed an award by the Workmen's 

Compensation Board, and entered judgment in favor of claimant. This appeal followed.

At the time of the accident, appellant operated a summer hotel at Cambridge Springs, Crawford County.¹ Appellee was employed for the summer season under a written contract which provided, inter alia, that she should "have charge of and prepare all cooking of food, pastries, cakes, et cetera, as may be required to feed and serve all patrons, help and management of said hotel; to live on the premises and be available at all times for the efficient operation of the kitchens of said Hotel". (Italics supplied.) 

At 8 p.m. on Sunday, August 24, 1947, appellee had finished serving supper and had made preparations to bake cakes for the next day. She told Mrs. Friedman, "I am running up to the shower to change my clothes, and in case anything goes on in the kitchen you be around". Mrs. 

Friedman replied, "Certainly".

While in the shower, appellee slipped and fell against the bathtub, injuring her leg, back and head. She was not certain what had caused her to slip, stating, "Maybe it was a piece of soap or something". Appellee testified that her working hours were unlimited, sometimes 18 to 20 hours a day. It was necessary for her to take several showers during that period because of the excessive heat, being customarily released by her employer for that purpose. 

1 The parties stipulated that the Court of Common Pleas of Erie 

County should take jurisdiction. See Act of June 2, 1915, P. L. 736, sec. 427, as amended, 77 PS §872.

1954] 

She also testified that, after the accident, she returned to the kitchen and directed the baking operation. 

Section 301 (c) of The Workmen's Compensation Act of 

June 2, 1915, P. L. 736, as amended, 77 PS §411, provides in pertinent part that the term "injury by an accident in the course of his employment" shall include injuries "sustained while the employe is actually engaged in the furtherance of the business or affairs of the employer, whether upon the employer's premises or elsewhere, and shall include all injuries caused by the condition of the premises or by the operation of the employer's business or affairs thereon, sustained by the employe, who, though not so engaged, is injured upon the premises occupied by or under the control of the employer, or upon which the employer's business or affairs are being carried on, the employe's presence thereon being required by the nature of his employment". 

Counsel for appellant contends that recovery by the appellee must be upon the basis that she was actually engaged in the furtherance of the business of the employer when the accident happened, irrespective of where it occurred. His position is that the accident here under consideration was not caused by the condition of the premises or by the operation of the employer's business thereon. 

Our Workmen's Compensation Act does not require that the injury “arise out of the employment", but only that it occur "in the course of the employment": Hale v. Savage 

Fire Brick Co., 75 Pa. Superior Ct. 454. The term "course of employment" has been uniformly held to include intervals of time for leisure interspersed in regular working hours for rest or refreshment on the premises. In the early case of Dzikowska v. Superior Steel Co., 259 Pa. 578, 103 A. 351, during an interval of leisure on the premises, an employe struck a match for the purpose of lighting a cigarette. 

His clothes, saturated with oil, caught fire and he was fatally burned. In allowing compensation, the Supreme

Court laid down the rule that acts of ministration by an employe to himself, performance of which while at work are reasonably necessary to his health and comfort, are incident to the employment. A more strict rule applies if the accident occurs off the premises: Palko v. Taylor-McCoy

Coal & Coke Co., 289 Pa. 401, 137 Atl. 625; Shoffler v. 

Lehigh Valley Coal Co., 290 Pa. 480, 139 Atl. 172.

In the case at bar, counsel for appellant concedes, as indeed he must, that the accident occurred on the premises. 

An employe is entitled to compensation for every injury received on the premises of his employer during the hours of employment, regardless of whether he is actually required to be at the particular place where the injury occurred if there is nothing to show that he had virtually abandoned the course of his employment, or that he was engaged in something wholly foreign thereto: Adams v. Colonial Colliery Co., 104 Pa. Superior Ct. 187, 158 Atl. 183; Haas v.

Brotherhood of Transportation Workers, 158 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 291, 44 A. 2d 776. It is not necessary that the employe be actually engaged in his work at the time of the accident: 

Dunphy v. Augustinian College of Villanova, 129 Pa. Su perior Ct. 262, 195 Atl. 782.

Considering the instant factual situation in the light of the applicable legal principles, we have concluded that appellee did not abandon the course of her employment when she left the kitchen for the purpose of taking a shower. It is important to note that she was obligated to live on the premises and to be available at all times. See 

Brown v. Elks Club No. 123, 113 Pa. Superior Ct. 226, 172

Atl. 408. Under such circumstances, it is only reasonable to assume that the employer and employe, in drawing the contract, realized that there would be intervals when appellee would be required to attend to matters of personal comfort. 

Cleanliness in the handling and preparation of food is an important consideration. Appellee's action in temporarily leaving the kitchen to take a shower on the premises was expressly approved by her employer. It did not constitute an abandonment of the employment: Boyd v. Philmont 

Country Club, 129 Pa. Superior Ct. 135, 195 Atl. 156. Nor was it an incident of such a pronounced character as to constitute a break therein: Hall v. Carnegie Institute of 

Technology, 170 Pa. Superior Ct. 459, 87 A. 2d 87. The right to compensation is not to be defeated on the ground that an injury on the premises did not occur in the course of employment unless the alleged departure is for an ac-

1954] 

 tivity wholly foreign to the employment or constitutes an abandonment thereof: Conley v. Pittsburgh Coal Co., 157 

Pa. Superior Ct. 567, 43 A. 2d 605.

In the instant case the board found that "the continuity of claimant's employment was not broken by taking the shower and that she did not remove herself from her employment in so doing". This conclusion was based upon competent and substantial evidence. See Werner v. Allegheny County, 153 Pa. Superior Ct. 10, 33 A. 2d 451. The accident happened during usual working hours. Appellee was in a portion of the premises where she had a right to be. Her purpose there was legitimately connected with her employment. The conclusion reached by the board, and approved by Judge Laub, was fully justified under the circumstances, and was a correct application of the law. See 

Hopwood v. Pittsburgh, 152 Pa. Superior Ct. 398, 33 A. 

2d 658. 

Judgment affirmed.

COMMON PLEAS COURT NO. 7 OF PHILADELPHIA COUNTY HACKER v. FISCHER & PORTER CO. et al. 

Workmen's compensation—Claim for disability arising beyond one year after accident-Absence of disability during first year. 

Compensation must be denied to a claimant where it appeared that he sustained injury in an accident in the course of employment, but suffered no compensable disability within the one-year period after the accident, although disability resulting from the accident occurred thereafter.

Appeal from decision of the Workmen's Compensation Board. C. P. No. 7 of Philadelphia County, June term, 1952, no. 5870. Affirmed. June 8, 1954. 

W. J. McKinley, Jr., for plaintiff. 

F. R. Ambler, for defendants.

PER CURIAM: 

This is an appeal by claimant from a decision of the Workmen's Compensation Board which affirmed the referee's disallowance of the claim petition. This is the second time the case has been before us on appeal. 

Detailed facts of the case are outlined in the opinion by 

Sloane, J., filed on November 12, 1952. Briefly stated, the accident occurred on March 17, 1949, when a wooden board platform on which appellant, a lathe operator, was standing slipped as he lifted a heavy chuck from the floor to the machine. He felt a "burning sensation-like a tear" in his back, but continued to work. On the thirty-first of that month, he reported to the plant dispensary and thereafter received a series of diathermy treatments. The carrier also supplied claimant with a special belt for support. Claimant lost only a few days from work at that time, but his back continued to bother him, and in January of 1951 defendant's physician directed hospitalization. Accordingly, claimant lost additional time from work in April of 1951. 

The medical testimony indicates beyond any doubt that claimant is now partially disabled, and the board found so as a matter of fact. No recovery was allowed as a result of the first hearing, however, because the board took the position that the claim was not filed within the one-year period after the accident as required by the Act. See The Work men's Compensation Act of June 2, 1915, P. L. 736, art. III, sec. 315, as amended, 77 PS §602.

This court, in the opinion referred to above by Sloane, J., reversed the board, holding that the statute of limitations was not a bar to recovery in this case because of complainant's reliance on defendant's agent. Our decision on that point is, of course, still controlling. Since, however, neither the referee nor the board had made any findings on disability resulting from the accident, the case was remitted to the board for further hearing and determination on that point.

At the second hearing the referee again denied recovery, because he found as a fact that no disability existed during

1954] 

 the year following the accident in 1949. This finding was affirmed by the board.

The degree of disability, if any, suffered by claimant was a question of fact for the board to determine: Leaver v. 

Midvale Co., 162 Pa. Superior Ct. 393 (1948); Wells v.

Lowber Gas Coal Co., 149 Pa. Superior Ct. 485 (1942). This court has no authority to make its own findings of fact contrary to the findings of the board. The weight to be attributed to the facts proved and the inferences to be drawn from them are for the compensation authorities to determine. Even where the evidence presented is subject to several inferences, the inference to be adopted is for the board as a fact finding body. See Kline v. Kiehl, 157 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 392 (1945). The question on review is whether there was a capricious disregard of competent evidence in the board's refusal to find facts in favor of the party having the burden of proof: Williams v. New Bethlehem Burial 

Service, 167 Pa. Superior Ct. 364 (1950).

The medical testimony in this case, while most favorable to claimant on many points, was conflicting in some aspects, and we cannot say that the board acted capriciously or without reason in reaching its conclusions. Even if the testimony was wholly uncontradicted, the board could have refused to accept it. In Bartha v. Campbell, 160 Pa. Superior Ct. 14 (1946), at page 17, the court said: "That the board is not required to accept even uncontradicted testimony as true is a well established principle of workmen's compensation law." See also Walsh v. Penn 

Anthracite Mining Co., 147 Pa. Superior Ct. 328 (1942).

In addition, the record shows that no appreciable time was lost from work for nearly two years after the accident and that there was no loss of wages or earnings due to the accident. We are satisfied that the board, in determining whether a disability in fact existed, considered the requirement set forth in Sekel v. Iagenamma, 170 Pa. Superior Ct. 621 (1952), on page 624, to the effect that "the disability contemplated by our compensation law is the loss of earning power as the result of any injury”.

The court in the Sekel case, supra, had this to say also, on page 624: "In the instant case since claimant lost no time from his work he did not suffer any disability within the meaning of the Workmen's Compensation Law." 

It is clear there is no basis for upsetting the finding of fact as to disability as determined by the board. 

The fact there was an accident in the course of employment and that claimant is now partially disabled due to that accident, is insufficient, under our law, to support an award of compensation. There must be a disability within the one-year period after the accident, since disability is the basis for an award. See Act of June 2, 1915, P. L. 736, sec. 306, as amended, 77 PS §512. Whether or not the claim is sustainable as the result of an accident must first be determined; then follows the question of injury for the purpose of determining the amount of compensation. A claim for personal injury arises simultaneously and is complete with the happening of an accident. There may be accidents without compensable injuries, but the converse is not true. See Lewis v. Carnegie Illinois Steel Corp., 159

Pa. Superior Ct. 226 (1946). 

It is regrettable, in our opinion, that recovery must be denied in this case, but the solution lies with the legislature, not the courts. See Poster v. Hill Co., 30 D. & C. 657 (1937).

The referee's finding of fact no. 10 and conclusion of law no. 3 must be set aside, since that issue was disposed of by this court's previous opinion. In all other respects the order of the referee as affirmed by the board will be affirmed. Order 

And now, to wit, June 8, 1954, finding of fact no. 10 and conclusion of law no. 3 of the referee's award of February 

25, 1953, as affirmed by the Workmen's Compensation Board in its opinion of December 19, 1954, are set aside. In all other respects the opinion of the board is affirmed, and the appeal of claimant is dismissed.

1954] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD 

BROCK, etc., v. McCUNE, etc.

Workmen's compensation—Accidental injury in course of employ ment—Employe of automobile agency—Attendance at meeting at employer's request after hours of work-Death from drinking poisoned wine.

An award of compensation will be affirmed where it appeared that decedent was employed by defendant as an automobile mechanic, that on a certain evening he went from his home to defendant's place of business where the personnel had been summoned to review a training film dealing with a new car model, that refreshments were served there, that decedent asked for some wine whereupon defendant's manager poured some contents from a jug into a glass from which decedent took a swallow, immediately became quite ill and died on the following day, subsequent analysis of the contents of the jug showing that it contained a poison. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Foust. District No. 9, claim petition no. 127,388, accident no. 5,901,259, A-38968. Affirmed, June 2, 1954.

Hardware Mutual Casualty Company, insurance carrier. 

Avra N. Pershing, Jr., for claimant. 

Gustav M. Berg, for defendant.

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

This case involves a death claim. It is before the board upon appeal by defendant, John McCune, trading as McCune 

Motor Company, from the order of Referee Foust, awarding compensation to claimant, Josephine Brock, for the death of her husband, Fred Brock, on February 20, 1952.

Decedent was employed by defendant as an automobile mechanic. The testimony of claimant and her supporting witnesses establishes that decedent came home from work about 5:30 p.m. on February 19, 1952, ate his evening meal and about 7:30 p.m. left for defendant's place of business, where the personnel had been summoned to view a training film dealing with the new Ford automobile. Refreshments, consisting of beer, soft drinks and sandwiches, provided by

the management, were to be served. There were five or six persons present in one end of the show room when the picture started. Decedent asked for some wine and James 

McCune, son of defendant and manager of the automobile agency, brought down a gallon jug which had been stored on the back porch of his upstairs apartment. McCune poured about half a tumbler of the jug contents, supposed to be "dago red" wine, from which Brock took a swallow, which he vomited. Another employe, Wilmer C. Auckerman, also took a drink but spit it out. He did not swallow any of it. 

Brock became quite ill and Dr. Bushager was summoned. 

The doctor had decedent taken by ambulance to the Westmoreland Hospital at Greensburg, where he died about noon on the following day.

McCune reported the matter to the State Police, who referred him to the district attorney's office where the jug was delivered the next day. The "wine" had been given to 

McCune at Christmas time 1951 and had been untouched until the night of the film showing. McCune testified that when the jug contents were served, he thought it was wine. 

Auckerman could not describe the taste of the liquid but said that it burned his mouth, and he kept rinsing his mouth for several hours for relief. There is cumulative and corroborative evidence from several employes as to the events which occurred after decedent partook of the glass contents and it would serve no useful purpose to recount that testimony in detail. It is convincing that the effects upon Brock were immediate; he vomited, complained of his throat and chest burning and that his stomach hurt. He was hospitalized within an hour or so after the drinking. About 3 or 4 a.m., decedent was paralyzed on the left side. It was necessary to shackle him to the bed. James Mitchell, a fellow employe, testified under cross-examination that he heard decedent say, prior to the drinking, that "he didn't feel too good". Claimant testified that her decedent enjoyed excellent health prior to the meeting at defendant's place of business. A brother, John Brock, stated that decedent was "never sick a day in his life".

Merle Musick, a Westmoreland County detective, deliv-

1954] 

 ered the jug to the Allegheny County Crime Laboratory for analysis. Another detective, Edward Gordon, took over the investigation on February 21, 1952, and took possession of the jug at that time from M. R. Watts, the chemist at the crime laboratory. A "short test" was made in the presence of the witness and an analysis reported of "cadmium". The chemist made a written report at a later date, stating that the jug contained an "alkaline solution of a cadmium compound". A further test was then requested from Dr. F. A. 

Taylor at the Allegheny Hospital in Pittsburgh and the jug was delivered to him. Gordon further testified that a sample of decedent's stomach content was obtained from the Westmoreland Hospital and also delivered to Dr. Taylor for analysis. Dr. Taylor's report stated: 

"Material in gallon bottle contains 3.8 lb. Arsenic Trioxide 

(in form of sodium arsenite) per gallon. Stomach contents contain 0.054 m.g. Arsenic as metal."

Dr. Albert Jablonsky, called by claimant, testified that he first saw decedent about 4 a.m. on February 20, 1952 and found the patient in shock and had a left-sided hemiplegia (paralysis). Decedent had a markedly distended abdomen, very low blood pressure of 80/60 and a complete blockage of urine flow. Following the death, an autopsy was per formed by the hospital resident physician. Dr. Jablonsky was present and observed at the post mortem a marked reddening along the greater curvature of the stomach and the inner lining of the esophagus was also reddened. The contents of the stomach were aspirated for further study. 

The doctor was of the opinion that the cause of death was metallic poisoning by ingestion. The doctor stated arsenic trioxide was such a metal and is one of the common sources of arsenic poisoning and that cadmium poisoning is similar to arsenic in its action. The doctor stated that the effect of a rapid poisoning is shock and toxic action rather than destruction of tissue, which is the case in a slow poisoning. 

He did not believe that cerebral hemorrhage alone was the cause of all the symptoms and chain of events that took place in this case. 

Claimant called Dr. J. M. Mayhew, a pathologist, at the

Westmoreland Hospital. The doctor was absent from the hospital at the time the autopsy was performed upon the body of decedent, but he did make microscopic examination of sections of certain organs. In answer to a hypothetical question as to cause of death, the doctor gave as his professional opinion that death was due to arsenic poisoning. The doctor testified: "Q. Do I understand the usual effect and evidences of heavy metal poisoning appear when death is slow rather than sudden and fast? "A. That is correct. "Q. What is the nature of the-what form does it take, we will say, the reaction to a very heavy dose, we will call it, of metal poisoning? How does that affect the body or parts of the body when death is sudden and the effects are so overwhelming as to preclude the slow death? "A. If the death is sudden, the findings of kidney damage, the findings of liver damage, the findings of gastrointestinal irritation do not occur, as they are due to the elimination by these organs of the body. Therefore, if the death is sudden and before the body has had time to eliminate the poisoning, these usual effects are not seen. "Q. What part of the system is affected in sudden death? "A. Sudden deaths are due to central nervous system or brain involvement."

Under cross-examination, Dr. Mayhew testified that, in his opinion, death was so sudden that the metal had not been eliminated by the kidneys or intestinal tract so that there were no findings present under the microscope and the tissues examined showed no evidence of arsenical poisoning. The doctor further stated that the patient's inability to urinate was additional proof that he was in a deep state of shock due to poisoning, because the kidneys continue to function in the presence of a cerebral hemorrhage or cerebral thrombosis. The lethal effect of the poison is in the absorption into the lymphatics and blood vessels. 

Dr. Fred A. Taylor, a biochemist at the Allegheny General Hospital for 30 years, testified in behalf of claimant that he made an analysis examination of the content of the

1954] 

 stomach and the gallon jug. He stated that he made qualitative and quantitative examination of the jug contents and found "3.8 lbs. arsenic compound. A.S.T.03. Arsenic dissolved in sodium oxide. A very strong solution." Dr. Taylor testified: "Q. What is the fluid used for that you found in this jug? "A. Well, the common use for such fluid would be as an insecticide. You would take a small amount of it and put it in gallons of water and spray vegetable matter to remove insects. "Q. Even to kill insects it would be diluted. "A. It would be diluted many times!" The doctor's analysis of the stomach content showed 0.054 mg. of arsenic. The chemist stated that vomiting would remove the arsenic so that very little arsenic would be left in the stomach. He explained that this was significant. He testified: "Q. Highly significant? "A. Yes. Even though the amount found was lethal, this amount that was still in the stomach content did him no harm. It was what he absorbed that did the damage.”

Defendant called M. R. Watts, a chemist who was employed by the Allegheny County Crime Laboratory at the time of decedent's death. He testified that he made a hurried analysis of the jug contents because of limitation of time. He testified: "Q. What type of test did you make? "A. I ran several tests. First, I wanted to see whether it did have any alcohol in it, and then I ran some other tests to give me some idea what it was, whether of an alkalin or acid nature. And then I ran a test to determine what the solution was made-up of and started running the customary grouping of metals and we come to the conclusion it was a solution of cadmium and that is as far as I went. I was sort of satisfied with the analysis and my reaction of cadmium, and I made no further test that way. I gave them some idea what to look for and where to look for it. I said, 'If you need some additional help I will be glad to help you but I can't spend my full time on it."

By "hurried examination", the chemist stated that he meant "just the ordinary qualitative examination" and he did not go into the quantitative matter. Under cross-examination, the witness admitted that cadmium, arsenic and mercury are all poisons that are analogous. 

Dr. Paul Gross, a pathologist called by defendant, testified that he reviewed the post mortem findings and noted that the weight of the heart and liver were excessive, which he deemed significant. In reply to a hypothetical question as to the cause of death, Dr. Gross testified that the probable cause of death was a cerebral hemorrhage, although he did not exclude the possibility of a cerebral thrombosis without hemorrhage. The doctor based his conclusions on the fact that the patient had a complete hemiplegia prior to death. 

Under cross-examination, the doctor testified that it is possible to explain the vomiting "on the central nervous system effect such as may have occurred in destruction of the brain". He admitted that strain of vomiting and reaction to a poison could produce a rupture of blood vessels in the brain resulting in a paralysis. He thought that the onset of the symptoms when decedent took some of the liquid in his mouth was a mere coincidence and that the burning sensation in mouth and throat, abdominal cramps, nausea and vomiting indicated the probability that the brain was being destroyed. Dr. Gross testified: "Q. Referring again to the microscopic copy of autopsy that you have read, will you state whether or not there was disclosed in the autopsy indication of sodium arsenite ingestion?"

By Mr. Pershing: 

"Objected to as being improper examination."

By the Referee: 

"Take the answer for what it is worth and note an exception. "A. I see no evidence for the conclusion that arsenic poisoning was present." 

This case poses a single question: Was decedent's death the result of an accident within the contemplation and in tendment of The Workmen's Compensation Act?

1954] 

Decedent was upon the employer's premises at the time he became ill at the request or direction of the employer, in the latter's interests and in furtherance of his employment. 

The liquid refreshment, which resulted fatally, was furnished by decedent's superior and served by him. Our appellate courts have consistently interpreted the word "accident" under the act in its usual, ordinary and popular sense and defined by Webster as "an event that takes place without one's foresight or expectation; an undesigned, sudden and unexpected event": Lacey v. Washburn & Williams Co., 309 Pa. 574, 164 Atl. 724.

The time of the accident is definite and precise. It stems from the sip of the liquid in the jug, setting in motion a series of events, which, without interruption, culminated in the demise. The testimony is wholly persuasive that the jug contained a poisonous substance, which decedent and others mistakenly believed to be wine, that decedent absorbed some of the deadly substance, which caused the death. 

Nor was it necessary for claimant to show the exact cause of a mistake or accident: Rittenberg v. Abbott Laboratories, 158 Pa. Superior Ct. 400.

We are satisfied that the vomiting, throat and abdominal pains, as well as the subsequent paralysis, are directly and causally connected to absorbing the deleterious liquid. We believe that the jug of wine and sample of the contents of stomach analyzed are the one involved in this case and that the analyses establish the presence of the same dangerous substance in both samples. Although the specific amount of arsenic found by Dr. Taylor was minimal, we accept the views of claimant's experts that the real damage occurred through exposure and absorption in the blood stream. We see no basis for accepting defendant's theory that a cerebral hemorrhage or thrombosis was the responsible cause of the death. Such a conclusion, in the light of the record, would be contrary to good sense and sound reason.

We hereby affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and award. 

The appeal of defendant is dismissed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD MINAKA v. HUDSON COAL COMPANY Workmen's compensation—Occupational disease—Anthraco-silicosis -Heart condition as concurrent cause of disability—Act of January 

2, 1952.

A referee's award denying compensation for disability alleged to have resulted from anthraco-silicosis will be reversed, where there was medical testimony that claimant was totally disabled from anthracosilicosis and a heart condition, since under the Act of January 2, 1952, P. L. 1811, occupational disease need not be the sole cause of disability. 

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Donlan. 

District No. 3, claim petition no. 130,132, accident no. 

6,069,243, A-38910. Reversed, June 2, 1954. 

Thomas L. Kennedy, Jr., for claimant. 

Francis D. Mahon, for defendant.

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

This case is before the board upon appeal by claimant, Peter Minaka, from the order of Referee Donlan disallowing compensation in an anthraco-silicosis claim. The question involved is whether claimant is totally disabled from anthraco-silicosis within the meaning and intendment of the act.

Dr. T. G. Killeen, claimant's witness, testified that he examined claimant on March 3, 1953, and found that he was totally disabled due to anthraco-silicosis. X-ray interpretation of Dr. Harry Kirban to Dr. Killeen on August 6, 1953, stated: "There is a nodular infiltration and densities throughout both lungs, indicating a third stage anthraco-silicosis with moderate emphysema. Heart is aortic in type and normal in size. There is slight accentuation of upper outer border of left ventricle. Ribs appear normal." 

An X-ray report of Dr. McGuire on August 12, 1953, stated a finding of third-stage anthraco-silicosis. Dr. Killeen testified, under cross-examination, that he did not have

1954] 

 an X-ray taken on his initial examination because claimant "was so obviously disabled and so obviously anthraco-silicotic that there wasn't an atom of doubt in my mind". He stated that there is definite cardiac pathology in a weakness of the muscles of the heart, which he attributed to the silicosis. The doctor testified that he gave claimant an exercise tolerance test with very unfavorable results. 

Dr. Milton J. Goldstein, called by defendant, Hudson Coal

Company, testified that he examined claimant on May 8, 1953, including exercise tolerance test, fluoroscopy, X-rays and electrocardiogram. The doctor's conclusions were early third-stage anthraco-silicosis, together with coronary artery disease and hypertension. Claimant, called as for crossexamination, testified that he was laid off on April 22, 1952, because he was unable to do his regular work, although he requested lighter work which was unavailable. Claimant stated that he received unemployment compensation benefits from April 30, 1952, to November 10, 1952 (26 weeks) and a miner's pension of $100 monthly since February, 1953. 

Minaka stated that he worked about four weeks as a bartender after he ceased defendant's employment but was unable to do the work.

In view of the conflicting medical opinion, the referee requested the board to appoint an impartial physician and Dr. Edward P. Swartz was so appointed. The doctor examined claimant on September 30, 1953. His pertinent findings and conclusions were as follows: "This patient has early third-stage anthraco-silicosis as well as myocardial damage. His left ventricle is hypertrophied. I believe that he is totally disabled as a result of the combination of these diseases."

The doctor testified that his electrocardiographic tracings, history of severe pain in the substernal area in the fall of 1951, and enlargment of the left ventricle, prompted a diagnosis of a myocardial infarction. In regard to claimant's very poor results in a maximum breathing test, Dr. 

Swartz testified: "Q. And that's due to his condition in the lungs, Doctor, his silicotic condition?

"A. Well, it's due to his anthraco-silicosis plus this left ventricular damage he has. It's impossible to separate the function of the heart and lungs in an individual when you subject him to these tests, because both the heart and the lungs are used in performing these tests. "Q. And both being used, Doctor, it's impossible for you to determine whether it is due primarily or actually due to the silicotic condition, or to whatever other condition that may be present, isn't that a fact? "A. My conclusion has to be that it is due to a combination of both of these elements, namely, I should say there is no doubt whatsoever that the function of his lungs is impaired as result of his anthraco-silicosis; there is also no doubt that his breathing is also impaired as result of his myocardial damage, and I am unable to separate those two. "Q. So that the presence of silicosis in this man is sufficient in itself to disable him, isn't it? "A. Well, I can't answer that question because, as I said before, in performing these tests both the heart and the lungs are used. "Q. So that you are unable to give an opinion on that, Doctor? . "A. I can't separate them. All I can say is that this man is unquestionably totally disabled and that his disability is due to a combination of his myocardial damage and his third stage anthraco-silicosis." 

Under questioning by defendant's counsel, Dr. Swartz testified candidly that he did not know what substantial degree the myocardial damage contributed to claimant's disability but that "it definitely is contributing".

The medical testimony is clear that claimant is totally disabled due to an early third stage anthraco-silicosis and a cardiac condition. Dr. Swartz, the impartial physician, was unwilling and unable to make any apportionment of the effect or weight of the two ailments in the disability picture. 

We doubt if any physician can say with any reasonable degree of accuracy how much disability stems from the

1954] 

 silicosis and how much emanates from the heart condition in a situation of this kind. Under these circumstances, is claimant entitled to compensation? Prior to the Act of 

January 2, 1952, P. L. 1811, the answer would be an unequivocal "no". The act in question eliminated the word "solely" as a limitation on silicosis, anthraco-silicosis or asbestosis as the cause of total disability. Since the legislature deleted the word "solely" as a prime requisite, it is clear to us that disability caused by the named occupational disease need no longer be the sole agency or instrumentality; that the presence of a concurrent ailment will not be a fatal bar to a meritorious claim. Otherwise, the action of the legislature would be nullified and meaningless. Where, as here, the admitted disability is caused, in a substantial measure, to the silicosis, we are of the opinion that an award is indicated, particularly in the light of the humane purposes of compensation legislation: Roschak v. Vulcan Iron Works, 

157 Pa. Superior Ct. 227; Nickolay v. Hudson Coal Co., 164

Pa. Superior Ct. 550. It is apparent from the referee's seventh finding of fact (that total disability is not due to anthraco-silicosis alone) that he proceeded upon the mistaken premise that the silicosis had to be the sole cause of disability, which is no longer the rule under the amendatory Act of 1952. We are of the opinion that the instant case is compensable.

We hereby vacate and set aside the referee's seventh finding of fact and in lieu thereof, we substitute the following 

Finding of Fact 

7. We find as a fact that claimant is totally disabled as a result of the combination of an early third-stage anthracosilicosis and a cardiac condition and that the anthracosilicosis contributes substantially to claimant's total disability and is a cause thereof.

As amended, the referee's findings of fact are affirmed. 

We hereby affirm the referee's first conclusion of law. We vacate and set aside the referee's second conclusion of law and substitute therefor, the following

Conclusions of Law 

2. Having found that claimant's total disability is caused by anthraco-silicosis within the intendment of the act, claimant is entitled to compensation. 

3. Since claimant's disability occurred only after an exposure of five or more years to a silica hazard defendant and the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania are jointly liable for compensation payments in the proportions specified in the act.

We hereby vacate and set aside the referee's order of disallowance and substitute therefor, the following Award

Defendant, Hudson Coal Company, and the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania are ordered and directed to pay to claimant, Peter Minaka, compensation for total disability at the weekly rate of $30 per week beginning April 23, 1953, and continuing thereafter, within the limitations of the act, but not to exceed in the aggregate, the sum of $6,500; defendant to pay 60 percent of said compensation, or $18 per week, and the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania to pay 40 percent of compensation, or $12 per week. 

Defendant is ordered and directed to pay to Dr. Edward 

P. Swartz, Connell Bldg., Scranton 3, impartial physician, the sum of $150.

The above award is to bear interest in accordance with the provisions of section 410 of the Occupational Disease Act.

The appeal of claimant is sustained.

1954] 

 COMMON PLEAS COURT NO. 5 OF PHILADELPHIA COUNTY BROWN, etc., v. ROSS MOTOR LINES et al. 

Workmen's compensation-Pennsylvania employe-Employment of nonresident truck driver by Pennsylvania employer to operate a truck outside the State.

A claim for compensation must be denied where it appeared that claimant's son, a resident of the State of South Carolina was employed by defendant, a Pennsylvania corporation as a truck driver hauling produce to Florida, that he was instructed to take a load of produce from New Jersey to Florida and was killed in the State of Georgia while on his way there, in the absence of any evidence that a major portion of the deceased's employment took place in Pennsylvania. 

Appeal by defendants from award of the Workmen's 

Compensation Board. C. P. No. 5 of Philadelphia County, 

March term, 1954, no. 58. Reversed. June 1, 1954. 

J. A. M. McCarthy and H. R. Mayer, for plaintiff. 

R. C. Duffy, for defendants.

SMITH, P. J.: 

This case comes before the court on an appeal by defend ants from the award by the Workmen's Compensation 

Board. 

The facts show that on Sunday, March 9, 1952, William

Thomas Brown, the operator of the truck of defendant, Rose Motor Lines, was killed in an accident caused by the collision of two trucks at a place south of Savannah, Ga., on the Orgeechee Road. A fatal claim petition was filed by 

Agnes K. Brown, the mother of deceased. Defendant and its insurance carrier, Pennsylvania Threshermen and Farmers Mutual Casualty Insurance Company, filed an answer to the said petition in which they denied all the material averments of the claim and moved for the dismissal of the petition on the ground that the fatal accident occurred in the State of Georgia; that decedent was a resident of the State of South Carolina; that the employment of deceased was not that of a Pennsylvania employe, and that his employ-

ment did not come within the scope or operation of the 

Workmen's Compensation Law of the State of Pennsylvania. 

Defendant is a Pennsylvania corporation having its principal office at 134 Walnut Street, Philadelphia. The facts show that deceased called the Philadelphia office of defendant from Rosehill, N. C., with respect to a job with defendant and came to Philadelphia on February 25, 1952, where he was hired by defendant as a truck driver, and he received his hauling instructions which were to transport produce to Florida. Deceased arrived in Philadelphia on or about 

March 5, 1952, and received instructions from defendant to go to Linden, N. J., and there take up a load of produce which he was to deliver to Miami, Fla. While on his way to Florida on March 19, 1952, he had an accident and was killed.

When the case came before the referee, he found as a fact at a hearing where defendants were not present: "That on March 9, 1952, decedent was not a Pennsylvania employe, very small portion of work was in Pennsylvania.” 

The referee concluded as a matter of law: "That claimant, Agnes E. Brown, mother of decedent, William Thomas Brown, should not receive compensation, since decedent was not a Pennsylvania employe, and further, since a very small portion of his work was in Pennsylvania."

The compensation was denied and the said claim petition was dismissed.

Claimant appealed from these findings of fact and conclu sions of law to the Workmen's Compensation Board. A 

hearing was then held before the Workmen's Compensation 

Board in Philadelphia on February 10, 1954, where the board in its decision reversed the referee and held that claimant was entitled to compensation at the partially dependent rate.

The board found, inter alia, that the deceased was a Pennsylvania employe at the time of his death and that the accident was covered by the Workmen's Compensation stat ute. Paragraph 3 of the findings of the board state: 

"Decedent had arrived in Philadelphia on or about March

1954] 

5, 1952, from a trip to Florida, serviced his truck at Philadelphia and received instructions from his employer to go to Linden, N. J., and to deliver a load of produce to Miami, Fla." 

The compensation board found as a fact:

Paragraph 7. "The defendant-employer's place of business was in Pennsylvania and claimant was a Pennsylvania employe." 

Paragraph 8. "Decedent's employment required him to make a temporary trip outside of Pennsylvania for a period not in excess of one week at the time he was fatally injured while in the course of his employment."

The primary question before us is, was the decision of the compensation board supported by any proper findings of fact or by the law of Pennsylvania. If it was, then, of course, the board, after a hearing, had the right to reverse the referee: Powell v. Hills Garage, 150 Pa. Superior Ct. 

17; Forsythe v. John F. Casey Co., 155 Pa. Superior Ct. 185.

We believe, however, that a more important matter is concerned in this case. There is no evidence before the compensation board to justify a conclusion of law that the major portion of deceased's employment took place in the 

Commonwealth of Pennsylvania. There is no doubt that defendant's place of business is in Philadelphia, and that the contract between them and deceased was made in Philadelphia. It appears that there is no provision for the payment of extraterritorial claim in the prior acts of the 

Workmen's Compensation Law, but in the Act of June 2, 1915, P. L. 736, as amended, it was provided:

"This Act shall apply to all accidents occurring within this Commonwealth, irrespective of the place where the contract of hiring was made and shall not apply to any accident occurring outside of the Commonwealth except to Pennsylvania employees whose duties require them to go temporarily beyond the territorial limits of the Commonwealth, not over 90 days, when said employees are performing services for employers whose place of business is within the Commonwealth."

The law seems to be that deceased does not have to be a

resident of the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, but it seems clear that he must be a Pennsylvania employe in order for his claimant to qualify. What makes him a Pennsylvania employe? The mere contract as made in this State does not make him so. The Act of Assembly does not expressly define the term "Pennsylvania employe". In the case of Stewart v. Thomas Earle & Sons, Inc., 150 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 591, 592, where Bock v. Frampton & Co., 105

Pa. Superior Ct. 380, 382, was affirmed, Judge Kenworthey 

"The Act does not expressly define 'Pennsylvania em ploye'. But in Bock v. Frampton & Co., 105 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 380, 382, 161 A. 762, 763, this court (Gawthrop, J.) said: 'Manifestly, this amendment was intended by the lawmakers to extend the benefits of the statute to persons normally performing services within the Commonwealth, for an employer whose place of business is within the Commonwealth but who happened to be injured while they had gone temporarily beyond the limits of the Commonwealth in the performance of their duties. It seems clear that the term "Pennsylvania employes" refers only to employes who perform the major portion of their services within the 

Commonwealth.'" (Italics supplied.)

This definition has had the approval of the Supreme 

Court in Salkind v. Pa. Threshermen & Farmers Mut. Cas. 

Ins. Co., 335 Pa. 326, 6 A. 2d 301, where it was said: "It has been held that the term 'Pennsylvania employes' as used in the Act of April 29, 1929, P. L. 853, refers only to employes who perform the major portion of their services within the Commonwealth, and not to every employe who is working for a Pennsylvania employer." 

In Duskin v. Pa. Central Airlines Corp., 167 F. 2d, 727, it was held: "Duskin was not a 'Pennsylvania employee'. The provision in the state statute extending coverage of the Act to employees who were injured in accidents in the course of employment while temporarily outside of the state, was enacted as an amendment to the statute in 1929, and in construing it, the Supreme Court of Pennsylvania held that

1954] 

 the term 'Pennsylvania employee', as used therein, 'refers only to employees who perform the major portion of their services within the Commonwealth, and not to every employee who is working for a Pennsylvania employer'. Salkind v. Pennsylvania Threshermen & Farmers Mut. Casualty Ins. Co., 335 Pa. 326, 6 A. 2d 301, 302. 'Manifestly, this amendment was intended by the lawmakers to extend the benefits of the statute to persons normally performing services within the Commonwealth for an employer whose place of business is within the Commonwealth, but who happened to be injured while they had gone temporarily beyond the limits of the Commonwealth in the performance of their duties.' Lutz v. State Workmen's Insurance Fund et al., 124 Pa. Superior Ct. 149, 188 A. 364."

Thus, the mere question as argued of whether deceased was only temporarily outside the State for a period of less than 90 days is not controlling. The question is, did the major part of his employment take place in the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania?

We do believe as a matter of law that the compensation board was in error in its finding in paragraph 7 that deceased was a Pennsylvania employe just because his employer's place of business was in Pennsylvania. There was no justification for such a finding under the facts as developed.

Order 

And now, to wit, June 1, 1954, the order and award of the Workmen's Compensation Board is set aside and the judgment is hereby entered for defendants. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD SHICK V. STATE WORKMEN'S INSURANCE FUND et al. 

Workmen's compensation-Second injury resulting in total disability-Crediting employer for payments made for first injury- 

Section 306.1 of The Workmen's Compensation Act.

Where claimant sustained an injury resulting in the loss of use of his right hand, having sustained an amputation of his left hand in a prior accident, so that he was entitled to an award of total disability and to the payment of additional compensation for a subsequent injury under section 306.1 of The Workmen's Compensation Act, for the re mainder of the 500-week period, the referee erred in granting credit for the payments made to claimant for his first injury; in such circumstances credit should be given for payments made pursuant to section 306 (c) of the act only as to the second injury.

Appeal by claimant from award of Referee Carmalt, District No. 8, claim petition no. 131,467, accident no. 

5,595,276, A-39087. Reversed. August 11, 1954.

State Workmen's Insurance Fund, insurance carrier. 

J. W. Stephenson, for claimant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for defendant.

KNOLL, Chairman: 

This is an appeal by claimant from the order of award of Referee Gladstone A. Carmalt, District No. 8. 

On June 1, 1950, while acting within the course of his employment, claimant sustained injury by accident. By virtue of the same, compensation agreement no. 5,595,276 was entered into between claimant and defendant, providing for the payment of compensation at the rate of $25 per week for a period of 160 weeks. The agreement described the accident and injury as: "While claimant was cleaning fan motor, the guard loosened and his right hand was drawn into the fan, resulting in the amputation of the right thumb, index, middle, ring and little fingers."

On February 12, 1951, a supplemental agreement was entered into between the parties, setting forth that claimant was suffering from the loss of use of his right hand and providing for the payment of compensation at the rate of $25 per week for a period of 175 weeks.

On September 11, 1953, claimant filed a petition, averring that he was now totally disabled as a result of the injuries

1954] 

 heretofore referred to and as a result of the injuries sustained by him on June 17, 1917. To this petition a responsive answer was filed by the State Workmen's Insurance 

Fund, as custodian of the Second Injury Reserve Fund, denying the allegations of the said petition. 

The matter was duly referred to Referee Carmalt before whom testimony was taken. The referee, by decision of 

February 1, 1954, found as a fact that, as a result of the accident of June 17, 1917, claimant sustained amputation of his left hand; that, as a result of the accident of June 1, 1950, claimant was suffering from the loss of use of his right hand, and that, as of October 15, 1953, claimant has been totally and permanently disabled. The referee thereupon awarded to claimant compensation for total disability, credit to be given for payments heretofore made to the claimant pursuant to the provisions of section 306 (c) of the act. From this ruling claimant has appealed. 

We have made a careful study of the record in this case and are of the opinion that the ruling of the referee should be reversed.

We are of the opinion that the testimony clearly establishes that claimant is totally disabled within the meaning of The Workmen's Compensation Act and, by reason thereof, is entitled to the payment of compensation, and that there should be credited, as against the said award, payments made to claimant pursuant to the provisions of section 

306 (c) as a result of the occurrence of June 1, 1950. We believe, however, that the referee was in error in awarding credit as to the payments made to claimant by virtue of the amputation of claimant's left hand arising out of the accident of June 17, 1917.

Section 306.1 of the act, which provides for the payment of additional compensation where total disability results through a subsequent injury, provides as follows: "After the cessation of payments by the employer for the period of weeks prescribed in Clause (c) thereof, for the subsequent injury, additional compensation shall be paid during the continuance of total disability, at the weekly compensation rate applicable for total disability, for the

remainder of the five hundred week period provided in 

Clause (a) hereof. . . ." (Italics supplied.)

It is to be noted that this section of the act provides for the payment of additional compensation where total disability occurs as a result of a second injury, which payments are to begin after the cessation of payments by the employer pursuant to the provisions of clause (c) for the second injury. It is to be further noted that no reference is made as to the payments made to the claimant pursuant to section 306 (c) of the act as to the first injury. It is, therefore, our opinion that it was the intent of the legislature that claimant receive payment for total disability after the cessation of the payments pursuant to section 306 (c) of the act for the second injury and that where an award is made for total disability, credit should be given for payments made pursuant to section 306 (c) of the act only as to the subsequent or second injury. We are of the opinion that the legislature did not intend that credit be likewise given for the payments made pursuant to section 306 (c) of the act as to the first injury. It is, therefore, our opinion that the referee erred in granting credit as to the payments made to claimant for the injury sustained by him on June 

17, 1917.

We, therefore, set aside the referee's tenth finding of fact, third conclusion of law and order of award and, in lieu thereof, substitute the following:

Findings of Fact 

10. We find as a fact from a preponderance of the testimony that claimant is entitled to the payment of compensation for total disability for a period of 500 weeks from the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania under the terms and provisions of the Second Injury Reserve Act, credit to be given for payments heretofore made to claimant as a result of compensation agreement no. 5,595,276 as modified. 

Conclusions of Law 

Since claimant is totally disabled, he is entitled to compensation pursuant to the provisions of section 306 (a) of the act, credit to be given for payments heretofore made to

1954] 

 claimant by virtue of the injury sustained by claimant on 

June 1, 1950.

Order 

Compensation is herewith awarded to claimant, Doyle

Shick, for total disability and against defendant, the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, Department of Labor and In dustry, Bureau of Workmen's Compensation and State Workmen's Insurance Fund, representing the Second In jury Reserve Fund, at the rate of $25 per week, beginning October 15, 1953, and continuing thereafter for a period of 325 weeks, or a total sum of $8,125. 

This award to bear interest at the rate of six percent per annum on all deferred payments in accordance with the provisions of section 410 of the act. 

No bill of costs filed.

The remaining findings of fact and conclusions of law of the referee are herewith affirmed. 

The appeal of claimant is sustained.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD FRANK V. STATE WORKMEN'S INSURANCE FUND et al. 

Workmen's compensation—Occupational disease-Tuberculosis in occupation of nursing-X-ray technician handling tubercular patients 

-Liability of employer and Commonwealth. 

1. Claimant an X-ray technician in a psychiatric hospital was entitled to compensation for disability resulting from tuberculosis where it appeared that claimant's duty was to take X-rays of all patients, including tubercular patients, and where she contracted her disease from such patients. 

2. Where claimant's disability from tuberculosis resulted from her exposure to tubercular patients while she was employed for a period of less than five years, the employer alone was liable; section 308 (a) providing for liability of the Commonwealth jointly with the employer expressly provides that the disease must have developed to the point of disablement after the exposure of five or more years.

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Carmalt, specially presiding in the 4th District. Claim petition no. 

132,372, accident no. 6172382, A-39423. Affirmed as amended, October 18, 1954.

State Workmen's Insurance Fund, insurance carrier. 

Philip J. Franzese, for claimant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for defendants.

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

Defendant, State Workmen's Insurance Fund, and the 

Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, have taken this appeal from the order of Referee Carmalt awarding compensation to claimant, Joan J. Frank, in an occupational disease case. 

We find the facts to be accurately stated in the brief filed in behalf of the State Fund as follows: 

"The claimant, Joan J. Frank, testified that she was employed at the Embreeville State Hospital from February 

1, 1951 to September 3, 1953 as an X-ray technician. Although this hospital is a psychiatric hospital, it had a few tubercular patients who were not then segregated from the other patients. It was claimant's duty to take X-rays of all the patients including the tubercular patients, and in doing so, she came into close contact with the latter. She not only took X-rays of patients who were sent to the X-ray room, but she also took X-rays in the wards with a mobile unit when the patients were not ambulatory. "The claimant further testified that in November, 1952, she was X-rayed and the result was negative. On September 

3, 1953, she was again X-rayed and this disclosed a tubercular lesion in the upper left lung. She was then advised by 

Dr. Jacob Sherson to give up her employment and take treatment for tuberculosis. She was admitted to the Rush 

Hospital where she was confined from September 9, 1953 to December 13, 1953. On the latter date, she was discharged from the hospital and then confined to her bed in her home approximately three months. "In response to questioning by the referee, the claimant

1954] 

 stated that she was never engaged in the occupation of nursing and that her occupation was strictly that of an X-ray technician. The claimant was cured and returned to work as an X-ray technician at the Embreeville State 

Hospital on April 1, 1954.”

Claimant was not represented by counsel. Dr. Jacob S. 

Sherson, claimant's witness, testified that he first examined claimant on September 1, 1953, and as a result of examination and X-ray, he made a diagnosis of "an active tuberculous lesion in the upper lobe". The doctor believed that claimant suffered the disease from handling tubercular patients in the hospital. The doctor testified: "Q. As an X-ray technician would she be required to do a number of things ordinarily done by a nurse in the hospital in order to take the X-ray and manage the uncoöperative patients? "A. Yes, she would be closer to the patient and spend a longer time." 

Under cross-examination, the doctor testified: "Q. Doctor, you said, being a mental hospital, these X-ray technicians perform some duties of a nurse, can you explain that? "A. A nurse in a general hospital would take a patient to the X-ray room and so the X-ray technician will take over, but in a mental hospital they do not respond to directions, you have to take them there and put the sheet around them and in that time they spit at you or cough, the danger is much greater."

Defendant offered no testimony. We are satisfied, as was the referee, that claimant became afflicted with the disease as a result of the tubercular patients which she handled. 

Is she entitled to compensation within the intendment of the amendatory Act of July 19, 1951, P. L. 1089? This is a legal question. Section 108 (m) of the act further defines an occupational disease to include: "Tuberculosis in the occupation of nursing in hospitals or sanitaria involving exposure to such disease."

The question narrows down then to whether claimant's X-ray technician duties constituted nursing as contemplated

by the law. In effect, defendant's contention urging a strict, literal and narrow interpretation of the statutory provision would have us apply the act only to registered nurses. We are of the opinion that the legislature intended a more liberal interpretation to anyone who performs nursing services and suffers tuberculosis as a consequence: Paul v. 

State Workmen's Insurance Fund, A-38686, 40 Dep. Rep. 

61. Our appellate courts have repeatedly held that compensation statutes are to be liberally interpreted in order to effect the humane purposes of the law: Roschak v. Vulcan 

Iron Works, 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 227; Nickolay v. Hudson 

Coal Co., 164 Pa. Superior Ct. 550.

In the instant case, we deem Dr. Sherson's testimony significant that in a mental hospital claimant worked with uncoöperative patients "so the technician has to spend more time to place the patient and put a restraining sheet around them to keep them that way", thus increasing the exposure to the active infection. We are in accord with the referee that the claim is compensable since it is clear that there is a causal connection between claimant's employment and the disease, and duties performed by claimant fall within purview of the statute, as nursing.

Defendant also contends that the referee erred in assessing compensation liability against the Commonwealth and defendant, and claims that the employer alone is liable for all compensation, if claimant is entitled to an award. There is merit in this contention. The applicable section of the act (section 308 (a) provides for liability upon the Commonwealth jointly with the employer in those cases when a claimant is afflicted with an occupational disease, "which developed to the point of disablement after an exposure of five or more years". Claimant's employment in the hospital was from February 1, 1951, to September 3, 1953. Defendant is the only one liable for compensation, and it is necessary to correct the award accordingly.

We hereby affirm the referee's findings of fact and conclusions of law. We vacate and set aside the referee's award and substitute the following:

1954] 

Award

Defendant company, Embreeville State Hospital, by its insurance carrier, the State Workmen's Insurance Fund, is ordered and directed to pay to claimant, Joan J. Frank, compensation at the rate of $32.50 per week (maximum under the act) for total disability from September 10, 1953, to April 1, 1954, when all total disability ceased, a period of 29 weeks, in the total sum of $942.50.

The above award is to bear interest on deferred payments in accordance with the provisions of section 410 of the 

Occupational Disease Act. 

Defendant is further ordered and directed to pay medical and hospital expenses incurred by claimant during the statutory period, in the sum of $166. Costs 

Claimant incurred the following costs: Dr. Jacob S. Sherson, one day $5.

The State Workmen's Insurance Fund, defendant, is directed to pay these costs.

The appeal of the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania is sustained.

The appeal of the State Workmen's Insurance Fund is dismissed.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA ELLIOTT v. UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION BOARD OF REVIEW 

Unemployment compensation-Leaving work without good cause— 

Refusing to comply with safety rules—Order to wear safety glasses— 

Termination of employment by employer.

Claimant quit his employment voluntarily and without good cause where it appeared that by a rule of his employer he was required to wear protective safety glasses during working hours, and where by reason of failure to do so, he was directed by his employer to check out and leave the plant and subsequently notified that his return to

work depended upon his willingness to wear glasses and abide by other safety rules in effect, following which he did not return to work. 

Appeal from Unemployment Compensation Board of Re view. Affirmed, 176 Pa. Superior Ct. 615. 

John C. Clemmens, with him Body, Muth, Rhoda & 

Stoudt, for appellant. 

William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, and Frank L. Truscott, Attorney General, for appellee. 

HIRT, J.:

The claimant in this unemployment compensation case had been in the employ of Bethlehem Cornwall Corporation, continuously since July 1950. The company rule for the protection of workmen, in effect at the time he was hired, provided: "Goggles must be worn when picking chipping, grinding, sledging, handling molten metals, or acids, or any other operation where flying particles might enter the eye. Proper colored goggles must be worn for cutting or welding."

On October 3, 1953, the company modified the above safety rule by "requiring that all employes must wear protective safety glasses during working hours". To induce compliance with the rule, as modified, the following penalty was prescribed: "Refusal to wear glasses at any time constitutes a flagrant violation and the employe must not be permitted to continue at work or return to work until he wears his glasses.” 

All employes had notice of the above change in the safety rule and of the penalty for violation of it. On October 12, 1953, claimant was directed to check out and leave the plant before the end of his shift when he refused to wear safety glasses. He had violated the rule on at least four previous occasions in the face of repeated warnings that he must comply with the regulation. Claimant, however, was not discharged and his contract of employment was not terminated. The action was merely a disciplinary

1954] 

 measure, in accordance with the provisions of the safety rule, imposed on claimant "for insubordination in refusing to put on safety glasses when requested to do so by his superior". He remained an employe of the company and his basic status as such was not affected by the disciplinary action. On October 15, 1953, the company by letter notified claimant that his return to work depended upon his willingness not only to wear safety glasses at all times during working hours but also to abide by all other safety rules in effect at the plant. The notice stated also that if "he did not return to his position by October 26, 1953, under the conditions stipulated he would be terminated as having left his employment voluntarily. By communications dated 

October 20th and October 22nd, claimant advised the employer he would not return under the conditions set forth by the employer and [he] did not return. Subsequent to 

October 26, 1953, the claimant's name was removed from the payrolls of the company."

We have quoted from the findings of the referee based upon testimony as to which there is no dispute. 

On October 23, 1953, claimant applied for unemployment compensation benefits. He subsequently lodged a grievance with his union, a local of United Steel Workers of America. 

The union filed a complaint in accordance with the procedure set up in its labor contract with the company, but in the midst of the proceedings withdrew the complaint and accepted the decision of the company. All of the facts to which we have referred are reflected in the findings of the referee based upon competent testimony. 

Because of claimant's failure to return to his work on or before October 26, 1953, in accordance with the company's notice to him, the referee ruled "that he left his employment voluntarily without good cause and must be disqualified under the provisions of section 402 (b) ." of the

Unemployment Compensation Law as last amended by the 

Act of May 23, 1949, P. L. 1738, 43 PS §802. The board on appeal, after hearing additional testimony, adopted the findings and conclusions of the Referee and affirmed the order denying benefits.

Section 402 (b) provides:

"An employe shall be ineligible for compensation for any week in which his unemployment is due to voluntarily leaving work without good cause." As to the test to be applied in determining whether there is good cause for voluntarily leaving work by an employe we have said: "The vitalizing element of good cause is good faith: 

Sturdevant Unemployment Compensation Case, 158 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 548, 45 A. 2d 898. There can be no good cause which does not rest in good faith": Brilhart Unemployment 

Compensation Case, 159 Pa. Superior Ct. 567, 49 A. 2d 260.

Claimant attempted to justify his refusal to wear safety glasses at all times during working hours on two grounds. 

When he was hired in 1950, the then operative safety rule required employes to wear glasses only when performing certain types of work and he contended that he was not bound by any subsequent modification of it. The contention is without merit. Within the terms of the company-union agreement, rules affecting employes at their work were subject to change by the employer at any time. The present rule must be regarded as having union approval as is indicated by the union's withdrawal of its complaint filed with the company on claimant's grievance, based on the enforcement of the present rule as to him. Claimant's second ground is equally without merit. He testified that he could not wear glasses at all times, stating, "they gave me headaches." According to his testimony he suffered these headaches "at least 50 times" during the three years he worked for the company and that they made him "generally sick". The difficulty with this contention is that the testimony does not establish that the glasses were the cause of the headaches. Both the company doctor and a doctor of claimant's choice found that his vision is normal. And the board adopted the finding and conclusion of the referee "that claimant has not offered any medical statement to the effect that wearing safety glasses actually caused his headaches".

The testimony in this case amply supports the conclusion that claimant in legal effect quit his employment voluntarily and without good cause on October 26, 1953; the order

1954] 

 denying compensation therefore is conclusive: Sauers Un employment Compensation Case, 168 Pa. Superior Ct. 373, 

77 A. 2d 891.

The fact that he, one month later on November 26, 1953, offered to return to his former employment and agreed to comply with all company rules, has no bearing on the result. He then had the status of one unemployed who was applying for work and, of course, the company was not obliged to hire him. 

Decision affirmed.
