SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA MUCHA v. M. L. BAYARD & COMPANY, INC., et al. (3) Workmen's compensation—Limitations-Eye injury-Promise by employer to pay for operation-Second promise more than three years later.

A claim for workmen's compensation was filed too late where it appeared that the injury occurred in 1941 when claimant was struck in the eye by a piece of metal, following which a cataract developed in the eye, that in 1946 his employer assured him that an operation would be taken care of, that claimant postponed the operation, that again in 1949 he was assured he would be taken care of, that when he finally decided to undergo an operation the insurance company refused to pay; in such circumstances where more than three years elapsed between the two declarations of the employer the right to file a claim petition expired.

Appeal from C. P. No. 1 of Philadelphia County. Re versed, 177 Pa. Superior Ct. 138. 

Paul H. Ferguson, for appellant.

Gregory G. Lagakos, Samuel Blank and Rappaport, Laga kos & Blank, for appellees.

GUNTHER, J.: 

This is an appeal by the employer from a workmen's compensation award granted claimant by the court below.

The referee found in favor of claimant, the board reversed the referee, and the court below reversed the board. 

Claimant, a machinist, was injured on October 14, 1941, when struck in the eye by a piece of metal. Two doctors determined that the accident caused a cataract in his eye. 

About a year later one of the doctors advised him to have an operation; the other felt he could wait longer for the cataract to develop. Claimant decided to postpone the operation and was assured by his employer that upon claimant's return from army service the employer would take care of the operation. Claimant then entered the army, returning to work, upon his discharge, in April, 1946. At that time he was totally blind in the injured eye and the medical testimony proved that his cataract was then ready for an operation. He was again assured by his employer that the operation would be taken care of. Claimant postponed action, however, for several years. Again in 1949 he was assured he would be taken care of. When he finally decided to undergo operation, the insurance company refused to pay and a claim petition was filed in March, 1950. 

The board found the above facts but concluded that the employer's representations related only to hospital and medical services and not to compensation and that claimant had, therefore, filed his claim petition too late. The court below, accepting the board's findings of fact, concluded as a matter of law that the employer was estopped from pleading the statute of limitations by its conduct in lulling claimant into a position of inaction. 

The limitation section of The Workmen's Compensation 

Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 520, sec. 315, 77 PS 602, provides that "all claims for compensation shall be forever barred, unless, within one year after the accident, the parties shall have filed a petition . one of

This is not a pure statute of limitations but one which qualifies the granting of a substantive right by a condition as to the time within which action to enforce it may be maintained: Lewis v. Carnegie-Ill. Steel Corp., 159 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 226, 48 A. 2d 120. 

However, the actions or promises of an employer may act as an estoppel to plead the one year limitation. The

1954] 

 declarations or conduct of the employer amounting to an estoppel do not give the claimant an indefinite time within which he must thereafter file his claim petition, but, on the contrary, the claimant must file his petition within one year from the time of the declarations or conduct relied upon. Guy v. Stoecklein Baking Co., 133 Pa. Superior Ct. 

38, 1 A. 2d 839.

The reason for this is that "the legislature thought that one year was a reasonable time within which to bring such actions and under the circumstances we think that same limitation should be applied to run from the date of the conversation between the claimant and the officer of the defendant company": Guy v. Stoecklein Baking Co., supra, page 50.

In this case the testimony of claimant discloses that his employer made a declaration which could be construed as an estoppel in April, 1946. The next declaration was in late 1949, more than three years later. During that period, claimant was medically ready for his operation, yet he postponed it by his own decision and through no fault or urging of the employer. It is clear, therefore, that his right to file a claim petition expired between the two declarations of the employer. The cases cited by appellee and the court below all involve instances in which claimant actually did file within one year after the employer's declarations or conduct: Paolis v. Tower Hill C. Coke Co., 

265 Pa. 291, 108 A. 638; Meyers v. Lehigh Valley Transportation Co., 138 Pa. Superior Ct. 569, 10 A. 2d 879.

Judgment reversed and entered for defendant. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD SABATELLI v. BUTTERTUB COAL COMPANY Workmen's compensation—Occupational disease-Award against employer and Commonwealth—Claim by widow for balance of award 

—Sole liability of Commonwealth Act of August 24, 1953. 

Where an employe was awarded compensation for disability resulting from anthraco-silicosis, part of which was payable by his em-

ployer and part by the Commonwealth, an award by the referee following his death to his widow for the balance of compensation was properly made against both the employer and the Commonwealth, although according to the medical testimony the disease resulted from an accumulation of silica during a long period of time that decedent had worked inside the mines; section 301 (g) of the Act of August 24, 1953, P. L. 1389, providing that all compensation shall be paid by the 

Commonwealth where disability or death is not conclusively proven to be the result of the last exposure, did not apply, since the widow's claim was for the balance of payments of compensation which had been awarded to decedent in his lifetime. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Olexy. District No. 2, claim petition no. 132,704, accident no. 5717370, A-39313. Affirmed. September 29, 1954. 

Eureka Casualty Co., insurance carrier. 

Thomas L. Kennedy, Jr., for claimant. 

F. B. Gelder, for defendant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for Commonwealth.

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

Defendant, Buttertub Coal Company, has taken this appeal from the order of Referee Olexy awarding compensation to claimant, Anna Sabatelli, for the death of her husband, Walter Sabatelli, on January 7, 1954, due to anthraco-silicosis. Decedent had filed a claim petition during his lifetime and compensation was paid thereon. Claimant testified as to marriage and dependency. She stated that her husband was employed by the Pennsylvania Coal 

Company from 1916 to 1935, for the Heidelberg Coal Company, No. Nine Coal Company and the Pennsylvania Coal 

Company from 1935 to 1943, inside the mines. From decedent's work record as established by the record, decedent worked for defendant from April 1, 1943, to September 8, 1950; for the Heidelberg Coal Company from September 12, 1950, to December 30, 1950; Guzior and Sons Coal Company from January 2, 1951, to January 27, 1951, and from February 5, 1951, to March 22, 1951, for the Lizak Coal Company. Decedent last worked on March 21, 1951.

1954] 

Doctor L. C. Druffner testified in behalf of claimant that he was in attendance upon decedent until the date of death, which he attributed to anthraco-silicosis. Under crossexamination, the doctor testified that he believed the silicosis disease resulted from an accumulation of silica during the 44-year period decedent worked inside the mines and this long exposure contributed to the death. Defendant offered no testimony. 

Defendant contends that all compensation should be paid by the Commonwealth pursuant to section 301 (g) of the 

Act of August 24, 1953, P. L. 1389, which provides: ". . . And provided further, That in those cases where disability or death is not conclusively proven to be the result of such last exposure, all compensation shall be paid by the Commonwealth."

Section 308 (a) of the same act provides: 

"When compensation is awarded because of disability or death caused by silicosis, anthraco-silicosis, asbestosis, or any other occupational disease which developed to the point of disablement only after an exposure of five or more years, the compensation for disability or death due to such disease shall, except as otherwise provided in subsection (g) of 

Sec. 301, be paid jointly by the employer and the Commonwealth, in accordance with the following provisions: . . . 

If disability begins after September 30, 1951, the employer shall be liable for sixty per centum of the compensation due and the Commonwealth forty per centum."

This question was previously before the board in the case of Hadley v. Pioneer City Coal Co., A-39107 (Board 1954), in which we said, in an opinion by Chairman Knoll: "The petition of the claimant, as the widow of William Hadley, was not a new petition, but was rather, in effect a claim for continuation of the payments of compensation that he had already been awarded for total disability. The award made on the petition of the widow was not based upon the death of the decedent; death was not the cause for compensation. This award was merely an order to pay the balance of the compensation due to the decedent's dependent widow. See Toffalori v. Donatelli Granite Co., 157 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 311.

"Insofar as the award of the referee was merely an order to pay the balance of the compensation due to the decedent's dependent widow and, since the original award was against both the employer and the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, there is no merit to the defendant employer's contention that the award of the referee should be paid solely by the 

Commonwealth of Pennsylvania."

Accordingly, the apportionment of compensation liability by the referee was proper. 

We hereby affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of award. 

The appeal of defendant is dismissed.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA MCCULLOUGH UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION CASE 

Unemployment compensation Voluntary cessation of work Agreement between employer and union for two week shut down in summer.

An order awarding compensation to claimant and other employes for a period during which the employer's plant was closed for a twoweek period in the summer pursuant to a provision in an agreement between the employer and the union of which claimant was a member by which it was agreed that the employer would make every effort wherever practical to discontinue operations for two weeks, will be reversed, since the circumstances did not create an involuntary unemployment. 

Appeal from decision of Unemployment Compensation 

Board of Review. Reversed, 175 Pa. Superior Ct. 402. 

Ronald Souser, with him Souser, Schumacker, Kleeb & 

Lunkenheimer for appellant. 

William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, with him Frank F. Truscott, Attorney General, for appellee. 

M. H. Goldstein, for intervenors, appellees.

1954] 

HIRT, J.:

The question in this appeal is whether 642 employes of the Philco Corporation are entitled to unemployment compensation benefits for any part of the vacation period between July 28 and August 10, 1952, when that employer shut down its plant for vacation purposes. It is agreed that the claim of Christine E. McCullough in the proceedings before the unemployment compensation authorities was typical both on the facts and the law and is determinative of all the above claims. Her last day of employment immediately prior to the shut down was July 25, 1952. The referee, in a well reasoned decision, denied benefits under sections 402 (b) and 401 (d) of the Unemployment Com pensation Law of December 5, 1936, Second Ex. Sess., P. L. 

(1937) 2897, as amended, 43 PS §§802, 801. The board, however, reversed and awarded compensation as claimed. 

All of the claimants are members of one or the other of locals 101 or 102 of Radio and Television Workers Union,

IUE-CIO. These unions were the collective bargaining agents for the claimants as to conditions relating to their employment. The subsisting agreements between Philco 

Corporation and each of the locals are identical in all material respects and provide for vacations with pay for various classes of employes based upon seniority in service.¹ 

The present claimants had less than three years of service and accordingly failed either to qualify for any vacation 1 The collective bargaining agreements, as found by the board, provided: "Article 4. Section 3. Vacation shall apply to all hourly rated employees on the active payroll for twenty five (25) weeks or more of the period from June 30th of the prior year to June 30th of the current year. The vacation pay shall be based upon the following schedule of seniority: Seniority 

Six (6) months to three (3) years. 

Three years and over... 

Fifteen years and over. Vacation . Forty (40) hours . Eighty (80) hours .Eighty (80) hours plus one week's additional pay "Employees who have over three (3) years seniority shall qualify for vacation if they have been on the active payroll for twenty (20) weeks or more of the above period. . . etc., etc."

pay, or qualified for less than full pay for the two-week period of the vacation shutdown, depending, in each case, on length of service. The findings of the board in substance are to the above effect. 

The sixth provision of article 4 of the agreements which give rise to the present controversy is quoted in the board's second finding, as follows: "Section 6. The Company will make every effort wherever practical (A) to discontinue operations for a two week period and (B) this period to be the latter part of July and the first of August."

The board made these additional findings: "3. In accordance with this contract, the company elected to close the plant from July 28 through August 10, 1952, for vacation purposes"; and "4. The claimants were ready and willing to accept suitable work if such work had been tendered to them during the period in question."

The board in construing the above provision of section 6 of the collective bargaining agreements held that "the vacation provisions here merely state that the company may, at its discretion, shut down its plants for a vacation period; albeit they will make every effort wherever practical to do so." In effect the holding was that the claimants' unemployment during the vacation period in this case was involuntary due to a layoff by the company, and not to a shut down of the plant agreed to by the union on behalf of its members. Accordingly, the board concluded that claimants were not disqualified under section 402 (b) of the act. 

The board, based on its fourth finding, also concluded that claimants on registering, were available for suitable work and accordingly were not disqualified under section 401 (d). 

In effect the board in awarding unemployment compensation, held that claimants' eligibility for benefits was ruled by our decision in Golubski Unempl. Compensation Case, 171 Pa. Superior Ct. 634, 91 A. 2d 315. We are unable to agree with the conclusions of the board. It is clear that claimants' unemployment during the vacation period was voluntary and therefore not compensable under section 

402(b) of the act.

1954] 

The two unions, between them, were the exclusive bargaining agents with the employer for all members of both unions. Claimants, therefore, were bound by the present agreements entered into on their behalf comprehending all phases of labor-management relationship, including vacations and vacation pay: Povey v. Midvale Company, 175 

Pa. Superior Ct. 395. The bargaining agreements are in the evidence in their entirety. To arrive at the intent of the parties, section 6 of article 4 of the contracts cannot be wrested from its context but must be considered in connection with the agreements as a whole. Article 4, in all of its six sections, relates to holidays with pay and to vacations with pay allowances. Section 3 of the article quoted in the margin, contains the agreement of the employer and the unions on behalf of claimants as to vacation cash payments which the company is obliged to make in accordance with the seniority schedules set forth therein. 

Section 3 is addressed to the amounts payable, not as bonuses to employes while working, but as cash benefits while on vacation.

Under our system of private enterprise an employer in general may operate his business as he sees fit, in the absence of restrictions imposed in a collective bargaining agreement. One of an employer's inherent rights is to determine vacation policy in his own business, particularly as to whether vacations shall be on a staggered basis or whether the plant shall be shut down so that the employes may enjoy their vacations all at the same time. Consequently, from the very fact that there are specific provisions in the present agreements for a vacation shut down, it is a necessary inference that the union wanted to eliminate company discretion in this respect. In this instance when the unions in their agreements with the employer, after setting up schedules as to eligibility of employes to vacation pay according to seniority, provided that "the company will make every effort wherever practical to discontinue operations" for two weeks beginning the latter part of July, the unions as agents for claimants specifically agreed upon the shut down. Certainly the phrase "wherever

practical" in the vacation provision of the contract does not in itself convert voluntary cessation of work into involuntary unemployment, compensable as such. The intent of the provision is not, as the board inferred, "that the Company may at its discretion shut down its plants for a vacation period". On the contrary, the only fair inference is that the contracting parties recognized that in an uncertain future a national, or even a lesser compelling emergency might make it impracticable to suspend production for two weeks or to discontinue operations in all departments for such period, or to shut down for vacation purposes during the latter part of July and the first of August, rather than at some other more favorable or convenient time. The limiting phrase "wherever practical" does not reduce the obligations of the negotiated contract into a course of action to be performed or not, in the discretion or at the whim of the employer; it implies performance if feasible and capable of being put into practice by the exercise of every possible effort. Cf. Joynes to use v. Railroad Co., 234 Pa. 321, 327, 83 Atl. 318. It is not without significance that the agreements on at least one other phase of employeremploye relationships limits the obligation of the employer to its "practical" aspects.2

The employes are protected against a decision of the employer, based upon whim or selfish motives by section 3 of article 8 of the contract which provides for common law arbitration of "all matters in controversy" between the company and the union, and names the arbitrator whose decision shall be final. Cf. Povey v. Midvale Company, supra. The unions in the agreements bound the company to discontinue operations for a two-weeks vacation period, as to hourly rated employes. On the refusal of the company to comply, the question of the practicability of a shut down as a matter in controversy could readily be decided with finality by invoking the arbitration provision of the agreements. Here the company recognized its obligation under the agreements and performed it by allowing a two- 2 In section 3 of article 5 it is provided: "Where practical the Company will not lay off employees until work falls below thirty-two (32) hours per week...' 

---

1954] 

 weeks vacation within the period designated and no question of practicability is involved. 

Moreover, in a real sense, claimants were not unemployed and for that reason their idleness during the vacation period was not compensable. They were not laid off; there was no termination of employment nor even suspension of the employer-employe relationship during the period. A vacation is a period of freedom from duty, not an end of employment: Dauber's Case, 151 Pa. Superior Ct. 293, 30 A. 2d 

214. Their continued employment was insured by their bargaining agreements; their names were still on the payroll even though they did not receive vacation pay. The contract of employment persisted, under which they returned to their work at the end of the vacation period without loss of seniority. The agreements in article 2 provide for a work-week of 40 hours of 8 hours per day, from Monday through Friday. The scheduled vacation period was no different in principle from the normal uncompensated weekend shut down from Friday to the following Monday, incident to the 40-hour week.

The above principles in our view clearly called for a reversal of the decision of the board. And we need not refer to the fourth finding supra, under which the board concluded that claimants were not disqualified under section 401(d), except to say that the record does not support the finding; the testimony of claimant McCullough is equivocal and indicates an intention to return to her employment with the appellant at the end of the vacation period and not to accept employment in the meantime. Here, clearly, the registration for work was an empty formality lacking in sincerity. 

This appeal in every aspect is ruled against the claimants by Mattey Unemployment Comp. Case, 164 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 36, 63 A. 2d 429, and not by the Golubski Case, supra.

In the Mattey case, as here, the shut down was for the benefit of the employes and their temporary unemployment was voluntary; in the Golubski case, the unemployment was involuntary and for the benefit of the employer. 

These are the reasons for our order of March 14, 1954, reversing the decision of the Unemployment Compensation

Board of Review.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD MARASCO v. STATE WORKMEN'S INSURANCE FUND et al. 

Workmen's compensation-Disability from beryllium poisoning— 

Act of May 14, 1949-Last exposure subsequent to effective date of act. 

Compensation for disability resulting from beryllium poisoning, was properly awarded to claimant, where it appeared that she was employed by defendant working on lamps, that beryllium compound was used to coat the inside of the lamps and that claimant's last exposure to the occupational hazard was subsequent to the effective date of the Act of May 14, 1949, P. L. 1379, which included disability from such poisoning, where it further appeared that her total disability occurred within three years from her last exposure. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Smith. District No. 10, claim petition no. 128,294, accident no. 

5973943, A-39291. Affirmed. September 8, 1954.

State Workmen's Insurance Fund, insurance carrier. 

Joseph H. Goldstein and Edward E. Petrillo, for claimant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for defendants.

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

Referee Smith awarded compensation to claimant, Angeline Marasco, for total disability due to beryllium poisoning, as of January 15, 1952. Defendant, State Workmen's

Insurance Fund, has appealed, claiming that claimant was not exposed to the occupational hazard within the statutory requirements. Claimant worked for defendant company, Sunray Electric, Inc., from April 1944, until July 28, 1951, when she quit because of ill health. 

Robert E. Dunham, president of the defendant company and its chief chemist, testified that defendant last used beryllium compound in the manufacture of fluorescent lamps in April or May 1949 and that he did not believe there was any of the substance in defendant's plant in 

1950, unless in sealed containers. The compound had been used to coat the inside of the lamps until halophosphates,

1954]  a better product, was substituted. The witness testified that the last purchase of beryllium was made in April 1949 in an amount of 50 or 100 pounds and that such a supply would last for two or three months. According to Dunham, claimant was transferred from the coating room on the second floor to the plug fuse department on the third floor, early in 1946 and worked there until 1949. 

Claimant testified that she worked on the second and third floors until July 28, 1951, and that from 1946 to 1949 she divided her time between working on lamps and making fuses. Dr. J. Theo. Valone, claimant's witness, testified that claimant has been his patient for some time, and that in March 1951 she complained of cough and chest pains. 

The doctor thought she might have an upper abdominal disturbance so that he had X-rays taken, which were negative, except to disclose a nonfunctioning gall bladder. Claimant continued to complain of cough and was referred to Dr. Herman Bozer for bronchoscopic examination. This doctor, after examination, referred her to Dr. Clayton 

Green, who reported that claimant "had a severe pulmonary condition which prohibits proper oxygenation of her blood and up until this time it was impossible to say that the cause of this was due to beryllium poisoning". In the spring of 1952, Dr. Valone suspected beryllium poisoning and referred the patient to Dr. H. S. VanOrdstrand, of the Cleveland Clinic. The latter doctor wrote to Dr. Valone on 

August 25, 1952, inter alia: "I do indeed certify to Mrs. Marasco's difficulty being chronic berylliosis (chronic beryllium poisoning), from which she is currently and is still disabled." 

Dr. Valone felt that claimant was totally disabled in 

January, 1952.

We are satisfied that claimant is totally disabled due to beryllium poisoning. Under the Occupational Disease Act of June 26, 1939, P. L. 566, total disability from beryllium poisoning was not grounds for compensation. By the amendatory Act of May 14, 1949, P. L. 1379, that disease is included: Section 108 (a). Section 301 (c) of the same act provides:

"Whenever compensable disability or death is mentioned as a cause for compensation under this Act, it shall mean only compensable disability or death resulting from occupational disease and occurring within three years after the date of his last employment in such occupation or industry." (Italics supplied.)

The act took effect on July 1, 1949. From all of the credible testimony, we are firmly convinced that claimant's last exposure to the occupational hazard was subsequent to the effective date of the act, and also that her total disability occurred within three years from such last exposure. 

Under the law, claimant is entitled to compensation for the beryllium poisoning, which she clearly incurred in the course of her employment by defendant.

We hereby affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and award. 

The appeal of defendant is dismissed.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA SPOTTS UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION CASE 

Unemployment compensation-Voluntarily leaving work without good cause-Refusal to accept work on different shift-Domestic obligations-Act of August 24, 1953—Constitutionality. 

1. Claimant was properly denied unemployment compensation where her original employment was on the second shift from 4 p.m. until 12:30 a.m., and where by reason of lack of work on the shift she was offered employment on the first shift from 7 a.m. until 3:30 p.m., which she refused on the ground that she had no one to stay with her three small children during those shift hours, since under the amendment of August 24, 1953, to section 402 (b) of the Unemployment Compensation Law, domestic obligation is not a good cause for voluntarily leaving work. 

2. The Act of August 24, 1953, P. L. 1397, which, inter alia, amends section 402(b) of the Unemployment Compensation Law does not violate the fourteenth amendment to the United States Constitution by discriminating against women working who are married and have children, since the act applies to everyone who comes within the scope of the Unemployment Compensation Law.

1954] 

 Appeal from Unemployment Compensation Board of Re view. Affirmed, 176 Pa. Superior Ct. 484.

David Cohen, with him Phyllis C. Halpern, for appellant. 

William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, with him Frank F. Truscott, Attorney General, for appellee.

WRIGHT, J.: 

In this unemployment compensation case Florence Z.

Spotts (hereinafter referred to as claimant) was denied benefits by the bureau, the referee, and the board of review on the ground that she voluntarily terminated her employment without good cause under the provisions of section 

402(b) of the Unemployment Compensation Law. Claimant has appealed to this court contending (1) that she did not voluntarily leave work; (2) that her unemployment was not due to her voluntarily leaving work, and (3) that the 1953 amendment¹ to section 402 (b) of said statute is unconstitutional. 

Prior to November 17, 1953, claimant was employed by

Sylvania Electric Products, Inc., in Emporium, Pa. She is married and has three children aged respectively six, five and three years. Her original employment was on the second shift, from 4 p.m. to 12:30 a.m. Due to lack of work on this shift, claimant received 40 hours advance notice that she was to be laid off from her usual work of loading tabulating machines. During this 40-hour period she attempted to exercise her seniority rights in order to obtain other work on the second shift. However, an employe with greater seniority obtained the position claimant was seeking, and claimant was thereupon notified that work would be available for her on the first shift from 7 a.m. to 3:30 p.m. Claimant then "accepted a voluntary lay-off", because she had no one to stay with her three small children during day shift hours.

The relevant provision of the Unemployment Compensa tion Law (1953 amendment italicized) reads as follows: 

¹ Act of August 24, 1953, P. L. 1397, sec. 4, 43 PS §802.

"An employe shall be ineligible for compensation for any week . . . (b) In which his unemployment is due to voluntarily leaving work without good cause, . . . provided, with respect to the determination of suitable work under section four (t), marital, filial and domestic circumstances and obligations shall not be deemed good cause within the meaning of this act · · • Prior to the enactment of the 1953 amendment, we had ruled that "good cause" 2 need not be directly connected with the employment, consistently recognizing that family obligations might constitute good cause. See Teicher Unem ployment Compensation Case, 154 Pa. Superior Ct. 250, 35 

A. 2d 739 (1944); Sturdevant Unemployment Compensation Case, 158 Pa. Superior Ct. 548, 45 A. 2d 898 (1946);

Mooney Unemployment Compensation Case, 162 Pa. Supe rior Ct. 183, 56 A. 2d 386 (1948); Hamilton Unemployment 

Compensation Case, 172 Pa. Superior Ct. 413, 94 A. 2d 63 (1953); Quiggle Unemployment Compensation Case, 172 

Pa. Superior Ct. 430, 94 A. 2d 367 (1953). The change in the language of the statute indicates a change of legislative intent: Ogilvie's Estate, 291 Pa. 326, 139 A. 826; Midvale

Co. v. Unemployment Compensation Board of Review, 165 

Pa. Superior Ct. 359, 67 A. 2d 380.

Claimant's first contention is that she was compelled to cease work, not because of her changed circumstances, but because of the inability of the company to provide continued employment "on the same terms as before". The answer is that a change in shifts cannot be considered a change in the terms of employment. As we said in Mehlbaum Unemployment Compensation Case, 175 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 497, 107 A. 2d 141: "We must not lose sight of the purpose of the Act, as expressed in the declaration of public policy. It was designed to benefit those persons who become unemployed through no fault of their own. . . . This public policy must be considered in construing every provision of the law and 2 This term was inserted in section 402 (b) by the 1942 amendment, Act of April 23, 1942, P. L. 60.

1954] 

 in determining eligibility for compensation in every case. 

The Act must be construed sensibly so that absurd results may be avoided."

To suggest that claimant did not "voluntarily" cease work, in the face of the offer of continued employment on a different shift, is to give the situation an unrealistic interpretation. It is clearly apparent that the employment relationship was severed by claimant and not by her employer, and claimant's reason for the severance cannot be considered good cause under the express language of the 1953 amendment.

Claimant next contends that, even though she may be found to have voluntarily quit work, her unemployment was not “due to” her act in voluntarily leaving, but was caused by a change in the economic circumstances of the company. 

This contention is but a rephrasing of claimant's first position. Reliance is placed upon Campbell Unemployment Compensation Case, 175 Pa. Superior Ct. 592, 106 A. 2d 687, but that case is not controlling. In the Campbell case we held that an employe who was laid off because of a slackening of work, and thereafter went on a pension, was not precluded from receiving benefits so long as his unemployment was due to the lay off rather than to his retirement. 

The situation presented in the case at bar is one in which claimant refused continued employment at available work because her family duties required her to stay at home during the daytime.

We had occasion to consider the 1953 amendment recently in Forsht Unemployment Compensation Case, 175 Pa. Superior Ct. 646, 106 A. 2d 871. In that case, claimant was employed on the first shift. She was injured in an accident and was unable to work for two months. Upon recovery, she applied for employment on the second shift so that her husband could care for their two small children while claimant was at work. Employment was not available on the second shift but was still available on the first shift. Claimant did not notify her employer that she was willing to go back to work on the first shift. Speaking through Judge 

Hirt, we said:

"The reason assigned by claimant for not resuming her work on the first shift is not good cause under the amendment. . . . Work on the first shift was suitable work, and claimant could refuse to return to it because of her family obligations only by terminating her employment without good cause under the 1953 amendment of section 402 (b), thus barring her right to unemployment compensation."

In the case at bar, claimant's reason for refusing work on the first shift was the same as that given in the Forsht case. And, as there pointed out, this reason has been the subject of legislative enactment excluding claimant from benefits.

Claimant contends finally that the 1953 amendment violates public policy and is in contravention of article III, sec. 7, of the Constitution of Pennsylvania and the fourteenth amendment to the Constitution of the United States. 

Her position is that the amendment discriminates against women workers who are married and have children. But the provision in question is not directed to or against any specific group or class of persons. It applies to everyone who comes within the coverage or scope of the Unemployment Compensation Law. Under such circumstances it cannot be said to be a local or special law prohibited by article 

III, sec. 7, of our Constitution, nor does it offend against the guaranty of equal protection of the law contained in the fourteenth amendment of the Constitution of the United States. "It thus appears from the foregoing . . that the Pennsylvania Unemployment Compensation Law is not a special or local Act 'regulating labor, trade, mining and manufacturing'; and that the classifications made by the Act are valid and proper. Therefore, we find that the said Act as we have heretofore construed it does not violate either the 

Fourteenth Amendment to the Constitution of the United

States or section 9 of Article I, section 7 of Article III, or section 1 of Article IX of the Pennsylvania Constitution": 

Boyertown Burial Casket Co. v. Commonwealth, 366 Pa. 

574, 79 A. 2d 449. And see Seifing Unemployment Compensation Case, 159 Pa. Superior Ct. 94, 46 A. 2d 598.

1954] 

Our conclusion is that it was not the intention of the legislature to permit an employe to insist upon working a particular shift because of marital, filial, or domestic circumstances. The wisdom and appropriateness of legislation are not for the judicial branch of the Government: 

Albright Unemployment Compensation Case, 162 Pa. Supe rior Ct. 98, 56 A. 2d 390. The general declaration of public policy must yield to the specific provisions of section 402: 

Midvale Co. v. Unemployment Compensation Board of Re view, supra, 165 Pa. Superior Ct. 359, 67 A. 2d 380. 

Decision affirmed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD RAFTER, etc., et al. v. SCHENLEY DISTRIBUTORS, INCORPORATED Workmen's compensation-Death in the course of employment— 

Salesman-Fatal injuries in accident following attendance at labor negotiations meeting.

An award of compensation to claimant for the benefit of her minor daughter on account of the death of claimant's ex-husband and father of the minor was reversed, where it appeared that his death resulted from an automobile collision late on the evening following his attendance at a contract negotiations meeting between representatives of decedent's employer and the union, decedent being one of the union representatives at the meeting; under the circumstances, decedent was not in the course of his employment or engaged in the furtherance of defendant's interests at the time of his injury, since the interests of the union and defendant to be composed at the meeting were adverse and the parties were dealing at arms' length. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee McGovern. 

District No. 8, claim petition no. 132,175, accident no. 

6172053, A-39402. Reversed. September 29, 1954. 

Liberty Mutual Insurance Co., insurance carrier.

Paul V. Mahoney, for claimant. 

V. C. Short, for defendant.

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by defendant, Schenley Distributors, Inc., from an award entered by Referee McGovern, June 

10, 1954, in favor of claimant, Bette J. Rafter, for the benefit of her 14-year-old daughter, Mona Joanne Rafter.

The claim petition in the case was filed November 25, 1953, with a view to recovering compensation for the death of 

Richard J. Rafter, a salesman in defendant's employ, which took place in the South Side Hospital in Pittsburgh on October 13, 1953, as a result of an automobile collision which occurred on Route 51 near Pleasant Hills in Allegheny County on October 8, 1953, at about 10:30 in the evening. Decedent was the ex-husband of claimant and father of the minor in whose behalf the award was entered. 

The record indicates that there is no dispute as to employment, accidental death, notice or dependency, the issue being concededly narrowed down to the question as to whether or not decedent was in the course of his employment or engaged in the furtherance of defendant's interests at the time of the fatal mishap. 

At the hearing, the first witness called was Howard E. 

Metzgar, defendant's field supervisor under whom decedent worked. He explained a salesman's duties as contacting bars with the idea of promoting defendant's products, placing advertising and selling in assigned territories. 

Under the Liquor Control Law the sales were, of course, in the nature of availability arrangements at the State stores. Metzgar traced decedent's territory as a rectangular area in the southwest corner of Pennsylvania, the northerly line of which intersected the easterly boundary of West Virginia just west of Claysville and thence ran eastwardly and slightly southwardly to a point northeast of Somerton. 

Decedent was paid a flat salary and there was a bonus arrangement but no provision for commissions on sales.

1954] 

The witness stated that regular activities were limited to the assigned territory but that all the salesmen "pushed" defendant's products at all times wherever they were. He admitted that one exception to decedent's territorial limits was the Twin Coaches Inn, about four miles north of the line above delineated. Decedent reported an order from this customer "every couple of weeks" and on this point, defendant presented as its exhibit "B" a photostatic copy of an order blank made out by decedent October 6, 1953, just two days before the fatal accident, calling for three cases of liquor on request of Twin Coaches Inn. 

The evidence as to decedent's activities on the date of the accident must be considered in the light of the duties outlined. By letter, dated April 27, 1953, J. L. Hague, in charge of defendant's industrial relations department, was notified by James Lebarty, international representative of 

Local No. 131 of the Wholesale Licensed Alcoholic Beverage 

Salesman's Union, American Federation of Labor, of the necessity of contract negotiation before July 1, 1954. 

Through subsequent correspondence and telephone conversations, October 8, 1953, was set as the date and the William Penn Hotel as the place for the meeting to be held between representatives of defendant and the union. Hague, Lee 

Krost and Paul Caffey attended for defendant and Lebarty, Frank Todd and decedent for the union. The meeting lasted until mid-afternoon and thereafter, decedent met R. S. Dummar, a salesman covering the Pittsburgh district, in the Oliver Building restaurant. Their next stop was Danny's 

Restaurant on Grant Street and their last, the Radio Cafe next door. Dummar, called as a witness, stated that these places represented some of his accounts and explained that decedent accorded him the indulgence of some good-will introductions in them. He admitted that decedent complained of being overtired; that he slept a while after dinner in the Radio Cafe; that he, Dummar, took him out for a walk in the air; that decedent, in answer to his suggestion of spending the night in Pittsburgh, said he "would rather be home in his own bed that night so he would be in his own territory in the morning", and that he took decedent

to the place where the latter's car was parked at some time between 9:30 and 10 o'clock in the evening.

Another witness called by claimant was Dorothy Mathias. 

She lived in Monongahela and had been keeping rather steady company with decedent. Her testimony indicates that 

Rafter called her from the Oliver Building restaurant at about 8:30 o'clock on the evening in question and told her he would not stop to see her that evening since he had to make a stop at the Twin Coaches Inn on the way home. She admitted that the call was a surprise to her because she did not have a prearranged engagement to see him that evening and that she hadn't known he was in Pittsburgh.

The salient point in Lebarty's testimony was that defendant liked to have some of its own men on the union committee at such negotiations, but he admitted that decedent, who was secretary-treasurer of the union, was chosen by vote of the local. Hague had no knowledge of such a precedent, stating that the company never had advance information as to whom they would deal with on such occasions. 

Considering the evidence as we have outlined it, we must submit that we have a different view of it and its legal effect than that which has been determined by the referee. We cannot understand the spontaneous mention of the proposed stop at the Twin Coaches Inn in the entirely unexpected call to Dorothy Mathias. This strikes us as pure fabrication. Dummar, in all of his testimony, does not even suggest that any such intention was expressed to him. Furthermore, it is inconceivable that decedent would have found it necessary to make such a business stop after such a long, fatiguing day with a long trip home facing him when he had received an order from the Inn just two days before. Neither can we credit the account of the good-will calls at the various places visited in Pittsburgh after the meeting with defendant's officials. It offers far too tenuous a basis upon which to predicate service in the line of duty. Our impression of this is that the decedent and Dummar found their chief interest in their common vocation and the places where they sold their goods the best places to patronize when consuming it.

There remains to discuss the point which claimant stresses on appeal. It has to do with the function being

1954] 

 served by decedent in attending the contract negotiation meeting. Claimant submits that the question posed by this situation is one of first impression in Pennsylvania and cites as the only authority to be found Kennedy v. Thompson Lumber Co., 26 N. W. 2d 459, decided under the compensation law of Minnesota, wherein an employe, acting in his capacity as shop steward, was injured crossing a street to use a telephone off the employer's premises for the purpose of calling a union business agent in an endeavor to prevent a work stoppage. Such may be the construction of the law of Minnesota, and we do not say that it would not be the law here, provided that the employer had some part in ordering such an errand. However, we do not see the present situation in that light. As we see it, the interests of the union and defendant to be composed at the meeting which decedent attended were adverse and the parties were dealing at arms' length. One of the principal elements of the master and servant relationship is control of the employe by the employer (Simonton v. Morton, 275 Pa. 562), and such control is the one thing that all recent labor legislation has been designed to eliminate from the process of bargaining. Labor has secured freedom from domination in contract negotiations and for every freedom there is a price. 

Unfortunately it falls upon claimant to pay that price in this case. 

The first, second, third, fourth, sixth, seventh and eighth findings of fact, first conclusion of law and bill of costs of 

Referee McGovern, dated June 10, 1954, are affirmed.

The fifth finding of fact and second conclusion of law and award of Referee McGovern of the same date are vacated and set aside and the following substituted therefor: 

Finding of Fact 

4. At the time of his fatal accident, decedent was on his way home from a meeting in which he was a representative of his local union engaged in contract negotiations with defendant.

Conclusion of Law 

2. Since decedent sustained his fatal injury by accident while returning home from a meeting in which he, as a representative of his union, was serving an interest adverse

to the interest of defendant, claimant is not entitled to compensation.

Order The claim petition filed in this case, November 25, 1953, is hereby dismissed. 

Defendant's appeal is sustained.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA WATSON UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION CASE 

Unemployment compensation-Refusal to accept suitable work- Domestic obligations-Section 402(a) of the Unemployment Compensation Law as amended by the Act of August 24, 1953. 

The Act of August 24, 1953, amending the Unemployment Compensation Law applies to section 402 (a) as well as section 402(b), and as a result, claimant was properly denied unemployment compensation benefits on the ground that her unemployment was due to her failure to accept suitable work, where it appeared that she had formerly worked on a second shift but was referred to work on a first shift with a new employer from 7 a.m. to 4 p.m., when work was not available on the second or third shifts, her refusal to accept work on the first shift being based on the alleged necessity of delivering her twelve-year-old daughter to school in the morning and from and back to school at noon; under the 1953 amendment to the law, domestic obligations are not good cause for refusal to accept suitable work. 

Appeal from Unemployment Compensation Board of Re view. Affirmed, 176 Pa. Superior Ct. 490. 

M. Herbert Syme, for appellant. 

William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, with him Frank F. Truscott, Attorney General, for appellee. 

WRIGHT, J.: 

In this unemployment compensation case Rose M. Watson (hereinafter referred to as claimant) was denied benefits by the bureau, the referee, and the board of review on the ground that she refused to accept suitable employment without good cause under the provisions of section 402 (a) of

1954] 

 the Unemployment Compensation Law. The question involved is whether the 1953 amendment to section 402 (b),¹ considered in Spotts Unemployment Compensation Case, 176 Pa. Superior Ct. 484, applies as well to section 402 (a). 

Claimant lives in Lebanon. She was last employed by the 

Hershey Chocolate Corporation at Hershey, Pa., from July 

13, 1953, to January 29, 1954, as the operator of a candy wrapping machine. On March 3, 1954, claimant was referred for employment as a sewing machine operator with the Barre Lynn Sportswear Company, at Annville, Pa.

Claimant does not challenge the suitability of the proffered employment. However, her work at Hershey was on the second shift, from 2:30 p.m. to 11 p.m., whereas the work at Annville was on the first shift, from 7 a.m. to 4 p.m. 

Claimant refused to accept work on the first shift because it was necessary for her to drive her 12-year-old daughter to school in the morning, and from and back to school at noon. Claimant was ready and willing to accept employment on the second or third shift, but such work was not available.

The contention of claimant's able counsel is that the 1953 amendment applies only to subsection (b) of section 402, which relates to situations in which the employe voluntarily leaves work, and has no application to subsection (a) of section 402, which relates to situations in which the claimant is laid off and subsequently refuses to apply for or accept suitable work. Insofar as material to our present discussion, subsection (a) of section 402 provides that "An employe shall be ineligible for compensation for any week in which his unemployment is due to failure, without good cause, either to apply for suitable work . . ., or to accept. suitable work . . ."; subsection (b) of section 402 provides that "An employe shall be ineligible for compensation for any week in which his unemployment is due to voluntarily leaving work without good cause. . .".

It is important to note that both subsections contain the phrase "without good cause". By the amendment of 1953, the words "marital, filial and domestic circumstances and 

1 Act of August 24, 1953, P. L. 1397, section 4, 43 PS 802.

obligations shall not be deemed good cause within the meaning of this act" were inserted in subsection (b). (Italics supplied.)

Prior to the 1953 amendment, domestic obligations were considered "good cause" under both section 402, subsection (a)2 and section 402, subsection (b). No distinction was made between situations in which the employe voluntarily quit work and situations in which the employe refused to accept suitable work. There can be no question that the 

1953 amendment has limited the meaning of "good cause" under subsection (b): Spotts v. Unemployment Compensation Board of Review, supra. However, it is urged that the legislative intent was to adopt this limitation only in cases arising under subsection (b), while still adhering to the definition heretofore supplied by judicial interpretation in cases arising under subsection (a). The anomalous situations which would result from such a construction readily illustrate the fallacy of the argument. Employes voluntarily leaving work because of marital, filial or domestic reasons would be denied benefits, while those who refused suitable work for the same reasons would be entitled to benefits.

In our opinion the legislature was aware of the interpretation given by the courts to the term "good cause" under both subsections. Instead of using the words "under this subsection", which appear with respect to an earlier clause, the legislature said "within the meaning of this act". It is obvious that these significant words were intended to have 

2 Mooney Unemployment Compensation Case, 162 Pa. Superior Ct. 

183, 56 A. 2d 386; Quiggle Unemployment Compensation Case, 172 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 430, 94 A. 2d 367. 

3 Teicher Unemployment Compensation Case, 154 Pa. Superior Ct. 

250, 35 A. 2d 739; Sturdevant Unemployment Compensation Case, 158 

Pa. Superior Ct. 548, 45 A. 2d 898; Hamilton Unemployment Compensation Case, 172 Pa. Superior Ct. 413, 94 A. 2d 63. 

4 Section 52 of article IV of the Statutory Construction Act of May 

28, 1937, P. L. 1019, 46 PS §552, provides, in part: "That the Legislature does not intend a result that is absurd, or unreasonable." · · Statutes should receive a sensible construction, and one by virtue of which absurdity and mischief may be avoided: Dawkins Unemployment Compensation Case, 358 Pa. 224, 56 A. 2d 254.

1954]  their literal import. A word or phrase, the meaning of which is clear when used in one place, will be construed to mean the same elsewhere in the same section of the statute: 

Commonwealth v. Stingel, 156 Pa. Superior Ct. 359, 40 A. 2d 

140; Bonomo Unemployment Compensation Case, 161 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 622, 56 A. 2d 288; Midvale Co. v. Unemployment Compensation Board of Review, 165 Pa. Superior Ct. 

359, 67 A. 2d 380.

Claimant also argues that "the enunciated policy of the act precludes an application of the 'domestic obligations' disqualification to section 402 (a)". We have disposed of this contention adversely to claimant's position in Spotts Unemployment Compensation Case, supra, wherein we pointed out that the general declaration of public policy in the statute must yield to the specific provisions of section 402. See Midvale Co. v. Unemployment Compensation 

Board of Review, supra, 165 Pa. Superior Ct. 359, 67 A. 

2d 380. 

Decision affirmed.

COMMON PLEAS NO. 6 OF PHILADELPHIA COUNTY RICCI v. JOHN BORDEN & BROS. et al. 

Workmen's compensation-Death-Coronary occlusion from injury while lifting-Unusual exertion-Steam fitter lifting 300-pound weight without equipment.

An award on account of death of claimant's decedent, who was a steam fitter, as a result of a coronary occlusion caused by an injury when he attempted to put a 300-pound weight aboard a truck was affirmed on the ground that the injury constituted an accident within the meaning of The Compensation Act, where there was evidence by a fellow steam fitter that it was not usual practice for steam fitters to lift pumps, that when a weight of 300 pounds was to be lifted, it was done with equipment and help. 

Appeal from Workmen's Compensation Board. C. P. 

No. 6 of Philadelphia County, June term, 1954, no. 128. 

Raymond J. Porreca, for claimant.

Frederick L. Fuges, for defendants.

FLOOD, J.: 

Claimant's decedent, who was a steam fitter, suffered an injury resulting in a coronary occlusion which in turn caused his death when he attempted to put a 300-pound pump aboard a truck. See the opinion of this court filed upon the first appeal. The question now before us is whether the injury constituted an accident within the meaning of the act. 

Since nothing untoward happened except in or to the plaintiff's body, the question is whether the action which he was performing at the time was of a different nature from his ordinary work, requiring materially greater exertion than was ordinarily required of him. 

Defendant argues that the burden of proving extraordinary exertion was upon claimant, that the testimony showed at most that it was extraordinary for claimant to lift such a weight as this pump without help and without mechanical aids, whereas there was no proof that he did not have help or mechanical aids. Defendant claims further that the evidence of a fellow worker, the only witness who was present at the time, shows that the fellow worker was assisting him and that they had aid in the form of two long boards slanting from the ground up to the truck. 

Claimant points out, however, that the witness Perna, a fellow steam fitter in defendant's employ who often worked with Ricci as a team, testified that it is not usual practice for steam fitters to lift pumps, that he, in the course of his work as a steam fitter for defendant, never lifted one and did not recall seeing decedent do so. This seems to us to be sufficient evidence of unusual exertion, of a different nature from his ordinary work, to sustain claimant's burden of proof and the board's award.

Defendant argues that the testimony just recited was so qualified by Perna as to destroy it. Perna said that among a steam fitter's duties would be included handling 300pound items under proper conditions and with proper equipment such as cranes, chain or rope blocks or the like and "plenty of manpower". It is to be noted that neither such equipment nor "plenty of manpower" was used here unless

1954] 

 two is equivalent to plenty. Nor did he qualify or retract his earlier statement that he never lifted such a pump and did not recall seeing decedent do so.

On cross-examination, he said that it would be unusual for one man to lift a pump and "a pretty good haul" for two to do it. He also admitted that a steam fitter handles the pipe with which he works "with manpower" and does not use cranes. He denied that such lifting would not be unusually strenuous for one man and to the question, "It would be, certainly, for two men?" he answered, "I would say it still would be a pretty good haul for two men". When asked, "A weight of 300 pounds is frequently lifted by two steam fitters?" Perna answered, "Yes". But this immediately followed the question and answer: "Q. Well, it is frequently done by steam fitters? A. Yes, with equipment and help."

He never said it was done without either equipment or "plenty of manpower”.

None of this seems to us to be, as claimed by defendant, an admission that the lift would not be unusual exertion for two men like decedent, or for a steam fitter with the help of another man, but without lifting devices. On the contrary, it seems to us that the board is justified in inferring that the exertion would be unusual for one in decedent's occupation, unless there were either lifting devices or "plenty of manpower" and that two men, for whom it would be "a pretty good haul", would not constitute "plenty of manpower".

Near the close of Perna's testimony, after repeated crossexamination, he said that two men probably could lift the pump, but whether it would be unusual would depend on what they had to work with, but if they had two long slanting boards and would gradually push it up from the ground, they could do it that way and it wouldn't be unusual. However, he did not say that it would not be unusual for decedent. We would hardly conclude that the board could not infer that it was unusual for a steam fitter to lift 300-pound items without proper equipment or "plenty of manpower" because of these statements upon cross-examination by the referee. But in any event, we should not reverse its finding that it was not within the decedent's usual employment to

lift such articles without extra help and the proper equipment in view of all of Perna's testimony. 

Consequently, we cannot reverse the finding of the board that decedent's death resulted from an accident in the course of his employment.

The appeal is dismissed and judgment is entered upon the award.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD WHYTE v. GLEN ALDEN COAL CO. 

Workmen's compensation-Occupational disease-Award against employer and Commonwealth—Claim by widow for balance of award 

-Sole liability of Commonwealth—Act of August 24, 1953. 

A referee's award of compensation to a widow following the death of her husband from anthraco-silicosis, is properly made against both the employer and the Commonwealth, where decedent had filed a claim petition during his lifetime so that the award to the widow represented a continuation of the payments of compensation due decedent for his disability, where his last employment and exposure occurred prior to the Act of August 24, 1953, P. L. 1389; the provisions of that act that all compensation be paid by the Commonwealth where disability is not conclusively proven to be the result of the last exposure, did not apply.

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Olexy. District No. 2, claim petition no. 132,636, accident no. 5944903. 

Affirmed. October 20, 1954. 

Thomas L. Kennedy, Jr., for claimant. 

F. B. Gelder, for defendant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for Commonwealth.

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

This case is before the board upon appeal by defendant, Glen Alden Coal Company, from the order of Referee Olexy awarding compensation to claimant, Margaret Whyte, for the death of her husband, William Whyte, on January 2,

1954] 

1954, due to anthraco-silicosis, accompanied by an active pulmonary tuberculosis. The referee assessed compensation liability in the proportions of 60 percent against defendant and 40 percent against the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania.

It is the contention of defendant that all liability should be assessed against the Commonwealth in accordance with the provisions of the Act of August 24, 1953, P. L. 1389, section 

301 (g) of the act provides: "And provided further, That in those cases where disability or death is not conclusively proven to be the result of such last exposure, all compensation shall be paid by the 

Commonwealth. . 99 

Section 308 (a) of the same act provides: 

"When compensation is awarded because of disability or death caused by silicosis, anthraco-silicosis, asbestosis, or any other occupational diease which developed to the point of disablement only after an exposure of five or more years, the compensation for disability or death due to such disease shall, except as otherwise provided in Subsection (g) of Section 301, be paid jointly by the employer and the Commonwealth, in accordance with the following provisions: . . . If disability begins after September 30, 1951, the employer shall be liable for sixty per centum of the compensation due and the Commonwealth forty per centum."

Decedent filed a claim petition for compensation for total disability and an award was entered, upon which payments were made to the date of death. The record establishes that the death in this case was due to anthraco-silicosis. Marriage and dependency of claimant were proved. An award is warranted. Claimant's testimony establishes and the referee found (4): "Decedent was employed by defendant in its anthracite mines from October 21, 1947, to July 14, 1950. He was employed by the Lehigh Valley Coal Company in its anthracite mines from June 1947, to October 1947 and by the Hudson

Coal Company in its anthracite mines from January 1946 to June 1947 and by the Lehigh Valley Coal Company in its anthracite mines from 1935 to 1942 and by the Glen Alden 

Company from January 1, 1930 to 1935 and in other anthracite mines from 1928 to January 1, 1930. During all of these

employments, decedent was continuously exposed to an anthraco-silica hazard."

Out of the 22-year period of decedent's employment prior to total disability, claimant worked approximately 19 years inside the coal mines. From 1943 to 1946, he worked for the 

General Aviation Company during which time he was evidently not exposed to a silica hazard. 

Dr. William T. Davis, defendant's witness, testified that claimant's decedent had been contracting anthraco-silicosis since 1928 when he was first exposed to a silica hazard in his employment as a coal miner; that all the exposures in his employment by the several coal companies contributed to decedent's disability, which culminated in total disability on April 17, 1952. Decedent's last period of employment by defendant was from October 21, 1947, to July 14, 1950.

This is a case of first impression under the recent Act of 1953. While claimant's right of compensation arose out of her husband's contract of employment and exposure to a silica hazard, it was an inchoate right during the husband's lifetime, and did not vest in her until his death: Toffalori v. Donatelli Granite Co., 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 311. Does the

Act of 1953 apply where decedent's last exposure and total disability occurred prior to the effective date of the act (August 24, 1953) and the death occurred after the effective date? We are of the opinion that it does not.

It is true that the claim of a widow dependent constitutes a separate, distinct and independent cause of action, where she files a claim petition in her own right. It is significant here that a valid claim petition was filed by decedent during his lifetime so that the present payments due the claimant widow is a continuation of the payments of compensation that had already been awarded for total disability: Toffalori v. Donatelli Granite Co., supra., under the circumstances, the referee's assessment of compensation liability upon the same basis as the original claim, i. e., of 80 percent against defendant, Glen Alden Coal Company, and 20 percent against the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, was proper and will be affirmed.

We hereby affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and award. 

The appeal of defendant is dismissed.

1954] 

1 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD GERBER v. CITY OF PITTSBURGH Workmen's compensation—Police officer-Disability resulting from heart disease attributable to over exertion-Act of September 27, 1951-Jurisdiction of Workmen's Compensation Board. 

A claim by a police officer for disability resulting from heart disease contracted as a result of over exertion, over a period of years in the performance of his duties, which claim is made under the Act of 

September 27, 1951, P. L. 1473, providing that policemen and firemen injured in the performance of their duties shall be paid their salaries and medical expenses during disability, must be dismissed for lack of jurisdiction, since the act in question was not part of the Workmen's Compensation Act, and no jurisdiction is conferred on the Workmen's 

Compensation Board by said act. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee McGovern. 

District No. 8, claim petition no. 128,663, accident no. 

5997658, A-39573. Reversed. December 8, 1954.

Matson & Gatz, for claimant. 

George Shorall, for defendant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for Commonwealth of Pennsylvania.

MCGRATH, Commissioner: 

This case comes before the Workmen's Compensation 

Board upon an appeal by City of Pittsburgh, defendant, from an award in favor of Jacob Gerber, claimant. 

The facts are not in serious dispute. Claimant was employed by defendant as a police officer since 1926. His duties included walking a beat, making investigations, arrests and rescues. On one occasion, claimant was punched and knocked down by an irate husband whom he was trying to arrest; on another occasion, claimant was slashed by a razor wielded by a man about to be arrested and 47 stitches were required to claimant's temple. In November, 1950, during a very heavy snow, claimant was directed to remove a woman and her daughter, who were both ill, from their home to a hospital. Claimant had to carry the woman, who

weighed about 250 pounds, and her daughter, a distance of a block, since the street the house was on was not accessible by automobile due to the unusual snowfall. Claimant often took people to the hospital on stretchers, worked long hours in emergencies, such as floods, and at times did not return home for days. On November 30, 1951, claimant made a "pull" at the police box and felt a severe pain in his shoulder. 

He summoned a radio car, which took him to the Presbyterian Hospital. A diagnosis of heart attack was made. 

Claimant complains of still having pain in his shoulder and being unable to walk due to shortness of breath.

Medical testimony reveals claimant is totally disabled due to arteriosclerosis of the brain and heart, that claimant suffered a coronary occlusion in November of 1951. Claimant's witness, Dr. Irwin M. Pochapin, felt that the arteriosclerotic changes developed progressively as a result of the various exertions and difficulties encountered by claimant during his 25 years as a police officer, that claimant's present condition is directly traceable to his occupation. Defendant's medical witness, Dr. Frank J. Gregg, a cardiologist, testified that a coronary occlusion is due to an abnormal laying down of fat-like material, called cholesterol, in the lining of the aorta and its immediate branches; that claimant's condition was due to natural causes; that claimant would have had his present condition irrespective of his occupation. Prior to his heart attack, claimant was obese and weighed about 235 pounds; the doctor felt this was the important factor in claimant's condition. 

The primary question raised here is one of jurisdiction of the Workmen's Compensation Board. 

The claim is made under the Act of September 27, 1951, P. L. 1473, sec. 2, 53 PS §327, which provides: "Any policeman or fireman of any county, city, borough, town or township, who is injured in the performance of his duties including, in the case of firemen, duty as special fire police, and by reason thereof is temporarily incapacitated from performing his duties, shall be paid by the county, township or municipality, by which he is employed, his full rate of salary, as fixed by ordinance or resolution, until the

1954] 

 disability arising therefrom has ceased. All medical and hospital bills, incurred in connection with any such injury, shall be paid by such county, township or municipality. 

During the time salary for temporary incapacity shall be paid by the county, city, borough, town or township, any workmen's compensation received or collected by a policeman or fireman for such period, shall be turned over to such county, city, borough, town or township, and paid into the treasury thereof, and if such payment shall not be so made by the policeman or fireman, the amount so due the county, city, borough, town or township shall be deducted from any salary then or thereafter becoming due and owing: Provided, however, That in the case of salaried policemen and firemen, the diseases of the heart and tuberculosis of the respiratory system, contracted or incurred by any such policeman or fireman after four years of continuous service as such, and caused by extreme overexertion in times of stress or danger or by exposure to heat, smoke, fumes or gases, arising directly out of the employment of any such policeman and fireman, shall be compensable in accordance with the terms hereof; and unless any such disability shall be compensable under the compensation laws as having been caused by accidental injury, such disability shall be compensable as occupational disease disabilities are presently compensable under the compensation laws of this Commonwealth."

The act quoted is not part of the Workmen's Compensation Act. It is a separate law and jurisdiction is not conferred on the compensation authorities. We conclude that our judgment here would be unenforceable at law. 

Since we have already reviewed the record in some detail, and the problem is a novel one and one of first impression, we deem it not amiss to express our opinions, however irrelevant. 

The act appears to allow policemen and firemen their salaries for "diseases of the heart and tuberculosis of the respiratory system, contracted or incurred . . . after four years of continuous service as such, and caused by extreme overexertion in times of stress or danger.

In our judgment, coverage does not exist for mere overexertion, as the verbage used is "extreme overexertion". 

Nor would the disease or condition have to be the result of one particular incident, since the act refers to "times of stress or danger." Act of September 27, 1951, P. L. 1473, 53 PS §327. (Italics supplied.) 

Claimant's condition is one that is typically due to natural and normal causes. The burden rested upon him to prove that in this case, it was occasioned by extreme overexertion in times of stress during his employment. In our considered judgment, this burden was met. The extreme exertion called for on claimant's part by the unusual and extraordinary occurrences described, such as the flood of 1936 and snowfall of 1950, seem to render this claimant eligible for benefits under this act of Legislature. Although the medical testimony is not in accord, the opinion of Dr. 

Pochapin is sufficient to establish causal connection. 

However, since we have already concluded we are without jurisdiction, we are duty bound to vacate the referee's award to this claimant. We vacate the referee's third finding of fact, first and second conclusions of law and award, substituting in lieu thereof the following 

Findings of Fact 

3. That claimant, on November 30, 1951, suffered a heart attack while in the course of his employment with defendant; same being brought about by repeated extreme overexertion throughout his service as a policeman.

Conclusions of Law 1. The Workmen's Compensation Board concludes as a matter of law that this claim does not come within the 

Workmen's Compensation Act, as amended. 

2. The Workmen's Compensation Board concludes as a matter of law that the Workmen's Compensation Board is without authority to administer the Act of September 

1951, P. L. 1473.

Order The claim petition of Jacob Gerber, claimant, is dismissed.

1954] 

We hereby affirm the balance of the referee's findings of fact. 

Defendant's appeal is sustained.

COMMON PLEAS COURT NO. 3 OF PHILADELPHIA COUNTY NOLAN v. HAKE et al. 

Workmen's compensation—Permanent partial disability—Injury to fingers—Loss of more than one-half of phalange-Award for loss of one-half of finger-Section 306(c) of The Workmen's Compensation Act. 

Under section 306 (c) of The Workmen's Compensation Act, as amended, providing, inter alia, that the loss of the first phalange of a finger shall be considered equivalent to the loss of one-half of such a finger, the word "phalange" refers only to the bony structure of the finger, hence where claimant sustained a 90 percent loss of the length of the bone in one finger, although including the soft tissue there was only a loss of 50 percent of the stump length, and in the case of another finger sustained a 70 percent loss of bone length, and a 50 percent loss of stump length, his loss amounted to the loss of a first phalange and he was entitled to an award for the loss of one-half of each finger.

Appeal from Workmen's Compensation Board. C. P. No. 

3 of Philadelphia County, June term, 1954, no. 4789. Reversed. November 24, 1954. 

L. I. Boonin, for claimant. 

R. A. White, Jr., for defendant.

MACNEILLE, P. J.: 

Claimant, William J. Nolan, has appealed from an order of the Workmen's Compensation Board affirming the decision of the referee allowing defendant's petition to terminate a compensation agreement.

On December 22, 1952, claimant, while acting within the course of his employment, sustained an injury to the index

fingers of both of his hands. A compensation agreement was entered into between claimant and defendant, providing for the payment of compensation at the rate of $30 per week for an indefinite number of weeks. The accident and injury were described as follows in the agreement: "Jacking up tank and skid boards slipped; tank dropped on fingers. Injury to index fingers of both hands."

On April 27, 1953, defendant filed a petition for termination of the agreement, averring that claimant returned to work on January 26, 1953, without further loss of earnings or earning power. Claimant, by way of answer, denied the allegations of the petition and averred that he was suffering disability within the meaning of section 306 (c) of 

The Workmen's Compensation Act as amended. 

The referee, Hobson R. Reynolds, after hearing, determined that "the first phalanges of each of the index fingers of both the right and left hands of claimant, at approximately the same points, to wit, immediately below the fingernails, about one-half of the bony structure, were amputated"; that there was no functional loss of the use of either of his index fingers and concluded that claimant was not entitled to further compensation. The board affirmed the referee's findings and ruled that the matter is governed by the decision of the Superior Court in Vince v. 

Allegheny Pittsburgh Coal Co., 153 Pa. Superior Ct. 333 (1943); that claimant is not suffering from the loss of the first phalanges of the index fingers of either his right or left hands.

Dr. Arthur Stiffel testified on behalf of defendant that there was no involvement of the joints in either index finger; that the joint space in each is clear and functional. 

He stated that the flesh or pulp extends beyond the bone toward the top of the finger; that considering both flesh and bone there has been approximately a 50 percent loss. 

Dr. Richard Kaplan also testified on behalf of defendant. 

1 The board modified and corrected the referee's findings with regard to the date of the accident and the duration of the period during which compensation was paid; but such modifications are not pertinent to the matter now before us.

1954]  1 He stated that the soft tissue and greater part of the distal phalanx of the right index finger has been amputated; that the left index finger shows a larger stump. He stated that there was "approximately a 50 percent loss of pulp but approximately 90 percent loss of the distal (right) phalangeal joint and 70 percent of the distal left phalangeal bone". 

He explained that there is 90 percent loss of the length of the bone in the right index finger, but including the soft tissue or pulp, there is only a loss of approximately 50 percent of the stump length; in the left index finger there is approximately a 70 percent loss of bone length and approximately a 50 percent loss of stump length. 

Section 306 (c) of The Workmen's Compensation Act of 

June 2, 1915, P. L. 736, as amended, 77 PS §513, under which compensation is here claimed, provides, inter alia, as follows: 

"For all disability resulting from permanent injuries of the following classes, the compensation shall be exclusively as follows: 

"For the loss of a first finger, commonly called index finger, sixty-six and two-thirds percentage of wages during thirty-five weeks. . . "The loss of the first phalange of the thumb, or of any finger, shall be considered equivalent to the loss of one-half of such thumb or finger and shall be compensated at the same rate as for the loss of a thumb or finger, but for onehalf of the period provided for the loss of a thumb or finger."

The first question for our consideration is whether the term "phalange" as used in the statute has reference only to the bony structure of the finger or whether it comprehends the combination of bone and soft tissue or pulp. Upon the answer to this question hinges the validity of the referee's findings of fact. Interestingly enough, both parties rely upon the Vince case, cited supra. Defendant contends that it was there held that the term "phalange" as used in 

The Workmen's Compensation Act has reference to both bone and flesh; claimant contends that it has reference to the bony structure alone. Both rely upon the footnote which appears as follows (153 Pa. Superior Ct. at pp. 335-6):

"Phalange' is used in our Statute but 'phalanx' seems to be preferred by our lexicographers. These words are synonymous and used interchangeably in the decisions of the various courts. Gould's Medical Dictionary, Third Edition, defines 'phalanx' as ‘one of the bones of the fingers or toes'.
