Webster's New International Dictionary defines it as ... one of the digital bones of the hand or foot beyond the metacarpus or metatarsus' so that a phalanx does not include flesh, only the bony structure of a finger or toe."

We think that it was the sufficiently clear meaning of the court that both "phalanx" and "phalange" are to be regarded as synonymous usage; that either word must be considered as being a reference only to the bony structure. 

So considered it is quite clear from this record that the referee's finding of fact that there was only a 50 percent, or about one-half, loss of the distal phalanges is incorrect; there was a 90 percent loss of the right distal phalange, and a 70 percent loss of the left distal phalange. Insofar as the referee phrased his finding as a loss of "about one-half of the bony structure" that is in patent conflict with the record and is not supported by the evidence there were 

90 percent and 70 percent losses of the bony structure. The board erred in affirming the referee's third finding of fact. 

The final question for our consideration is whether such losses as described above come within the province of the statutory language providing compensation for “loss of the first phalange". The defendant again relies upon the Vince case and contends that by its authority "to get one-half of the loss of use of a finger, all of the said joint, first phalange, must have been amputated, or lost-and if some part remains, there cannot be any award".

We have carefully reviewed the Vince case and the dissent thereto and are of the opinion that it is not authority for defendant's contention. In that case the crucial conclusion of the majority of the court was to the effect that the evidence did not warrant a finding that more than one-half of the phalanx had been lost. The majority did not reject 

Judge Rhodes' view of the law but, instead, deemed it inapplicable to the facts. The majority opinion by Judge Bal-

1954] 

 dridge makes reference to the rule in In re Petri, 215 N. Y. 

335, 109 N. E. 549, that compensation should be allowed, in accordance with the canon of fair and liberal construction of the compensation laws, when it appears that substantially all of the portion of the finger so designated has been lost. The case of Forbes v. Evening Mail, 185 N. Y. 

Supp. 592, 194 App. Div. 563, is an affirmation of the Petri rule. We do not think the reference to Maxwell's Case, 119 

Me. 504, 111 Atl. 849, by Judge Baldridge (at page 339) was intended as a repudiation of the rule in the Petri case. 

In Nixon v. Allegheny Pittsburgh Coal Co., 55 D. & C. 

509 (1945), where claimant sustained a loss of three-fourths of the distal phalange of his left middle finger, the court concluded that the Vince case did not pass upon the question of whether or not a loss of more than one-half of the phalange constitutes substantially all of the phalange and, therefore, supports an award under section 306 (c). We share this view that the holding of the Vince case is not controlling in the matter before us. 

With the question then here raised squarely, we are of the opinion that the Petri 2 case sets forth the correct view to be taken in this matter. In the Petri case, the New York 

Court of Appeals stated: 

"Under these provisions it is argued by the appellants that compensation can be awarded as for the loss of a phalange only in case the entire phalange has been lost, and that, in a case where only part thereof has been lost, compensation must be sought under the general clause last quoted. We are not able to agree with this contention on the evidence and findings made in this case. It very likely may be that the loss might be of such a minor portion of the phalange that an award could not be sustained under the clauses which have been quoted, as for a loss of the entire phalange, but that does not seem to be this case. 

While the findings of the commission are somewhat contradictory and rather unsatisfactory, in that they state in one place, that the amputation of the phalange occurred 

2 Cited with approval in Chingola v. Penn Iron & Steel Co., 86 Pa.

Superior Ct. 514 (1926), for the doctrine of liberal construction.

near the first joint and in another place that about one-third of the bone of the distal phalange was cut off, we think that construed together, and in the light of the evidence, they may be regarded as stating that substantially all of the phalange was cut off, and on that theory, the award may be sustained. (Italics supplied.) "The Workmen's Compensation Law was adopted in deference to a widespread belief and demand that compensation should be awarded to workmen who were injured and disabled temporarily or permanently in the course of their employment, even though sometimes the accident might occur under such circumstances as would not permit a recovery in an ordinary action at law. The underlying thought was that such a system of compensation would be in the interest of the general welfare by preventing a workman from being deprived of means of support as the result of an injury received in the course of his employment. The 

Statute was the expression of what was regarded by the Legislature as a wise public policy concerning injured employees. Under such circumstances, we think that it is to be interpreted with fair liberality, to the end of securing the benefits which it was intended to accomplish. Applying these rules to what happens to be in this case, an accident of minor importance, we think we should hold that the provisions of the Statute providing compensation for the loss of a certain portion of the finger become operative and applicable when it appears that substantially all of the portion of the finger so designated has been lost, and that we should not interpret such provisions too narrowly for the purpose of defeating a recovery.”

In this case, claimant sustained losses of substantially all of the bony structure of the first phalange of each of the index fingers. He is entitled to an award under the provi sions of section 306 (c) of The Workmen's Compensation 

Act.

Order 

And now, November 4, 1954, the within case is remanded to the Workmen's Compensation Board for the entry of an award in accordance with the annexed opinion.
